# Full reader source bundle

Line numbers refer to frozen Markdown files. Hidden Vale comments are not reader text. Sidecar blocks supply captions, tables, code and figures referenced by {{block:...}}. A source link is evidence access, not by itself a missing prerequisite.

## FILE guide/00-reading-guide.md | ch:READING-GUIDE
L0001: <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerHeadings = NO -->
L0002: # What the three papers establish {#ch:READING-GUIDE}
L0003: <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerHeadings = YES -->
L0004: 
L0005: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0006: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0007: The subject is neuroscience: how groups of nerve cells represent information used in memory, eye control, and navigation. The three papers examine mathematical models that store a quantity such as an angle, eye movements and neural activity recorded in macaque monkeys, and location-dependent neural activity recorded in rodents and bats. [They ask how activity can retain a value, how an internal fluctuation can move the eyes, and why a cell can respond at several separate locations.]{.key-statement}
L0008: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0009: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0010: 
L0011: ## Variable neural signals must support reliable behavior {#sec:READING-FIELD}
L0012: 
L0013: <!-- vale ai-tells.BareHolds = NO -->
L0014: A remembered direction must remain useful after its cue disappears. The eyes must hold an angle while an animal looks at a target. A moving animal must keep track of where it is. These tasks depend on groups of [[**neuron**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}s, the brain's signaling cells]{.definition-explanation}, whose electrical signals are brief and variable. Computational and systems neuroscience studies how these cells and their interactions support such behavior. Mathematical models connect assumptions about cells and their interactions to predictions. Recordings test those predictions against neural activity and movement.
L0015: <!-- vale ai-tells.BareHolds = YES -->
L0016: 
L0017: ## Persistent activity can still lose memory accuracy {#sec:READING-MEMORY}
L0018: 
L0019: Burak and Fiete (2012) study short-term memory for a continuous quantity, such as a direction or eye angle. A proposed mechanism is that interacting neurons keep activity going after the original input ends. [Yet the remembered value can change while the activity persists.]{.key-statement} The problem is to explain how the properties of the cells and their connections set the rate of this loss of accuracy.
L0020: 
L0021: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0022: The authors analyze a network with a continuous family of persistent activity patterns, each representing a different value. Each cell sends signals at random times. They use an approximation for small fluctuations to derive how these signals move the stored value, compare the calculation with network simulations, and relate the motion to the best accuracy possible when estimating the stored value from those signals.
L0023: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0024: 
L0025: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0026: [Their central result is a lower bound: the uncertainty in reading the network's current value limits how slowly that value can wander.]{.key-statement} Under the model's assumptions, the variance of the memory error grows with storage time. Reading for longer also has diminishing benefit because the state continues moving during observation. This gives a benchmark for evaluating proposed memory circuits and their recall errors. It applies to the analyzed family of continuous-state memory models, with the required noise assumptions and no external signal anchoring the value. Chapters 3–6 develop the [diffusion calculation](../chapters/ch-memory-dynamics.html), its long-delay behavior, the [information bound](../chapters/ch-memory-information.html), and the limits of [readout](../chapters/ch-memory-readout.html).[^orientation-memory]
L0027: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0028: 
L0029: ## Eye drift points to a shared neural source {#sec:READING-EYE}
L0030: 
L0031: <!-- vale ai-tells.BareReaches = NO -->
L0032: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0033: [Even while gaze is held on a stationary target, the eyes move smoothly and irregularly between brief, rapid movements. This smooth motion is called [**fixational drift**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}.]{.definition-explanation} It changes the image reaching the [[**retina**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, the light-sensitive tissue at the back of the eye]{.definition-explanation}. Understanding its source is therefore part of understanding the visual signals the brain receives. [Ben-Shushan and colleagues (2022) ask whether the motion begins mainly in muscles or in neural commands sent to them.]{.key-statement}
L0034: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0035: <!-- vale ai-tells.BareReaches = YES -->
L0036: 
L0037: They reanalyze recordings from 57 neurons that send commands to eye muscles in two macaques, paired with precise eye-position measurements. They first calibrate the relation between each cell's activity and larger eye movements. They then use it during fixation to compare the movement predicted from one cell with the measured movement. A separate circuit model tests whether a wandering stored eye-position command could explain the movement's amplitude and time dependence.
L0038: 
L0039: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0040: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0041: For 350-millisecond intervals, their analysis estimates that about 82% of horizontal displacement variance comes from a common source before the final motor neurons, assuming the fitted movement relation also holds during fixation and the relevant noise contributions do not vary together. This supports a central neural contribution to drift. The memory-circuit model provides a possible explanation of that contribution, linking an internal storage error to observable behavior. The recordings do not uniquely locate the noise inside that memory circuit. Chapters 7–9 separate the [shared-drive inference](../chapters/ch-eye-drift.html), the [timing of drift](../chapters/ch-eye-timing.html), and the [proposed circuit mechanism](../chapters/ch-eye-circuit.html).[^orientation-eye]
L0042: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0043: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0044: 
L0045: ## Smooth random maps explain the variety of place fields {#sec:READING-PLACE}
L0046: 
L0047: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPaddingStacked = NO -->
L0048: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0049: [A [**place cell**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} in the [**hippocampus**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a brain region involved in memory and navigation, becomes more active in particular locations.]{.definition-explanation} [Each connected region of elevated activity is a [**place field**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}.]{.definition-explanation} Small environments encouraged a picture of one smooth field per cell. Larger environments reveal multiple fields with widely varying sizes and shapes. [Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak (2025) ask whether one statistical account can explain this variety across species and spatial dimensions.]{.key-statement}
L0050: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0051: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPaddingStacked = YES -->
L0052: 
L0053: Their model gives each cell a smooth random function of location and allows activity where that function exceeds a threshold. The authors derive predictions for the resulting regions, simulate the model, and compare it with existing rodent and bat recordings from one-, two- and three-dimensional environments. Parameters fitted to field sizes and their spatial arrangement also support further tests of field shapes and connectedness.
L0054: 
L0055: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0056: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0057: The model explains different size distributions across dimensions and predicts additional features, such as multiple peaks within a field and inactive holes surrounded by activity. This makes irregular spatial responses quantitatively understandable and supports a common statistical description across the examined settings. Such statistics can arise when cells combine many inputs through largely random connections. The study does not directly measure those connections. Chapters 10–12 develop the [spatial model](../chapters/ch-place-fields.html), its [size and shape predictions](../chapters/ch-place-sizes.html), and the [tests and circuit interpretation](../chapters/ch-place-tests.html). They distinguish exact predictions, limiting approximations and empirical comparisons.[^orientation-place]
L0058: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0059: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0060: 
L0061: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0062: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0063: <!-- vale ai-tells.ServesAsDodge = NO -->
L0064: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0065: The three contributions therefore have different meanings: a mathematical constraint on memory models, evidence about the source of eye movement, and a statistical explanation of spatial activity. Together they show how a representation can be described, maintained and related to behavior. Combining all three in one biological circuit remains a further research question, developed in [Chapter 13](../chapters/ch-synthesis.html).
L0066: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0067: <!-- vale ai-tells.ServesAsDodge = YES -->
L0068: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0069: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0070: 
L0071: ## Electronic implementations make memory mechanisms testable {#sec:READING-HARDWARE}
L0072: 
L0073: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0074: [Part III](../parts/part-hardware.html) asks how the neural memory mechanisms can be implemented in electronic circuits. The setting is neuromorphic computing, where engineered systems implement computations inspired by neural organization and dynamics. The chapters distinguish connection weights from changing neural states, explain how feedback affects retention, and examine selected hardware research through October 30, 2026. They end with a proposed experiment that separates systematic drift, random spreading, and readout error. This extension is a comparison of mechanisms and evidence. The original papers remain the foundation of Parts I and II.
L0075: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0076: 
L0077: ## Simulations separate the model from the evidence used to test it {#sec:READING-SIMULATIONS}
L0078: 
L0079: [Part IV](../parts/part-simulations.html) follows the computational methods in Burak and Fiete (2012), Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), and Mainali et al. (2025). It distinguishes simulated neural trajectories, recorded-data analysis, and generated spatial maps. Each chapter identifies the model states, numerical procedure, measurements, available code, and limits on reproducing the published figures. The final chapter compares tools and explains resolution and repeatability checks. These are explanations of the sources, not a claim that the guide reran their full simulations.
L0080: 
L0081: Part introductions identify the paper behind each chapter's result using author and year. **Central** marks a main finding of that paper. **Supporting** marks an analysis used to establish or qualify it. **Background** supplies needed concepts. **Guide-derived** marks expanded explanations, comparisons, or proposed tests developed here. A result can be central in its own paper while serving as supporting evidence in another argument.
L0082: 
L0083: ## Starting knowledge and reading paths {#sec:READING-START}
L0084: 
L0085: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0086: The guide assumes undergraduate calculus, basic probability, and linear algebra. Neuroscience terms and the required stochastic-process, information, filtering, and random-field ideas are taught where needed. The reading path builds from the signals to the models and then their tests:
L0087: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->
L0088: 
L0089:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0090: - **Chapters 1–2:** establish [neural signals and variability](../chapters/ch-foundations.html), then [persistence and accumulated error](../chapters/ch-persistence.html).
L0091:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0092:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0093:   <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0094: - **Chapters 3–6:** derive memory diffusion, its long-delay behavior, the information bound, and readout.
L0095:   <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0096:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0097:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0098: - **Chapters 7–9:** follow the eye study from shared-drive evidence through timing to a circuit model.
L0099:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0100:   <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0101: - **Chapters 10–12:** develop spatial maps, their size predictions, and tests of their shapes and possible origins.
L0102:   <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0103: - **Chapter 13:** connect the results and compare related work.
L0104: 
L0105: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = NO -->
L0106: For a path focused on particular papers:
L0107: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = YES -->
L0108: 
L0109: - **Memory and eye drift:** follow chapters 1 through 9 in order.
L0110:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0111: - **Place fields:** start with [tuning curves, population codes, and rate maps](../chapters/ch-foundations.html#sec-foundations-spikes), then [variable spike counts](../chapters/ch-foundations.html#sec-foundations-poisson), and [fixed spatial maps versus values that change over time](../chapters/ch-persistence.html#sec-foundations-randomness). Continue with chapters 10–12. This short route supplies the shared foundations without requiring the eye-mechanics or memory-readout calculations.
L0112:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0113: 
L0114: The Glossary gives short definitions for lookup after their first explanation. The References section gives full source details. The original paper figures are discussed by number and can be inspected in the linked PDFs.
L0115: 
L0116: After the foundations, the [guide map](../chapters/ch-persistence.html#fig-foundations-paper-map) shows the three paper questions together.
L0117: 
L0118: On a first reading, follow the main text, figures, assumptions, and results. The expandable notes preserve longer calculations and extensions. Open one when you want to check the steps behind a result. The explanations needed for the next section remain in the main text.
L0119: 
L0120: ## Neural mechanisms and mathematical derivations answer different questions {#sec:READING-MECHANISM-MATH}
L0121: 
L0122: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0123: Chapters 3–6 explain [how interacting neurons can retain a value while random spike timing changes it.]{.key-statement} Their treatment of the Burak and Fiete appendix separates three parts of each substantial topic:
L0124: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0125: 
L0126:    <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0127: 1. **Neural mechanism:** which cells and signals interact, what changes, and which features are assumptions of the model. For readout, this part also states what an observer can measure and which value it must estimate.
L0128:    <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0129:    <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0130: 2. **Mathematical derivation:** the general tool, its conditions, and the calculation after neural quantities are assigned to the symbols.
L0131:    <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0132: 3. **Interpretation:** what the result predicts about memory or readout, and what its assumptions leave unresolved.
L0133: 
L0134: <!-- vale ai-tells.StackedAnaphora = NO -->
L0135: [An equation that specifies how a cell responds belongs to the model. A derivation works out what follows from that choice.]{.key-statement}
L0136: <!-- vale ai-tells.StackedAnaphora = YES -->
L0137: 
L0138: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0139: For example, a spike can disturb several cells' activity. The circuit's interactions remove some changes, while others leave the represented angle displaced. Linear algebra calculates which part survives. Probability gives the spread of that displacement. The result then predicts how quickly memory errors accumulate in this model. The mathematical tools also apply to other systems, but this neural prediction depends on the circuit and spike statistics used in the calculation.
L0140: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0141: 
L0142: The same distinction matters for readout. A calculation of the best possible estimate sets a benchmark under specified access to neural signals. Establishing that an animal's neurons implement the assumed computation requires separate evidence.
L0143: 
L0144: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeHolds = NO -->
L0145: For a first conceptual pass, read the mechanism, inspect the displayed result and its variables, then read the interpretation. Follow the mathematical derivation when you want to understand why the result holds or calculate with it. Assumptions remain part of both readings.
L0146: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeHolds = YES -->
L0147: 
L0148: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0149: The [PNAS appendix](https://www.pnas.org/action/downloadSupplement?doi=10.1073%2Fpnas.1117386109&file=Appendix.pdf) supplies both neural-model choices and mathematical arguments. This map links its seven sections to the guide's explanations. Page links open the local copy. The guide sometimes uses an equivalent teaching derivation and identifies source inconsistencies beside the affected calculation.
L0150: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->
L0151: 
L0152: {{block:tbl:READING-APPENDIX}}
L0153: 
L0154: ## How to interpret the calculations and evidence {#sec:READING-SCOPE}
L0155: 
L0156: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0157: Examples chosen for the guide distinguish independent from shared fluctuations, complete from limited access to neural signals, and exact formulas from approximations for rare fields. Their numbers are checked separately from the published experimental measurements. A teaching reconstruction is labeled when its simplified model differs from the paper's full model.
L0158: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->
L0159: 
L0160: <!-- vale ai-tells.RestatementMarkers = NO -->
L0161: The guide explains how to interpret the experiments and their figures. It does not rerun the original animal recordings, data processing, or full neural-network simulations. [A successful numerical check of a probability identity is different from an independent reproduction of a paper's empirical result.]{.key-statement} Related work is selected to clarify the assumptions and alternatives rather than to claim an exhaustive literature search.
L0162: <!-- vale ai-tells.RestatementMarkers = YES -->
L0163: 
L0164: The following map is a **guide-derived comparison** of the source papers' contributions. Read each paper's column downward: the mathematical prediction, numerical comparison, and animal-data comparison answer different questions.[^orientation-memory][^orientation-eye][^orientation-place]
L0165: 
L0166: <div class="table-responsive">
L0167: 
L0168: {{block:tbl:READING-EVIDENCE}}
L0169: 
L0170: </div>
L0171: 
L0172: Throughout the guide, $D$ denotes the [[**diffusion coefficient**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, which sets how quickly uncertainty spreads]{.definition-explanation}. In the basic one-dimensional model, $D$ is constant, neither direction is favored, and no boundary limits the motion. Its [[**mean squared displacement**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} (MSD), the average squared change in the represented coordinate after elapsed time $t$]{.definition-explanation}, is $2Dt$. [Chapter 2](../chapters/ch-persistence.html#sec-foundations-diffusion) explains this model and its assumptions.
L0173: 
L0174: The other recurring symbols are:
L0175: 
L0176: - $d$: spatial dimension.
L0177: - $\theta$: the represented coordinate in the memory chapters.
L0178: - $q$: the normalized threshold in the place-field chapters, called $\theta$ in that paper.
L0179: - $C$: the spatial covariance in the place-field chapters.
L0180: 
L0181: ## The original papers {#sec:READING-PAPERS}
L0182: 
L0183: The copies are the published versions. Supporting information is linked separately.
L0184: 
L0185:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0186: - **Burak and Fiete (2012):** [Paper PDF](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf) and [20-page supporting information PDF](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf). *Fundamental limits on persistent activity in networks of noisy neurons*. PNAS, 109(43), 17645–17650. [Published DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109). The local main PDF uses the six-page Early Edition layout, so PDF page locators run from 1 to 6.
L0187:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0188:   <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0189: - **Ben-Shushan, Shaham, Joshua, and Burak (2022):** [Paper PDF](../papers/ben-shushan-2022-fixational-drift.pdf), [supplementary notes and figures PDF](../papers/ben-shushan-2022-supplement-1.pdf), and [open peer review PDF](../papers/ben-shushan-2022-supplement-2.pdf). *Fixational drift is driven by diffusive dynamics in central neural circuitry*. Nature Communications, 13, 1697. [Published DOI](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y). Ben-Shushan and Shaham contributed equally.
L0190:   <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0191:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0192: - **Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak (2025):** [Paper and STAR Methods PDF](../papers/mainali-2025-universal-statistics.pdf) and [supplementary figures and Methods S1–S4 PDF](../papers/mainali-2025-supplement.pdf). *Universal statistics of hippocampal place fields across species and dimensionalities*. Neuron, 113(7), 1110–1120.e3. [Published DOI](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017). The main PDF has a cover before the article. Guide citations distinguish printed and PDF page numbers when needed.
L0193:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0194: 
L0195: The supporting-information copies preserve their original formulas. The guide identifies apparent inconsistencies in several printed intermediate equations beside the affected calculations and follows an explicitly shown derivation. Such observations are independent mathematical checks, not author-confirmed corrections.
L0196: 
L0197: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0198: [^orientation-memory]: Burak, Y., and Fiete, I. R. (2012). *Fundamental limits on persistent activity in networks of noisy neurons*. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 109(43), 17645–17650. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109). Abstract and Introduction, PDF p. 1; Results, Eqs. 1–5 and Figs. 1–3, PDF pp. 2–4; Discussion, PDF p. 5. The readout duration follows the recall cue and is distinct from the preceding storage delay.

Introduction and model, journal pp. 1110–1112; field sizes and shape tests, Figs. 1–3, pp. 1112–1116; Discussion, pp. 1117–1118.   - Appendix section
  - Neural question
  - Mathematical explanation in the guide
  rows:
  - - '[I: large-network limit, p. 1](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=1)'
    - How can many variable spike inputs produce small fluctuations in total input?
    - '[Mean and variance scaling; Gaussian approximation](../chapters/ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-weak-noise)'
  - - '[II: diffusion coefficient, p. 3](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=3)'
    - Which changes in neural activity leave the stored value displaced?
    - '[Linearization and projection](../chapters/ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-linearization),
      [diffusion](../chapters/ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-diffusion), and [systematic
      drift](../chapters/ch-memory-long-delays.html#sec-memory-drift-scope)'
  - - '[III: motion along the attractor, p. 6](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=6)'
    - How do memory errors grow over long delays or around an angular range?
    - '[Probability-density evolution and boundary conditions](../chapters/ch-memory-long-delays.html#sec-memory-long-time)'
  - - '[IV: internal Fisher information, p. 9](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=9)'
    - How well can nearby stored values be distinguished from their spikes?
    - '[Spike likelihood and information per unit time](../chapters/ch-memory-information.html#sec-memory-fi)'
  - - '[V: information–diffusion inequality, p. 10](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=10)'
    - How does uncertainty in the circuit’s own spikes constrain memory stability?
    - '[Scalar and matrix bounds, with equality conditions](../chapters/ch-memory-information.html#sec-memory-bound)'
  - - '[VI: readout, p. 12](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=12)'
    - How much does longer observation help when the stored value keeps moving?
    - '[Storage and readout error](../chapters/ch-memory-readout.html#sec-memory-readout-setup),
      [equal weighting](../chapters/ch-memory-readout.html#sec-memory-naive), [ideal
      readout](../chapters/ch-memory-readout.html#sec-memory-ideal), and [partial
      access](../chapters/ch-memory-readout.html#sec-memory-partial)'
  - - '[VII: model extensions, p. 17](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=17)'
    - What changes when firing rates respond gradually or the noise law changes?
    - '[Two time scales and alternative noise statistics](../chapters/ch-memory-readout.html#sec-memory-extensions)'

- id: tbl:READING-EVIDENCE
  kind: table
  placement: sec:READING-SCOPE
  headers:
  - Step in the evidence
  - Burak and Fiete (2012)
  - Ben-Shushan et al. (2022)
  - Mainali et al. (2025)
  rows:
  - - Physical system and question
    - A proposed neural circuit maintaining a continuous value after a cue ends. How
      quickly does its accuracy deteriorate?
    - Motor-neuron activity and eye motion during macaque fixation. Where does the
      shared motion originate?
    - Place-cell responses in rodent and bat environments. Why do field sizes and
      shapes vary?
  - - Actors, variables, and interactions
    - Synaptic activations $s_i$ set rates $r_i=\phi(\sum_j W_{ij}s_j+b_i)$ through
      weights $W_{ij}$ and constant inputs $b_i$. Spikes renew activation and disturb
      the represented coordinate.
    - Two neural populations produce command $E_{\mathrm{OI}}$. Motor spikes, muscles,
      and delayed feedback connect it to actual eye angle $E$. The two angles are
      different signals.
    - Cell $i$ receives smooth Gaussian input $h_i(\mathbf x)$ at position $\mathbf
      x$. Activity occurs above a threshold; the spatial map is fixed during analysis.
  - - Derived prediction
    - Spike fluctuations produce memory diffusion. An information bound limits stability
      under the model assumptions.
    - Shared and private fluctuations imply displacement-covariance relations. Filtering
      a diffusing command predicts the time dependence of eye motion.
    - Covariance, threshold, and dimension determine field statistics, with exact
      results and high-threshold approximations kept separate.
  - - Simulation comparison
    - Stochastic ring and two-population networks test theoretical memory diffusion.
      These are model tests, not animal-data validation.
    - A noisy integrator and motor pathway test whether the proposed mechanism can
      produce the observed motion and analysis results.
    - Generated spatial maps test analytical approximations and provide finite-threshold
      comparisons with recorded field statistics.
  - - Empirical comparison
    - This study does not directly test the bound against animal recordings.
    - Paired single-neuron and eye recordings support a substantial shared source
      upstream of the final motor neurons, under the inference assumptions.
    - Existing rodent and bat recordings test field sizes, arrangements, shapes, and
      connectedness. Parameters fitted to summaries remain distinct from additional
      comparisons.
  - - What remains unknown
    - Whether a biological memory circuit meets the assumptions or approaches the
      bound.
    - Whether the shared source is specifically the modeled integrator. A successful
      model does not uniquely locate it.
    - Which connections, learning rules, and dynamics generate the spatial inputs.
      A statistical fit does not establish a memory circuit.
  caption: Guide-derived map of the three source papers' evidence. Each column follows
    a separate study; the table does not claim a jointly tested circuit.
  first_column_role: row-labels
  html_fold: never


# PART: Noise limits memory accuracy in continuous attractor models
In a model neural circuit, a pattern of firing rates across many nerve cells can represent a remembered angle. Random spike timing can change that angle while activity persists. The central source, [Burak and Fiete (2012)](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109), derives how quickly accuracy can be lost and how well the value can be read. Chapters 1 and 2 establish the background. Chapters 3 and 5 develop the central diffusion and information results. Chapters 4 and 6 explain long-delay behavior and readout. Expanded examples and marked checks of printed formulas are guide-derived.
In a model neural circuit, a pattern of firing rates across many nerve cells can represent a remembered angle. Random spike timing can change that angle while activity persists. The central source, [Burak and Fiete (2012)](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109), derives how quickly accuracy can be lost and how well the value can be read. Chapters 1 and 2 establish the background. Chapters 3 and 5 develop the central diffusion and information results. Chapters 4 and 6 explain long-delay behavior and readout. Expanded examples and marked checks of printed formulas are guide-derived.
The contribution labels describe the role of each result here. **Central** is a main finding of the cited paper. **Supporting** establishes or qualifies that finding. **Background** supplies needed concepts. **Guide-derived** identifies an explanation, comparison, or proposed test developed in this guide.
::: {.part-chapter-map}

- **[Chapter 1: Patterns of variable spikes can represent a value](../chapters/ch-foundations.html)**

  **Background and model:** Spikes leave decaying synaptic activation; weighted activation sets input, and input sets firing rate. Equations 7–9 of Burak and Fiete (2012) establish these dynamics before the chapters on stability and diffusion. Neural coding and spike variability supply the remaining quantities for the memory calculation. [Burak and Fiete (2012)](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109) supply the Poisson network model. The chapter also explains its **supporting simulation test**: simulated recurrent networks reproduce the predicted memory spreading in the modeled circuits. [Ben-Shushan et al. (2022)](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y) and [Mainali et al. (2025)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017) supply eye-control and spatial-coding context. The elementary numerical examples are guide-derived.

- **[Chapter 2: Stable representations can accumulate error](../chapters/ch-persistence.html)**

  **Background and mechanism:** A stable pattern of neural activity can retain a value while noise moves that value along a family of states. This is the continuous-memory setup of [Burak and Fiete (2012)](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109). The two-variable illustrations are guide-derived.

- **[Chapter 3: Spiking noise produces diffusion along the memory attractor](../chapters/ch-memory-dynamics.html)**

  **Central result:** [Burak and Fiete (2012)](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109) derive memory diffusion by projecting spike fluctuations through the network dynamics. The symmetric formula uses the authors' [2017 correction](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1706051114). Expanded projection examples are guide-derived.

- **[Chapter 4: Boundaries and drift change memory error over long delays](../chapters/ch-memory-long-delays.html)**

  **Supporting results:** [Burak and Fiete (2012)](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109) explain long-delay behavior, angular boundaries, and state-dependent diffusion. These qualify how the central diffusion result can be used. The guide adds explicit boundary calculations and distinguishes its checks of other printed equations from the official correction.

- **[Chapter 5: Spike information limits memory stability](../chapters/ch-memory-information.html)**

  **Central result:** [Burak and Fiete (2012)](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109) relate the information in spikes to a lower bound on memory diffusion. The guide expands the proofs and labels its counterexamples and comparisons of fixed quantities.

- **[Chapter 6: Readout accuracy depends on how a moving memory is observed](../chapters/ch-memory-readout.html)**

  **Central readout result and supporting extensions:** [Burak and Fiete (2012)](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109) derive limits on estimating a value that moves during observation. Partial access and alternative time scales extend that result. The guide supplies intermediate calculations and identifies source formulas needing qualification.

:::

## FILE guide/01-foundations.md | ch:FOUNDATIONS
L0001: # Patterns of variable spikes can represent a value {#ch:FOUNDATIONS}
L0002: 
L0003: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0004: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0005: The eyes can hold a position after a brief movement command has ended. An animal can also keep track of its location while the activity of individual neurons fluctuates. [Both abilities require a useful quantity to remain represented in a changing biological system.]{.key-statement} The three papers study different parts of this problem: the loss of a stored value, the origin of slow eye motion, and the arrangement of spatial firing patterns.
L0006: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0007: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0008: 
L0009: Before asking how a circuit keeps a value, we need to know what its signals do. We begin with brief neural spikes and the eye muscles they activate, explaining how repeated activation can maintain an eye angle. We next establish the equations that connect spikes, synaptic input and firing rate. With that physical meaning in place, we ask how a pattern of rates represents an angle or an animal's location. A model of spike counts shows why one brief observation can be uncertain and how combining counts from several cells can help. We then follow these ingredients through the 2012 network simulation: what the computer updates, how the cells interact, and what the comparison with theory establishes. Finally, we separate shared variation, random spreading and a change in the average value. These distinctions prepare us to interpret both a memory model and measurements of eye motion.
L0010: 
L0011: ## Repeated spikes maintain eye muscle tension {#sec:FOUNDATIONS-EYE-ACTION}
L0012: 
L0013: For the eye to stay directed at a target, its muscles must keep exerting force after the movement command ends.
L0014: 
L0015: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO -->
L0016: [A neuron communicates through brief electrical events called [**spike**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}s.]{.definition-explanation} [A [**firing rate**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the expected number of spikes per unit time.]{.definition-explanation} A rate of 20 hertz means an average of 20 spikes per second under the specified conditions, rather than a promise of one spike every 50 milliseconds.
L0017: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES -->
L0018: 
L0019: **Anatomy: muscles turn the eyeball.** In humans and macaques, the eyeball rotates within the bony eye socket, or orbit. [The six [**extraocular muscles**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} attach to the outside of the eyeball and control its orientation.]{.definition-explanation} The medial rectus pulls it toward the nose; the lateral rectus pulls it away. The superior and inferior rectus and the two oblique muscles contribute to vertical movement and rotation about the line of sight. Their actions depend on the eye's orientation.[^p-eye-anatomy]
L0020: 
L0021: [**Ocular motoneurons (OMNs)**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} [are neurons that activate these muscles through long nerve fibres.]{.definition-explanation} Their cell bodies lie in brainstem groups, and these fibres travel through three cranial nerves. The abducens nerve, VI, supplies the lateral rectus; the trochlear nerve, IV, supplies the superior oblique; the oculomotor nerve, III, supplies the other four. For horizontal gaze, changing the relative pull of the medial and lateral rectus rotates the eye. [A force's turning effect is its [**torque**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}.]{.definition-explanation} Opposing torques can balance even while the muscles remain under tension.[^p-eye-anatomy]
L0022: 
L0023: **Repeated spikes maintain tension.** Each motor spike affects muscle force for longer than the electrical event lasts. Repeated activation and the combined action of many muscle fibres give a smoother force than a sequence of isolated eye movements. [**Tonic firing**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} [means sustained firing during the holding period.]{.definition-explanation} It need not be perfectly periodic. During a rapid repositioning movement, called a [**saccade**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a brief strong drive moves the eye; a sustained position-dependent drive then holds the new angle. This is the pulse-and-step description: the step means continuing drive, not one more spike.[^p-eye-mechanics][^p-eye-plasticity]
L0024: 
L0025: The mechanical properties contribute in different ways:
L0026: 
L0027: - [**Elasticity**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} [creates restoring forces when muscles and surrounding tissues are stretched.]{.definition-explanation} At an off-centre gaze angle, active muscle torque must balance the restoring tendency.
L0028: - [**Viscosity**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} [resists motion and dissipates mechanical energy.]{.definition-explanation} It damps rapid changes but supplies no holding torque once velocity is zero.
L0029: - [**Inertia**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} [is resistance to a change in angular velocity.]{.definition-explanation} It matters during acceleration, not as a force holding a stationary eye. In primate eye movements, elastic and viscous effects dominate inertia.[^p-eye-mechanics]
L0030: 
L0031: [Mechanical damping smooths movement; sustained neural drive selects the angle around which the eye is held. Inertia cannot preserve a stationary gaze against an unbalanced elastic pull.]{.key-statement}
L0032: 
L0033: So far we have followed the output from motor neurons to muscles. The next step is a model of how neurons act on one another and sustain a signal.
L0034: 
L0035: ## Recurrent spikes renew decaying synaptic activity {#sec:MEMORY-SPIKES}
L0036: 
L0037: **Neural mechanism.** How can brief spikes maintain a state for much longer than the duration of a spike? In this model, each spike leaves a synaptic activation that decays gradually. [Incoming activations determine new firing rates. The resulting spikes renew those activations. This feedback can support activity after the external stimulus has ended.]{.key-statement}
L0038: 
L0039: [Neurons affect one another through connections called [**synapse**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}s.]{.definition-explanation} In the models used here, inputs have two contrasting effects:
L0040: 
L0041: - [An [**excitatory input**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} increases a receiving neuron's tendency to fire.]{.definition-explanation}
L0042: - [An [**inhibitory input**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} reduces it.]{.definition-explanation}
L0043: 
L0044: The effect of a spike can last beyond the spike itself because synaptic input decays over time. [When neurons send signals back into the same population, the circuit has [**recurrent connectivity**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}.]{.definition-explanation} Such feedback can maintain activity after an external signal disappears.
L0045: 
L0046: Burak and Fiete (2012) use the following equations for interactions inside a neural circuit. These describe synaptic activity; muscle motion is a later part of the pathway. The model assumes fixed connection weights, constant input biases and a common synaptic decay time. Its statistical rule for spike timing is explained in the [spike-count section](#sec-foundations-poisson).[^p1-model]
L0047: 
L0048: **Model dynamics: Burak and Fiete (2012), Equation 7.** [The [**synaptic activation**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} $s_i$ tracks the decaying effect of neuron $i$'s outgoing spikes on receiving cells.]{.definition-explanation} The dot means $\dot s_i=ds_i/dt$, and $\tau$ is the decay time in seconds. The index $\alpha$ numbers the spikes of neuron $i$, with spike $\alpha$ occurring at time $t_i^\alpha$. [The [**Dirac delta**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} $\delta(t-t_i^\alpha)$ represents an instantaneous unit pulse at that spike time; its integral across that time is one.]{.definition-explanation} The paper writes the activation dynamics as
L0049: 
L0050: $$
L0051: \dot s_i+\frac{s_i}{\tau}=\sum_\alpha\delta(t-t_i^\alpha).
L0052: $$
L0053: 
L0054: Between spikes, the right-hand side is zero and activation decays. Integrating through one spike gives a one-unit increase in $s_i$. [Read Eq. 7 as a balance: spikes add activation, while the term $s_i/\tau$ removes it.]{.key-statement} [Original Eq. 7, PDF p. 5](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=5).
L0055: 
L0056: **Equivalent count form.** Let $N_i(t)$ count neuron $i$'s spikes up to time $t$. Integrating the pulse train counts the spikes, so the same dynamics can be written as
L0057: 
L0058: $$
L0059: ds_i=-\frac{s_i}{\tau}\,dt+dN_i(t).
L0060: $$
L0061: 
L0062: Equivalently, if its spikes occur at times $t_i^\alpha$,
L0063: 
L0064: $$
L0065: s_i(t)=\sum_{t_i^\alpha\le t}\exp[-(t-t_i^\alpha)/\tau]
L0066: $$
L0067: 
L0068: once the contribution from any activation present at the starting time has decayed. The sum includes a spike at time $t$, so it gives the activation immediately after that spike. Here $s_i$ is dimensionless in the convention that each spike adds one unit.
L0069: 
L0070: For five illustrative spike times, the plot shows the events and the activation they leave behind.
L0071: 
L0072: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0073: 
L0074: {{block:fig:MEMORY-SPIKE-TRACE}}
L0075: 
L0076: </div>
L0077: 
L0078: The activation retains a decaying contribution from earlier spikes, so a spike train and its activation trace are different objects.
L0079: 
L0080: **From activation to new spikes: Equations 8 and 9.** The total input $g_i$ to neuron $i$ combines the activation arriving from other cells with a constant input bias $b_i$. The fixed connection weight $W_{ij}$ says how the activation of neuron $j$ affects the input to neuron $i$:
L0081: 
L0082: $$
L0083: g_i=\sum_jW_{ij}s_j+b_i.\qquad\text{(Burak and Fiete, Eq. 8)}
L0084: $$
L0085: 
L0086: [The [**neural transfer function**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} $\phi$ converts total input into a nonnegative firing rate $r_i$, in spikes per second.]{.definition-explanation} Thus
L0087: 
L0088: $$
L0089: r_i=\phi(g_i).\qquad\text{(Burak and Fiete, Eq. 9)}
L0090: $$
L0091: 
L0092: These equations specify how the input sets the rate; they do not prescribe each spike's time.
L0093: 
L0094: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0095: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0096: For example, increasing input might increase firing linearly over some input range, or exponentially. The paper allows general connectivity and transfer functions at first ([Model, Eqs. 7–9, p. 5](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=5&zoom=125,0,372)).[^p1-model]
L0097: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0098: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0099: 
L0100: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0101: A spike from cell $j$ raises $s_j$ by one and therefore changes the input to cell $i$ by $W_{ij}$. That contribution then decays over time $\tau$. For an increasing transfer function, a positive weight raises the receiving cell's rate and a negative weight lowers it. The resulting new spikes affect other cells in turn. Thus the weights set where a disturbance goes, the transfer function sets the rate response, and $\tau$ sets how long each synaptic contribution lasts. The input $g_i$ is in the model's input units. It is not specified as a measured membrane voltage.
L0102: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0103: 
L0104: Together, Eqs. 7–9 describe one feedback cycle: spikes change synaptic activation, weighted activation changes input, and input changes the rate of new spikes. The next question is how the resulting pattern of rates can represent an eye angle or location.
L0105: 
L0106: ## Patterns of firing rates represent a value {#sec:FOUNDATIONS-SPIKES}
L0107: 
L0108: [A [**tuning curve**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} gives a neuron's expected firing rate as a function of a represented variable.]{.definition-explanation} For eye angle $\theta$, a simple local model is $r_i(\theta)=r_{i,0}+k_i\theta$. Neuron $i$ has baseline rate $r_{i,0}$ in spikes per second and slope $k_i$ in spikes per second per degree. This rate baseline is different from the input bias $b_i$ in Eq. 8. The linear expression can describe an active range, but it must be modified where it would predict negative firing. The eye paper includes a firing threshold and terms for movement as well as position.[^f-eye]
L0109: 
L0110: Different cells have different tuning curves. The vector
L0111: 
L0112: $$
L0113: \mathbf r(\theta)=\bigl(r_1(\theta),\ldots,r_N(\theta)\bigr)
L0114: $$
L0115: 
L0116: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeCarries = NO -->
L0117: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO -->
L0118: [is a [**population code**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}: the pattern across $N$ neurons carries information about $\theta$.]{.definition-explanation} The position of a cell in this vector is a neuron identity, rather than a location in physical space. [Reading a value from observed spikes is [**decoding**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}.]{.definition-explanation} [Establishing a population pattern from an input is [**encoding**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}.]{.definition-explanation} [The 2012 paper asks how accurately the network can maintain such a pattern using its own noisy activity.]{.key-statement}
L0119: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES -->
L0120: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeCarries = YES -->
L0121: 
L0122: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0123: Spatial tuning gives a second example. In the hippocampus, a brain structure involved in memory and navigation, a place cell fires strongly in one or more localized regions of an environment. A connected region of elevated spatial firing is a place field. One neuron can have several fields. [A [**rate map**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} estimates the rate at each position by dividing the spikes recorded there by the time spent there.]{.definition-explanation} Sparse visits, smoothing, and firing cutoffs therefore affect the estimated map and its fields. A field in a model's expected rate and a field extracted from finite recordings are related objects with different measurement uncertainties.[^f-place]
L0124: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0125: 
L0126: The hippocampal areas CA3 and CA1 contain place cells, but their connections differ:
L0127: 
L0128: - CA3 supplies major input to CA1 and has substantial recurrent excitation.
L0129: - CA1 also receives input from entorhinal cortex and has much less local recurrent excitation.
L0130: 
L0131: [A [**grid cell**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, found in entorhinal cortex, has repeated spatial fields that often form an approximately hexagonal pattern in a two-dimensional arena.]{.definition-explanation} These repeated grid fields and a place cell's irregular multiple fields should be kept distinct. A model explaining CA1's output maps need not identify where the animal's position estimate is maintained.[^f-anatomy]
L0132: 
L0133: ## A variable spike count at a fixed rate {#sec:FOUNDATIONS-POISSON}
L0134: 
L0135: A tuning curve describes expected activity, but a brief spike observation can differ each time the same value is represented. [The 2012 model describes this variability with a [**Poisson process**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}. At constant rate $r$, the number $K$ of spikes in an interval of length $\Delta t$ obeys]{.definition-explanation}
L0136: 
L0137: $$
L0138: \Pr(K=k)=e^{-\lambda}\frac{\lambda^k}{k!},
L0139: \qquad \lambda=r\Delta t,
L0140: \qquad k=0,1,2,\ldots .
L0141: $$
L0142: 
L0143: Here $\lambda$ is an expected count, so it has no units. For this distribution,
L0144: 
L0145: $$
L0146: \mathbb E[K]=\lambda,
L0147: \qquad \operatorname{Var}(K)=\lambda.
L0148: $$
L0149: 
L0150: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Derivation: where the Poisson count formula and its variance come from"}
L0151: 
L0152: **From random spike times to a count.** Hold the rate $r$ fixed over the observation of length $\Delta t$. Divide that observation into $n$ short bins of width $\delta t=\Delta t/n$. Approximate each bin by one independent spike-or-no-spike trial, with spike probability
L0153: 
L0154: $$
L0155: p=r\delta t=\frac{\lambda}{n},\qquad \lambda=r\Delta t.
L0156: $$
L0157: 
L0158: Take $n$ large enough that $p<1$. This construction assumes a constant chance of a spike per unit time and independence between nonoverlapping bins. In the continuous-time limit, the chance of two or more spikes in one bin becomes negligible compared with its width. These are assumptions of the Poisson model, not properties of every recorded neuron.[^f-poisson-limit]
L0159: 
L0160: A particular pattern with spikes in exactly $k$ bins and silence in the other $n-k$ bins has probability $p^k(1-p)^{n-k}$. There are
L0161: 
L0162: $$
L0163: \binom nk=\frac{n(n-1)\cdots(n-k+1)}{k!}
L0164: $$
L0165: 
L0166: ways to choose the occupied bins. The numerator chooses them in order; dividing by $k!=1\cdot2\cdots k$ removes the repeated counting of the same set in different orders. For $k=0$, there is one empty set and $0!=1$. Adding the probabilities of all these patterns gives
L0167: 
L0168: $$
L0169: \begin{aligned}
L0170: \Pr(K_n=k)
L0171: &=\binom nk\left(\frac{\lambda}{n}\right)^k
L0172:   \left(1-\frac{\lambda}{n}\right)^{n-k}\\
L0173: &=\frac{\lambda^k}{k!}
L0174:   \left[\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\left(1-\frac{j}{n}\right)\right]
L0175:   \left(1-\frac{\lambda}{n}\right)^{n-k}.
L0176: \end{aligned}
L0177: $$
L0178: 
L0179: Here $K_n$ is the count in the finite-bin construction. Keep the observation duration, $\lambda$, and the integer $k$ fixed while making the bins finer. The finite product tends to one. The remaining factor tends to
L0180: 
L0181: $$
L0182: \lim_{n\to\infty}\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{n}\right)^{n-k}
L0183: =e^{-\lambda}.
L0184: $$
L0185: 
L0186: For example, its logarithm is $(n-k)\log(1-\lambda/n)$, which tends to $-\lambda$ because $\log(1-u)/u\to-1$ as $u\to0$. Exponentiating gives the displayed limit. The limiting count $K$ therefore obeys
L0187: 
L0188: $$
L0189: \boxed{\Pr(K=k)=e^{-\lambda}\frac{\lambda^k}{k!}.}
L0190: $$
L0191: 
L0192: [The exponential comes from requiring silence in all the unoccupied bins. The factor $\lambda^k$ comes from the $k$ spike probabilities together with the number of possible bin locations, and $k!$ corrects repeated counting.]{.key-statement} In particular, $e^{-\lambda}$ is the probability of no spikes. A positive expected count can therefore coexist with a silent observation.
L0193: 
L0194: **From the count formula to its mean and variance.** Shifting the summation index by one gives
L0195: 
L0196: $$
L0197: \begin{aligned}
L0198: \mathbb E[K]
L0199: &=e^{-\lambda}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k\frac{\lambda^k}{k!}\\
L0200: &=\lambda e^{-\lambda}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda^j}{j!}
L0201: =\lambda,
L0202: \end{aligned}
L0203: $$
L0204: 
L0205: where the last sum is $e^\lambda$. Shifting by two similarly gives
L0206: 
L0207: $$
L0208: \mathbb E[K(K-1)]
L0209: =e^{-\lambda}\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}
L0210:   \frac{\lambda^k}{(k-2)!}
L0211: =\lambda^2e^{-\lambda}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda^j}{j!}
L0212: =\lambda^2.
L0213: $$
L0214: 
L0215: Since $K^2=K(K-1)+K$,
L0216: 
L0217: $$
L0218: \mathbb E[K^2]=\lambda^2+\lambda,
L0219: \qquad
L0220: \operatorname{Var}(K)=\mathbb E[K^2]-\mathbb E[K]^2=\lambda.
L0221: $$
L0222: 
L0223: :::
L0224: 
L0225: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0226: The count variance grows with rate, while its size relative to the mean falls. Its relative standard deviation is $\sqrt\lambda/\lambda=1/\sqrt\lambda$. This is why collecting more independent spikes can improve a rate estimate. It also means that increasing a firing-rate scale does not keep absolute noise amplitude fixed. Comparisons between networks must specify which quantities are held constant.[^f-poisson]
L0227: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0228: 
L0229: <div class="publisher-worked-calculation publisher-fold-always">
L0230: 
L0231: [**Example: an apparently silent cell and an informative population**]{#sec-foundations-count-example}
L0232: 
L0233: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0234: Suppose the expected rate is 20 spikes per second and an observation lasts 50 ms. The expected count is $20\times0.05=1$. The probability of seeing no spike is $e^{-1}=0.368$. A silent interval is therefore common even though the expected rate is positive.
L0235: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0236: 
L0237: Now suppose 20 conditionally independent cells each have that rate. Their total count is Poisson with mean 20 and standard deviation $\sqrt{20}=4.47$. The total rate estimate has a relative standard deviation of about 22.4%, compared with 100% for one cell. [Population averaging helps because the independent count variances add while the mean signal adds directly. If a common varying input makes several cells' firing rates rise and fall together, that shared variation will not disappear in the same way.]{.key-statement}
L0238: 
L0239: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0240: The two count distributions can be compared on the same rate per cell scale.
L0241: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0242: 
L0243: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0244: 
L0245: {{block:fig:FOUNDATIONS-POISSON-POPULATION}}
L0246: 
L0247: </div>
L0248: 
L0249: Dividing the pooled rate estimate by 20 keeps the expected rate per cell fixed, while independent averaging reduces its relative spread. See [Poisson count likelihood, SI Eq. S43, p. 9](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=9).
L0250: 
L0251: </div>
L0252: 
L0253: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0254: For a predetermined rate function $r(t)$, the time-varying Poisson model has a count parameter $\lambda=\int r(t)\,dt$ over the observation. In a recurrent network, however, $r(t)$ itself depends on earlier spikes. The whole-interval count need not then be Poisson. The useful independence statement is local and conditional: given the current modeled state, the elementary spike-generation noise is independent across cells in the basic 2012 model. The changing state can still produce correlations across time and across neurons. This qualification prevents treating a recurrent Poisson network as a collection of independent complete spike trains.
L0255: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0256: 
L0257: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0258: Two other measurements describe different aspects of variability:
L0259: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0260: 
L0261: - [The [**coefficient of variation**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, or CV, of interspike intervals, the times between successive spikes, is their standard deviation divided by their mean.]{.definition-explanation} A homogeneous Poisson process has exponential intervals and CV equal to one. More regular firing can have CV below one, as in the ocular motor neurons analyzed in 2022.
L0262: - [The count variance divided by mean count is the [**Fano factor**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}]{.definition-explanation}. Its value also depends on the counting interval.
L0263: 
L0264: CV and Fano factor cannot be substituted for one another without additional assumptions.
L0265: 
L0266: ## Network simulations test whether spike noise moves a stored value {#sec:FOUNDATIONS-SIMULATION}
L0267: 
L0268: Can a network keep firing while losing the value it represents? Burak and Fiete (2012) test this by following the spikes and synaptic activations generated by Eqs. 7–9. These simulations support the paper's central theory of memory error: they test whether the directly simulated spikes produce the predicted changes in the stored value.[^f-network-simulation]
L0269: 
L0270: **The simulated system.** The computer stores one changing activation $s_i$ for each neuron. It calculates each input $g_i$ and rate $r_i$ from the current activations. The connections $W_{ij}$, input biases $b_i$, decay time $\tau$ and transfer function $\phi$ stay fixed during a run. Constant bias remains present, but no continuing stimulus fixes the remembered value. The model follows recurrent neural activity; it does not calculate muscle forces, membrane-voltage waveforms or learning of connections.
L0271: 
L0272: In the ring example, 1,024 neurons are ordered by their [**preferred angle**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, [the represented angle at which each neuron's expected firing rate is highest.]{.definition-explanation} [An [**activity bump**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is a localized peak of firing rates across these neurons; its position represents the stored angle.]{.definition-explanation} The ring describes the ordering of preferred angles, not an anatomical circle of cells. The published connections are inhibitory and depend on angular separation. Nearby preferred angles have weak mutual inhibition, while the active group suppresses competing activity farther away. The chosen connection profile and firing rule sustain a localized pattern. Random spike timing can move its peak even while the pattern persists.
L0273: 
L0274: **How neurons depend on one another.** A spike in cell $j$ increases $s_j$ by one. Every receiving cell $i$ then gets an input change $W_{ij}$, which changes its rate through $\phi$. Its later spikes feed back into the network. [Independent random spike draws at given rates do not make the neurons' activity independent: earlier spikes change the rates used for later draws.]{.key-statement}
L0275: 
L0276: For the ring simulation, one step of duration $h$ follows this cycle:
L0277: 
L0278: 1. Calculate $g_i$ and $r_i$ from all current activations.
L0279: 2. Draw a spike indicator $X_i$: it is one with probability $r_i h$ and zero otherwise, independently across cells conditional on those current rates.
L0280: 3. Reduce the old activation by its decay over the step and add the generated spike:
L0281: 
L0282: $$
L0283: s_i(t+h)\approx s_i(t)\left(1-\frac{h}{\tau}\right)+X_i.
L0284: $$
L0285: 
L0286: The next step uses these updated activations. This [**Euler update**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} [approximates continuous decay by the current rate of change times the step duration.]{.definition-explanation} The one-spike draw requires $r_i h$ to be small. The authors use $h=0.1$ ms and $\tau=10$ ms. Their two-population example requires a spike-count rule suitable for aggregate population activity; the one-spike restriction above belongs to the ring implementation. The [simulation chapter](ch-simulating-memory.html#sec-simulating-memory-state) gives the full settings and numerical cautions.
L0287: 
L0288: Before comparing simulated motion with theory, we need a measure of how far the represented value moves. Let $x(t)$ be that value at absolute time $t$, and let $\ell$ be the time between two observations, called the lag. [The [**displacement**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is $\Delta x=x(t+\ell)-x(t)$.]{.definition-explanation} The mean squared displacement, or MSD, is the average of $(\Delta x)^2$ over intervals with the same lag. The average can use different starting times along a recording, repeated trials, or both; which intervals can be combined depends on whether their movement statistics are comparable. Squaring prevents leftward and rightward changes from cancelling.
L0289: 
L0290: For an angle measured in degrees, MSD has units degrees squared. An MSD plot puts lag on the horizontal axis and MSD on the vertical axis. Lag is a duration, not the clock time at which an interval starts. If the statistics change during a trial, the MSD can depend on that starting time as well as on lag.
L0291: 
L0292: [A straight MSD curve on ordinary axes means that the average squared displacement grows at a constant rate over that range.]{.key-statement} The zero-mean diffusion model taught in the next chapter gives $\operatorname{MSD}(\ell)=2D\ell$, where the diffusion coefficient $D$ is half the rate of displacement-variance growth. The MSD slope is $2D$ in degrees squared per second. On axes that plot logarithms, a relation $\operatorname{MSD}\propto\ell^\beta$ has slope $\beta$: diffusion has slope one, while constant-velocity motion has slope two. A slope alone does not identify the neural cause. Bias, smoothing by muscles, boundaries and the range of lags measured can change the curve.
L0293: 
L0294: **What is measured and compared.** At each step, the ring's angle is read from the preferred angle of the neuron with the highest instantaneous rate. The authors measure how much that angle changes over different time intervals and average the squared changes. They independently calculate the predicted spreading from the same network's steady activity in the absence of noise. In [Figure 2C–D](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=3), simulation points agree with these theoretical curves without fitting a free diffusion parameter. Over short intervals, the mean squared change grows approximately linearly with elapsed time. Over long intervals, wrapping angle differences into a single turn limits the plotted error. That flattening does not mean the original angle has been recovered.
L0295: 
L0296: [Figure 2E–G](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=3) repeats the test with two mutually inhibiting units, which can represent two groups of neurons. Their difference in firing rates represents the stored value. Agreement in this second design checks that the prediction is not specific to the ring's symmetry.
L0297: 
L0298: **Relation to biological data.** [Figure 2 compares theory with simulated data. It does not fit the memory errors of a recorded animal.]{.key-statement} The paper motivates persistent activity and variable spiking using earlier experiments, and proposes tests using measured tuning curves. It also notes that eye-position neurons can spike more regularly than a Poisson process, so the Poisson formula cannot be applied directly to those recordings. The [2022 eye-drift study](ch-eye-drift.html) supplies a separate comparison with recorded neural activity and eye motion. Here the established result is narrower: the specified interacting network models produce the memory spreading predicted by the theory.[^f-network-data]
L0299: 
L0300: The next section explains how measured changes can distinguish shared neural variation from a directional bias when the output is an eye movement.
L0301: 
L0302: ## Shared neural signals and a biased command leave different signs in eye motion {#sec:FOUNDATIONS-COVARIANCE}
L0303: 
L0304: During fixation, a monkey tries to keep looking at a stationary target, yet its eye moves slightly. A changing command from the brain can make many motor neurons change their firing together. Their spikes act on the eye muscles, which turn the eye. An individual motor neuron also adds spike variability of its own. The 2022 paper asks how much of the observed movement follows a shared neural command, and whether that command itself wanders over time.[^f-eye]
L0305: 
L0306: <!-- vale ai-tells.NegatedSubject = NO -->
L0307: [Covariance asks which recorded changes occur together. Displacement statistics ask how far the eye moves and how that movement grows with elapsed time.]{.key-statement} Neither measurement by itself identifies the neural circuit responsible.
L0308: <!-- vale ai-tells.NegatedSubject = YES -->
L0309: 
L0310: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0311: **Physical meaning: a shared command can survive averaging across neurons.** If a common input raises the firing of many motor neurons that pull the eye in the same direction, their effects add. Independent extra and missing spikes partly cancel when those neurons act together. The paper compares the measured eye displacement with a displacement predicted from one neuron's spikes, after converting both to degrees. That prediction is an analysis of a recording, not an extra command sent to the muscles. A relationship between the two is evidence to test against a model of shared input and private noise. It does not mean that the one recorded cell causes all the movement.
L0312: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0313: 
L0314: [**Covariance**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} [measures whether two signals tend to be above their own averages together or on opposite sides of those averages.]{.definition-explanation} Positive covariance between the two displacements means that above-average changes in the neural prediction tend to accompany above-average changes in eye position. [The [**correlation coefficient**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, written $\rho$, expresses this shared variation relative to the spread of each signal.]{.definition-explanation} Extra private noise can lower $\rho$ even when the shared drive has not weakened. The [eye-drift calculation](ch-eye-drift.html#sec-eye-chi) explains why a modest correlation need not mean a small shared contribution to movement.
L0315: 
L0316: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Mathematical definitions: covariance, variance and correlation"}
L0317: 
L0318: Let $X$ and $Y$ be the two displacements, in degrees, across repeated intervals of the same duration. Subtracting each average leaves its fluctuation. Their covariance is
L0319: 
L0320: $$
L0321: \operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)=\mathbb E[(X-\mathbb E[X])(Y-\mathbb E[Y])].
L0322: $$
L0323: 
L0324: Here it has units degrees squared. In general its units are the product of the two signals' units. Variance is $\operatorname{Cov}(X,X)$. Expanding the square of a centered sum or difference gives
L0325: 
L0326: $$
L0327: \operatorname{Var}(X\pm Y)=\operatorname{Var}(X)+\operatorname{Var}(Y)\pm2\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y).
L0328: $$
L0329: 
L0330: For nonzero variances, correlation is
L0331: 
L0332: $$
L0333: \rho_{XY}=\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)}{\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(X)\operatorname{Var}(Y)}}.
L0334: $$
L0335: 
L0336: <!-- vale ai-tells.NegatedObject = NO -->
L0337: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0338: It is dimensionless and lies between $-1$ and $1$. Independence gives zero covariance when these moments exist. Zero covariance alone does not imply independence.
L0339: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0340: <!-- vale ai-tells.NegatedObject = YES -->
L0341: 
L0342: To see the physical roles, write $X=g_X S+\epsilon_X$ and $Y=g_Y S+\epsilon_Y$. Here $S$ is a shared displacement signal in degrees, the dimensionless [**gain**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}s $g_X,g_Y$ multiply its contribution in each recording, and $\epsilon_X,\epsilon_Y$ are private contributions in degrees. If all three random contributions are independent,
L0343: 
L0344: $$
L0345: \operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)=g_Xg_Y\operatorname{Var}(S),\qquad
L0346: \operatorname{Var}(X)=g_X^2\operatorname{Var}(S)+\operatorname{Var}(\epsilon_X).
L0347: $$
L0348: 
L0349: Increasing private variance increases the denominator of $\rho$ without increasing the covariance. Changing a gain changes the measured covariance even if the shared source is unchanged. This simple construction explains the need for calibration and explicit noise assumptions. It is not the paper's full inference model.
L0350: 
L0351: :::
L0352: 
L0353: **Physical meaning: random wandering differs from a persistent directional error.** Apply the displacement measure above to horizontal eye angle $x(t)$ in degrees, using a lag $\ell$ in seconds. A random change in the maintained command can move the eye left in one interval and right in another. The average displacement can then be close to zero while individual displacements are large. A persistent imbalance in the command can instead make the average displacement grow in one direction. Muscles smooth the command before it becomes eye movement, so the timescale of the observation also matters.
L0354: 
L0355: The MSD of this eye displacement has units degrees squared. Its two contributions are
L0356: 
L0357: $$
L0358: \mathbb E[(\Delta x)^2]=\operatorname{Var}(\Delta x)+\mathbb E[\Delta x]^2.
L0359: $$
L0360: 
L0361: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0362: [A larger MSD can mean stronger random movement, a larger directional bias, or both. Measuring the mean displacement separately prevents mistaking bias for increased noise.]{.key-statement}
L0363: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0364: 
L0365: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Derivation: separating spread from mean displacement"}
L0366: 
L0367: Write $\mu=\mathbb E[\Delta x]$ and $\eta=\Delta x-\mu$, so $\mathbb E[\eta]=0$. Squaring and averaging gives
L0368: 
L0369: $$
L0370: \mathbb E[(\mu+\eta)^2]=\mu^2+2\mu\mathbb E[\eta]+\mathbb E[\eta^2]
L0371: =\mu^2+\operatorname{Var}(\Delta x).
L0372: $$
L0373: 
L0374: The cross term vanishes because the fluctuations are measured relative to their own mean. This identity needs finite second moments, but no diffusion assumption.
L0375: 
L0376: :::
L0377: 
L0378: **What changes the physical movement?** The model must say which part of the pathway a parameter controls:
L0379: 
L0380: - **Random changes in the stored command:** more frequent or larger lasting changes make the stored angle spread faster. The next chapter explains why the diffusion coefficient $D$ depends on spike variability and the circuit's response to each spike.
L0381:   <!-- vale ai-tells.LabelAndExplain = NO -->
L0382: - **A persistent directional imbalance:** this changes the mean displacement. In a constant-velocity approximation, doubling that velocity doubles the mean displacement at a fixed lag and quadruples its squared contribution to MSD.
L0383:   <!-- vale ai-tells.LabelAndExplain = YES -->
L0384: - **The gain from command to eye angle:** multiplying the command's contribution to each displacement by a gain $G$ multiplies that contribution's variance and MSD by $G^2$, with the input fluctuations held fixed. Any noise added after that gain needs to be counted separately. A fitted tuning slope, which describes rate as a function of angle, is not by itself a causal muscle gain.
L0385: - **Muscle response time:** a slower response smooths rapid command changes more strongly. It changes the short-lag shape of the MSD curve, not just its height. The [timing chapter](ch-eye-timing.html#sec-eye-filtered-msd) separates this smoothing from growth of noise inside the memory circuit.
L0386: 
L0387: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0388: Measurement error is a separate contribution: it changes recorded positions without moving the eye. Independent zero-mean position errors, each of variance $\sigma_m^2$ and independent of true motion, add $2\sigma_m^2$ to displacement MSD at nonzero lags. Thus an upward shift in the observed curve need not be a physical increase in movement. The [measurement-noise calculation](ch-eye-timing.html#sec-eye-measurement-noise) shows the conditions needed to remove it.
L0389: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0390: 
L0391: The next chapter follows the proposed source upstream: how a circuit can keep a pattern of activity while random spikes change the angle that pattern represents.
L0392: 
L0393: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0394: [^f-eye]: Ben-Shushan, N., Shaham, N., Joshua, M., and Burak, Y. (2022). *Fixational drift is driven by diffusive dynamics in central neural circuitry*. Nature Communications, 13, 1697. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y). Introduction, Figs. 1–2, and Methods, Eqs. 2–7.
L0395: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0396: 
L0397: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0398: [^f-place]: Mainali, N., Azeredo da Silveira, R., and Burak, Y. (2025). *Universal statistics of hippocampal place fields across species and dimensionalities*. Neuron, 113(7), 1110–1120.e3. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017). Introduction, Results, and STAR Methods. The distinction between map randomness and visit-to-visit spike variation follows from what the model's random function indexes.
L0399: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0400: 
L0401: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0402: [^f-anatomy]: Maimon, S. R., and colleagues (2026). *Sparse-to-dense coding transformation between hippocampal areas CA3 and CA1*. Nature. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-026-10537-0). Main text, Introduction and Figs. 1–2. The present guide uses this later comparison for anatomical and coding context. It is not evidence reported in the 2025 paper.
L0403: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0404: 
L0405: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0406: [^f-poisson]: Burak, Y., and Fiete, I. R. (2012). *Fundamental limits on persistent activity in networks of noisy neurons*. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 109(43), 17645–17650. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109). Results and Methods, Eqs. 7–10, describe the Poisson spike model and weak-fluctuation approximation. The finite count calculations in this section are direct consequences of the Poisson distribution and are guide derivations.
L0407: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0408: 
L0409: [^f-poisson-limit]: Massachusetts Institute of Technology, OpenCourseWare (2018). *Introduction to Probability: Lecture 22 notes, the Poisson process*. [PDF, pp. 3–5](https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/res-6-012-introduction-to-probability-spring-2018/d92a00a2bc9d20d84ad11d43be0e7ae0_MITRES_6_012S18_L22.pdf#page=3). The guide reconstructs the independent-bin limit using $r$ for rate and $\lambda=r\Delta t$ for expected count.
L0410: 
L0411: [^p-eye-anatomy]: Dragoi, V. (2026, July 7 review). *Ocular Motor Control*. Neuroscience Online, Section 3, Chapter 8, McGovern Medical School, UTHealth Houston. [Sections 8.2–8.3 and Fig. 8.1](https://nba.uth.tmc.edu/neuroscience/m/s3/chapter08.html). Anatomy, muscle innervation and visual/vestibular control.
L0412: 
L0413: [^p-eye-mechanics]: Anderson, S. R., Porrill, J., Sklavos, S., Gandhi, N. J., Sparks, D. L., and Dean, P. Introduction and stimulation/relaxation results. The spring-and-damper calculation is a guide reconstruction of the physical roles, not a fitted reproduction of these experiments.

[DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109).
