## FILE guide/01b-persistence.md | ch:PERSISTENCE
L0001: # Stable representations can accumulate error {#ch:PERSISTENCE}
L0002: 
L0003: Holding the eye at an angle requires muscles to maintain tension after the command to move has ended. The brain must therefore keep producing a position-dependent signal. The horizontal eye angle considered here is one number, but a circuit can represent it through thousands of neural activities. A disturbance can change those activities without changing the represented value. Another disturbance can leave a coherent activity pattern while changing the value. The geometry of these two changes will explain why a stable-looking circuit can still accumulate memory error.[^f-memory]
L0004: 
L0005: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeStays = NO -->
L0006: Chapter 1 established how spikes act on eye muscles and how firing rates can represent an angle. We now examine the mechanical balance and continuing neural command needed to hold that angle. A small circuit example then shows why some disturbances fade while others leave a different stored angle. Repeating those lasting changes gives the diffusion calculation. The final distinction is between this changing value and an irregular spatial map that stays fixed. It explains why the memory, eye-motion, and place-field papers need different models even though all three concern variable neural signals.
L0007: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeStays = YES -->
L0008: 
L0009: ## Sustained muscle tension holds the eye against elastic restoring forces {#sec:PERSISTENCE-EYE-MECHANICS}
L0010: 
L0011: The [eye anatomy and motor pathway](ch-foundations.html#sec-foundations-eye-action) explain why holding an angle needs continuing muscle force. We now separate two questions: how that force balances the eye's elastic pull, and how the brain maintains the command that produces it. The folded calculation answers the mechanical question; the experiments below test the neural one.
L0012: 
L0013: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Mechanical calculation: what holds an angle, and what only slows its change"}
L0014: 
L0015: For a small angular range, a teaching approximation treats the eye as a rotating mass with a spring and viscous resistance. Let $\phi$ be angle in radians measured from the position where the passive forces balance. Let $I$ be the rotational inertia, $B>0$ the viscous resistance, $K>0$ the angular stiffness, and $\tau_m(t)$ the net active muscle torque. The angular force balance is
L0016: 
L0017: $$
L0018: I\ddot\phi+B\dot\phi+K\phi=\tau_m(t).
L0019: $$
L0020: 
L0021: The terms are torques: $I$ multiplies angular acceleration, $B$ multiplies angular velocity, and $K$ multiplies displacement. This is a guide approximation, not the full model of eye mechanics in Ben-Shushan et al. (2022). Real muscle force is nonlinear and depends on activation and movement history.[^p-eye-mechanics]
L0022: 
L0023: At a held angle, $\dot\phi=\ddot\phi=0$, so
L0024: 
L0025: $$
L0026: \tau_m=K\phi.
L0027: $$
L0028: 
L0029: The steady angle therefore depends on active torque and stiffness. At fixed torque, doubling $K$ halves the angle in this linear model. Increasing $I$ or $B$ cannot replace the torque required to hold that angle.
L0030: 
L0031: If the active torque is removed, neglecting the relatively small inertial term gives
L0032: 
L0033: $$
L0034: B\dot\phi+K\phi=0,\qquad
L0035: \phi(t)=\phi(0)e^{-t/\tau_p},\qquad
L0036: \tau_p=\frac{B}{K}.
L0037: $$
L0038: 
L0039: Greater viscous resistance slows the return; greater stiffness speeds it up at fixed $B$. Neither creates a choice of indefinitely held angles. Actual eye mechanics include several relaxation times, so $\tau_p$ is an effective timescale in this simplified calculation.
L0040: 
L0041: :::
L0042: 
L0043: **The brain maintains the holding command.** [The [**oculomotor integrator**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} converts movement commands into a maintained eye-position signal.]{.definition-explanation} This signal sustains the motor output after a saccade. The name refers to accumulating an eye-velocity command over time into a position signal. In monkeys, Cannon and Robinson (1987) reversibly disrupted brainstem regions involved in this function. Horizontal gaze then drifted back with a time constant of about 0.2 s, compared with more than 20 s before disruption in darkness. The muscles alone did not provide the original persistence.[^p-eye-integrator]
L0044: 
L0045: Visual feedback can correct gaze errors, and signals from the inner ear help compensate for head motion. Holding can nevertheless continue in darkness, so immediate visual correction is not the whole mechanism. [**Plasticity**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} [is a lasting, experience-dependent change in neural responses or connections.]{.definition-explanation} It can adjust how well the holding circuit is calibrated. Major et al. (2004) altered visual feedback in goldfish and trained their gaze holding toward drift or instability over tens of minutes to hours; normal feedback could reverse the change. That is a change in neural control over a longer timescale, distinct from the immediate elastic and viscous response of the tissues. The experiment did not identify the exact cellular learning rule.[^p-eye-anatomy][^p-eye-plasticity]
L0046: 
L0047: The next model isolates the remaining neural question: how can a circuit preserve an activity pattern while losing the angle that pattern was supposed to hold?
L0048: 
L0049: ## Stable activity with a vulnerable stored value {#sec:FOUNDATIONS-ATTRACTOR}
L0050: 
L0051: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeKeeps = NO -->
L0052: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0053: Imagine a group of neurons maintaining a pattern that represents an eye angle after a movement command has ended. Random spikes disturb that pattern. Recurrent input can correct some disturbances by changing the cells' firing back toward a persistent pattern. But if a disturbance produces the pattern for a different angle, an ideal memory circuit has no record of which angle was originally intended. [Keeping a pattern stable is therefore different from keeping the stored value correct.]{.key-statement}
L0054: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0055: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeKeeps = YES -->
L0056: 
L0057: [A circuit's current activities are its [**state**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}.]{.definition-explanation} A dynamical equation specifies how that state changes. [A [**fixed point**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is a state where the deterministic rate of change is zero.]{.definition-explanation} It is stable in a direction if a small disturbance in that direction decays.
L0058: 
L0059: Memory of a continuous quantity calls for a family of persistent states. [A [**continuous attractor**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is a continuous family of fixed points with restoring dynamics toward the family and a neutral direction along it. Neutral means that there is no restoring force along that direction in the ideal deterministic model.]{.definition-explanation} [The family is an [**attractor manifold**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}. Its dimension counts independent coordinates represented on it, rather than neurons in the circuit.]{.definition-explanation} A line attractor has one represented coordinate. A ring can represent a periodic coordinate such as heading.[^f-memory]
L0060: 
L0061: In the illustrative two-cell model below, the difference between the firing rates represents the angle. Raising both rates equally leaves that difference unchanged. The model's feedback removes the common increase. Raising one rate while lowering the other changes the represented angle. That second change persists. The calculation makes these two responses explicit. It is not a fitted biological circuit.
L0062: 
L0063: <div class="publisher-worked-calculation publisher-fold-always">
L0064: 
L0065: [**Example: two disturbances to the same stored angle**]{#sec-foundations-attractor-example}
L0066: 
L0067: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0068: Consider a teaching model with two activities $r_1,r_2$ in spikes per second. Let $b=40$ spikes per second and let $k=2$ spikes per second per degree. A stored angle $\theta$ corresponds to
L0069: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->
L0070: 
L0071: $$
L0072: r_1=b+k\theta,\qquad r_2=b-k\theta.
L0073: $$
L0074: 
L0075: The fixed points lie on the line $r_1+r_2=2b$. Restrict attention to angles where both rates remain positive. Define the deviation in their mean activity and the represented angle as
L0076: 
L0077: $$
L0078: x=\frac{r_1+r_2}{2}-b,
L0079: \qquad
L0080: \theta=\frac{r_1-r_2}{2k}.
L0081: $$
L0082: 
L0083: Suppose each activity changes according to
L0084: 
L0085: $$
L0086: \dot r_1=-a x,\qquad \dot r_2=-a x,
L0087: $$
L0088: 
L0089: where $a>0$ has units inverse seconds. Adding and subtracting these equations gives
L0090: 
L0091: $$
L0092: \dot x=-a x,\qquad \dot\theta=0.
L0093: $$
L0094: 
L0095: The first equation has solution $x(t)=x(0)e^{-at}$, so disturbances in the common activity decay. The second equation retains any angle set by the initial state.
L0096: 
L0097: Increasing $a$ shortens the recovery time $1/a$ for the common activity disturbance. It does not correct the angle error, because $\dot\theta$ is still zero. At fixed rate disturbance, a larger tuning slope $k$ gives a smaller decoded angle change through division by $2k$. Changing $k$ in an actual circuit may also change its dynamics.
L0098: 
L0099: At $\theta=5$ degrees, the rates are $(50,30)$. Compare two disturbances from this same state:
L0100: 
L0101: - A disturbance of $(+3,+3)$ changes the rates to $(53,33)$. It creates $x=3$ but leaves the represented angle at 5 degrees. The restoring dynamics return to $(50,30)$.
L0102: - A disturbance of $(+3,-3)$ gives $(53,27)$, with $x=0$ and angle $26/(2\times2)=6.5$ degrees. This state lies on the fixed-point line, so there is no restoring response.
L0103: 
L0104: [In the second case, the circuit remains coherent while holding the wrong angle.]{.key-statement}
L0105: 
L0106: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0107: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO -->
L0108: The two changes occupy different directions in the activity plane. The solid arrow in the figure is the restoring motion produced by the model. The dashed arrow shows the imposed disturbance along the fixed-point line, rather than a deterministic restoring flow.
L0109: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES -->
L0110: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0111: 
L0112: {{block:fig:FOUNDATIONS-ATTRACTOR}}
L0113: 
L0114: In a larger network, the lasting change depends on its connection weights and cannot always be found by an ordinary perpendicular projection. The [network calculation](ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-linearization) derives the correct projection from the network's linearized dynamics.
L0115: 
L0116: </div>
L0117: 
L0118: The paper's two-unit mutual-inhibition network has this same geometry. Over its positive linear firing range, its rate dynamics reproduce the example with common-mode decay rate $a=2/\tau$, where $\tau$ is the synaptic decay time. The difference between the rates remains neutral. The [network chapter](ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-symmetric) uses synaptic activations to describe this network and gives the conversion between its coordinate and the angle used here. This small network illustrates one possible memory mechanism; the gaze-holding circuit also needs the motor and muscle stages described above.
L0119: 
L0120: ## Independent steps with equal duration and variance give linear variance growth {#sec:FOUNDATIONS-DIFFUSION}
L0121: 
L0122: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0123: Each extra or missing spike changes synaptic input. After the circuit has corrected the disturbances it can remove, a small change in the represented angle may remain. Repeating this process makes trials that began at the same angle gradually spread apart. We first model these lasting changes as independent, with no preferred direction and a constant variance per unit time. These are assumptions about the surviving changes, not about complete spike trains.
L0124: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0125: 
L0126: [**Brownian motion**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} [$W(t)$ models the accumulated effect of many small fluctuations. It starts at zero, has continuous paths and independent Gaussian increments with mean zero, and satisfies $\operatorname{Var}[W(t)-W(s)]=t-s$ for $t\geq s$.]{.definition-explanation} The independence concerns changes over disjoint time intervals. A physical network need only approach this description over the time and noise scales relevant to the calculation.
L0127: 
L0128: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Mathematical model: independent steps and Brownian motion"}
L0129: 
L0130: Divide time into intervals of length $\Delta t$. Let $\xi_j$ be the change in the represented coordinate during interval $j$. The calculation assumes:
L0131: 
L0132: - Increments are independent.
L0133: - Each increment has mean zero.
L0134: - Each increment has variance $2D\Delta t$, where $D$ has units coordinate squared per time.
L0135: 
L0136: After $n$ intervals,
L0137: 
L0138: $$
L0139: \theta_n-\theta_0=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\xi_j,
L0140: \qquad
L0141: \operatorname{Var}(\theta_n-\theta_0)
L0142: =\sum_{j=1}^{n}\operatorname{Var}(\xi_j)
L0143: =2Dn\Delta t.
L0144: $$
L0145: 
L0146: Writing $t=n\Delta t$ gives linear variance growth $2Dt$. The continuous approximation expresses this accumulated change through Brownian motion.
L0147: 
L0148: In this continuous diffusion approximation,
L0149: 
L0150: $$
L0151: \theta(t)=\theta(0)+\sqrt{2D}\,W(t),
L0152: \qquad
L0153: \mathbb E[(\theta(t)-\theta(0))^2]=2Dt.
L0154: $$
L0155: 
L0156: :::
L0157: 
L0158: The diffusion coefficient $D$ measures half the rate at which the variance of the stored angle grows. With independent, zero-mean increments of constant variance per unit time,
L0159: 
L0160: $$
L0161: \operatorname{Var}[\theta(t)-\theta(0)]=2Dt.
L0162: $$
L0163: 
L0164: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO -->
L0165: Here $D$ has units degrees squared per second when $\theta$ is in degrees. More frequent retained disturbances, or larger ones at the same frequency, increase $D$. At a fixed delay, doubling $D$ doubles error variance and multiplies its standard deviation by $\sqrt{2}$. [A single memory trajectory can turn back even while the spread across trials grows.]{.key-statement} The [network calculation](ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-diffusion) obtains $D$ from the spike variability and the lasting effect of each cell's disturbance.
L0166: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES -->
L0167: 
L0168: The mean squared displacement, or MSD, is the expected squared change across a lag. In the zero-mean diffusion just defined, MSD is $2Dt$. [A [**root mean squared displacement**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is its square root, $\sqrt{2Dt}$, and has the same units as the represented coordinate.]{.definition-explanation} In several dimensions, adding independent coordinate contributions gives an overall MSD of $2dDt$ only when each of the $d$ coordinates has the same diffusion coefficient. A paper that defines its diffusion parameter as the entire two-dimensional MSD slope uses a different convention.
L0169: 
L0170: [A [**systematic drift**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is a nonzero mean velocity.]{.definition-explanation} The eye paper's term “fixational drift” names the eye's slow motion between saccades, which can be largely random; it does not imply a nonzero mean velocity.
L0171: 
L0172: <div class="publisher-worked-calculation publisher-fold-always">
L0173: 
L0174: [**Example: why the noise grows slower than a fixed bias**]{#sec-foundations-diffusion-example}
L0175: 
L0176: <!-- vale ai-tells.NounString = NO -->
L0177: Take an illustrative angular diffusion coefficient $D=0.02$ degrees squared per second. At 0.35 s its variance is $0.014$ degrees squared and its root mean squared displacement is 0.118 degrees, or about 7.10 arcminutes. At 1 s the root mean squared displacement is 0.20 degrees.
L0178: <!-- vale ai-tells.NounString = YES -->
L0179: 
L0180: If there is also a constant bias velocity $v=0.10$ degrees per second, then
L0181: 
L0182: $$
L0183: \theta(t)-\theta(0)=vt+\sqrt{2D}\,W(t),
L0184: \qquad
L0185: \operatorname{MSD}(t)=v^2t^2+2Dt.
L0186: $$
L0187: 
L0188: The deterministic and random contributions are equal at $t=2D/v^2=4$ seconds. Their equal contributions there are each $0.16$ degrees squared. At longer times the bias dominates this unbounded model even though it was small over a short observation. A theory of random diffusion must account separately for this systematic drift.
L0189: 
L0190: Sample paths show the irregular motion. Exact displacement scales show how its spread differs from a fixed bias.
L0191: 
L0192: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0193: 
L0194: {{block:fig:FOUNDATIONS-DIFFUSION-BIAS}}
L0195: 
L0196: </div>
L0197: 
L0198: A constant bias grows linearly, so it eventually exceeds the square root growth of the random displacement scale. See [Gaussian displacement density, SI Eq. S30, p. 7](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=7).
L0199: 
L0200: </div>
L0201: 
L0202: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0203: A later memory state contains earlier accumulated errors as well as new ones. Observing it twice therefore does not give two independent copies of the original memory. The covariance calculation below quantifies the shared history used in the readout chapter.
L0204: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0205: 
L0206: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Mathematical detail: why two times share accumulated noise"}
L0207: 
L0208: Two times along a Brownian path are correlated even though its increments are independent. For $0\leq s\leq t$, write $W(t)=W(s)+[W(t)-W(s)]$. The bracketed increment is independent of $W(s)$ and has mean zero, giving
L0209: 
L0210: $$
L0211: \operatorname{Cov}(W(s),W(t))
L0212: =\operatorname{Var}(W(s))=s,
L0213: \qquad
L0214: \operatorname{Cov}(W(s),W(t))=\min(s,t).
L0215: $$
L0216: 
L0217: The same argument gives covariance $2D\min(s,t)$ for a diffusing coordinate started at a fixed value. This identity is the intermediate step needed to compute the error from averaging a memory that moves during readout. It also helps explain why smoothing a diffusive input is different from smoothing a stationary noise source.
L0218: 
L0219: :::
L0220: 
L0221: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0222: A finite range, a periodic coordinate, external feedback, or a position-dependent diffusion coefficient changes long-time behavior. For example, ordinary squared angular distance on a ring cannot keep growing linearly forever. The basic $2Dt$ result is local or unbounded unless those conditions are treated separately.
L0223: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0224: 
L0225: ## A fixed spatial map differs from a value that wanders over time {#sec:FOUNDATIONS-RANDOMNESS}
L0226: 
L0227: The memory and eye papers study fluctuations that accumulate as time passes. The place-field paper instead starts with a random function of physical position. Once drawn for a cell and an environment, that function is fixed while the animal moves through the map. Revisiting a place evaluates the same expected-rate function, although the actual spikes can still vary from visit to visit.
L0228: 
L0229: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0230: These two kinds of randomness can occur together. A circuit may maintain a position estimate with time-dependent noise, while downstream cells respond to position through irregular but repeatable tuning curves. [Observing irregular spatial maps does not tell us how fast the maintained position drifts. Conversely, finding diffusion of a stored value does not determine the shapes of individual place fields.]{.key-statement} The following chapters develop the dynamical calculation first, then the experiment that tests a possible consequence, and finally the spatial statistics.[^f-place]
L0231: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0232: 
L0233: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0234: The three questions can now be placed beside the kind of variation each paper studies.
L0235: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0236: 
L0237: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0238: 
L0239: {{block:fig:FOUNDATIONS-PAPER-MAP}}
L0240: 
L0241: </div>
L0242: 
L0243: The rows compare different questions. They do not establish one circuit connecting all three papers.
L0244: 
L0245: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0246: [^f-memory]: Burak, Y., and Fiete, I. R. (2012). *Fundamental limits on persistent activity in networks of noisy neurons*. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 109(43), 17645–17650. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109). Introduction, Fig. 1, and SI I–III. The two-activity example is a guide construction illustrating the geometry, not the paper's simulated network.
L0247: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0248: 
L0249: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0250: [^f-place]: Mainali, N., Azeredo da Silveira, R., and Burak, Y. (2025). *Universal statistics of hippocampal place fields across species and dimensionalities*. Neuron, 113(7), 1110–1120.e3. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017). Introduction, Results, and STAR Methods. The distinction between map randomness and visit-to-visit spike variation follows from what the model's random function indexes.
L0251: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0252: 
L0253: [^p-eye-anatomy]: Dragoi, V. (2026, July 7 review). *Ocular Motor Control*. Neuroscience Online, Section 3, Chapter 8, McGovern Medical School, UTHealth Houston. [Sections 8.2–8.3 and Fig. 8.1](https://nba.uth.tmc.edu/neuroscience/m/s3/chapter08.html). Anatomy, muscle innervation and visual/vestibular control.
L0254: 
L0255: [^p-eye-mechanics]: Anderson, S. R., Porrill, J., Sklavos, S., Gandhi, N. J., Sparks, D. L., and Dean, P. (2009). *Dynamics of primate oculomotor plant revealed by effects of abducens microstimulation*. Journal of Neurophysiology, 101(6), 2907–2923. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1152/jn.91045.2008). Introduction and stimulation/relaxation results. The spring-and-damper calculation is a guide reconstruction of the physical roles, not a fitted reproduction of these experiments.
L0256: 
L0257: [^p-eye-integrator]: Cannon, S. C., and Robinson, D. A. (1987). *Loss of the neural integrator of the oculomotor system from brain stem lesions in monkey*. Journal of Neurophysiology, 57(5), 1383–1409. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1152/jn.1987.57.5.1383). Abstract reports the reversible disruption and gaze-holding time constants.
L0258: 
L0259: [^p-eye-plasticity]: Major, G., Baker, R., Aksay, E., Mensh, B., Seung, H. S., and Tank, D. W. (2004). *Plasticity and tuning by visual feedback of the stability of a neural integrator*. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 101(20), 7739–7744. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.0401970101). Fig. 1 for movement and holding signals; Figs. 2–4 and Discussion for goldfish training, reversibility and unresolved cellular mechanisms.

## FILE guide/02-memory-dynamics.md | ch:MEMORY-DYNAMICS
L0001: # Spiking noise produces diffusion along the memory attractor {#ch:MEMORY-DYNAMICS}
L0002: 
L0003: [A network can keep firing after a stimulus disappears and still lose the value that the stimulus supplied.]{.key-statement} Burak and Fiete study this distinction in a network whose stable activity patterns form a continuous set.[^p1-model]
L0004: 
L0005: Starting from the neural dynamics established in Chapter 1, we first express the random spike input as continuous noise under the paper's weak-fluctuation assumptions. We then follow a small disturbance through the circuit's return toward a stable pattern and identify the change in stored value that remains. Adding these lasting random changes gives a local rate of spreading. When connections have equal weights in both directions, we can express that rate using the cells' tuning curves and check its meaning in a two-cell example. The following chapter extends this local prediction to longer delays.
L0006: 
L0007: Sources: Burak and Fiete (2012), [paper](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf) and [Supporting Information (SI)](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf).
L0008: 
L0009: Here the physical network state is the vector of synaptic activations $\mathbf s=(s_1,\ldots,s_N)$; firing rates follow from that state through the input and transfer function. The memory coordinate is $\theta$, and its initial value is $\Theta$. “Stored value” and “represented value” refer to this same coordinate, rather than to the whole activation vector. An observer's estimate $\widehat\theta$, obtained from a finite spike sample, can differ from $\theta$.
L0010: 
L0011: Noise generally puts $\mathbf s$ slightly away from the attractor. To assign it a memory coordinate, imagine turning off further noise and following the deterministic return flow. [The fixed point reached by this flow is the paper's [**instantaneous attractor state**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}; $\theta$ labels that point.]{.definition-explanation} This local definition assumes that nearby states return to a unique point on the continuous family. It uses the circuit's return dynamics, which can differ from a perpendicular geometric projection. [Original definition and Figure 1A, p. 2](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=2).
L0012: 
L0013: A bar denotes a noise free steady state, so $\bar r_i(\theta)$ is neuron $i$'s steady firing rate at memory value $\theta$, and $\bar s_i=\tau\bar r_i$. The common synaptic time constant is $\tau$. A one dimensional diffusion coefficient $D$ has units of memory coordinate squared per time, with the convention $\operatorname{Var}[\theta(t)-\Theta]=2Dt$ when $D$ is constant and boundaries do not matter.
L0014: 
L0015: ## Many weak inputs allow a continuous approximation to spike noise {#sec:MEMORY-WEAK-NOISE}
L0016: 
L0017: **Neural mechanism.** [Chapter 1 established the spike, activation and firing-rate model, Eqs. 7–9](ch-foundations.html#sec-memory-spikes). Here we calculate its fluctuations. The connections are tuned to a continuous family of steady patterns, and the Gaussian approximation assumes small fluctuations in each neuron's total input.
L0018: 
L0019: **Mathematical derivation.** We start from $ds_i=-s_i\,dt/\tau+dN_i$. The elementary spike-generation noise is independent across neurons conditional on the present rates.
L0020: 
L0021: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0022: Conditioned on the present rates, spike counts over a short interval $dt$ are independent Poisson variables. Let $\delta_{ij}$ be one when $i=j$ and zero otherwise. Thus
L0023: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0024: 
L0025: $$
L0026: E[dN_i\mid r_i]=r_i\,dt,
L0027: \qquad
L0028: \operatorname{Cov}(dN_i,dN_j\mid\mathbf r)
L0029: =\delta_{ij}r_i\,dt.
L0030: $$
L0031: 
L0032: Write $dM_i=dN_i-r_i\,dt$ for the spike count minus its conditional mean: the surplus or deficit of spikes in this interval. Splitting the count into its mean and this fluctuation gives
L0033: 
L0034: $$
L0035: ds_i=\left[-\frac{s_i}{\tau}+\phi(g_i)\right]dt+dM_i,
L0036: \qquad
L0037: E[dM_i]=0,
L0038: \quad E[dM_i dM_j]=\delta_{ij}r_i\,dt.
L0039: $$
L0040: 
L0041: The split into a mean and a fluctuation leaves the individual spike events unchanged. Each spike raises $s_i$ by one, and $s_i$ decays between spikes. [In the paper's [**weak fluctuation approximation**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, many individually small spike contributions produce only small changes in each neuron's total input. Their summed fluctuation can then be represented by Gaussian noise with the same leading mean and covariance:]{.definition-explanation}
L0042: 
L0043: Let $F_i(\mathbf s)$ be the mean rate of change of neuron $i$'s activation. Let $B_i$ be independent standard [Brownian motions](ch-persistence.html#sec-foundations-diffusion). The increment $dB_i=B_i(t+dt)-B_i(t)$ has mean zero and variance $dt$. Multiplying it by $\sqrt{r_i}$ gives the same leading variance $r_i\,dt$ as the centered spike count. The stochastic equation is
L0044: 
L0045: $$
L0046: ds_i=F_i(\mathbf s)dt+\sqrt{r_i}\,dB_i,
L0047: \qquad
L0048: F_i(\mathbf s)=-s_i/\tau+\phi\!\left(\sum_jW_{ij}s_j+b_i\right),
L0049: $$
L0050: 
L0051: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0052: The noise coefficient has units $\mathrm{s}^{-1/2}$, so its product with $dB_i$ is a dimensionless activation increment. The Gaussian approximation is for small fluctuations in total input. It does not require each single neuron's entire spike train to look like a Gaussian process ([SI Eqs. S2–S5, p. 2](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=2&zoom=125,0,128)).[^p1-weak]
L0053: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0054: 
L0055: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0056: The supplement makes this limit concrete by replacing each population with $n$ equivalent neurons and dividing each connection weight by $n$. A summed input has a mean proportional to $n/n=1$, while its variance is proportional to $n/n^2=1/n$. Increasing population size therefore keeps the mean dynamics fixed and reduces input fluctuations. For a ring network, a neuron's preferred angle is an angle where its tuning curve peaks. Increasing the density of equally spaced preferred angles and scaling weights as $1/N$ serves the same purpose. The paper's formulas give the leading terms in this large population limit, rather than exact finite network identities.[^p1-large]
L0057: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0058: 
L0059: Setting noise to zero gives the deterministic rate dynamics $\dot{\mathbf s}=\mathbf F(\mathbf s)$. A continuous attractor is a family of fixed points satisfying
L0060: 
L0061: $$
L0062: 0=-\frac{\bar s_i(\theta)}{\tau}
L0063: +\phi[\bar g_i(\theta)],
L0064: \qquad
L0065: \bar s_i(\theta)=\tau\bar r_i(\theta).
L0066: $$
L0067: 
L0068: **Interpretation.** In the noise free dynamics, recurrent firing balances synaptic decay at each fixed point. The network must be tuned to possess this family. An arbitrary recurrent network will generally have isolated fixed points rather than a whole line or ring of them.
L0069: 
L0070: ## The network's return flow determines the lasting memory change {#sec:MEMORY-LINEARIZATION}
L0071: 
L0072: **Neural mechanism.** A spike perturbs many aspects of a network's state. Most of those changes decay as the state returns to the attractor. [To calculate memory loss, we need the lasting change: the displacement to a different fixed point. We assume a smooth continuous family with one neutral memory direction, while all other local modes decay. The disturbance must be small enough that the first order response describes the return flow.]{.key-statement}
L0073: 
L0074: **Mathematical derivation.** [A [**linearization**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} keeps the terms proportional to a small perturbation and drops terms quadratic or higher in it.]{.definition-explanation} Here the network activation is $\mathbf s=\bar{\mathbf s}(\theta)+\delta\mathbf s$. Taylor expansion of the transfer function gives
L0075: 
L0076: $$
L0077: \phi\!\left(\bar g_i+\sum_j W_{ij}\delta s_j\right)
L0078: \simeq\phi(\bar g_i)+\phi'(\bar g_i)\sum_jW_{ij}\delta s_j.
L0079: $$
L0080: 
L0081: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0082: The constant terms cancel because $\bar{\mathbf s}$ is a fixed point. Let $K$ be the linear response matrix: $K\delta\mathbf s$ is the mean rate of change of the activation disturbance. Let $R$ be the diagonal matrix of spike-count variance rates, evaluated at that fixed point. Then
L0083: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0084: 
L0085: $$
L0086: d\delta\mathbf s=K\delta\mathbf s\,dt+R^{1/2}d\mathbf B,
L0087: \qquad
L0088: K_{ij}=-\frac{\delta_{ij}}{\tau}+\phi'(\bar g_i)W_{ij},
L0089: \qquad R=\operatorname{diag}(\bar r_i).
L0090: $$
L0091: 
L0092: In this leading local calculation the noise strengths are evaluated at the fixed point. The entries of $K$ have units of inverse time.[^p1-projection]
L0093: 
L0094: The term $-\delta_{ij}/\tau$ removes a disturbance as synaptic activation fades. The term $\phi'(\bar g_i)W_{ij}$ describes recurrent renewal: the connection converts a change in cell $j$'s activation into input to cell $i$, and the local slope of the transfer function converts that input into a rate change. A steeper slope makes this response stronger. [Whether a disturbance decays, persists, or grows depends on the combined feedback through the whole network.]{.key-statement} A stronger connection alone does not establish better memory.
L0095: 
L0096: Moving a little along the fixed point family changes the activation by
L0097: 
L0098: $$
L0099: \delta\mathbf s\simeq\mathbf u\,\delta\theta,
L0100: \qquad u_i=\frac{d\bar s_i}{d\theta}=\tau\frac{d\bar r_i}{d\theta}.
L0101: $$
L0102: 
L0103: Differentiate the fixed point equation $\mathbf F[\bar{\mathbf s}(\theta)]=0$. The chain rule gives
L0104: 
L0105: $$
L0106: K\mathbf u=0.
L0107: $$
L0108: 
L0109: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0110: [Thus $\mathbf u$ is a [**right zero eigenvector**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}: a state direction that the linear dynamics neither restores nor amplifies.]{.definition-explanation} It is the tangent to the attractor. The other modes must decay for this local family to be attracting.
L0111: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0112: 
L0113: <!-- vale ai-tells.NegatedObject = NO -->
L0114: [To extract the lasting memory displacement from a general perturbation, we need a [**left zero eigenvector**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a vector $\mathbf v$ satisfying]{.definition-explanation}
L0115: <!-- vale ai-tells.NegatedObject = YES -->
L0116: 
L0117: $$
L0118: \mathbf v^T K=0,
L0119: \qquad \mathbf v^T\mathbf u=1.
L0120: $$
L0121: 
L0122: The normalization makes $\mathbf v^T(\mathbf u\,\delta\theta)=\delta\theta$. If a stable mode $\mathbf z$ satisfies $K\mathbf z=\lambda\mathbf z$ with $\lambda\ne0$, then
L0123: 
L0124: $$
L0125: 0=\mathbf v^T K\mathbf z=\lambda\mathbf v^T\mathbf z,
L0126: $$
L0127: 
L0128: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0129: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0130: so $\mathbf v^T\mathbf z=0$. The left vector discards every decaying mode ([SI Eqs. S12–S13, p. 3](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=3&zoom=125,0,409)). Consequently the surviving part of a small perturbation is
L0131: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0132: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0133: 
L0134: $$
L0135: \delta\mathbf s_{\mathrm{surviving}}
L0136: =\mathbf u\,(\mathbf v^T\delta\mathbf s).
L0137: $$
L0138: 
L0139: **Interpretation.** This generally differs from a perpendicular geometric projection. Decay can be tilted relative to the tangent. Even symmetric synaptic weights do not usually make $K$ symmetric, because $\phi'(\bar g_i)$ multiplies different rows by different amounts.
L0140: 
L0141: <div class="publisher-worked-calculation publisher-fold-always">
L0142: 
L0143: **Example: a disturbance that slides while it decays.**
L0144: 
L0145: To see the distinction without a neural simulation, consider the local system
L0146: 
L0147: $$
L0148: \dot x=ay,\qquad \dot y=-\lambda y,\quad\lambda>0.
L0149: $$
L0150: 
L0151: Every point with $y=0$ is fixed. Starting from $(x_0,y_0)$ gives $y(t)=y_0e^{-\lambda t}$ and
L0152: 
L0153: $$
L0154: x(\infty)=x_0+\frac{a}{\lambda}y_0.
L0155: $$
L0156: 
L0157: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0158: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0159: Here $\mathbf u=(1,0)^T$ and $\mathbf v=(1,a/\lambda)^T$. A vertical perturbation changes the final memory whenever $a\ne0$. A perpendicular projection would miss that change. This is a teaching example of the projection geometry, not an additional network used in the paper.
L0160: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0161: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->
L0162: 
L0163: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0164: 
L0165: {{block:fig:MEMORY-TILTED-DECAY}}
L0166: 
L0167: </div>
L0168: 
L0169: As $y$ decays, $x$ changes by one. The left vector extracts that lasting shift.
L0170: 
L0171: </div>
L0172: 
L0173: ## Projecting spike fluctuations gives the diffusion coefficient {#sec:MEMORY-DIFFUSION}
L0174: 
L0175: **Neural mechanism.** We can now calculate how much random displacement remains after the stable components have relaxed. [Each cell's random spikes disturb its outgoing synaptic activation. The circuit's return flow determines the lasting shift in memory.]{.key-statement} This calculation uses the weak noise and conditional independence assumptions, with rates and return flow evaluated near one fixed point.
L0176: 
L0177: **Mathematical derivation.** Recall the [vector of weights $\mathbf v$ derived above](#sec-memory-linearization): a small disturbance $\delta\mathbf s$ leaves an angle shift $\delta\theta=\sum_i v_i\delta s_i$ after the other components decay. Thus $v_i$ is the lasting angular effect per unit change in neuron $i$'s synaptic activation, in this local linear model. The weights reflect the whole circuit's response.
L0178: 
L0179: The variance of a linear combination of independent noise increments is the sum of their variances multiplied by squared weights. Multiplying the linear stochastic equation by $\mathbf v^T$ removes its deterministic return term because $\mathbf v^TK=0$, leaving
L0180: 
L0181: $$
L0182: d\theta=\mathbf v^T R^{1/2}d\mathbf B
L0183: =\sum_i v_i\sqrt{\bar r_i}\,dB_i.
L0184: $$
L0185: 
L0186: The independent Brownian increments have covariance $E[dB_i dB_j]=\delta_{ij}dt$, so
L0187: 
L0188: $$
L0189: E[(d\theta)^2]
L0190: =\sum_{ij}v_iv_j\sqrt{\bar r_i\bar r_j}\,E[dB_i dB_j]
L0191: =\left(\sum_i v_i^2\bar r_i\right)dt.
L0192: $$
L0193: 
L0194: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0195: Matching this to $2D\,dt$ yields the general local result ([SI Eqs. S14–S15, p. 3](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=3&zoom=125,0,576))
L0196: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0197: 
L0198: $$
L0199: \boxed{2D(\theta)=\sum_i v_i(\theta)^2\bar r_i(\theta).}
L0200: $$
L0201: 
L0202: For several memory coordinates $\theta_\alpha$, choose tangent vectors $u_i^\alpha=\partial\bar s_i/\partial\theta_\alpha$ and left vectors with $\sum_i v_i^\alpha u_i^\beta=\delta_{\alpha\beta}$. The same covariance calculation gives
L0203: 
L0204: $$
L0205: 2D_{\alpha\beta}=\sum_i v_i^\alpha\bar r_i v_i^\beta.
L0206: $$
L0207: 
L0208: **Interpretation.** For one memory coordinate, each neuron's contribution combines its spike variability, $\bar r_i$, with the squared effect of one activation fluctuation on the final memory, $v_i^2$. The formula does not say that neurons with high rates necessarily damage memory more: changing rates can also change the tuning curves and the projection weights. Those changes must be included in a comparison.[^p1-projection]
L0209: 
L0210: The result is local in state and leading order in weak noise. The time interval must satisfy two conditions:
L0211: 
L0212: - It must be long enough for the approximation based on many spike contributions to apply.
L0213: - It must be short enough that the coefficients do not change substantially as the memory moves.
L0214: 
L0215: These conditions justify a local spreading rate. Systematic drift requires a [separate check in the next chapter](ch-memory-long-delays.html#sec-memory-drift-scope).
L0216: 
L0217: [The [**diffusion tensor**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the matrix of displacement covariances per twice the elapsed time.]{.definition-explanation} Its diagonal entries describe spreading in individual coordinates. Its other entries describe how coordinate displacements vary together. Even independently generated spike-count fluctuations can generate coupled motion of the represented coordinates.[^p1-multid]
L0218: 
L0219: ## Symmetric weights express diffusion through tuning curves {#sec:MEMORY-SYMMETRIC}
L0220: 
L0221: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0222: **Neural mechanism.** The general formula uses the weights $\mathbf v$ derived from the network's dynamics. The matrix $W$ contains the [connection weights](ch-foundations.html#sec-memory-spikes): $W_{ij}$ describes the effect of neuron $j$'s synaptic activation on neuron $i$'s input. [If each pair has equal connection weights in opposite directions, $W_{ij}=W_{ji}$, can we express $\mathbf v$ through the tuning curves and neural transfer function?]{.key-statement} Equal connection weights do not require equal firing responses. We retain the common synaptic time constant, a stable continuous family, and the weak noise and conditional independence assumptions.
L0223: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0224: 
L0225: **Mathematical derivation.** The chain rule relates the slope of a response curve to the slope of its input curve. Here let $g_i'=d\bar g_i/d\theta$ and $r_i'=d\bar r_i/d\theta$. Differentiating the fixed point identities gives
L0226: 
L0227: $$
L0228: r_i'=\phi'(\bar g_i)g_i',
L0229: \qquad \mathbf g'=\tau W\mathbf r'.
L0230: $$
L0231: 
L0232: With symmetric $W$, the transpose of the linearized matrix satisfies
L0233: 
L0234: $$
L0235: K^T\mathbf g'
L0236: =-\mathbf g'/\tau+W\operatorname{diag}[\phi'(\bar g_i)]\mathbf g'
L0237: =-\mathbf g'/\tau+W\mathbf r'=0.
L0238: $$
L0239: 
L0240: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0241: Therefore $\mathbf v$ is proportional to $\mathbf g'$. Its normalization is
L0242: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0243: 
L0244: $$
L0245: v_i=\frac{g_i'}{\tau\sum_jr_j'g_j'},
L0246: $$
L0247: 
L0248: because $u_i=\tau r_i'$. Substituting into the noise projection gives
L0249: 
L0250: $$
L0251: \boxed{
L0252: 2D(\theta)=
L0253: \frac{\sum_i\bar r_i(g_i')^2}
L0254: {\tau^2[\sum_i r_i'g_i']^2}.
L0255: }
L0256: $$
L0257: 
L0258: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0259: **Interpretation.** The input slopes $g_i'$ now determine the projection weights, so the diffusion coefficient can be calculated from the tuning curves and transfer function. This follows from [SI Eq. S18, p. 4](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=4&zoom=125,0,239), with $\bar g_i=\phi^{-1}(\bar r_i)$ wherever the transfer function is invertible over the relevant range. The supplied Early Edition [main Eq. 2, p. 2](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=2&zoom=125,0,88) omits the square on $g_i'$ in the numerator. [Burak and Fiete's published correction (2017)](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1706051114) confirms the squared expression shown here, which also follows from the projection calculation and SI S18. The guide's checks of other printed formulas remain separate from that correction. The result requires:
L0260: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0261: 
L0262: - Symmetric weights.
L0263: - A stable continuous family.
L0264: - The noise assumptions already introduced.
L0265: 
L0266: The result does not require [**translation symmetry**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, in which shifting an activity pattern along the represented coordinate leaves the network dynamics unchanged in form.[^p1-symmetric]
L0267: 
L0268: <div class="publisher-worked-calculation publisher-fold-always">
L0269: 
L0270: **Example: opposite firing changes with constant diffusion.**
L0271: 
L0272: Take two mutually inhibiting cells with $W_{12}=W_{21}=-1$, zero self weights, equal biases $b$, and $\phi(g)=g/\tau$ over the positive input range. Its fixed points obey
L0273: 
L0274: $$
L0275: \bar s_1=\frac{b+\theta}{2},\qquad
L0276: \bar s_2=\frac{b-\theta}{2},\qquad |\theta|<b.
L0277: $$
L0278: 
L0279: Here $b$ is an activation bias and $\theta=s_1-s_2$ is an activation difference. These have different units from the rate baseline and angle in the [earlier two-cell example](ch-persistence.html#sec-foundations-attractor-example). Writing that example's baseline as $b_{\mathrm{rate}}$ and its angle as $\theta_{\mathrm{angle}}$, the conversion is $b=2\tau b_{\mathrm{rate}}$ and $\theta=2\tau k\theta_{\mathrm{angle}}$. The sum $s_1+s_2$ relaxes to $b$ at rate $2/\tau$, while the difference is neutral. Here $\mathbf u=(1/2,-1/2)^T$, $\mathbf v=(1,-1)^T$, and
L0280: 
L0281: $$
L0282: 2D=\bar r_1+\bar r_2=b/\tau.
L0283: $$
L0284: 
L0285: <!-- vale ai-tells.DefensiveHedges = NO -->
L0286: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0287: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0288: Although the two firing rates change with the stored value, their summed spike variability is constant. Therefore this network has constant local diffusion throughout its valid range. Near an endpoint, a negative linear rate would be impossible. A physical implementation must alter the transfer rule, and the interior formula alone does not describe that boundary. This model underlies the mutual inhibition example in [Fig. 2E–G, p. 3](ch-memory-long-delays.html#fig-paper-memory-2).[^p1-two]
L0289: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0290: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0291: <!-- vale ai-tells.DefensiveHedges = YES -->
L0292: 
L0293: The plot uses $b=20$ activation units and $\tau=0.1\,\mathrm{s}$.
L0294: 
L0295: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0296: 
L0297: {{block:fig:MEMORY-INHIBITION-RATES}}
L0298: 
L0299: </div>
L0300: 
L0301: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeStays = NO -->
L0302: The dashed rate sum in the upper plot stays fixed, so the lower plot has the same $D$ at every interior value of $\theta$.
L0303: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeStays = YES -->
L0304: 
L0305: </div>
L0306: 
L0307: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->

[DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109). [Main Eq. 1 and the steady state, p. 2](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=2&zoom=125,0,60); [Model, Eqs. 7–9, p. 5](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=5&zoom=125,0,372).
L0309: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

L0322: <!-- vale ai-tells.SemicolonUsage = YES -->

- id: fig:MEMORY-TILTED-DECAY
  kind: figure
  placement: sec:MEMORY-LINEARIZATION
  asset: assets/approved/visuals/memory-tilted-decay.png
  caption: Original guide construction of the tilted decay example, with dimensionless
    coordinates and $a/\lambda=1/2$. After an imposed jump from $(0,0)$ to $(0,2)$,
    the state relaxes toward $(1,0)$. Successive time marks crowd toward that endpoint.
    This illustrates the left and right zero vector construction in Burak and Fiete
    (2012), Supporting Text, Section II, pp. 3–4, Eqs. S10–S16. It is not a paper
    network simulation.
  alt: A line of fixed points lies at y equals zero. An imposed vertical jump from
    zero zero to zero two is followed by a straight tilted flow toward one zero. Equal
    time marks crowd toward the endpoint. Perpendicular projection would return to
    zero zero and miss the lasting shift of one. The tangent is one zero. The left
    vector measures delta x plus one half delta y.
  width_inches: 7.29167
  html_fold: never
- id: fig:MEMORY-INHIBITION-RATES
  kind: figure
  placement: sec:MEMORY-SYMMETRIC
  asset: assets/approved/visuals/memory-inhibition-rates.png
  caption: For the chapter’s two mutually inhibiting cells, b = 20 activation units
    and tau = 0.1 s give opposite changes in the two rates. Their sum remains 200
    spikes/s, so local D remains 100 coordinate units squared/s. Open endpoints mark
    the excluded boundaries. The linear interior model does not determine boundary
    behavior. These curves are calculated examples, not simulation data.
  alt: As theta increases through the interior from minus 20 to 20 activation units,
    r1 increases toward 200 spikes per second and r2 decreases toward zero. Their
    dashed sum stays at 200. A lower aligned plot shows D constant at 100 coordinate
    units squared per second. Open endpoint circles exclude both boundaries.
  width_inches: 7.29167
  html_fold: never


## FILE guide/02b-memory-long-delays.md | ch:MEMORY-LONG-DELAYS
L0001: <!-- vale ai-tells.NounString = NO -->
L0002: # Boundaries and drift change memory error over long delays {#ch:MEMORY-LONG-DELAYS}
L0003: <!-- vale ai-tells.NounString = YES -->
L0004: 
L0005: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0006: [A rate of random spreading measured near one stored value does not yet tell us how memory error behaves after a long wait.]{.key-statement} The value may approach a boundary, wrap around an angular range, or reach a region where the spreading rate differs. A consistent movement of the average value would also change the error without being captured by the rate of random spreading alone.
L0007: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0008: 
L0009: We begin by following the distribution of stored values across repeated trials, using the local diffusion coefficient derived in the preceding chapter. This shows why errors can keep growing on a line but level off when angular error is measured within one turn. We then separate random spreading from systematic drift and make the noise assumptions explicit. Finally, the paper's figures show what its network simulations test and distinguish movement of the memory from uncertainty when another circuit reads it. That second uncertainty motivates the information calculation that follows.
L0010: 
L0011: Here $\theta$ is the current stored value and $\Theta$ its common initial value across trials. The local diffusion coefficient $D(\theta)$ comes from the weak spike fluctuations derived in the [preceding chapter](ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-diffusion), after stable disturbances have relaxed. For constant $D$, displacement variance is $2Dt$ before boundaries matter and when there is no systematic drift.
L0012: 
L0013: ## Long delay errors depend on boundaries and local diffusion {#sec:MEMORY-LONG-TIME}
L0014: 
L0015: **Neural mechanism.** Spike fluctuations keep shifting the stored value after the cue has ended. The local diffusion coefficient describes the variance of those shifts after stable disturbances have relaxed. It does not by itself say that variance grows at the same rate forever: boundaries and variation of $D$ can change the long time behavior. We first keep $D$ constant, so the line and circle differ only in how the memory coordinate is bounded.
L0016: 
L0017: **Mathematical derivation.** Let $p(\theta,t)$ be the density of the memory coordinate across repeated trials initialized at $\Theta$. [The [**Itô convention**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} evaluates a state-dependent noise coefficient at the beginning of each increment.]{.definition-explanation} A density equation tracks how small random increments redistribute probability. The calculation below first obtains that equation for a general state-dependent $D$. Constant $D$ then gives the line and circle solutions.
L0018: 
L0019: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Derivation: from random increments to the memory density equation"}
L0020: 
L0021: To pass from random increments to a density equation, choose any smooth function $F(\theta)$. In a short interval $dt$, the zero-drift Itô increment has conditional mean zero and conditional second moment $2D(\theta)dt$. Taylor expansion at the starting coordinate gives
L0022: 
L0023: $$
L0024: \begin{aligned}
L0025: \mathbb E[F(\theta+d\theta)-F(\theta)\mid\theta]
L0026: &=F'(\theta)\mathbb E[d\theta\mid\theta]
L0027: +\tfrac12F''(\theta)\mathbb E[(d\theta)^2\mid\theta]+o(dt)\\
L0028: &=D(\theta)F''(\theta)dt+o(dt).
L0029: \end{aligned}
L0030: $$
L0031: 
L0032: The higher-order terms vanish faster than $dt$ in this diffusion approximation. Averaging over the starting-coordinate density and dividing by $dt$ gives
L0033: 
L0034: $$
L0035: \frac{d}{dt}\int F(\theta)p(\theta,t)\,d\theta
L0036: =\int F''(\theta)D(\theta)p(\theta,t)\,d\theta.
L0037: $$
L0038: 
L0039: Take $F$ to vanish outside an interior region, or use boundaries for which the integration terms vanish. Integrating by parts twice moves both derivatives from $F$ onto the product $Dp$. Because the resulting identity holds for every such smooth $F$, it gives the density equation below.
L0040: 
L0041: :::
L0042: 
L0043: [In the zero-drift diffusion approximation, this density evolves according to the [**Fokker Planck equation**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}:]{.definition-explanation}
L0044: 
L0045: $$
L0046: \frac{\partial p}{\partial t}
L0047: =\frac{\partial^2}{\partial\theta^2}[D(\theta)p],
L0048: \qquad p(\theta,0)=\delta(\theta-\Theta).
L0049: $$
L0050: 
L0051: Here the Dirac delta denotes a point mass: initially all probability is at $\Theta$. It differs from the earlier zero-or-one symbol $\delta_{ij}$. For constant $D$ on an unbounded line, the Gaussian solution is
L0052: 
L0053: $$
L0054: p(\theta,t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi Dt}}
L0055: \exp\left[-\frac{(\theta-\Theta)^2}{4Dt}\right].
L0056: $$
L0057: 
L0058: Its variance is $2Dt$. For an angular coordinate, add the densities at all displacements $\theta-\Theta+2\pi k$, $k\in\mathbb Z$, to wrap the Gaussian around the circle. This wrapped sum appears in [SI Eq. S31, p. 7](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=7&zoom=125,0,483). At long times the density approaches $1/(2\pi)$. If angular errors are reported in $[-\pi,\pi)$, their limiting mean squared value is
L0059: 
L0060: $$
L0061: \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}x^2dx=\frac{\pi^2}{3}.
L0062: $$
L0063: 
L0064: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0065: **Interpretation.** [The flattening of a ring network's error curve can therefore occur while its local noise mechanism remains unchanged. It reflects the bounded way angular error is reported.]{.key-statement}[^p1-fp]
L0066: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0067: 
L0068: For $D=0.5\,\mathrm{rad}^2/\mathrm{s}$, the plots show how the density and squared error change with delay.
L0069: 
L0070: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0071: 
L0072: {{block:fig:MEMORY-LINE-RING}}
L0073: 
L0074: </div>
L0075: 
L0076: As the wrapped density approaches uniform, its mean squared error approaches $\pi^2/3$. The unbounded line error keeps growing.
L0077: 
L0078: **Neural mechanism.** The rate of random spreading can change as the stored value changes, because the current firing rates set the spike variability. The supplement illustrates this dependence with mutual excitation, $W_{12}=W_{21}=1$, biases $(b_0,-b_0)$, and the same linear transfer rule. The fixed activation states are $(\theta+b_0/2,\theta-b_0/2)$. Now $\mathbf u=(1,1)^T$, $\mathbf v=(1/2,1/2)^T$.
L0079: 
L0080: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0081: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeFires = NO -->
L0082: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeStays = NO -->
L0083: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0084: Here $\theta$ is an activation coordinate, not an angle, and both steady rates are positive only when $\theta>|b_0|/2$. When both activations rise together, each cell raises the other's firing enough to replace the extra synaptic decay. This common increase persists and changes the stored value. A disturbance in their difference instead decays toward the difference set by the biases. Larger $\theta$ raises both firing rates and hence both Poisson count variances. Because the projection weights stay fixed in this example, more of that variability remains as memory error.
L0085: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0086: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeStays = YES -->
L0087: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeFires = YES -->
L0088: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0089: 
L0090: **Mathematical derivation.** When $D$ varies with state, the zero drift stochastic equation is $d\theta=\sqrt{2D(\theta)}\,dB$ with the Itô convention. Under conditions that remove boundary terms, the density equation gives
L0091: 
L0092: $$
L0093: \frac{d}{dt}E[\theta]=0,
L0094: \qquad
L0095: \frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{Var}(\theta)=2E[D(\theta)].
L0096: $$
L0097: 
L0098: <!-- vale ai-tells.ConsequenceParticiple = NO -->
L0099: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0100: To obtain these identities, multiply the density equation by $\theta$ or $(\theta-\Theta)^2$, integrate over $\theta$, and integrate by parts twice. The second derivatives of these functions are respectively zero and two, giving the two identities. A similar calculation for the smooth function $D$ gives $dE[D]/dt=E[DD'']$. Expanding at $t=0$ therefore yields
L0101: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0102: <!-- vale ai-tells.ConsequenceParticiple = YES -->
L0103: 
L0104: $$
L0105: \operatorname{Var}(\theta)
L0106: =2D(\Theta)t+D(\Theta)D''(\Theta)t^2+O(t^3).
L0107: $$
L0108: 
L0109: This is [SI Eq. S37, p. 8](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=8&zoom=125,0,602).[^p1-variable] For the mutual excitation network, substituting $v_1=v_2=1/2$ into the diffusion formula gives
L0110: 
L0111: $$
L0112: 2D=\frac{1}{4}(\bar r_1+\bar r_2)=\frac{\theta}{2\tau},
L0113: \qquad D=\frac{\theta}{4\tau}.
L0114: $$
L0115: 
L0116: **Interpretation.** A state dependent $D$ need not produce a nonlinear increase of variance with time. If $D$ is linear in $\theta$, then $E[D(\theta)]=D(E[\theta])=D(\Theta)$, so the variance remains linear while the process stays within the model's valid domain. The implied variance for this network is $\Theta t/(2\tau)$, agreeing with [SI Eq. S41, p. 9](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=9&zoom=125,0,383) and the slope tested in [Fig. S1, p. 10](ch-memory-long-delays.html#fig-paper-memory-s1). SI Eq. S40 instead prints $D=\theta/(2\tau)$. Under the convention used throughout this guide, that displayed value differs by a factor of two from the direct projection and S41.[^p1-excitation]
L0117: 
L0118: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0119: <summary>Original paper figure: Burak and Fiete (2012), Figure S1</summary>
L0120: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0121: 
L0122: {{block:fig:PAPER-MEMORY-S1}}
L0123: 
L0124: </div>
L0125: </details>
L0126: 
L0127: ## A diffusion calculation does not determine systematic drift {#sec:MEMORY-DRIFT-SCOPE}
L0128: 
L0129: **Neural mechanism.** [Repeated trials can spread apart even if their average stays at the original value. That random spreading differs from a reproducible movement in one direction.]{.key-statement} Both can occur, and the derivation of $D$ alone does not settle the second question.
L0130: 
L0131: <!-- vale ai-tells.ConsequenceParticiple = NO -->
L0132: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0133: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0134: [The paper assumes that the network is tuned to eliminate systematic drift, meaning a nonzero mean velocity along the attractor.]{.definition-explanation} A deterministic mismatch in weights can create such a velocity. In addition, state dependent noise and curved network dynamics can create a small mean motion even if the noise free model has an exact continuous attractor. The supplement explains that deriving the exact mean motion requires the microscopic jump process, beyond the leading variance calculation ([SI “Systematic drift,” pp. 4–5](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=4&zoom=125,0,362)).[^p1-systematic]
L0135: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0136: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0137: <!-- vale ai-tells.ConsequenceParticiple = YES -->
L0138: 
L0139: **Mathematical derivation.** For state dependent $D$, where the coefficient is evaluated affects the process. [The [**Stratonovich convention**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} uses midpoint evaluation in its limiting increments.]{.definition-explanation} With the same written coefficient and zero written drift, it describes a different process from the Itô equation. If noise is stronger at larger values, a positive step samples stronger noise at its midpoint and a negative step samples weaker noise. Their average no longer cancels. Converting to Itô form expresses this effect as a drift $D'/2$. The supplement uses the difference to estimate a possible scale of noise induced drift, not to identify the exact drift of every neural network.
L0140: 
L0141: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Derivation: midpoint evaluation adds a mean displacement"}
L0142: 
L0143: Write $b(\theta)=\sqrt{2D(\theta)}$ in a region where $D$ is positive and smooth. A Stratonovich step uses $\Delta\theta=b(\theta+\Delta\theta/2)\Delta W$. Expand about the starting value and use $\Delta\theta\simeq b\Delta W$ in the correction:
L0144: 
L0145: $$
L0146: \Delta\theta\simeq b\Delta W+\frac{b'}2\Delta\theta\Delta W
L0147: \simeq b\Delta W+\frac{bb'}2(\Delta W)^2.
L0148: $$
L0149: 
L0150: Since $E[\Delta W]=0$ and $E[(\Delta W)^2]=\Delta t$, the added mean step is $bb'\Delta t/2$. Differentiating $b^2=2D$ gives $bb'=D'$, so the mean velocity is $D'/2$. The squared-increment term contributes to the limit as the time steps shrink. If $D$ is constant, the correction vanishes. This expansion explains the convention change. It does not determine which convention a microscopic neural process produces.
L0151: 
L0152: :::
L0153: 
L0154: With $D$ of order $1/N$, random displacement over $dt$ is of order $\sqrt{dt/N}$, while such a mean displacement is of order $dt/N$. 
L0155: 
L0156: **Interpretation.** The variance calculation captures the leading random term. [Small changes to weights or biases might cancel the mean drift. They cannot remove the random increments by subtracting a known average.]{.key-statement} A known mean drift can in principle be accounted for by a decoder, but any actual implementation must know that drift and respect the domain and boundary conditions.
L0157: 
L0158: The independence assumption also has a precise scope. Newly generated spike-count fluctuations are conditionally independent given the present state. Shared recurrent fluctuations can still correlate the observed spike trains over time. An extra source of correlated fluctuations would change the projection formula to $2D=\mathbf v^TQ\mathbf v$, where $Q\,dt$ is their increment covariance. A matching calculation of information would need the probabilities of joint spike patterns. The paper discusses such correlated models as future extensions ([Discussion, p. 5, paragraph beginning “Our spiking model”](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=5&zoom=125,0,142)). It does not prove that every correlated spiking model obeys its Poisson formulas.[^p1-correlation]
L0159: 
L0160: ## Figures distinguish memory motion from readout error and test diffusion {#sec:MEMORY-FIGURES}
L0161: 
L0162: **Neural mechanism in the figures.** [Figure 1A, p. 2](ch-memory-long-delays.html#fig-paper-memory-1) illustrates the [coordinate assigned by the return flow in Chapter 3](ch-memory-dynamics.html). The green state returns to the black attractor point under noise free dynamics. Comparing the endpoints before and after one noisy interval isolates the retained memory change. The [tilted two variable example](ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-linearization) shows why the return path matters.
L0163: 
L0164: <!-- vale Google.Units = NO -->
L0165: In Fig. 1B, an activity bump is a localized peak of population firing rates across the neurons' preferred angles. The figure separates two sources of error:
L0166: <!-- vale Google.Units = YES -->
L0167: 
L0168: - During storage, the bump's location has moved away from its initial value $\Theta$.
L0169: - At the later time, an observer has additional uncertainty about the bump's current location because it sees only a finite sample of spikes.
L0170: 
L0171: The [information calculation](ch-memory-information.html#sec-memory-fi) measures this second uncertainty and relates it to diffusion. The [readout chapter](ch-memory-readout.html) then follows observation of the moving state.[^p1-fig1]
L0172: 
L0173: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0174: <summary>Original paper figure: Burak and Fiete (2012), Figure 1</summary>
L0175: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0176: 
L0177: {{block:fig:PAPER-MEMORY-1}}
L0178: 
L0179: </div>
L0180: </details>
L0181: 
L0182: [Figure 2, p. 3](ch-memory-long-delays.html#fig-paper-memory-2) tests the diffusion calculation in two different network designs. Panel A shows a ring with weights determined by differences of preferred angles. This gives rotational symmetry: rotating the activity pattern around the ring leaves its dynamics unchanged in form. In B, compare the firing rate curves and spike marks in the inset before reading the wandering angular trace. The curve describes rates. The marks show individual spikes. The trace shows the represented angle rather than overall activity amplitude.
L0183: 
L0184: **Interpretation of the simulation tests.** Panels C and D test different parts of the prediction:
L0185: 
L0186:   <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0187: - Panel C compares simulated mean squared angular displacements with the prediction from the tuning curves and transfer function. The gray exponential case and black sigmoidal, or S-shaped, case have different transfer functions. The dashed bound is a lower limit for the sigmoidal case, not a separate fitted trajectory. A model can lie above its information bound while still agreeing with the derived diffusion formula.
L0188:   <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0189: - Panel D follows angular error to longer intervals. Its flattening uses angular wrapping, not a disappearance of noise. The expected limiting value with the caption's error convention is $\pi^2/3$.
L0190: 
L0191: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0192: Panels E to G use mutual inhibition rather than a ring. The two rates in F vary in opposite directions. Their difference represents memory. Panel G compares their displacement statistics with the analytic prediction. This second design matters because the projection argument does not require rotational symmetry. These are simulations checking the model calculation. They are not direct measurements of memory noise in an animal.[^p1-fig2]
L0193: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0194: 
L0195: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0196: <summary>Original paper figure: Burak and Fiete (2012), Figure 2</summary>
L0197: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0198: 
L0199: {{block:fig:PAPER-MEMORY-2}}
L0200: 
L0201: </div>
L0202: </details>
L0203: 
L0204: The derivation has now supplied a dynamical quantity, $D$, from spike variability and the way a network returns to its attractor. We next derive a statistical quantity from the same tuning curves and ask why it puts a lower limit on $D$.
L0205: 
L0206: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0207: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->

    opposing rate traces.
  width_inches: 6.5
  html_fold: never

  kind: figure

    growth in the plotted simulation. Original Figure S1 from Burak and Fiete (2012),
