## FILE guide/03-memory-information.md | ch:MEMORY-INFORMATION
L0001: <!-- vale ai-tells.NounString = NO -->
L0002: # Spike information limits memory stability {#ch:MEMORY-INFORMATION}
L0003: <!-- vale ai-tells.NounString = YES -->
L0004: 
L0005: A neuron that receives spikes from a memory circuit can use them to estimate the value stored by that circuit. [Collecting more spikes helps distinguish nearby activity patterns, but during collection the pattern itself keeps moving.]{.key-statement} The same variability that limits readout also perturbs the recurrent network. Burak and Fiete connect these two effects by deriving a lower bound on memory diffusion from the information in the spikes.[^p1-info-main]
L0006: 
L0007: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = NO -->
L0008: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeStays = NO -->
L0009: The useful quantity is how clearly a short spike sample distinguishes nearby memory values. Comparing that quantity with the random motion already derived gives a minimum rate of memory spreading. This is a limit for a particular network, so using it to compare different networks also requires keeping track of what changes and what stays fixed. More information can permit better stability without ensuring that a particular circuit achieves it.
L0010: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeStays = YES -->
L0011: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = YES -->
L0012: 
L0013: We retain the memory coordinate $\theta$, initial value $\Theta$, firing rate $\bar r_i(\theta)$, synaptic time constant $\tau$, and diffusion convention $\operatorname{Var}(\Delta\theta)=2D\Delta t$. The [network projection](ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-linearization) determines which activity changes remain as changes in the stored value.
L0014: 
L0015: ## Rate slopes and spike variability determine local information {#sec:MEMORY-FI}
L0016: 
L0017: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0018: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeStays = NO -->
L0019: **Neural mechanism.** A receiving neuron gets spikes, not a label saying which angle the memory circuit represents. The same angle can produce different spike counts on different trials. To read the angle, an observer needs to know how each cell's expected count changes with angle and how much that count fluctuates when the angle stays fixed.
L0020: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeStays = YES -->
L0021: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0022: 
L0023: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = NO -->
L0024: [[**Fisher information**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} measures how sensitively the probabilities of possible observations change when the value being estimated changes slightly.]{.definition-explanation} Here the observations are spike counts and the unknown value is the circuit's present memory coordinate $\theta$. [Information is large when a small change in the stored value changes the expected counts strongly compared with their random spread.]{.key-statement} It is a property of the spike model at a particular value of $\theta$, before choosing a rule for decoding the spikes.
L0025: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = YES -->
L0026: 
L0027: “Local” means comparing values close to the current one. A neuron can distinguish small changes near one angle yet respond identically at two distant angles. Fisher information describes the nearby distinction. A large value alone does not resolve that distant ambiguity.
L0028: 
L0029: Let $h$ be the observation duration in seconds and $n_i$ neuron $i$'s spike count. For this calculation, hold $\theta$ effectively fixed during observation and assume positive rates. Once that state is fixed, the model treats the counts from different cells as independent Poisson variables. Their mean and variance are both $\bar r_i(\theta)h$. This is a short observation approximation to the recurrent circuit, whose state can otherwise move while spikes are collected.
L0030: 
L0031: <div class="publisher-worked-calculation publisher-fold-always">
L0032: 
L0033: **Example: extra background spikes can make an angle harder to read.**
L0034: 
L0035: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0036: Consider an illustrative neuron with rate 40 spikes per second at the angle of interest. Near that angle, each degree of change increases its rate by 20 spikes per second. In a 0.1-second observation, its mean count is 4 and its standard deviation is $\sqrt{4}=2$ spikes. The mean count changes by 2 spikes per degree. Thus the change per degree is as large as one standard deviation of the count noise.
L0037: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0038: 
L0039: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = NO -->
L0040: Now add 120 spikes per second of background firing that does not depend on angle. The mean count becomes 16 and its standard deviation becomes 4 spikes. The change per degree remains 2 spikes. The same angle change is now half as large relative to the noise. Squaring that comparison gives one quarter of the original information. More spikes have made this particular comparison worse because they added variability without increasing the response to angle.
L0041: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = YES -->
L0042: 
L0043: These numbers describe a chosen local tuning curve and a Poisson count model, not measured neurons. Multiplying the entire tuning curve would be a different change: it would increase the slope as well as the mean rate.
L0044: 
L0045: </div>
L0046: 
L0047: **Mathematical result.** Write $r_i'=d\bar r_i/d\theta$ for the rate change per unit change in the represented value. In an interval $h$, the expected count changes by $h r_i'\,\delta\theta$ for a small change $\delta\theta$. Dividing the squared change per unit $\theta$ by the count variance gives
L0048: 
L0049: $$
L0050: I_{h,i}(\theta)=\frac{(h r_i')^2}{\bar r_i h}
L0051: =h\frac{(r_i')^2}{\bar r_i}.
L0052: $$
L0053: 
L0054: This sensitivity divided by noise expression is exactly the single-cell Fisher information for the Poisson model. For independent counts, the contributions add:
L0055: 
L0056: $$
L0057: I_h(\theta)=h\sum_i\frac{(r_i')^2}{\bar r_i}=hJ(\theta),
L0058: \qquad
L0059: \boxed{J(\theta)=\sum_i\frac{(r_i')^2}{\bar r_i}.}
L0060: $$
L0061: 
L0062: [The [**internal Fisher information rate**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} $J$ is the information per second in these spikes about the circuit's current represented value.]{.definition-explanation} “Internal” identifies what is being estimated: the state maintained by the circuit after the cue has disappeared. If angles are measured in radians, $I_h$ has units $\mathrm{rad}^{-2}$ and $J$ has units $\mathrm{rad}^{-2}\mathrm{s}^{-1}$. They are not numbers of stored bits.[^p1-fi-source]
L0063: 
L0064: The expression explains three ways a code can change:
L0065: 
L0066: - Doubling a cell's tuning slope while keeping its rate fixed gives four times the local information.
L0067: - Adding constant background firing while keeping the slope fixed lowers its contribution, as in the example.
L0068: - Adding another independently fluctuating, tuned cell adds information. Shared fluctuations require a different calculation.
L0069: 
L0070: A rate peak has zero slope, so that cell contributes no information about infinitesimal angle changes at that point under this positive-rate Poisson formula. Other cells with nonzero slopes can still inform the population's estimate. The following curves show where each cell's contribution comes from.
L0071: 
L0072: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0073: 
L0074: {{block:fig:MEMORY-TUNING-INFORMATION}}
L0075: 
L0076: </div>
L0077: 
L0078: Both curves change by the same amount with angle. The higher curve has more Poisson count noise, which explains its lower information away from the peak. The formal definition below shows why the sensitivity divided by noise calculation is Fisher information.
L0079: 
L0080: To compare possible angles, hold the observed spike counts fixed and vary the proposed $\theta$. [A [**likelihood**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} compares proposed parameter values by the probability each assigns to those observations.]{.definition-explanation} It is not, by itself, a probability distribution over the proposed angles.
L0081: 
L0082: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Derivation: likelihood, score, and Fisher information"}
L0083: 
L0084: Suppose four spikes were recorded during the short observation. A candidate angle predicting four spikes on average and one predicting sixteen assign different probabilities to that same count. Evaluating those probabilities as the proposed angle changes gives the likelihood.
L0085: 
L0086: For the full vector $\mathbf n$ of observed counts, independence gives
L0087: 
L0088: $$
L0089: p(\mathbf n\mid\theta)=\prod_i
L0090: \frac{[\bar r_i(\theta)h]^{n_i}e^{-\bar r_i(\theta)h}}{n_i!}.
L0091: $$
L0092: 
L0093: Taking the logarithm turns the product into a sum. With $r_i'=d\bar r_i/d\theta$,
L0094: 
L0095: $$
L0096: \ell(\theta)=\log p(\mathbf n\mid\theta)
L0097: =\sum_i\{n_i\log[\bar r_i(\theta)h]-\bar r_i(\theta)h-\log(n_i!)\},
L0098: $$
L0099: 
L0100: $$
L0101: S(\theta)=\frac{d\ell}{d\theta}
L0102: =\sum_i\frac{r_i'}{\bar r_i}(n_i-\bar r_i h).
L0103: $$
L0104: 
L0105: <!-- vale ai-tells.StackedAnaphora = NO -->
L0106: [The [**score**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} $S$ is the slope of the log likelihood with respect to the proposed value.]{.definition-explanation} A positive score means that a small increase in the proposed angle makes the observed count pattern more likely. A negative score favors a small decrease. At the true value, these signed slopes average to zero over repeated spike samples because $E[n_i]=\bar r_i h$. Squaring before averaging prevents the positive and negative slopes from canceling:
L0107: <!-- vale ai-tells.StackedAnaphora = YES -->
L0108: 
L0109: $$
L0110: E[S^2]
L0111: =\sum_i\frac{(r_i')^2}{\bar r_i^2}\operatorname{Var}(n_i)
L0112: =h\sum_i\frac{(r_i')^2}{\bar r_i}.
L0113: $$
L0114: 
L0115: Products of centered counts from different cells average to zero by conditional independence. The remaining terms use $\operatorname{Var}(n_i)=\bar r_i h$. The general definition of Fisher information is $I_h(\theta)=E_\theta[S(\theta)^2]$: the mean squared score over data generated at $\theta$. Here it gives the Poisson expression $I_h=hJ$ derived above. Other spike distributions can give a different expression.
L0116: 
L0117: :::
L0118: 
L0119: **Interpretation: information limits how accurately the value can be read.** [An [**estimator**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is a rule that turns observed data into an estimated value, here $\hat\theta(\mathbf n)$.]{.definition-explanation} [It is [**unbiased**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} if repeating the observation at a fixed true value gives estimates whose average equals that value.]{.definition-explanation} Individual estimates can still be wrong. Their variance measures their trial-to-trial spread around that average.
L0120: 
L0121: [The [**Cramér Rao bound**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} puts a lower limit on that variance for estimators unbiased across the nearby values, under the smoothness conditions used in the proof:]{.definition-explanation}
L0122: 
L0123: $$
L0124: \boxed{\operatorname{Var}(\hat\theta)\ge\frac{1}{I_h(\theta)}=\frac{1}{hJ(\theta)}.}
L0125: $$
L0126: 
L0127: <!-- vale ai-tells.BareReaches = NO -->
L0128: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = NO -->
L0129: Four times the information halves this lower bound on the standard deviation. It does not promise that a particular decoder reaches the bound. The paper uses an approximation in which the spike sample is informative enough for a suitable estimator to approach it.[^p1-cr]
L0130: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = YES -->
L0131: <!-- vale ai-tells.BareReaches = YES -->
L0132: 
L0133: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Derivation: why inverse information bounds estimation variance"}
L0134: 
L0135: Assume the same estimator is unbiased throughout a neighborhood of $\theta$, the probabilities vary smoothly with $\theta$, and differentiation can be exchanged with summation over the counts. Differentiate $E_\theta[\hat\theta]=\theta$:
L0136: 
L0137: $$
L0138: 1=\sum_{\mathbf n}\hat\theta(\mathbf n)\partial_\theta p(\mathbf n\mid\theta)
L0139: =E[\hat\theta S]
L0140: =E[(\hat\theta-\theta)S].
L0141: $$
L0142: 
L0143: The last equality uses $E[S]=0$. The Cauchy Schwarz inequality says that the squared average product of two variables is no greater than the product of their mean squares. Applying it to estimation error $\hat\theta-\theta$ and score $S$ gives
L0144: 
L0145: $$
L0146: 1\le E[(\hat\theta-\theta)^2]E[S^2],
L0147: \qquad
L0148: \boxed{\operatorname{Var}(\hat\theta)\ge\frac{1}{Jh}.}
L0149: $$
L0150: 
L0151: Unbiasedness makes the mean squared error equal to the variance. Dividing by $E[S^2]=I_h=hJ$ gives the bound when that information is positive and finite.
L0152: 
L0153: :::
L0154: 
L0155: [A [**prior distribution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} describes which parameter values were more plausible before observing these spikes.]{.definition-explanation} A decoder that uses this additional knowledge, or accepts a systematic error, has a different error problem. The bound above also needs care near boundaries or zero firing rates. For sensory estimation the same Poisson formula applies when the rates depend on a present stimulus, whereas here they depend on a maintained internal value.
L0156: 
L0157: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0158: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeStays = NO -->
L0159: <!-- vale ai-tells.OverusedVocabulary = NO -->
L0160: Finally, $I_h=hJ$ assumes that the represented value stays effectively fixed during observation. Longer collection in a freely evolving memory circuit also allows that value to wander. The next section links $J$ to this physical wandering, and the [readout chapter](ch-memory-readout.html) treats what changes when the observer tries to recover the initial value. See [main Eq. 3, p. 2](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=2) and [SI Section IV, pp. 9–10](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=9).
L0161: <!-- vale ai-tells.OverusedVocabulary = YES -->
L0162: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeStays = YES -->
L0163: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0164: 
L0165: ## Spike information sets a lower bound on memory diffusion {#sec:MEMORY-BOUND}
L0166: 
L0167: **Neural mechanism.** The network has no access to its exact firing rates as noiseless numbers. Its recurrent input is built from the same random spikes that an external observer sees. Burak and Fiete give this physical interpretation: maintaining the state acts like estimating it again from roughly one synaptic time constant $\tau$ of the network's own spikes. For a state held fixed, that sample would supply information $J\tau$, so even an ideal locally unbiased estimate would have variance at least $1/(J\tau)$. Comparing this with the variance $2D\tau$ accumulated over the same time suggests $2D\tau\ge1/(J\tau)$. One factor of $\tau$ comes from collecting information; the other comes from accumulating displacement variance. This is the paper's heuristic interpretation; the proof below establishes the inequality from the projection assumptions. [Paper interpretation, p. 3](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=3).
L0168: 
L0169: **Mathematical derivation.** Cauchy Schwarz bounds a dot product by the product of the two vector lengths. Here the normalization of the memory projection fixes that dot product, while the two lengths measure diffusion and information. From the [projection and diffusion calculation](ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-diffusion),
L0170: 
L0171: $$
L0172: 2D=\sum_i v_i^2\bar r_i,
L0173: \qquad
L0174: u_i=\tau r_i',
L0175: \qquad
L0176: \sum_i v_i u_i=1.
L0177: $$
L0178: 
L0179: Define two vectors $a_i=v_i\sqrt{\bar r_i}$ and $b_i=r_i'/\sqrt{\bar r_i}$. Their dot product is
L0180: 
L0181: $$
L0182: \sum_i a_i b_i=\sum_i v_i r_i'=\frac{1}{\tau}.
L0183: $$
L0184: 
L0185: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0186: Their squared lengths are $\sum_i a_i^2=2D$ and $\sum_i b_i^2=J$. Cauchy Schwarz therefore gives
L0187: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0188: 
L0189: $$
L0190: \left(\frac1\tau\right)^2
L0191: \le\left(\sum_i a_i^2\right)\left(\sum_i b_i^2\right)=2DJ,
L0192: $$
L0193: 
L0194: or
L0195: 
L0196: $$
L0197: \boxed{D\ge\frac{1}{2\tau^2J}.}
L0198: $$
L0199: 
L0200: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0201: **Interpretation.** This proof uses the general left vector formula, so symmetric weights are unnecessary. It is a shorter teaching reconstruction of the scalar proof in SI Section V. Its assumptions are those used to derive $D$ and $J$. It is not a general statement about all possible noisy memory systems.[^p1-bound-source]
L0202: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->
L0203: 
L0204: The units agree: $\tau^2J$ has units of time per coordinate squared, so its reciprocal has the units of diffusion. [Small internal information forces a large minimum diffusion coefficient. Large information allows small diffusion, but does not guarantee it.]{.key-statement}
L0205: 
L0206: **Neural mechanism.** [Reaching the bound requires the network's retained response to each cell's spike noise to match that cell's information about memory.]{.key-statement} A cell that adds retained noise without helping distinguish nearby values prevents equality.
L0207: 
L0208: **Mathematical derivation.** Equality requires the two vectors in the proof to be proportional:
L0209: 
L0210: $$
L0211: v_i=c\frac{r_i'}{\bar r_i}
L0212: \quad\text{for every contributing cell},
L0213: \qquad c=\frac{1}{\tau J}.
L0214: $$
L0215: 
L0216: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0217: Cells with zero tuning slope cannot contribute extra projected noise if equality is to hold. In a symmetric network, $v_i$ is proportional to $g_i'$, and $r_i'=\phi'(\bar g_i)g_i'$. At each state, the participating cells therefore need a common value of $\phi'(\bar g_i)/\phi(\bar g_i)$. A sufficient way to satisfy this at all states is to choose that ratio equal to the same constant $k$ throughout the relevant input range. Solving
L0218: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0219: 
L0220: $$
L0221: \frac{d}{dg}\log\phi(g)=k
L0222: $$
L0223: 
L0224: gives $\phi(g)=A e^{kg}$.
L0225: 
L0226: **Interpretation.** This exponential family attains equality for symmetric weights with Poisson noise, under the other model assumptions. It is a sufficient condition, rather than a general necessity: the common ratio may depend on the current memory state.[^p1-equality]
L0227: 
L0228: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Check: equality can also hold with a linear transfer rule"}
L0229: 
L0230: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0231: The [mutual-excitation example](ch-memory-long-delays.html#sec-memory-long-time) shows why equality does not require an exponential transfer rule. Set its bias $b_0=0$ and use the linear rule $\phi(g)=g/\tau$ on positive inputs. Its fixed states are $s_1=s_2=\theta>0$, with $\mathbf u=(1,1)^T$ and $\mathbf v=(1/2,1/2)^T$. Both rates are $\theta/\tau$, and both rate derivatives are $1/\tau$. Therefore
L0232: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0233: 
L0234: $$
L0235: 2D=\frac{\theta}{2\tau},\qquad
L0236: J=\frac{2}{\tau\theta},\qquad
L0237: 2DJ=\frac{1}{\tau^2}.
L0238: $$
L0239: 
L0240: <!-- vale ai-tells.ConclusionMarkers = NO -->
L0241: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0242: Equality holds throughout this family even though its transfer rule is linear. At each state both cells receive the same input, and the ratio $\phi'/\phi=1/\theta$ changes between states. To conclude that equality forces an exponential rule, we need additional restrictions linking the participating input values across cells and states. Continuous variation of the represented value alone does not supply them. This qualification concerns the source's claim that equality requires an exponential rule. The information–diffusion inequality remains unchanged.
L0243: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0244: <!-- vale ai-tells.ConclusionMarkers = YES -->
L0245: 
L0246: :::
L0247: 
L0248: <div class="publisher-worked-calculation publisher-fold-always">
L0249: 
L0250: **Example: the same diffusion with a looser information bound.**
L0251: 
L0252: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0253: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0254: Return to the [two cell mutual inhibition model](ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-symmetric), now using $b=200$ activation units and $\tau=0.1\,\mathrm{s}$. This changes the illustrative bias from the preceding chapter's plot, while retaining the same network equations. The memory coordinate is an activation difference, not an angle. At $\theta=0$ both rates are $1000\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$ and their slopes are $\pm5\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$ per activation unit. Thus
L0255: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0256: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0257: 
L0258: $$
L0259: J=\frac{25}{1000}+\frac{25}{1000}=0.05,
L0260: \qquad D=\frac{b}{2\tau}=1000.
L0261: $$
L0262: 
L0263: Here $J$ has units of inverse activation unit squared per second, and $D$ has activation unit squared per second. The bound is $1/(2\tau^2J)=1000$, so equality holds at this balanced state. At $\theta=100$, the rates become $1500$ and $500\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}$, while their slopes stay $\pm5$. Now
L0264: 
L0265: $$
L0266: J=\frac{25}{1500}+\frac{25}{500}=\frac1{15}\simeq0.06667,
L0267: \qquad \frac1{2\tau^2J}=750.
L0268: $$
L0269: 
L0270: The actual diffusion remains $1000$. The inequality is strict because these two unequal rates no longer align the network's projection weights with the information optimal weights. This also shows why equality at a special state does not force the transfer function to be exponential on its whole domain. A constant lower bound attained everywhere and an accidental equality at one state are different claims.
L0271: 
L0272: 
L0273: 
L0274: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0275: 
L0276: {{block:fig:MEMORY-BOUND-STRICTNESS}}
L0277: 
L0278: </div>
L0279: 
L0280: The gap at $\theta=100$ shows why a lower minimum allowed by the inequality is not a reduction in the network’s actual diffusion. See [Diffusion bound, Eq. 4, p. 3](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=3).
L0281: 
L0282: </div>
L0283: 
L0284: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0285: **Neural mechanism.** When a circuit stores several coordinates, a spike can shift more than one of them. Comparing each coordinate separately would miss combinations of shifts. The information and diffusion must therefore describe these shared directions.
L0286: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0287: 
L0288: **Mathematical derivation.** For $m$ memory coordinates the information is a matrix,
L0289: 
L0290: $$
L0291: J_{\alpha\beta}=\sum_i
L0292: \frac{\partial_{\theta_\alpha}\bar r_i\,
L0293: \partial_{\theta_\beta}\bar r_i}{\bar r_i}.
L0294: $$
L0295: 
L0296: The matrix calculation assumes that every independent small coordinate change can be distinguished through a change in rates, so $J$ is invertible. The matrix bound $2D\succeq\tau^{-2}J^{-1}$ means that every linear combination of coordinate displacements obeys the corresponding variance lower bound. Here $A\succeq B$ means $\mathbf z^T(A-B)\mathbf z\ge0$ for every real vector $\mathbf z$. To derive it, form $N\times m$ matrices $U_{i\alpha}=u_i^\alpha/\sqrt{\bar r_i}$ and $V_{i\alpha}=v_i^\alpha\sqrt{\bar r_i}$. Then
L0297: 
L0298: $$
L0299: U^TV=I,\qquad U^TU=\tau^2J,\qquad V^TV=2D.
L0300: $$
L0301: 
L0302: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Check: the residual matrix proves the lower bound"}
L0303: 
L0304: Set $Q=V-U(U^TU)^{-1}$. Expanding $Q^TQ$ and using $U^TV=I$ gives
L0305: 
L0306: $$
L0307: Q^TQ=V^TV-(U^TU)^{-1}
L0308: =2D-\tau^{-2}J^{-1}\succeq0.
L0309: $$
L0310: 
L0311: :::
L0312: 
L0313: **Interpretation.** The bound controls every combination of stored coordinates, including coupled directions that separate scalar bounds would miss. If $J$ is singular, this ordinary inverse formula cannot be used.[^p1-matrix]
L0314: 
L0315: <!-- vale ai-tells.BareHolds = NO -->
L0316: <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerHeadings = NO -->
L0317: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeHolds = NO -->
L0318: ## Improving persistence depends on what a comparison holds fixed {#sec:MEMORY-COMPARISONS}
L0319: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeHolds = YES -->
L0320: <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerHeadings = YES -->
L0321: <!-- vale ai-tells.BareHolds = YES -->
L0322: 
L0323: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeStays = NO -->
L0324: <!-- vale ai-tells.HedgingPhrases = NO -->
L0325: **Neural mechanism.** [The inequality compares diffusion and information for the same network. To compare different networks, we must say what stays fixed.]{.key-statement} Otherwise an apparent benefit of a parameter change may come from changing another property at the same time. The comparisons below control how added neurons, altered firing rates, or slower synaptic decay affect the tuning curves and recurrent input.
L0326: <!-- vale ai-tells.HedgingPhrases = YES -->
L0327: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeStays = YES -->
L0328: 
L0329: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0330: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0331: **Mathematical derivation.** Increasing $N$ while retaining the same distribution of tuning curves and scaling weights as $1/N$ keeps individual inputs and rates fixed. The sums in the symmetric formula scale as $N$ in the numerator and $N^2$ in the denominator. Consequently $D\propto1/N$, while $J\propto N$. This is a controlled density or population replication comparison, not a promise about adding arbitrary neurons.
L0332: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0333: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0334: 
L0335: If all tuning rates are multiplied by a factor $a$, their slopes are also multiplied by $a$, so $J\mapsto aJ$. For equality achieving exponential networks, $D\mapsto D/a$ immediately follows. The same scaling follows from the symmetric diffusion formula when the change preserves $g_i'$ up to a common scale and preserves the tuning geometry. For a general fixed nonlinear transfer rule, multiplying rates need not preserve those quantities or even the attractor. The paper's rate comparison is made with the tuning shapes and appropriate network scaling held under control.[^p1-scaling]
L0336: 
L0337: Changing $\tau$ while holding the rate tuning curves and their input relation fixed gives $D\propto\tau^{-2}$ in the symmetric formula. Maintaining those curves requires retuning the network, because $\bar s=\tau\bar r$.
L0338: 
L0339: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeStays = NO -->
L0340: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0341: At fixed rates, longer-lasting spike effects give larger mean activations. The activation difference between two nearby stored values also grows in proportion to $\tau$. In this symmetric comparison, one spike's unit activation change therefore produces a smaller displacement of the stored value: the projection weights scale as $1/\tau$. The spike variance per unit time stays fixed, so the retained variance scales as $1/\tau^2$.
L0342: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0343: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeStays = YES -->
L0344: 
L0345: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0346: In contrast, holding activation curves and dimensionless weights fixed in the linear two cell example makes rates scale as $1/\tau$, so $D=b/(2\tau)\propto\tau^{-1}$.
L0347: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0348: 
L0349: **Interpretation.** These two statements concern different controlled comparisons. A larger cellular time constant can improve memory stability while the exponent of its effect depends on what the experiment keeps fixed.
L0350: 
L0351: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0352: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO -->
L0353: **Neural mechanism.** The paper also links tuning width to memory persistence. The reason for its opposite predictions in one and three dimensions can be derived from the number of active cells and their slopes. This derivation is a teaching reconstruction of the tuning width argument cited in the main text, rather than a proof that every tuning family follows one power law.[^p1-width]
L0354: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES -->
L0355: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->
L0356: 
L0357: Assume preferred positions $\mathbf c$ have uniform density $\rho$ in a $d$ dimensional stimulus or memory space. Away from boundaries, let identical tuning curves have fixed peak rate $a$ and a common shape $f$:
L0358: 
L0359: $$
L0360: \bar r_{\mathbf c}(\boldsymbol\theta)
L0361: =a f[(\boldsymbol\theta-\mathbf c)/w].
L0362: $$
L0363: 
L0364: The width $w$ is varied while $a$, $\rho$, and the shape $f$ are fixed.
L0365: 
L0366: **Mathematical derivation.** For one coordinate direction $\alpha$, replace the population sum by an integral and substitute $\mathbf z=(\boldsymbol\theta-\mathbf c)/w$:
L0367: 
L0368: $$
L0369: J_{\alpha\alpha}
L0370: \simeq\rho\int d^d c\,
L0371: \frac{a^2w^{-2}(\partial_\alpha f)^2}{af}
L0372: =\rho a w^{d-2}
L0373: \int d^d z\,\frac{(\partial_\alpha f)^2}{f}.
L0374: $$
L0375: 
L0376: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0377: Two factors determine how information changes with tuning width:
L0378: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0379: 
L0380: - The volume element contributes $w^d$ because broader fields recruit more neurons.
L0381: - The squared slope contributes $w^{-2}$ because broader curves change more slowly.
L0382: 
L0383: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0384: Each diagonal entry therefore scales as $J_{\alpha\alpha}\propto w^{d-2}$. Now restrict the tuning shape $f$ to depend only on distance from its center. Uniformly distributing those centers makes every coordinate direction equivalent: off-diagonal information terms vanish, and all diagonal entries are equal. For an exponential network that attains the matrix bound, $D_{\alpha\alpha}=1/(2\tau^2J_{\alpha\alpha})\propto w^{2-d}$.
L0385: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0386: 
L0387: **Interpretation.** The resulting comparisons are:
L0388: 
L0389: - Narrower curves help in $d=1$.
L0390: - Width has no leading effect in $d=2$.
L0391: - Broader curves help in $d=3$.
L0392: 
L0393: <!-- vale ai-tells.BareReaches = NO -->
L0394: [These conclusions change under another resource constraint. Fixing the total population spike rate instead of peak rate makes $a\propto w^{-d}$, giving $J\propto w^{-2}$ in every dimension.]{.key-statement} Sparse coverage, a large baseline rate, finite boundaries, correlated noise, or a width too small for the population integral can also change the comparison. Attaining equality means the network reaches the diffusion lower bound for its information rate and synaptic time constant. Minimizing diffusion still requires deciding how much information the allowed architecture and firing budget can supply.
L0395: <!-- vale ai-tells.BareReaches = YES -->
L0396: 
L0397: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = NO -->
L0398: <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerLeads = NO -->
L0399: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0400: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0401: If an external stimulus produces the same tuning curves as the network's input free memory states, the sensory and internal Fisher information are equal. In a symmetric exponential network, maximizing this information then improves sensory estimation and minimizes memory diffusion together. This is why the paper suggests that one circuit could support both sensory estimation and memory storage. The mathematical compatibility does not establish where a particular animal stores a memory, and the equality of tuning curves must be checked rather than inferred from the shared formula.[^p1-colocation]
L0402: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0403: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0404: <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerLeads = YES -->
L0405: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = YES -->
L0406: 
L0407: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->

L0409: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0410: 
L0411: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0412: [^p1-fi-source]: Burak, Y., & Fiete, I. R. (2012). *Fundamental limits on persistent activity in networks of noisy neurons*, Supporting Text, pp. 9–10, Section IV, Eqs. S42–S44; main Eq. 3. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109). The score variance calculation is expanded for teaching.
L0413: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0414: 
L0415: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0416: [^p1-cr]: Burak, Y., & Fiete, I. R. (2012). *Fundamental limits on persistent activity in networks of noisy neurons*. PNAS, 109(43), 17645–17650, p. 3, Information-Diffusion Inequality and Saturation. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109). The scalar Cramér Rao proof is a teaching reconstruction of the bound invoked there.
L0417: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->

[DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109). The linear-family counterexample is a guide calculation qualifying the source's converse.

  asset: assets/approved/visuals/memory-tuning-information.png
  caption: Analytic teaching curves $r_A(\theta)=5+35e^{-\theta^2/(2w^2)}$ and $r_B(\theta)=r_A(\theta)+20$,
    in spikes per second, with chosen width $w=0.6$ rad. The rates have the same derivative.
    Their individual Poisson information contributions $(r_i^{\prime})^2/r_i$ vanish
    at the rate peak, where the slope is zero, and are positive on the flanks. Adding
    baseline firing raises the count noise without changing the slope, reducing local
    information. These are illustrative tuning curves, not recorded neurons or a demonstrated
    attractor network.
  alt: The first plot shows two positive peaked rate curves separated by a constant
    twenty spikes per second. The second shows their shared derivative, positive to
    the left, zero at the central peak and negative to the right. The third shows
    squared slope divided by rate for each curve. Both contributions vanish at the
    center and rise on the flanks. The higher baseline curve contributes less at every
    point with nonzero slope.

- id: fig:MEMORY-BOUND-STRICTNESS
  kind: figure
  placement: sec:MEMORY-BOUND
  asset: assets/approved/visuals/memory-bound-strictness.png
  caption: Analytic teaching calculation for the chapter’s mutual inhibition example,
    with $b=200$ activation units and $\tau=0.1$ s. The coordinate $\theta$ is an
    activation difference. For positive rates $r_{1,2}=(b\pm\theta)/(2\tau)$, $J=2000/(40000-\theta^2)$
    while $D=1000$ activation units squared per second. The lower bound $1/(2\tau^2J)$
    equals $D$ at $\theta=0$ and falls to 750 at $\theta=100$. Higher local information
    permits a lower diffusion bound but leaves the actual diffusion unchanged in this
    model. The plotted range stays inside the positive-rate domain $|\theta|<200$.
  alt: Across memory coordinates from minus one hundred sixty to one hundred sixty
    activation units, internal information has a minimum of 0.05 at zero and grows
    toward either side. Actual diffusion is a horizontal line at one thousand. The
    information lower bound meets it at zero and bends downward, reaching seven hundred
    fifty at coordinate one hundred. A marked gap separates actual diffusion from
    the bound there.

## FILE guide/03b-memory-readout.md | ch:MEMORY-READOUT
L0001: <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerHeadings = NO -->
L0002: # Readout accuracy depends on how a moving memory is observed {#ch:MEMORY-READOUT}
L0003: <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerHeadings = YES -->
L0004: 
L0005: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = NO -->
L0006: When a recall cue arrives, the stored value has already moved. Later spikes can help estimate the value present at that cue, but they cannot reveal the particular random displacements that occurred before it. Waiting for more spikes also allows the network to move further. [The question is how much observation helps when the value being read keeps changing.]{.key-statement}
L0007: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = YES -->
L0008: 
L0009: Separating the error accumulated during storage from the error of reading the cue state makes that question precise. Equal weighting of later observations shows why waiting can become harmful. Allowing for the state's motion then shows how observation can keep helping toward a limit, and how access to only some spikes changes the useful duration. The time constants and noise assumptions also need checking before the resulting limits can be compared with the paper's final error claim.
L0010: 
L0011: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0012: As before, the memory coordinate is $\theta$, its initial value is $\Theta$, and its displacement variance is $2Dt$ for constant diffusion coefficient $D$ without boundaries or systematic drift. The storage delay $T$ ends at the recall cue. The observation interval $L$ begins there. The [spike information rate $J$](ch-memory-information.html#sec-memory-fi) measures how clearly nearby present states can be distinguished, and $\tau$ is the basic model's common synaptic time constant.
L0013: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0014: 
L0015: ## Storage loss and readout error add under specific assumptions {#sec:MEMORY-READOUT-SETUP}
L0016: 
L0017: **Neural question and model.** At time $T$, a recall cue arrives. The observer must estimate the original value $\Theta$, but only has access to future spikes. The network has already diffused during storage.
L0018: 
L0019: To solve the readout problem, the supplement approximates the [likelihood](ch-memory-information.html#sec-memory-fi) from each short bin of duration $h$ as Gaussian. Let $x=\theta(T)$ and index subsequent bins by $k$, starting with $\theta_0=x$. The increment $\eta_k$ is the change in the stored value between bins. The observation $y_k$ summarizes the bin's population spikes as an estimate of $\theta_k$, and $\epsilon_k$ is its estimation error. In this approximation, these increments and errors are independent, zero-mean Gaussian variables. This treats the noise moving the state as separate from the noise in the observed counts, excluding the dependence that can arise when the same spikes both change recurrent activation and supply the observation. The equivalent observation model is
L0020: 
L0021: $$
L0022: \theta_{k+1}=\theta_k+\eta_k,
L0023: \qquad y_k=\theta_k+\epsilon_k,
L0024: $$
L0025: 
L0026: $$
L0027: \operatorname{Var}(\eta_k)=2Dh,
L0028: \qquad \operatorname{Var}(\epsilon_k)=\frac1{Jh}.
L0029: $$
L0030: 
L0031: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0032: The value $y_k$ is inferred from spikes, not an additional directly measured physical coordinate. The continuous limit is a convenient mathematical description of the many spike, weak fluctuation regime. It does not assert that an arbitrarily short physical bin already contains enough spikes for a Gaussian fit. The derivations below are exact within this local Gaussian readout model.[^p1-read-gaussian]
L0033: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0034: 
L0035: **Mathematical derivation.** For an unbounded coordinate with constant $D$ and no drift, let $x=\theta(T)$ and let $\widehat x$ estimate that state from later spikes. Write the storage displacement as $s=x-\Theta$ and the readout error as $e=\widehat x-x$. The total recall error is their sum:
L0036: 
L0037: $$
L0038: \widehat x-\Theta=s+e,\qquad
L0039: \operatorname{Var}(s+e)=\operatorname{Var}(s)+\operatorname{Var}(e)
L0040: +2\operatorname{Cov}(s,e).
L0041: $$
L0042: 
L0043: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0044: Assume the readout is locally unbiased at every possible $x$ and the future noise is independent of the storage history. Conditional on that history, the readout error then has mean zero. Since $s$ is fixed by the history, $\mathbb E[se]=\mathbb E[s\,\mathbb E(e\mid\text{storage history})]=0$. Both errors have zero mean, so their covariance vanishes. [In this readout model, [**translation invariance**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} means that shifting the represented value does not change the statistics of the readout error.]{.definition-explanation} Its variance is therefore the same at every $x$. Denote it by $V_{\mathrm{read}}(L)$. Using $\operatorname{Var}(s)=2DT$ gives
L0045: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0046: 
L0047: $$
L0048: V_{\mathrm{total}}(T,L)=2DT+V_{\mathrm{read}}(L).
L0049: $$
L0050: 
L0051: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0052: **Interpretation.** The two terms describe different sources of error:
L0053: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0054: 
L0055: - $2DT$ comes from storage.
L0056: - $V_{\mathrm{read}}(L)$ concerns estimation of $\theta(T)$ from spikes after $T$.
L0057: 
L0058: The supplement derives this decomposition for a locally translation invariant diffusion and readout model. State dependence, boundaries, informative priors, or uncertain initial encoding require corresponding changes.[^p1-read-setup]
L0059: 
L0060: A timeline separates the displacement accumulated before the cue from the error in estimating the cue state afterward.
L0061: 
L0062: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0063: 
L0064: {{block:fig:MEMORY-READOUT-TIMELINE}}
L0065: 
L0066: </div>
L0067: 
L0068: 
L0069: 
L0070: ## Equal weighting becomes harmful when observation lasts too long {#sec:MEMORY-NAIVE}
L0071: 
L0072: **Neural question and model.** A decoder designed for a stationary state averages its observations with equal weight. That reduces independent observation noise, but late observations reflect a state that has moved further from $x$.
L0073: 
L0074: For the recurring mutual-inhibition example, opposite changes in the two rates signal a change in the activation difference. Its $D$ describes how that difference wanders; its $J$ describes how well the spikes distinguish nearby values. The readout calculation combines those same quantities: longer collection reduces count uncertainty while allowing more motion. Here we apply the local model with $D$ and $J$ held fixed near the chosen state. The two-cell network has state-dependent $J$, so these constant-coefficient formulas do not describe excursions across its whole range.
L0075: 
L0076: **Mathematical derivation.** Write $\theta(T+t)=x+B_D(t)$, where $B_D$ has Brownian covariance
L0077: 
L0078: $$
L0079: E[B_D(t)B_D(s)]=2D\min(t,s).
L0080: $$
L0081: 
L0082: The covariance arises because the two values share increments up to the earlier time. Their later increments are independent. The continuous average is
L0083: 
L0084: $$
L0085: \hat x_{\mathrm{naive}}=\frac1L\int_0^L y(t)dt.
L0086: $$
L0087: 
L0088: Count noise contributes $1/(JL)$: $n=L/h$ bins, each of variance $1/(Jh)$, give average variance $[n/(Jh)]/n^2=1/(JL)$. The motion contribution is
L0089: 
L0090: $$
L0091: \frac{2D}{L^2}\int_0^L\int_0^L\min(t,s)\,dt\,ds.
L0092: $$
L0093: 
L0094: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0095: Split the square into its two equal triangles. On the triangle $0\le s\le t\le L$, the minimum is $s$. Therefore
L0096: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0097: 
L0098: $$
L0099: \int_0^L\int_0^L\min(t,s)\,dt\,ds
L0100: =2\int_0^L\!dt\int_0^t\!s\,ds
L0101: =\int_0^L t^2dt=\frac{L^3}{3}.
L0102: $$
L0103: 
L0104: Combining the terms gives
L0105: 
L0106: $$
L0107: \boxed{V_{\mathrm{naive}}(L)=\frac{2DL}{3}+\frac1{JL}.}
L0108: $$
L0109: 
L0110: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0111: The two contributions have opposite dependence on $L$. Differentiating,
L0112: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0113: 
L0114: $$
L0115: \frac{dV_{\mathrm{naive}}}{dL}=\frac{2D}{3}-\frac1{JL^2}=0
L0116: \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
L0117: L_{\mathrm{naive}}^*=\sqrt{\frac3{2DJ}}.
L0118: $$
L0119: 
L0120: At that duration the two error terms are equal, and
L0121: 
L0122: $$
L0123: V_{\mathrm{naive}}^{\min}
L0124: =\frac2{\sqrt3}\sqrt{\frac{2D}{J}}.
L0125: $$
L0126: 
L0127: **Interpretation.** [Waiting longer eventually increases error, even with more spikes available. The problem is their equal weighting for a moving state.]{.key-statement}[^p1-naive]
L0128: 
L0129: ## Accounting for diffusion makes readout improve toward a limit {#sec:MEMORY-IDEAL}
L0130: 
L0131: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0132: **Neural question and model.** An observer that accounts for diffusion can use later observations without confusing them with direct measurements of $x$. Their value is limited by the motion separating them from $x$. This is an ideal inference rule for an observer. The readout calculation does not demonstrate a neural circuit implementing it. Its error can be derived by combining information backward from the end of the observation interval.
L0133: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0134: 
L0135: **Mathematical derivation.** [The [**precision**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} of a scalar Gaussian estimate is the reciprocal of its variance.]{.definition-explanation} Independent Gaussian measurements of one value add precisions. To verify this, multiply likelihoods proportional to $\exp[-(x-a)^2/(2V_a)]$ and $\exp[-(x-b)^2/(2V_b)]$: the coefficient of $x^2/2$ becomes $1/V_a+1/V_b$.
L0136: 
L0137: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0138: Let $P(L)$ be the precision about the state at the beginning of an interval of length $L$, using all observations in that interval and no informative prior. Suppose we have already compressed the later part into a likelihood about the next bin's state with variance $1/P$. Bringing that likelihood one step backward adds transition variance $2Dh$, since the next state equals the earlier state plus an independent Gaussian increment. Its precision about the earlier state is therefore
L0139: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0140: 
L0141: $$
L0142: \frac{1}{1/P+2Dh}=\frac{P}{1+2DhP}.
L0143: $$
L0144: 
L0145: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0146: The new earliest observation adds precision $Jh$. Thus, up to bin endpoint conventions that disappear as $h\to0$,
L0147: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0148: 
L0149: $$
L0150: P(L+h)=Jh+\frac{P(L)}{1+2DhP(L)}.
L0151: $$
L0152: 
L0153: Expand the denominator for small $h$:
L0154: 
L0155: $$
L0156: P(L+h)=P(L)+[J-2DP(L)^2]h+O(h^2).
L0157: $$
L0158: 
L0159: The continuous equation and starting condition are
L0160: 
L0161: $$
L0162: \frac{dP}{dL}=J-2DP^2,
L0163: \qquad P(0)=0.
L0164: $$
L0165: 
L0166: With no observations, precision starts at zero. The term $J$ adds information from new observations. The term $2DP^2$ reduces that gain because later observations are separated from the cue state by diffusion.
L0167: 
L0168: For $D>0$ and $J>0$, the solution gives the readout variance at any observation duration $L>0$:
L0169: 
L0170: $$
L0171: \boxed{V_{\mathrm{ideal}}(L)
L0172: =\sqrt{\frac{2D}{J}}\coth(\sqrt{2DJ}\,L).}
L0173: $$
L0174: 
L0175: [The [**hyperbolic cotangent**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is $\coth z=(e^{2z}+1)/(e^{2z}-1)$.]{.definition-explanation} For positive $z$, it falls from large values toward one. This function describes how the readout variance decreases toward its limit as observation continues.
L0176: 
L0177: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Mathematical solution: hyperbolic functions and finite-time readout error"}
L0178: 
L0179: [The [**hyperbolic tangent**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is $\tanh z=(e^z-e^{-z})/(e^z+e^{-z})$.]{.definition-explanation} [Its [**inverse hyperbolic tangent**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} on $(-1,1)$ is $\operatorname{artanh}z=\tfrac12\log[(1+z)/(1-z)]$, whose derivative is $1/(1-z^2)$.]{.definition-explanation} Set $a=\sqrt{J/(2D)}$ and $k=\sqrt{2DJ}$. Separating variables gives
L0180: 
L0181: $$
L0182: \int_0^{P/a}\frac{dz}{1-z^2}=kL,
L0183: \qquad
L0184: \operatorname{artanh}(P/a)=kL.
L0185: $$
L0186: 
L0187: Applying $\tanh$ to both sides and multiplying by $a$ gives the precision
L0188: 
L0189: $$
L0190: P(L)=\sqrt{\frac{J}{2D}}\tanh(\sqrt{2DJ}\,L),
L0191: $$
L0192: 
L0193: Taking the reciprocal of $P(L)$ gives the displayed finite-time readout variance.
L0194: 
L0195: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0196: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0197: The identity $\coth z=1/\tanh z$ connects this result to the finite-time variance. This backward precision derivation is a teaching reconstruction equivalent to the Gaussian covariance calculation in SI Eqs. S58–S75. It makes explicit how diffusion limits the value of later observations.[^p1-ideal]
L0198: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0199: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->
L0200: 
L0201: For short intervals, $\coth z=1/z+z/3+O(z^3)$, so
L0202: 
L0203: $$
L0204: V_{\mathrm{ideal}}(L)=\frac1{JL}+\frac{2DL}{3}+O(L^3).
L0205: $$
L0206: 
L0207: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0208: The naive and ideal estimators therefore agree through the displayed terms.
L0209: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0210: 
L0211: :::
L0212: 
L0213: For long intervals,
L0214: 
L0215: $$
L0216: V_{\mathrm{ideal}}(L)\longrightarrow\sqrt{2D/J},
L0217: \qquad L^*=\frac1{\sqrt{2DJ}}.
L0218: $$
L0219: 
L0220: **Interpretation.** The ideal observer keeps improving, but the improvement becomes small. Its variance relative to the limiting variance is:
L0221: 
L0222: - $1.313$ at $L=L^*$.
L0223: - $1.037$ at $2L^*$.
L0224: - $1.005$ at $3L^*$.
L0225: 
L0226: These are variances, so their ratios are not ratios of standard deviations.
L0227: 
L0228: Using $2DJ\ge\tau^{-2}$ gives
L0229: 
L0230: $$
L0231: L^*\le\tau,
L0232: \qquad L_{\mathrm{naive}}^*=\sqrt3L^*\le\sqrt3\tau.
L0233: $$
L0234: 
L0235: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0236: [Thus the paper's statement about readout within a cellular time constant concerns a characteristic saturation time under full spike access. It does not mean all benefit ends abruptly at $\tau$.]{.key-statement} The naive observer's best variance is only $2/\sqrt3\simeq1.155$ times the ideal observer's asymptotic variance. Its best standard deviation is about $1.075$ times as large.[^p1-time]
L0237: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0238: 
L0239: ## Limited access can make observation slower {#sec:MEMORY-PARTIAL}
L0240: 
L0241: **Neural mechanism.** [A downstream area may receive only some of the memory network's spikes. That reduces its information without reducing the fluctuations that already move the full memory network.]{.key-statement}
L0242: 
L0243: **Mathematical derivation.** If every spike is independently retained with probability $\alpha$, the observed rates and their slopes both scale by $\alpha$. Substitution in the Poisson information formula gives
L0244: 
L0245: $$
L0246: J_{\mathrm{obs}}=\sum_i\frac{(\alpha r_i')^2}{\alpha\bar r_i}=\alpha J.
L0247: $$
L0248: 
L0249: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0250: The network diffusion coefficient stays $D$. Hence
L0251: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0252: 
L0253: $$
L0254: L_{\mathrm{obs}}^*=\frac1{\sqrt{2D\alpha J}}
L0255: =\frac{L^*}{\sqrt\alpha},
L0256: \qquad
L0257: V_{\mathrm{ideal,obs}}(\infty)=\sqrt{\frac{2D}{\alpha J}}.
L0258: $$
L0259: 
L0260: **Interpretation.** The same factor $1/\sqrt\alpha$ multiplies the naive optimal interval and its minimum variance. The information bound implies $L_{\mathrm{obs}}^*\le\tau/\sqrt\alpha$, which can exceed $\tau$. A selected subset of neurons has exactly $\alpha J$ only when it carries that fraction of the information. A fraction of neurons or spikes chosen by a biased rule need not do so.[^p1-partial]
L0261: 
L0262: <div class="publisher-worked-calculation publisher-fold-always">
L0263: 
L0264: **Example: limited spike access changes the useful interval.**
L0265: 
L0266: Take $\tau=0.1\,\mathrm{s}$, $J=10{,}000\,\mathrm{rad}^{-2}\mathrm{s}^{-1}$, and $D=0.005\,\mathrm{rad}^2/\mathrm{s}$. These values attain the information bound. Full access gives $L^*=0.1\,\mathrm{s}$ and limiting readout variance $0.001\,\mathrm{rad}^2$. The naive optimum is $0.1732\,\mathrm{s}$ with variance $0.001155\,\mathrm{rad}^2$.
L0267: 
L0268: If $\alpha=0.1$, then $J_{\mathrm{obs}}=1000\,\mathrm{rad}^{-2}\mathrm{s}^{-1}$. The ideal characteristic interval becomes $0.3162\,\mathrm{s}$ and its limiting variance becomes $0.003162\,\mathrm{rad}^2$. The naive optimum becomes $0.5477\,\mathrm{s}$. A two second storage delay contributes $2DT=0.020\,\mathrm{rad}^2$ in both cases, giving total ideal asymptotic variances $0.021$ and $0.023162\,\mathrm{rad}^2$. Limited access leaves the storage error unchanged but increases the time and uncertainty involved in reading the cue state.
L0269: 
L0270: </div>
L0271: 
L0272: ## Time scales and noise laws change the bound and its equality condition {#sec:MEMORY-EXTENSIONS}
L0273: 
L0274: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeCarries = NO -->
L0275: **Neural mechanism.** The time constant in the inequality comes from the dynamics that carry an activation fluctuation into a lasting memory change. If both synaptic activation and firing rate adjust gradually, which time constant matters?
L0276: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeCarries = YES -->
L0277: 
L0278: In the basic model, the firing rate changes immediately when the summed input changes. Assume symmetric weights. The extension instead lets $r_i$ approach $\phi(g_i)$ gradually, with time constant $\tau_m$, while synaptic activation still decays with time constant $\tau$.
L0279: 
L0280: The symbol $\xi_i$ writes the spike fluctuation as a drive in a rate-of-change equation. [[**White noise**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is an ideal zero-mean input whose fluctuations at different times are uncorrelated. Its continuous-time strength is defined through a delta-function covariance, rather than a finite variance at an individual time.]{.definition-explanation} In the Gaussian approximation used here, $\xi_i\,dt=\sqrt{r_i}\,dB_i$, with independent Brownian increments $dB_i$. Conditional on the current rates, its contribution over $dt$ has variance $r_i\,dt$, the same spike-count variance as in the basic model. The two dynamical equations are
L0281: 
L0282: $$
L0283: \dot s_i=-s_i/\tau+r_i+\xi_i,
L0284: \qquad
L0285: \tau_m\dot r_i=-r_i+\phi(g_i).
L0286: $$
L0287: 
L0288: **Mathematical derivation.** In this symmetric weight extension, the linear state is $(\delta\mathbf s,\delta\mathbf r)$ and its deterministic matrix is
L0289: 
L0290: $$
L0291: A=\begin{pmatrix}
L0292: -I/\tau&I\\
L0293: \operatorname{diag}(\phi')W/\tau_m&-I/\tau_m
L0294: \end{pmatrix}.
L0295: $$
L0296: 
L0297: <!-- vale ai-tells.NegatedObject = NO -->
L0298: The right tangent is $(\tau\mathbf r',\mathbf r')$. Write the left zero vector as $(\mathbf a,\mathbf b)$. The second block of $(\mathbf a^T,\mathbf b^T)A=0$ gives $\mathbf b=\tau_m\mathbf a$. The first then gives $W^T\operatorname{diag}(\phi')\mathbf a=\mathbf a/\tau$, so symmetry again permits $\mathbf a\propto\mathbf g'$. Normalizing its dot product with the right tangent yields
L0299: <!-- vale ai-tells.NegatedObject = YES -->
L0300: 
L0301: $$
L0302: a_i=\frac{g_i'}{(\tau+\tau_m)\sum_jg_j'r_j'}.
L0303: $$
L0304: 
L0305: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0306: Only the $s$ block receives the modeled spike noise, so $2D=\sum_i a_i^2\bar r_i$. The symmetric formula consequently becomes
L0307: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0308: 
L0309: $$
L0310: 2D=\frac{\sum_i\bar r_i(g_i')^2}
L0311: {(\tau+\tau_m)^2[\sum_i g_i'r_i']^2},
L0312: \qquad
L0313: 2D\ge\frac{J^{-1}}{(\tau+\tau_m)^2}.
L0314: $$
L0315: 
L0316: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0317: **Interpretation.** The sum of the two time constants replaces $\tau$. When one time constant is much larger, it supplies most of that sum. This result belongs to the two stage model and noise placement just described. The paper does not prove that arbitrary collections of biological time constants can always be added.[^p1-two-times]
L0318: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0319: 
L0320: **Neural question and model.** The equality condition also depends on the noise law. To see why, replace Poisson spike-count fluctuations with independent additive Gaussian noise of constant strength $q>0$:
L0321: 
L0322: $$
L0323: ds_i=F_i(\mathbf s)dt+\sqrt q\,dB_i.
L0324: $$
L0325: 
L0326: **Mathematical derivation.** The short interval observation of the mean rate is now Gaussian with variance $q/h$. Repeating the score calculation gives $J=\sum_i(r_i')^2/q$, while projection gives $2D=q\sum_i v_i^2$. The same Cauchy Schwarz proof yields $2DJ\ge\tau^{-2}$. Equality requires $v_i\propto r_i'$ because all cells have the same noise strength. In a symmetric network $v_i\propto g_i'$, so a linear transfer rule supplies the condition by keeping $\phi'(g)$ constant across participating inputs.
L0327: 
L0328: **Interpretation.** In the Poisson case, variance grows with rate and an exponential rule is a sufficient way to attain equality. The pointwise projection condition remains the general criterion, including the [linear-family counterexample](ch-memory-information.html#sec-memory-bound) already derived. The difference between these sufficient classes follows from the noise weighting, not from linearity being universally preferable.[^p1-gaussian]
L0329: 
L0330: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0331: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0332: This explicit $q$ formulation is a teaching reconstruction that keeps dimensions and the noise normalization visible. The supplement's Gaussian subsection establishes the change in equality condition. Here the matched noise and likelihood derivation supplies all factors of $q$ and $\tau$ without requiring the reader to infer a normalization from SI Eqs. S94–S95.
L0333: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0334: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->
L0335: 
L0336: ## Readout saturates, and one printed error bound needs qualification {#sec:MEMORY-FIG3-AND-CAUTION}
L0337: 
L0338: Figure 3 isolates readout uncertainty, rescaling variance by $\sqrt{2D/J}$ and duration by $L^*$. Write $z=L/L^*=\sqrt{2DJ}\,L$ for this dimensionless observation duration. In these axes the ideal curve is $\coth z$ and the naive curve is $z/3+1/z$. Both diverge like $1/z$ at short durations because too few spikes are available. The plotted features are:
L0339: 
L0340: - The ideal solid curve approaches one.
L0341: - The naive dashed curve reaches $2/\sqrt3$ at $z=\sqrt3$, then rises because its equal weighting gives increasing importance to displaced states.
L0342: - The dotted vertical line marks that naive optimum.
L0343: 
L0344: The storage delay contribution $2DT$ is not part of these rescaled curves. Figure S2 repeats the comparison for partial access, showing the rightward and upward shifts when $\alpha=0.1$.[^p1-fig3]
L0345: 
L0346: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0347: <summary>Original paper figure: Burak and Fiete (2012), Figure 3</summary>
L0348: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0349: 
L0350: {{block:fig:PAPER-MEMORY-3}}
L0351: 
L0352: </div>
L0353: </details>
L0354: 
L0355: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0356: <summary>Original paper figure: Burak and Fiete (2012), Figure S2</summary>
L0357: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0358: 
L0359: {{block:fig:PAPER-MEMORY-S2}}
L0360: 
L0361: </div>
L0362: </details>
L0363: 
L0364: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0365: 
L0366: {{block:fig:MEMORY-READOUT-CURVES}}
L0367: 
L0368: </div>
L0369: 
L0370: More observation keeps helping the ideal reader, with diminishing benefit, while equal weighting eventually gives too much influence to a state that has moved.
L0371: 
L0372: One printed inequality deserves special care. Main Eq. 6 says that total error is at least $2D(T+\tau)$. SI Eq. S83 uses a lower bound with readout term $2D\tau/\sqrt\alpha$. But the paper's other derived results imply
L0373: 
L0374: $$
L0375: 2DJ\ge\tau^{-2}
L0376: \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
L0377: J\ge\frac1{2D\tau^2}
L0378: \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
L0379: \sqrt{\frac{2D}{J}}\le2D\tau.
L0380: $$
L0381: 
L0382: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0383: Thus the ideal asymptotic readout term is at most $2D\tau$, with equality when the information diffusion inequality is saturated. Substituting an upper bound for this term cannot establish the printed universal lower bound on total error. Within the local Gaussian model, the defensible statement is
L0384: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0385: 
L0386: $$
L0387: V_{\mathrm{total}}(T,L)
L0388: \ge2DT+\sqrt{2D/J},
L0389: $$
L0390: 
L0391: with equality approached as $L\to\infty$ for the ideal observer. At saturation this asymptotic expression becomes $2D(T+\tau)$. For partial access the same issue occurs with the factor $1/\sqrt\alpha$. This is an apparent inconsistency between printed formulas, identified by algebra using the supplied sources. It is not a claim that the authors issued a correction.[^p1-printed]
L0392: 
L0393: For a concrete strict inequality case, keep $\tau=0.1\,\mathrm{s}$ and $D=0.005\,\mathrm{rad}^2/\mathrm{s}$ but set $J=40{,}000\,\mathrm{rad}^{-2}\mathrm{s}^{-1}$. Then $2DJ=400\,\mathrm{s}^{-2}$ exceeds $\tau^{-2}=100\,\mathrm{s}^{-2}$. The limiting readout variance is $0.0005\,\mathrm{rad}^2$, whereas $2D\tau=0.001\,\mathrm{rad}^2$. At $T=2\,\mathrm{s}$, the Gaussian ideal total limit is $0.0205\,\mathrm{rad}^2$, below $2D(T+\tau)=0.021\,\mathrm{rad}^2$. These values expose the algebraic problem without assuming the bound is saturated.
L0394: 
L0395: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = NO -->
L0396: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0397: When a continuous attractor circuit is used to accumulate an incoming signal over time, its stored value represents the integral of that signal. The short useful readout interval does not imply a short lifetime for this accumulated value. A tuned circuit can continue to represent the mean of accumulated inputs while its variance grows. A leak would instead pull its mean toward a particular value. [Diffusion limits the accuracy of the integral and the usefulness of long observation. It does not by itself introduce that restoring pull.]{.key-statement} Applying these predictions to an animal also requires checking its spike statistics, tuning, drift, access to spikes, and other sources of movement noise.[^p1-integrator]
L0398: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0399: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = YES -->
L0400:

L0402: 
L0403: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0404: [^p1-read-gaussian]: Burak, Y., & Fiete, I. R. (2012). *Fundamental limits on persistent activity in networks of noisy neurons*, Supporting Text, p. 13, Eqs. S58–S63 and the approximation paragraph preceding them. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109).
L0405: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0406:

[DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109). The double integral calculation exposes the covariances between different times compressed in the printed derivation.
L0409: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

[DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109). The supplement identifies the solution with a reverse Kalman filter. Here the equivalent precision recursion supplies a derivation without assuming prior knowledge of that filter. The printed plus sign in S71 does not match its hyperbolic cosine solution S73. The recursion independently gives the final S75 result.

    error is x hat minus x. The estimate is drawn separately from the path endpoint.
    Under the listed independence, unbiasedness and translation assumptions, total
    variance is two D T plus readout variance.
