# PART: Eye motion and place fields constrain different parts of a neural code
These studies connect neural activity to eye control in monkeys and location in rodents and bats. [Ben-Shushan et al. (2022)](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y) supply the central shared-drive inference and a possible integrator explanation in Chapters 7 to 9. [Mainali et al. (2025)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017) supply the spatial model, field-size predictions, and supporting shape tests in Chapters 10 to 12. Chapter 13 is a guide-derived comparison of those results with Burak and Fiete (2012). The eye chapters begin with recordings and then test a possible neural source. The place chapters begin with a spatial input model and then test its field statistics.
These studies connect neural activity to eye control in monkeys and location in rodents and bats. [Ben-Shushan et al. (2022)](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y) supply the central shared-drive inference and a possible integrator explanation in Chapters 7 to 9. [Mainali et al. (2025)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017) supply the spatial model, field-size predictions, and supporting shape tests in Chapters 10 to 12. Chapter 13 is a guide-derived comparison of those results with Burak and Fiete (2012). The eye chapters begin with recordings and then test a possible neural source. The place chapters begin with a spatial input model and then test its field statistics.
The contribution labels describe the role of each result here. **Central** is a main finding of the cited paper. **Supporting** establishes or qualifies that finding. **Background** supplies needed concepts. **Guide-derived** identifies an explanation, comparison, or proposed test developed in this guide.
::: {.part-chapter-map}

- **[Chapter 7: Eye drift points to a shared neural drive](../chapters/ch-eye-drift.html)**

  **Central empirical result:** [Ben-Shushan et al. (2022)](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y) infer a substantial shared drive upstream of ocular motor neurons from recorded eye motion and spikes. The filter and covariance calculations explain the assumptions behind that inference.

- **[Chapter 8: Drift growth distinguishes diffusion from filtered white noise](../chapters/ch-eye-timing.html)**

  **Supporting test:** [Ben-Shushan et al. (2022)](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y) compare displacement statistics with the predictions of mechanical filtering and private motor noise. The one-filter calculation is a guide-derived teaching model, distinct from the full simulation.

- **[Chapter 9: A noisy memory circuit can produce eye drift](../chapters/ch-eye-circuit.html)**

  **Central proposed explanation:** [Ben-Shushan et al. (2022)](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y) show that a noisy integrator model can account for the motion after muscle filtering and delayed feedback. [Aksay et al. (2007)](https://doi.org/10.1038/nn1877) supply supporting circuit architecture. The match supports a possible source, rather than uniquely identifying it.

- **[Chapter 10: Field counts and gaps reveal threshold and scale](../chapters/ch-place-fields.html)**

  **Central spatial model:** [Mainali et al. (2025)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017) describe fields and gaps as regions of a smooth random input above or below threshold. Expanded crossing and parameter-inference calculations are guide-derived.

- **[Chapter 11: High peaks explain size laws across dimensions](../chapters/ch-place-sizes.html)**

  **Central size predictions:** [Mainali et al. (2025)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017) derive dimension-dependent field-size statistics and compare them with recordings. The guide expands the local-peak derivation and separates limiting laws from finite-threshold behavior.

- **[Chapter 12: Holes and measurements test the spatial explanation](../chapters/ch-place-tests.html)**

  **Supporting tests and circuit interpretation:** [Mainali et al. (2025)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017) examine shapes, slices, and connectedness beyond the fitted summaries. The underlying recordings include [Eliav et al. (2021)](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.abg4020), [Harland et al. (2021)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cub.2021.03.003), and [Yartsev and Ulanovsky (2013)](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1235338). Gaussian maps constrain possible inputs without uniquely identifying wiring.

- **[Chapter 13: Spatial maps and memory dynamics need separate tests](../chapters/ch-synthesis.html)**

  **Guide-derived synthesis:** The comparison joins the questions asked by [Burak and Fiete (2012)](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109), [Ben-Shushan et al. (2022)](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y), and [Mainali et al. (2025)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017). Its common-displacement calculation and proposed joint experiment are developed here. The three papers did not jointly test that model.

:::

## FILE guide/04-eye-drift.md | ch:EYE-DRIFT
L0001: # Eye drift points to a shared neural drive {#ch:EYE-DRIFT}
L0002: 
L0003: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = NO -->
L0004: Looking steadily at a small spot does not hold the eye perfectly still. [Between brief, rapid movements called [**microsaccades**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, the eye makes a small, smooth, irregular movement called fixational drift.]{.definition-explanation} Here drift names the slow eye movement, which can be largely random. Systematic drift in the memory equations instead means a nonzero mean velocity. [Does this movement begin as noise in the muscles, or does the brain send a changing command to them?]{.key-statement} Ben-Shushan and colleagues answer this in two stages. Their recordings support a large shared drive upstream of the motor neurons. Their model then proposes a particular source: random motion of the eye position stored in a memory circuit.[^p2-question]
L0005: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = YES -->
L0006: 
L0007: This distinction connects the previous chapters to a measurement outside the brain. A memory coordinate can diffuse inside a neural network, but the movement produced by that coordinate also depends on the muscles and the mechanics of the eye. We need to follow the signal through those stages before comparing a neural diffusion law with an eye trajectory.
L0008: 
L0009: A recording from one motor neuron becomes useful for this comparison only after its spikes and the eye's movement can be expressed on the same scale. The larger movements provide that calibration. Applying it during fixation then lets us ask whether independent motor noise could explain the observed relationship, and estimate the shared contribution under explicit gain and independence assumptions.
L0010: 
L0011: ## From stored eye position to muscle action {#sec:EYE-PATHWAY}
L0012: 
L0013: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0014: The [eye anatomy and motor action](ch-foundations.html#sec-foundations-eye-action) explain why a position must be maintained by an ongoing command. The oculomotor integrator supplies the lasting position signal. In the proposed mechanism, the continuous attractor described in the [foundations](ch-persistence.html#sec-foundations-attractor) and [network model](ch-foundations.html#sec-memory-spikes) stores horizontal eye position. Noise moves the stored value along that attractor.[^p2-pathway]
L0015: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0016: 
L0017: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeCarries = NO -->
L0018: The signal reaches ocular motoneurons (OMNs), whose spikes activate the eye muscles. [The recordings came from the [**abducens nucleus**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a group of brainstem neurons that includes OMNs activating the lateral rectus muscle, which turns the eye outward.]{.definition-explanation} Other neurons in that nucleus carry signals to other neurons rather than directly to muscle. The study separated these cell types by how their firing changed with eye position and movement. [Finally, the muscles and the mechanical properties of the eye, together called the [**ocular plant**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, turn neural activity into movement.]{.definition-explanation}[^p2-recordings]
L0019: <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeCarries = YES -->
L0020: 
L0021: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0022: <summary>Original paper figure: Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), Extended Data Figure 6</summary>
L0023: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0024: 
L0025: {{block:fig:PAPER-EYE-ED6}}
L0026: 
L0027: </div>
L0028: </details>
L0029: 
L0030: There are three possible contributions to an observed fixational drift:
L0031: 
L0032: - A changing shared command sent to many OMNs.
L0033: - Private spike variability in individual OMNs.
L0034: - Variability after those neurons, including muscle and measurement noise.
L0035: 
L0036: <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerLeads = NO -->
L0037: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0038: <!-- vale ai-tells.MortalityMetaphors = NO -->
L0039: [Private spike fluctuations can partly cancel when many neurons act together. A common command survives this averaging.]{.key-statement} This is why the noisy activity of one neuron can reveal a source that dominates the much smoother eye movement. The shared drive and common command below mean the signal sent to many OMNs. The variable $C$ later measures its contribution to eye displacement after motor and muscle filtering.
L0040: <!-- vale ai-tells.MortalityMetaphors = YES -->
L0041: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0042: <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerLeads = YES -->
L0043: 
L0044: ## The recordings pair one motor neuron with movement at a time {#sec:EYE-RECORDINGS}
L0045: 
L0046: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0047: The authors reanalyzed earlier experiments in two male rhesus macaques. With their heads held still, the monkeys first looked at a stationary spot of light on a monitor and then followed the moving spot. A wire search coil on one eye measured the eye's angle while an electrode recorded one neuron's spikes. There were 57 identified OMNs. [Each recording paired one OMN with eye movement. There were no simultaneous recordings of multiple OMNs. Thus the analysis cannot directly measure the covariance between two recorded motor neurons.]{.key-statement}[^p2-recordings][^p2-single]
L0048: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0049: 
L0050: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0051: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0052: The initial stationary target was followed by 500–700 ms of required fixation before the target stepped and began moving. Neural voltage was sampled at 25 kHz and eye position at 1 kHz. The authors detected microsaccades using eye speed and acceleration, removed 30 ms on each side of them, and manually checked the remaining segments. For the main displacement analysis, segments shorter than 350 ms were excluded and longer ones were split into nonoverlapping 350 ms pieces. There were 302 such pieces per cell on average, with different counts across cells. These repeated observations improve estimates within two animals. They do not create 57 independent animal samples.[^p2-segments]
L0053: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0054: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0055: 
L0056: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0057: <summary>Original paper figure: Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), Extended Data Figure 7</summary>
L0058: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0059: 
L0060: {{block:fig:PAPER-EYE-ED7}}
L0061: 
L0062: </div>
L0063: </details>
L0064: 
L0065: The large movements have a useful role even though the scientific question concerns tiny drifts. They produce enough rate change to estimate how each cell's activity depends on eye movement. The fitted relationship is then applied during fixation. This transfer is an assumption to examine, because an incorrect gain during drift would change the inferred common variance. Gain is the scale that converts a change in one signal into a change in the other.
L0066: 
L0067: 
L0068: 
L0069: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0070: 
L0071: {{block:fig:EYE-PHYSICAL-AND-RECORDED}}
L0072: 
L0073: </div>
L0074: 
L0075: 
L0076: 
L0077: ## A fitted model links movement to firing rate {#sec:EYE-FORWARD-FIT}
L0078: 
L0079: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0080: Position alone does not predict a motor neuron's activity during a movement. At the same position, firing can differ when the eye is moving or accelerating. The paper therefore fits
L0081: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0082: 
L0083: $$
L0084: \widehat f(t)=k[E(t+\Delta t)-E_T]
L0085:  +r\dot E(t+\Delta t)+m\ddot E(t+\Delta t).
L0086: $$
L0087: 
L0088: Here $E$ is horizontal eye position in degrees and $\widehat f$ is predicted firing rate in spikes/s. The position threshold $E_T$ sets where the position term reaches zero. The coefficients have units
L0089: 
L0090: $$
L0091: [k]=\frac{\text{spikes}}{\text{s}\,\text{deg}},\qquad
L0092: [r]=\frac{\text{spikes}}{\text{deg}},\qquad
L0093: [m]=\frac{\text{spikes}\,\text{s}}{\text{deg}}.
L0094: $$
L0095: 
L0096: Multiplying by position, velocity, and acceleration gives spikes/s in all three terms.
L0097: 
L0098: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0099: The position term describes ongoing firing associated with holding the eye at an angle. The velocity term allows a different rate while the eye passes through that same angle. The acceleration term allows another change while its velocity changes. Thus $k$, $r$, and $m$ describe different demands on the motor signal. They are fitted coefficients of the combined neural and mechanical relationship. In particular, $m$ is not a direct measurement of the eye's mass. The delay $\Delta t$ is in seconds: firing at time $t$ predicts movement at the later time $t+\Delta t$ for a positive neural lead. This is the paper's Eq. 2, including its threshold and time shift.[^p2-forward]
L0100: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0101: 
L0102: The observed rate $f(t)$ was estimated as the inverse of the interval between successive spikes, constant between those spikes. For each cell, the authors minimized the sum of squared rate errors,
L0103: 
L0104: $$
L0105: L=\sum_{a=1}^{n_{\rm trials}}\sum_{t_j\in[0,T_a]}
L0106: \left[f_a(t_j)-\widehat f_a(t_j;k,r,m,\Delta t,E_T)\right]^2.
L0107: $$
L0108: 
L0109: $a$ labels trials and $T_a$ is trial duration. This is Eq. 3 with the trial count renamed so that it cannot be confused with a neuron count. Fits used full trials, including fixation and pursuit. To predict a trial's rate, that trial was left out of the parameter fit. Acceleration was estimated from velocity after local polynomial smoothing over 21 ms. Trials in which the rate fell below 20 Hz were discarded: the linear formula is intended for active cells above firing threshold, whereas firing cannot become negative.[^p2-forward]
L0110: 
L0111: <!-- vale Google.Units = NO -->
L0112: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0113: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0114: During fixation, the authors subtracted each segment's mean firing rate, joined the segments, and compared observed and predicted rate fluctuations. Figure 1c illustrates a weak relationship for one cell. Fig. 1d shows a positive population effect. The authors used a [**one sided t test**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}: it compares the mean correlation across 57 cells with zero, after dividing that mean by its estimated sampling uncertainty. The positive direction was the one being tested.
L0115: 
L0116: [The [**p value**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the probability that this test would give a value at least as large as observed if the population mean correlation were zero and the test's sampling assumptions held.]{.definition-explanation} The reported $p=3\times10^{-9}$ measures how unusual the result is under that reference. It is not the probability that the zero-mean hypothesis, or a proposed neural mechanism, is true. A weak single cell relationship is expected here because the tiny movement produces small rate changes against much larger spike variability. This is evidence of a relationship, before any estimate of the fraction of common motion.[^p2-rate-result]
L0117: 
L0118: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Calculation: what the correlation test compares"}
L0119: 
L0120: Let $R_i$ be the correlation for cell $i$, $\bar R$ the mean across $n=57$ cells, and $s_R$ their sample standard deviation. The number compared with the reference distribution, called the test statistic, is $t=\bar R/(s_R/\sqrt{n})$. The denominator estimates the uncertainty in the mean. The reference is the Student t distribution with $n-1=56$ degrees of freedom: the distribution of this ratio for independent, identically distributed normal observations with true mean zero. Applying it to the cell correlations uses that sampling approximation. The one sided p value is the reference probability above the observed $t$. The observations in this calculation are cells recorded from two monkeys, not 57 independent animals.
L0121: 
L0122: :::
L0123: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0124: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->
L0125: <!-- vale Google.Units = YES -->
L0126: 
L0127: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0128: <summary>Original paper figure: Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), Figure 1</summary>
L0129: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0130: 
L0131: {{block:fig:PAPER-EYE-1}}
L0132: 
L0133: </div>
L0134: </details>
L0135: 
L0136: ## A calibrated filter predicts eye position from one cell's spikes {#sec:EYE-INVERSE-FILTER}
L0137: 
L0138: <!-- vale ai-tells.ServesAsDodge = NO -->
L0139: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO -->
L0140: To ask how much eye movement is shared with a neuron, it helps to express both signals in degrees. [The paper reverses the fitted relationship: a cell's spike train becomes the input, and its prediction of eye position becomes the output. This operation is an inverse of the movement-to-rate fit, rather than a new fit to small drift residuals.]{.key-statement}
L0141: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES -->
L0142: <!-- vale ai-tells.ServesAsDodge = YES -->
L0143: 
L0144: Let $x(t)=E(t)-E_T$. Ignoring the time shift for a moment, the deterministic relation is
L0145: 
L0146: $$
L0147: m\ddot x+r\dot x+kx=f.
L0148: $$
L0149: 
L0150: [A [**causal filter**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} computes its present output from present and past input. For the equation above, its [**impulse response**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} $h_0(u)$ is the position response to one instantaneous spike at time zero.]{.definition-explanation} To factor the equation, consider a time component $e^{pt}$: differentiation multiplies it by $p$. The algebraic ratio of output to input for that component is
L0151: 
L0152: $$
L0153: H_0(p)=\frac{1}{mp^2+rp+k}
L0154: =\frac{1}{m(p+1/\tau_+)(p+1/\tau_-)}.
L0155: $$
L0156: 
L0157: Factoring the quadratic gives the slow and fast decay times
L0158: 
L0159: $$
L0160: \tau_+=\frac{2}{r/m-\sqrt{(r/m)^2-4k/m}},\qquad
L0161: \tau_-=\frac{2}{r/m+\sqrt{(r/m)^2-4k/m}}.
L0162: $$
L0163: 
L0164: These are the paired signs in the paper's Eq. 7, with $\tau_+>\tau_-$ when $k,r,m>0$ and $r^2>4km$. Under these conditions both components decay without oscillation.
L0165: 
L0166: At rest, the inverse equation gives $x=f/k$: with the threshold fixed, doubling $k$ halves the position offset predicted from the same steady firing rate. During a change, $r$ and $m$ also affect the response through the two timescales. This is a consequence of the fitted relationship, not evidence that changing one recorded neuron's rate alone sets eye position. The same factorization gives two useful identities:
L0167: 
L0168: $$
L0169: \tau_+\tau_-=\frac{m}{k},\qquad
L0170: \tau_++\tau_-=\frac{r}{k}.
L0171: $$
L0172: 
L0173: The corresponding impulse response is
L0174: 
L0175: $$
L0176: h_0(u)=\frac{\Theta(u)}{m}\,
L0177: \frac{\tau_+\tau_-}{\tau_+-\tau_-}
L0178: \left(e^{-u/\tau_+}-e^{-u/\tau_-}\right).
L0179: $$
L0180: 
L0181: $\Theta(u)$ is zero for $u<0$ and one for $u>0$, which makes the response zero before the input. Restoring the fitted neural lead delays this response: $h(u)=h_0(u-\Delta t)$. Write the spike train as $\xi(t)=\sum_j\delta(t-t_j)$, where $\delta$ is an ideal instantaneous impulse of unit area, so integration over one spike counts one event. The prediction is
L0182: 
L0183: $$
L0184: \widehat E(t)=E_T+\int_{-\infty}^{t}h(t-v)\xi(v)\,dv
L0185: =E_T+\sum_j h(t-t_j).
L0186: $$
L0187: 
L0188: These expressions reproduce Eqs. 4–6, including the factor $\tau_+\tau_-/m$ and the delay. [The [**convolution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} integral adds the responses to all past spikes.]{.definition-explanation} With spike count treated as a count, $\xi$ has units spikes/s and $h$ has units degrees/spike. Each spike contributes a decaying position response. Adding those responses produces degrees.[^p2-inversion]
L0189: 
L0190: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Derivation: the weights of the two decaying responses"}
L0191: 
L0192: We can obtain the response to a spike directly from the differential equation. After the spike, the input is zero, and each of the two exponentials solves the remaining homogeneous equation. Write their combination as
L0193: 
L0194: $$
L0195: h_0(u)=A e^{-u/\tau_+}+B e^{-u/\tau_-},\qquad u>0.
L0196: $$
L0197: 
L0198: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = NO -->
L0199: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0200: To find $A$ and $B$, treat the spike as a short pulse of unit area and shrink its duration to zero. The position change during that pulse tends to zero, so $h_0(0^+)=0$. Integrating $m h_0''+r h_0'+k h_0$ across the pulse gives $m h_0'(0^+)=1$: the velocity changes by $1/m$, while position and velocity before the causal response are zero. Therefore
L0201: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0202: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = YES -->
L0203: 
L0204: $$
L0205: A+B=0,\qquad
L0206: -\frac{A}{\tau_+}-\frac{B}{\tau_-}=\frac1m.
L0207: $$
L0208: 
L0209: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0210: Substituting $B=-A$ gives $A(1/\tau_- -1/\tau_+)=1/m$, hence $A=\tau_+\tau_-/[m(\tau_+-\tau_-)]$. Making the response zero before the spike gives the impulse response $h_0(u)$.
L0211: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0212: 
L0213: :::
L0214: 
L0215: For a constructed example, take $k=4$ spikes/(s deg), $\tau_+=0.120$ s, and $\tau_-=0.005$ s. The identities above require $r=0.500$ spikes/deg and $m=0.00240$ spikes s/deg. Then
L0216: 
L0217: $$
L0218: h_0(u)=2.174\left(e^{-u/0.120}-e^{-u/0.005}\right)
L0219: \ \text{deg/spike},\quad u>0,
L0220: $$
L0221: 
L0222: and its area is $2.174(0.120-0.005)=0.250$ deg s/spike, equal to $1/k$. A steady rate of 80 spikes/s predicts an offset of 20 degrees above $E_T$. This checks the gain and the time constants.
L0223: 
L0224: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Check: static gain and equal decay times"}
L0225: 
L0226: The kernel's area checks its normalization:
L0227: 
L0228: $$
L0229: \int_0^\infty h_0(u)\,du
L0230: =\frac{\tau_+\tau_-}{m(\tau_+-\tau_-)}(\tau_+-\tau_-)
L0231: =\frac1k.
L0232: $$
L0233: 
L0234: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0235: Thus a constant input rate $f_0$ predicts $E_T+f_0/k$, exactly the static forward relation. A kernel with unit area would give the wrong conversion unless $k$ had already been absorbed into the input. When the two decay times coincide, $r^2=4km$, the limit is $h_0(u)=\Theta(u)u e^{-u/\tau}/m$. The distinct exponential formula is used for the fitted responses in the paper.
L0236: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0237: 
L0238: :::
L0239: 
L0240: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0241: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO -->
L0242: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO -->
L0243: Figure 2a–b shows typical fitted times of about 5 ms and 120 ms. Figure 2c shows that a single cell's reconstructed drift is much noisier than the measured movement. Pearson correlation is the ordinary linear correlation defined as $\rho$ in the [foundations](ch-foundations.html#sec-foundations-covariance); this paper writes it as $R$. Across 350 ms displacement pairs, the mean Pearson correlation was $\langle R\rangle=0.17\pm0.02$, [where the error is the [**standard error of the mean (SEM)**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}. SEM estimates uncertainty in the across-cell mean, rather than the spread of individual cells or uncertainty across animals.]{.definition-explanation} The one sided test gave $p=3.8\times10^{-12}$. Shuffling which measured segment was paired with which spike prediction centered the comparison near zero. Here and below $\langle\cdot\rangle$ over cells means a population average.[^p2-correlation]
L0244: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES -->
L0245: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES -->
L0246: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0247: 
L0248: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0249: <summary>Original paper figure: Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), Figure 2</summary>
L0250: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0251: 
L0252: {{block:fig:PAPER-EYE-2}}
L0253: 
L0254: </div>
L0255: </details>
L0256: 
L0257: ## The measured correlation challenges independent motor noise alone {#sec:EYE-SHARED-VARIANCE}
L0258: 
L0259: A correlation of 0.17 does not immediately tell us how much motion comes from the common command. First we can ask a more limited question: could all the motion come from independent motor neuron fluctuations?
L0260: 
L0261: For a centered displacement $Y$ and one centered prediction $X$, the best linear prediction $bX$ minimizes $\operatorname{Var}(Y-bX)$. Differentiating with respect to $b$ gives $b=\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)/\operatorname{Var}(X)$. Its explained fraction of variance is
L0262: 
L0263: $$
L0264: \frac{b^2\operatorname{Var}(X)}{\operatorname{Var}(Y)}
L0265: =\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)^2}{\operatorname{Var}(X)\operatorname{Var}(Y)}
L0266: =R^2.
L0267: $$
L0268: 
L0269: [This is the [**coefficient of determination**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} for a linear prediction with a fitted gain.]{.definition-explanation} It does not assert that the raw, unscaled position predictor itself has a small squared error.
L0270: 
L0271: Suppose there are $N_m$ independent single neuron predictors $X_i$. Standardize each to variance one and also standardize $Y$. Independence makes the $X_i$ uncorrelated, so the residual after using them all has variance
L0272: 
L0273: $$
L0274: \operatorname{Var}\left(Y-\sum_i R_iX_i\right)
L0275: =1-2\sum_iR_i^2+\sum_iR_i^2
L0276: =1-\sum_iR_i^2\ge0.
L0277: $$
L0278: 
L0279: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0280: Consequently $\sum_iR_i^2\le1$, and an average neuron explains at most $1/N_m$. This proves the bound used in Supplementary Eq. S1. It does not require equal muscle effects from all neurons. Applying it to the sampled cells does require their average explanatory power to represent the relevant motor population.[^p2-independent-bound]
L0281: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0282: 
L0283: The authors avoid directly averaging noisy squared correlations. Instead,
L0284: 
L0285: $$
L0286: \langle R^2\rangle-\langle R\rangle^2=\operatorname{Var}(R)\ge0,
L0287: \qquad
L0288: \langle R^2\rangle\ge(0.17)^2=0.0289.
L0289: $$
L0290: 
L0291: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO -->
L0292: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0293: [Thus the estimated mean correlation implies an average explained fraction of at least about 2.9%. For 1,000 independently contributing motor neurons, the bound would be 0.1%. For several thousand it is smaller. The mismatch argues against independent OMN noise as the sole source.]{.key-statement} Shared fluctuations can allow many cells to explain the same part of the movement, so their explained fractions can overlap.[^p2-independent-bound]
L0294: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0295: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES -->
L0296: 
L0297: This step finds shared variation. Locating its source upstream also uses a biological premise: the private variability generated within OMNs is taken to be independent across cells. The paper supports that premise using anatomical and stimulation studies indicating little local recurrent coupling in the relevant motor nuclei. It was not tested by simultaneous recordings in this data set. Common inputs can correlate the total activity of OMNs even when their privately generated spike noise is independent.[^p2-independence]
L0298: 
L0299: ## Calibrated covariance estimates the common share of movement variance {#sec:EYE-CHI}
L0300: 
L0301: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO -->
L0302: The variance bound establishes that a shared signal exists, but leaves its size open. The next calculation uses the inverse filter’s calibrated gain in degrees. For one 350 ms interval, write the measured displacement and the single cell prediction as
L0303: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES -->
L0304: 
L0305: $$
L0306: Y=\delta E=C+\eta,\qquad
L0307: X=\delta\widehat E=C+\epsilon,
L0308: $$
L0309: 
L0310: where $C=\delta E_C$ is the common upstream contribution, $\eta$ combines motor neuron, muscle, and measurement contributions to measured displacement, and $\epsilon$ is the prediction error from the individual cell. All are displacement variables in degrees. The calculation assumes:
L0311: 
L0312:   <!-- vale ai-tells.ConsequenceParticiple = NO -->
L0313: - The common contribution enters both signals with unit gain, meaning the same one-for-one displacement scale.
L0314:   <!-- vale ai-tells.ConsequenceParticiple = YES -->
L0315: - $C,\eta,\epsilon$ are pairwise uncorrelated to the required accuracy.
L0316: 
L0317: Expanding the covariance shows why those assumptions matter:
L0318: 
L0319: $$
L0320: \operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)=\operatorname{Var}(C)
L0321: +\operatorname{Cov}(C,\eta)+\operatorname{Cov}(\epsilon,C)
L0322: +\operatorname{Cov}(\epsilon,\eta)
L0323: \simeq\operatorname{Var}(C).
L0324: $$
L0325: 
L0326: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0327: The common fraction is therefore estimated by
L0328: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0329: 
L0330: $$
L0331: \chi=\frac{\operatorname{Var}(C)}{\operatorname{Var}(Y)}
L0332: \simeq\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)}{\operatorname{Var}(Y)}.
L0333: $$
L0334: 
L0335: These are Eqs. 11–15 in displacement form. The gain assumption requires that the relation calibrated during larger movements still applies during drift. There is also a small covariance between $\epsilon$ and $\eta$, because the recorded neuron's private spikes contribute both to its predictor and to actual eye motion. The paper neglects this contribution as order $1/N_m$ under its many neuron model.[^p2-chi-method]
L0336: 
L0337: Correlation divides the same covariance by a different denominator:
L0338: 
L0339: $$
L0340: R=\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)}{\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(X)\operatorname{Var}(Y)}},
L0341: \qquad
L0342: \chi\simeq R\sqrt{\frac{\operatorname{Var}(X)}{\operatorname{Var}(Y)}}.
L0343: $$
L0344: 
L0345: [Large private noise in a cell inflates $\operatorname{Var}(X)$ and makes $R$ small. It need not make the common contribution to the real movement small, because many independent private contributions average down there.]{.key-statement}
L0346: 
L0347: <div class="publisher-worked-calculation publisher-fold-always">
L0348: 
L0349: **Example: correlation 0.17 with a common fraction 0.82.**
L0350: 
L0351: These numbers are constructed to expose the distinction, not extracted from individual cells. Take independent centered variables with
L0352: 
L0353: $$
L0354: \operatorname{Var}(C)=0.00820\ \text{deg}^2,\quad
L0355: \operatorname{Var}(\eta)=0.00180\ \text{deg}^2,\quad
L0356: \operatorname{Var}(\epsilon)=0.22446\ \text{deg}^2.
L0357: $$
L0358: 
L0359: Then $\operatorname{Var}(Y)=0.01000$, $\operatorname{Var}(X)=0.23266$, and $\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)=0.00820$, all in deg$^2$. It follows that
L0360: 
L0361: $$
L0362: R=\frac{0.00820}{\sqrt{0.01000\times0.23266}}\simeq0.170,
L0363: \qquad \chi=\frac{0.00820}{0.01000}=0.820.
L0364: $$
L0365: 
L0366: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO -->
L0367: One cell explains $R^2\simeq2.89\%$ of displacement variance after linear rescaling, while the common signal supplies 82% of actual displacement variance. The larger, poorly predicted part of the single cell signal is private prediction noise.
L0368: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES -->
L0369: 
L0370: 
L0371: 
L0372: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0373: 
L0374: {{block:fig:EYE-SHARED-PRIVATE-VARIANCE}}
L0375: 
L0376: </div>
L0377: 
L0378: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0379: The bars use the same physical scale, so the equal common pieces have equal lengths. See [Shared and private variability, Eqs. 11–15, p. 10](../papers/ben-shushan-2022-fixational-drift.pdf#page=10).
L0380: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0381: 
L0382: </div>
L0383: 
L0384: In the experiment, each cell gives a noisy estimate $\chi_i$, and those estimates can lie outside $[0,1]$. The underlying variance fraction remains in that interval under the model, but the sample covariance need not. The paper estimates each standard error $\Delta\chi_i$ mainly from covariance uncertainty, treating the measured displacement variance in the denominator as comparatively well determined. It then uses inverse variance weighting:
L0385: 
L0386: $$
L0387: w_i=\frac{1}{(\Delta\chi_i)^2},\qquad
L0388: \widehat\chi=\frac{\sum_iw_i\chi_i}{\sum_iw_i},\qquad
L0389: \sigma_{\widehat\chi}=\left(\sum_iw_i\right)^{-1/2}.
L0390: $$
L0391: 
L0392: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0393: To see the weighting, let normalized weights $a_i$ sum to one. Independent errors give variance $\sum_i a_i^2(\Delta\chi_i)^2$ for $\sum_i a_i\chi_i$. Minimizing this subject to $\sum_i a_i=1$ gives $a_i\propto1/(\Delta\chi_i)^2$ and the error above. The paper additionally assumes approximately normal errors around a common value. The last expression is a propagated estimation error under those assumptions. It does not measure variation across animals or uncertainty in the gain and independence assumptions. [The reported result was $0.82\pm0.07$, a weighted mean and weighted error for horizontal displacement over 350 ms. It is neither a fraction of distance traveled nor the fraction that one neuron's spikes accurately predict.]{.key-statement}[^p2-chi-error]
L0394: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0395: 
L0396: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0397: [^p2-question]: Ben-Shushan, N., Shaham, N., Joshua, M., & Burak, Y. (2022). *Fixational drift is driven by diffusive dynamics in central neural circuitry*. Nature Communications, 13, 1697. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y). Main pp. 1–2, abstract and introduction.
L0398: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

[DOI](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y). Main pp. 5–8, “Stochastic diffusion in a memory circuit,” Figs. 4–5.

## FILE guide/04b-eye-timing.md | ch:EYE-TIMING
L0001: # Drift growth distinguishes diffusion from filtered white noise {#ch:EYE-TIMING}
L0002: 
L0003: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO -->
L0004: The shared movement estimate concerns a single interval length. [It leaves open whether the command merely fluctuates around a stable value or keeps accumulating changes. Comparing displacements over different time separations can distinguish these possibilities, but the smallest measured displacements may include a large contribution from recording error.]{.key-statement}
L0005: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES -->
L0006: 
L0007: The mean squared displacement measures the average squared position change over a chosen interval. Separating the recording error from that quantity is necessary before interpreting its growth. To test whether the command accumulates changes, we compare two possible inputs after a decaying muscle response: fluctuations that do not accumulate and a position that wanders by diffusion. Their different long interval behavior explains how a smooth eye movement can still reflect a wandering stored value.
L0008: 
L0009: ## Independent measurement error adds a constant offset to the MSD {#sec:EYE-MEASUREMENT-NOISE}
L0010: 
L0011: Using the [earlier definition of mean squared displacement](ch-foundations.html#sec-foundations-covariance),
L0012: 
L0013: $$
L0014: M(\ell)=\mathbb E\{[E(t+\ell)-E(t)]^2\}
L0015: $$
L0016: 
L0017: is the mean squared displacement, or MSD, at lag $\ell$. The data estimate it within each microsaccade free segment, then average the available segment estimates at each lag. Larger lags have fewer eligible segments. The 1 ms sampling sets the available lag spacing.[^p2-msd]
L0018: 
L0019: At short lags, the displacement can be smaller than the search coil's measurement error. Suppose its reading is $E_{\rm obs}(t)=E(t)+\nu(t)$. The measurement error is assumed to have:
L0020: 
L0021: - Zero mean and variance $\sigma_m^2$.
L0022: - Independence from true movement.
L0023: - No correlation between distinct sampled times.
L0024: 
L0025: For a positive lag,
L0026: 
L0027: $$
L0028: \begin{aligned}
L0029: M_{\rm obs}(\ell)
L0030: &=\mathbb E[(\delta E+\delta\nu)^2]\\
L0031: &=M(\ell)+\mathbb E[\nu(t+\ell)^2]+\mathbb E[\nu(t)^2]
L0032: -2\mathbb E[\nu(t+\ell)\nu(t)]\\
L0033: &=M(\ell)+2\sigma_m^2.
L0034: \end{aligned}
L0035: $$
L0036: 
L0037: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0038: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO -->
L0039: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO -->
L0040: The mixed movement-error term vanishes by independence. This is Eqs. 18–19. The correction is the variance of a difference of two errors, $2\sigma_m^2$, rather than the variance of a single position error. At exactly zero lag the same error is subtracted from itself, so the MSD is zero. A positive lag plateau near zero lag can still occur when successive measurement errors are independent.[^p2-msd-noise]
L0041: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES -->
L0042: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES -->
L0043: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0044: 
L0045: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0046: The authors estimated the plateau contribution at order $10^{-3}$ deg$^2$, checked its scale with a coil fixed in space, and subtracted that contribution from the observed MSD. [This is a correction of an average statistic, not knowledge of each trial's unknown noise.]{.key-statement} Smoothing the eye trajectories instead would also change the movement statistics the analysis seeks to measure. If the plateau estimate has uncertainty $10^{-4}$ deg$^2$, a corrected MSD of that size is unreliable even when the plateau itself is much larger. The peer review response uses this example to explain the shortest lag limitation.[^p2-noise-limit]
L0047: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0048: 
L0049: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0050: <summary>Original paper figure: Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), Extended Data Figure 2</summary>
L0051: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0052: 
L0053: {{block:fig:PAPER-EYE-ED2}}
L0054: 
L0055: </div>
L0056: </details>
L0057: 
L0058: <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerLeads = NO -->
L0059: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0060: For instance, a constructed measured MSD of $0.0014$ deg$^2$ with an estimated noise contribution $0.0010$ deg$^2$ leaves $0.0004$ deg$^2$. A $0.0001$ deg$^2$ uncertainty in the subtraction is already 25% of that result. This is why a corrected short lag point needs an uncertainty assessment as well as a positive value. If measurement errors were temporally correlated, the correction would instead be $2[\sigma_m^2-C_\nu(\ell)]$, where $C_\nu$ is their covariance at lag $\ell$. A constant subtraction would then need revision.
L0061: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0062: <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerLeads = YES -->
L0063: 
L0064: For a constructed Brownian movement with $D=0.010$ deg$^2$/s, adding independent position measurement errors with $2\sigma_m^2=0.0010$ deg$^2$ shifts every positive-lag MSD upward by the same amount.
L0065: 
L0066: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0067: 
L0068: {{block:fig:EYE-MSD-MEASUREMENT-OFFSET}}
L0069: 
L0070: </div>
L0071: 
L0072: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0073: The Brownian movement is chosen only to make the constant offset visible. See [Measurement noise correction, Eqs. 18–19, p. 10](../papers/ben-shushan-2022-fixational-drift.pdf#page=10).
L0074: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0075: 
L0076: ## The MSD plateaus for filtered white noise but grows for filtered diffusion {#sec:EYE-FILTERED-MSD}
L0077: 
L0078: The corrected MSD keeps increasing over the observed range, toward a second, while its logarithmic slope decreases and remains between one and two. Define that local slope by
L0079: 
L0080: $$
L0081: \alpha(\ell)=\frac{d\log M(\ell)}{d\log\ell}
L0082: =\frac{\ell M'(\ell)}{M(\ell)}.
L0083: $$
L0084: 
L0085: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO -->
L0086: Ordinary diffusion has $M=2D\ell$ and $\alpha=1$. A roughly constant velocity over a short interval gives displacement $v\ell$ and $M=\mathbb E[v^2]\ell^2$, so $\alpha=2$. [Growth faster than linear is called [**superdiffusion**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}. Here it describes a finite range of lags. It does not assert a single power law for arbitrarily long times.]{.definition-explanation}[^p2-msd-result]
L0087: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES -->
L0088: 
L0089: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0090: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO -->
L0091: Could temporally uncorrelated motor noise acquire the observed MSD just by passing through muscles? [A [**low pass filter**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} suppresses rapid input changes relative to slow ones.]{.definition-explanation} The input is idealized as [white noise](ch-memory-readout.html#sec-memory-extensions), so fluctuations at unequal times are uncorrelated. [A stable, decaying response to this noise produces a [**stationary process**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} in the sense needed here: its mean is constant and its covariance depends only on time separation.]{.definition-explanation} Its position variance consequently does not grow with absolute time. Let $E=h*\xi$, where
L0092: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES -->
L0093: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0094: 
L0095: $$
L0096: \mathbb E[\xi(t)\xi(t')]=A\delta(t-t').
L0097: $$
L0098: 
L0099: The noise intensity $A$ has units input$^2$ times seconds. Expanding the convolution and using the delta function yields, for $\ell\ge0$,
L0100: 
L0101: $$
L0102: C_E(\ell)=\mathbb E[E(t)E(t+\ell)]
L0103: =A\int_0^\infty h(u)h(u+\ell)\,du.
L0104: $$
L0105: 
L0106: For a stationary centered position, expanding the squared difference then gives
L0107: 
L0108: $$
L0109: M(\ell)=2[C_E(0)-C_E(\ell)].
L0110: $$
L0111: 
L0112: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0113: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = NO -->
L0114: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0115: If the kernel decays sufficiently rapidly, its overlap with a shifted copy tends to zero. The MSD consequently approaches $2C_E(0)$, a finite plateau. This reconstructs the reasoning of Eqs. 20–22.[^p2-white-noise]
L0116: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0117: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = YES -->
L0118: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0119: 
L0120: For the one exponential filter $h(u)=\Theta(u)e^{-u/\tau}/\tau$,
L0121: 
L0122: $$
L0123: C_E(\ell)=\frac{A}{2\tau}e^{-\ell/\tau},\qquad
L0124: M_{\rm white}(\ell)=\frac A\tau(1-e^{-\ell/\tau}).
L0125: $$
L0126: 
L0127: This simple filter has a short lag MSD proportional to $\ell$ and a long lag plateau. A double exponential filter is smoother at short lags, but still gives a plateau because its kernel also decays. Increasing $A$ raises the curve without removing its flattening. The paper's simulated private OMN noise, passed through muscle dynamics, is both too small and too flat at long lags. In Fig. 3b, using 2,000 heterogeneous OMNs, its contribution is about 1% of the observed variance near 900 ms.[^p2-msd-result]
L0128: 
L0129: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0130: <summary>Original paper figure: Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), Figure 3</summary>
L0131: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0132: 
L0133: {{block:fig:PAPER-EYE-3}}
L0134: 
L0135: </div>
L0136: </details>
L0137: 
L0138: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0139: Filtered white noise cannot explain continued long-lag growth. Suppose instead the input is a stored position $B(t)$ undergoing Brownian motion with $\mathbb E[(B(t+\ell)-B(t))^2]=2D\ell$. Its position variance accumulates. A low pass filter with nonzero static gain smooths the path while retaining that accumulating long time displacement variance. The following one exponential example is a teaching reconstruction, not the paper's full muscle model. It derives how smooth movement can have a slope between one and two even though the stored position undergoes ordinary diffusion.
L0140: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->
L0141: 
L0142: Let
L0143: 
L0144: $$
L0145: \tau\dot E+E=B,\qquad dB=\sqrt{2D}\,dW.
L0146: $$
L0147: 
L0148: Here $B$ is the changing position command and $E$ is the eye angle that follows it, both in degrees. The mismatch $B-E$ drives velocity: a command to the right of the current position makes the eye move right. For the same mismatch, a larger response time $\tau$ gives a smaller velocity, so the eye takes longer to catch up and smooths rapid command changes more strongly. This response time belongs to the simplified eye filter. It is not automatically the synaptic decay time used in the memory model.
L0149: 
L0150: $W$ is a Brownian process with unit increment variance per unit time, so $D$ has units deg$^2$/s. Define velocity $v=\dot E=(B-E)/\tau$. Taking its increment gives
L0151: 
L0152: $$
L0153: \tau\,dv=-v\,dt+\sqrt{2D}\,dW.
L0154: $$
L0155: 
L0156: Velocity loses its past value over time $\tau$ and receives new random input. To add the surviving effect of those inputs, divide the past into short intervals of width $h$. The Brownian change $\Delta W_j=W(u_j+h)-W(u_j)$ has mean zero and variance $h$. Changes in disjoint intervals are independent. Its contribution to the velocity at time $t$ is approximately $(\sqrt{2D}/\tau)e^{-(t-u_j)/\tau}\Delta W_j$: the initial change has decayed between $u_j$ and $t$.
L0157: 
L0158: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = NO -->
L0159: The integral against $dW(u)$ denotes the limit of these weighted sums as $h$ tends to zero. It does not require an ordinary derivative of the Brownian path. After the initial velocity has decayed, extending the input history into the distant past gives
L0160: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = YES -->
L0161: 
L0162: $$
L0163: v(t)=\frac{\sqrt{2D}}{\tau}\int_{-\infty}^{t}e^{-(t-u)/\tau}\,dW(u).
L0164: $$
L0165: 
L0166: For a lag $s\geq0$, multiply the sums for $v(t)$ and $v(t+s)$ and average. Products of distinct increments have zero mean. The same increment contributes its variance $h$, so the sum becomes an ordinary overlap integral over their shared past:
L0167: 
L0168: $$
L0169: \begin{aligned}
L0170: C_v(s)
L0171: &=\frac{2D}{\tau^2}\int_{-\infty}^{t}
L0172: e^{-(t-u)/\tau}e^{-(t+s-u)/\tau}\,du\\
L0173: &=\frac{2D}{\tau^2}e^{-s/\tau}\int_0^\infty e^{-2r/\tau}\,dr
L0174: =\frac D\tau e^{-s/\tau}.
L0175: \end{aligned}
L0176: $$
L0177: 
L0178: Here $r=t-u$ is the age of an input increment. Covariance is even in the lag, so $C_v(s)=(D/\tau)e^{-|s|/\tau}$ for either sign of $s$. Integrating velocity over an interval gives position change. The double integral over its covariance reduces to
L0179: 
L0180: $$
L0181: \begin{aligned}
L0182: M_{\rm Brownian}(\ell)
L0183: &=\int_0^\ell\int_0^\ell C_v(u-v)\,du\,dv\\
L0184: &=2\int_0^\ell(\ell-s)C_v(s)\,ds\\
L0185: &=2D[\ell-\tau(1-e^{-\ell/\tau})].
L0186: \end{aligned}
L0187: $$
L0188: 
L0189: At short lags, $1-e^{-\ell/\tau}=\ell/\tau-\ell^2/(2\tau^2)+\cdots$, so
L0190: 
L0191: $$
L0192: M_{\rm Brownian}(\ell)\simeq(D/\tau)\ell^2.
L0193: $$
L0194: 
L0195: The eye keeps approximately the same velocity over that interval. At long lags,
L0196: 
L0197: $$
L0198: M_{\rm Brownian}(\ell)\simeq2D(\ell-\tau),
L0199: $$
L0200: 
L0201: which grows linearly rather than approaching a plateau.
L0202: 
L0203: Holding the internal diffusion coefficient $D$ fixed, increasing $\tau$ reduces short-lag displacement and moves the transition to linear growth to longer lags. It does not stop accumulating error: the long-lag MSD growth rate still approaches $2D$. Increasing $D$ instead raises the entire MSD curve in proportion, at fixed $\tau$. The local logarithmic slope of this MSD is
L0204: 
L0205: $$
L0206: \alpha(\ell)=\frac{\ell(1-e^{-\ell/\tau})}
L0207: {\ell-\tau(1-e^{-\ell/\tau})},
L0208: $$
L0209: 
L0210: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0211: approaching two at short lags and one at long lags. At $\ell=\tau$ it is $e-1\simeq1.718$. At $\ell=5\tau$ it is about $1.239$. [Thus an intermediate slope can arise from filtering ordinary diffusion, without faster than Brownian accumulation inside the memory circuit.]{.key-statement}
L0212: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0213: 
L0214: The curves compare the two inputs after the same filter with one exponential response, also called a one pole filter.
L0215: 
L0216: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0217: 
L0218: {{block:fig:EYE-FILTERED-NOISE-MSD}}
L0219: 
L0220: </div>
L0221: 
L0222: The white-input short-lag slope of one belongs to this one-pole example.
L0223: 
L0224: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0225: The actual muscle response contains fast and slow exponential components, and the full model also contains motor neuron filtering and feedback. Those ingredients change the detailed curve. The one exponential derivation teaches the distinction relevant to Fig. 3: [a decaying low pass filter of stationary white noise saturates, whereas the same filter, with nonzero static gain, retains accumulating displacement from a wandering position. A slow correlated peripheral source is a different hypothesis from the temporally uncorrelated noise rejected by this comparison.]{.key-statement}
L0226: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->
L0227: 
L0228: [^p2-msd]: Ben-Shushan, N., Shaham, N., Joshua, M., & Burak, Y. (2022). *Fixational drift is driven by diffusive dynamics in central neural circuitry*. Nature Communications, 13, 1697. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y). Main p. 10, “Calculation of empirical MSD.”
L0229: 
L0230: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0231: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO -->
L0232: [^p2-msd-noise]: Ben-Shushan, N., Shaham, N., Joshua, M., & Burak, Y. (2022). *Fixational drift is driven by diffusive dynamics in central neural circuitry*. Nature Communications, 13, 1697. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y). Main p. 10, “Removal of measurement noise,” Eqs. 18–19; Supplementary Information, p. 8, Extended Data Fig. 2. The plotted plateau is the displacement noise contribution, twice the single position noise variance.
L0233: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES -->
L0234: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0235: 
L0236: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0237: [^p2-noise-limit]: Ben-Shushan, N., Shaham, N., Joshua, M., & Burak, Y. (2022). *Fixational drift is driven by diffusive dynamics in central neural circuitry*. Nature Communications, 13, 1697, peer review file, pp. 20–21, measurement noise subtraction, uncertainty example, and short lag restriction. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y).
L0238: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0239: 
L0240: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0241: <!-- vale ai-tells.NounString = NO -->
L0242: [^p2-msd-result]: Ben-Shushan, N., Shaham, N., Joshua, M., & Burak, Y. (2022). *Fixational drift is driven by diffusive dynamics in central neural circuitry*. Nature Communications, 13, 1697. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y). Main pp. 5–6, “Central source” timing argument and Fig. 3.
L0243: <!-- vale ai-tells.NounString = YES -->
L0244: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0245: 
L0246: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0247: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0248: [^p2-white-noise]: Ben-Shushan, N., Shaham, N., Joshua, M., & Burak, Y. (2022). *Fixational drift is driven by diffusive dynamics in central neural circuitry*. Nature Communications, 13, 1697. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y). Main p. 10, “MSD curve of low-pass filtered white noise,” Eqs. 20–22. The normalized one exponential example and filtered Brownian derivation are teaching reconstructions.
L0249: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

## FILE guide/04c-eye-circuit.md | ch:EYE-CIRCUIT
L0001: # A noisy memory circuit can produce eye drift {#ch:EYE-CIRCUIT}
L0002: 
L0003: <!-- vale ai-tells.NegatedSubject = NO -->
L0004: The recordings support a shared source before the motor neurons, and the movement's timing is compatible with a stored position that wanders. [Neither result identifies the circuit that supplies that command. A noisy memory circuit is a proposed explanation whose output must pass through the motor neurons and muscles before it can be compared with eye movement.]{.key-statement}
L0005: <!-- vale ai-tells.NegatedSubject = YES -->
L0006: 
L0007: Following that complete path lets the model test whether neural noise can produce both the amount and timing of the observed drift. Its spike variability, neuron count and time constants determine how much freedom this match allows. Comparing the model with the separate recording and timing results then shows what the study supports, and why it still leaves other upstream sources possible.
L0008: 
L0009: ## A noisy integrator can reproduce drift after muscle filtering {#sec:EYE-CIRCUIT-MODEL}
L0010: 
L0011: The recording analysis places much of the drive upstream of OMNs. To test whether integrator noise could supply it, the authors build a model from a noisy memory circuit through to the eye. The model adapts an earlier goldfish integrator architecture to primate tuning and variability. The model has two groups of integrator neurons. One group fires more when the eye points farther right. The other fires more when it points farther left. Neurons excite cells in their own group and inhibit cells in the other group. The connection strengths are tuned so that the circuit can approximately maintain a range of represented eye positions.[^p2-network]
L0012: 
L0013: For the right population, the model's rate is
L0014: 
L0015: $$
L0016: \lambda_i^R(t)=\left[\zeta_i^R E_{OI}(t)+\lambda_i^0+\zeta_i^R F(t)\right]_+,
L0017: \qquad [a]_+=\max(a,0).
L0018: $$
L0019: 
L0020: $E_{OI}$ is the internally represented position in degrees, $\zeta_i^R$ is a tuning slope in spikes/(s deg), and $\lambda_i^0$ is a rate offset in spikes/s. Taking the maximum with zero prevents negative firing. $F$ is a feedback input in degrees, defined below. The matching left neuron has the opposite slope, $\zeta_i^L=-\zeta_i^R$. In an active neuron, a change of one degree changes its rate by $\zeta_i$.
L0021: 
L0022: The model scales each spike train into an input $X_i$ with a saturating mean response. This input changes the neuron's synaptic output $S_i$, the lingering effect of its spikes on neurons receiving them. A weighted difference between the two groups' synaptic outputs gives the represented eye angle $E_{OI}$. This value sets the firing rates again, closing the feedback loop:
L0023: 
L0024: $$
L0025: \tau_s\dot S_i^R=-S_i^R+X_i^R,\qquad
L0026: X_i^R(t)=\frac{\xi_i^R(t)}{\lambda_t+\lambda_i^R(t)},\qquad
L0027: E_{OI}(t)=\sum_i\eta_i[S_i^R(t)-S_i^L(t)].
L0028: $$
L0029: 
L0030: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO -->
L0031: Here $\xi_i^R$ is a spike train generated at rate $\lambda_i^R$, $\tau_s$ is the synaptic decay time, and $\lambda_t=60$ Hz sets the rate scale in the nonlinear synaptic response. Treating spikes as counts, $X_i$ and $S_i$ are dimensionless, and the weights $\eta_i$ are in degrees. Between spikes, $S_i$ decays exponentially. One spike adds a jump of $1/[\tau_s(\lambda_t+\lambda_i^R)]$. Replacing spikes by their mean rate gives a drive $\lambda_i^R/(\lambda_t+\lambda_i^R)$ that approaches one as the rate increases. This is the model's saturating synaptic effect, rather than the purely linear synapses of the basic 2012 example.[^p2-network]
L0032: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES -->
L0033: 
L0034: Persistence requires a further condition on that feedback. Set $F=0$ and replace spikes by their mean rates. Define the mean saturated drives at a proposed stored value $z$ by
L0035: 
L0036: $$
L0037: q_i^R(z)=\frac{\lambda_i^R(z)}{\lambda_t+\lambda_i^R(z)},
L0038: \qquad
L0039: q_i^L(z)=\frac{\lambda_i^L(z)}{\lambda_t+\lambda_i^L(z)}.
L0040: $$
L0041: 
L0042: Multiply each synaptic equation by its readout weight, subtract the left contribution, and sum. Since the same $\tau_s$ applies to every unit, the readout obeys
L0043: 
L0044: $$
L0045: \tau_s\dot E_{OI}=-E_{OI}+G(E_{OI}),
L0046: \qquad
L0047: G(z)=\sum_i\eta_i[q_i^R(z)-q_i^L(z)].
L0048: $$
L0049: 
L0050: [An exact continuum of persistent readout values would require $G(z)=z$ over the represented range.]{.key-statement} For a small displacement from a fixed value, Taylor expansion gives
L0051: 
L0052: $$
L0053: \tau_s\,\frac{d\,\delta E_{OI}}{dt}
L0054: =[G'(z)-1]\delta E_{OI}.
L0055: $$
L0056: 
L0057: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0058: Thus $G'(z)=1$ is the local neutral condition for the stored coordinate. Stable directions of the remaining synaptic state are also needed for an attractor. The weights were optimized to approximate the fixed-point relation across $-50$ to $50$ degrees. [Residual mismatch can produce deterministic drift, so this finite optimized circuit provides approximate persistence rather than an exactly neutral continuum.]{.key-statement} The tuning slopes and thresholds were sampled to match reported macaque relationships and variation. With spikes, perturbations along the nearly neutral direction also accumulate. This reduction is a teaching derivation from the paper's equations. Figure 4 separates the resulting internal diffusion from later muscle smoothing.[^p2-network]
L0059: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->
L0060: 
L0061: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0062: <summary>Original paper figure: Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), Figure 4</summary>
L0063: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0064: 
L0065: {{block:fig:PAPER-EYE-4}}
L0066: 
L0067: </div>
L0068: </details>
L0069: 
L0070: The motor neurons receive this position signal:
L0071: 
L0072: $$
L0073: \lambda_i^M(t)=[k_i(E_{OI}(t)-E_{T_i})]_+,
L0074: \qquad
L0075: \tau_{MN}\dot s_i^M=-s_i^M+\xi_i^M.
L0076: $$
L0077: 
L0078: $\lambda_i^M$ is a motor rate in spikes/s, $E_{T_i}$ is that neuron's position threshold in degrees, and $k_i$ is its position sensitivity. $\xi_i^M$ is its generated spike train. The filtered output $s_i^M$ also has units spikes/s, with decay time $\tau_{MN}$. One spike makes a jump of $1/\tau_{MN}$. At constant mean rate, the mean output is that rate.
L0079: 
L0080: Each motor unit's muscle and plant response is represented by a position contribution $E_i$ satisfying
L0081: 
L0082: $$
L0083: s_i^M=k_i(E_i-E_{T_i})+r_i\dot E_i+m_i\ddot E_i,
L0084: \qquad E=\frac1{N_m}\sum_{i=1}^{N_m}E_i.
L0085: $$
L0086: 
L0087: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0088: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0089: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0090: These are Eqs. 28–31. The second order equation uses the same roles and units as the forward fit. At constant $E_{OI}$, an active unit has mean $s_i^M=k_i(E_{OI}-E_{T_i})$, giving mean $E_i=E_{OI}$ when derivatives vanish. Averaging therefore transmits the common position while reducing independent private fluctuations. This is an effective model of combined motor output, not a claim that one motor unit moves an isolated eye of its own.[^p2-motor]
L0091: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0092: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0093: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0094: 
L0095: The model used diverse thresholds and response coefficients, with motor thresholds sampled between $-45$ and $-5$ degrees, and imposed $m_i<r_i^2/(4k_i)$ to give nonoscillatory individual response kernels. Main Methods report $N=30{,}000$ integrator neurons, $N_m=1{,}000$ OMNs, and $\tau_s=20$ ms for the main full simulation. The separate private OMN comparison in Fig. 3 used 2,000 OMNs. These neuron counts belong to different simulation comparisons.[^p2-motor]
L0096: 
L0097: As the eye moves while viewing a stationary target, the target's image moves across the retina. The authors assume that visual feedback responds to this recent image motion rather than to an absolute target-position error. Their delayed feedback model is:
L0098: 
L0099: $$
L0100: F(t)=A[E(t-\tau_d)-E_{OI}(t)],
L0101: \qquad \tau_d\simeq70\ \text{ms}.
L0102: $$
L0103: 
L0104: Both positions are in degrees, so $A$ is dimensionless in this formulation. The main value is $A=0.015$. If gaze and its internal representation have recently moved rightward, the delayed position is to their left, making $F$ negative. Its contribution to the integrator rates then opposes continued rightward movement. For a slowly varying position that the eye tracks, $E(t-\tau_d)-E_{OI}(t)\simeq-\tau_d\dot E(t)$, showing the velocity-like action and sign. The approximation requires tracking and slow change over the delay.[^p2-feedback]
L0105: 
L0106: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0107: The effect on the ongoing MSD must be checked from the coupled model, rather than inferred from the delay alone. A short MSD lag is a separation between two samples in a running process, not the time since a new perturbation. Past measurements can already affect both samples, and $F$ also contains the immediate term $-A E_{OI}(t)$. Extended Data Fig. 3 compares the model with and without feedback: feedback changes amplitude across lags, while its larger effect on logarithmic slope occurs at longer lags. Figure 5 shows the full model. [Muscle filtering supplies the short-lag smoothness, and feedback improves the detailed longer-lag fit. These are results of this model comparison, not a hard delay-based cutoff or a claim excluding visual control under other tasks.]{.key-statement}[^p2-feedback]
L0108: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0109:

L0127: 
L0128: ## Spike regularity and circuit parameters set the model's drift scale {#sec:EYE-SCALING}
L0129: 
L0130: An agreement in amplitude is informative only if we know how much freedom the model has. The paper uses regular spike trains. The [spike variability section](ch-foundations.html#sec-foundations-poisson) defined the interspike interval coefficient of variation (CV) as the interval standard deviation divided by its mean. A Poisson process at constant rate has CV one. The model uses about 0.22 for integrator neurons and about 0.07 for OMNs. [Such firing is called [**sub-Poisson spiking**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} here because its long window count variability is lower than a Poisson process at the same rate, under the interval construction used in the model.]{.definition-explanation}[^p2-cv]
L0131: 
L0132: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0133: The construction starts with a Poisson process at $M$ times the desired rate and retains every $M$th spike. At constant rate, a retained interval is the sum of $M$ independent exponential intervals. Each initial interval has mean $1/(M\lambda)$ and variance $1/(M\lambda)^2$. The retained interval therefore has mean $1/\lambda$, variance $1/(M\lambda^2)$, and CV $1/\sqrt M$. This preserves the mean rate while reducing variability. Its use at changing rates is part of the model. The simple interval derivation assumes a constant rate.
L0134: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0135: 
L0136: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0137: At constant rate, the retained intervals are independent. [This is a [**renewal process**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a spike process whose successive intervals are independent and identically distributed.]{.definition-explanation} Let their mean be $\mu=1/\lambda$ and standard deviation $\sigma_I$. The time to $n$ spikes has variance $n\sigma_I^2$. For a long fixed window $L\simeq n\mu$, a fluctuation in total interval time produces the opposite fluctuation in count divided by $\mu$. Thus
L0138: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0139: 
L0140: $$
L0141: \operatorname{Var}(n)\simeq\frac{n\sigma_I^2}{\mu^2}
L0142: \simeq\lambda L\left(\frac{\sigma_I}{\mu}\right)^2
L0143: =\lambda L\,\mathrm{CV}^2.
L0144: $$
L0145: 
L0146: This explains why the diffusion scale contains CV squared: it is the strength of the accumulated spike fluctuations relative to the Poisson case. The [diffusion calculation](ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-diffusion) and [information bound](ch-memory-information.html#sec-memory-bound) show how projecting those fluctuations onto a neutral memory direction adds a factor proportional to $1/\tau_s^2$ and, for a comparable independent population, $1/N$.
L0147: 
L0148: Rate and sensitivity need more care. Write the absolute local rate slope as $a=d\lambda/dE$, in spikes/(s deg). For a scale estimate, first use the simpler Poisson model with linear synapses. If $N$ similar cells fire near rate $\lambda_0$, their Poisson information per unit time about position is
L0149: 
L0150: $$
L0151: J\simeq\frac{Na^2}{\lambda_0}.
L0152: $$
L0153: 
L0154: Using the [information-diffusion relation](ch-memory-information.html#sec-memory-bound) as a scale, including the $\mathrm{CV}^2$ factor just derived and holding the network geometry fixed,
L0155: 
L0156: $$
L0157: D\ \propto\ \frac{\mathrm{CV}^2}{\tau_s^2J}
L0158: =\frac{\mathrm{CV}^2\lambda_0}{N a^2\tau_s^2}.
L0159: $$
L0160: 
L0161: If instead sensitivity is expressed as the fractional slope $b=a/\lambda_0$, in inverse degrees, the same expression becomes
L0162: 
L0163: $$
L0164: D\ \propto\ \frac{\mathrm{CV}^2}{N\lambda_0 b^2\tau_s^2}.
L0165: $$
L0166: 
L0167: This second form has the dependence printed in the paper's Eq. 1,
L0168: $D\propto\mathrm{CV}^2N^{-1}\lambda_0^{-1}k_0^{-2}\tau_s^{-2}$.
L0169: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0170: However, its surrounding text calls $k_0$ position sensitivity without specifying fractional normalization. If it means the absolute slope in Hz/degree, the Poisson estimate above instead puts $\lambda_0$ in the numerator at fixed slope. The printed inverse-rate dependence matches a fractional slope. The printed expression is a proportionality, so an omitted factor can carry units; units alone do not establish a printing error. This calculation is a scale estimate under fixed network geometry. The full 2022 model has saturating synapses and sub-Poisson spike trains, so inserting CV squared into the simpler Poisson relation does not give an exact formula for that network.[^p2-scaling]
L0171: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0172: 
L0173: Within this scale estimate, at fixed network geometry and under the noise assumptions above:
L0174: 
L0175: - Doubling $N$ halves it.
L0176: - Doubling CV multiplies it by four.
L0177: - Doubling $\tau_s$ divides it by four.
L0178: - Doubling the absolute slope at fixed rate also divides it by four.
L0179: 
L0180: [Increasing rate reduces diffusion when the fractional slope is fixed, because more spikes then also supply a proportionally steeper absolute slope. Increasing rate at fixed absolute slope is a different comparison and increases the scale in the first formula. A rate change alone does not specify which comparison is intended.]{.key-statement}
L0181: 
L0182: Could the measured CV itself mostly reflect drift rather than private spike variability? Supplementary Notes test this. Let $\lambda(t)=\bar\lambda+aB(t)$ during a fixation of length $T$, with the rate changes small relative to $\bar\lambda$. For a Brownian path starting at zero, the expected time averaged squared position is $DT$, and the expected squared time mean is $2DT/3$. Subtracting gives the expected within-segment position variance $DT/3=2DT/6$:
L0183: 
L0184: $$
L0185: \mathbb E\!\left[\frac1T\int_0^T(B(t)-\bar B)^2dt\right]
L0186: =\frac{DT}{3}.
L0187: $$
L0188: 
L0189: The second term follows from $\mathbb E[B(t)B(s)]=2D\min(t,s)$ and
L0190: $T^{-2}\int_0^T\int_0^T2D\min(t,s)\,dt\,ds=2DT/3$.
L0191: Expanding $1/(\bar\lambda+aB)$ to first order gives $1/\bar\lambda-aB/\bar\lambda^2$. Dividing its standard deviation by its mean then gives
L0192: 
L0193: $$
L0194: \mathrm{CV}_{\rm drift}\simeq
L0195: \frac{|a|}{\bar\lambda}\sqrt{\frac{2DT}{6}}.
L0196: $$
L0197: 
L0198: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0199: With the supplement's values $a=1/15$ spikes/(s arcmin), $2D=100$ arcmin$^2$/s, $T=0.5$ s, and $\bar\lambda=80$ Hz, this is about $0.0024$, well below the OMN CV near 0.07. These are an order of magnitude check, not a fitted diffusion constant for the whole model. Since one degree is 60 arcmin, that $2D$ equals $0.0278$ deg$^2$/s, or $D=0.0139$ deg$^2$/s.[^p2-cv-drift]
L0200: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0201: 
L0202: ## The figures test shared drive, diffusion, and the full circuit model {#sec:EYE-FIGURES}
L0203: 
L0204: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0205: The five figures address different steps. Their agreement is easier to assess when those questions remain separate.
L0206: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0207: 
L0208: <div class="table-responsive">
L0209: 
L0210: {{block:tbl:EYE-FIGURE-GUIDE}}
L0211: 
L0212: </div>
L0213: 
L0214: The most useful supplemental comparisons are Extended Data Fig. 1 for individual uncertainty, Fig. 2 for noise subtraction and its simulated control, Fig. 3 for feedback, Fig. 4 for a no-central-drive control, and Fig. 5 for the other monkey's fit. In the no-central-drive control, $\widehat\chi=-0.09\pm0.22$ is compatible with zero. That negative estimate is sampling variation. Figures 6–8 document cell classification, segment sampling, and a rank correlation check. [The [**Spearman correlation**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} used in Figure 8 replaces each set of measured values by their ranks before computing correlation.]{.definition-explanation} It checks whether larger predicted displacements tend to accompany larger recorded displacements, without requiring the relationship to be linear. Figure 9 shows the heterogeneous model parameters. Figure 10 separates spiking noise passed directly to the output from spiking noise that drives diffusion inside the integrator: internal stochastic spikes remain in the latter comparison. Its caption reports little direct output-noise effect beyond 50 ms.[^p2-supp-figures]

L0270: ## The evidence places shared drive upstream without identifying one circuit {#sec:EYE-LIMITS}
L0271: 
L0272: The data and model answer different questions about where the drive originates:
L0273: 
L0274: - The common variance estimate supports a drive upstream of OMNs, given the calibration and noise assumptions.
L0275: - The MSD comparison supports a command that accumulates variation over the measured interval, rather than stationary white noise passed through a decaying muscle filter.
L0276: - The circuit model shows that integrator noise with plausible parameters can generate such a command and explain its filtered amplitude and timing.
L0277: 
L0278: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0279: Those results do not uniquely locate the noise inside integrator neurons. Two missing tests limit that conclusion:
L0280: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0281: 
L0282:   <!-- vale ai-tells.NegatedSubject = NO -->
L0283: - No integrator neurons were recorded in this study.
L0284:   <!-- vale ai-tells.NegatedSubject = YES -->
L0285: - No selective change of that circuit tested whether it was necessary for drift.
L0286: 
L0287: [Noisy inputs to the integrator, or an already diffusive upstream signal, remain possible. The authors make this distinction in the Discussion and explicitly in their peer review response. Several combinations of neuron count, variability, and synaptic time constant can also fit an amplitude, so matching one curve does not uniquely recover those parameters.]{.key-statement}[^p2-localization]
L0288: 
L0289: <!-- vale ai-tells.ServesAsDodge = NO -->
L0290: The study's empirical scope is horizontal motion during these fixation segments in two animals. Visual tasks, feedback, and other sources of input can alter the statistics. Its contribution to the connection with the 2012 theory is more precise: a continuous memory can diffuse internally, while a mechanical output of that memory looks smooth and superdiffusive at shorter lags. To compare a memory theory with behavior, the mapping from stored value to measured movement is part of the calculation.
L0291: <!-- vale ai-tells.ServesAsDodge = YES -->

chapter_id: ch:EYE-CIRCUIT
blocks:
- id: tbl:EYE-FIGURE-GUIDE
  kind: table
  placement: sec:EYE-FIGURES
  headers:
  - Figure
  - What to read
  - What it establishes or checks
  rows:
  - - 1, main p. 3
    - Start with the large movement, then the expanded fixation. Compare the small
      predicted rate change with the larger observed fluctuation in b. Read c as one
      cell and d as the population.
    - A weak, positive rate relationship exists during fixation. The large movement
      is used for calibration.
  - - 2, main p. 4
    - The spike response kernel in a has a delay and two decay times; b shows their
      spread. In c, a noisy cell prediction can coexist with a smooth eye trace. Panel
      d plots $R$, while e–f plot $\chi$ and estimation error.
    - Shared movement and a model-based fraction of common variance. The vertical
      axis in f is error in $\chi$, not eye position or another variance fraction.
      Large error cells receive little weight.
  - - 3, main p. 6
    - In a, distinguish the raw MSD, plateau estimate, and corrected curve. In b,
      compare both height and slope. Translating the private-noise curve upward does
      not remove its flattening.
    - Independent filtered motor noise fails both the amplitude and time dependence
      comparison. The noise subtraction is part of this evidence.
  - - 4, main p. 7
    - The valley in a sketches stable directions across the attractor and a neutral
      direction along it. It is not a measured energy function of this brain circuit.
      Panels c–d show simulated internal drift and its nearly linear MSD.
    - The mechanism by which neural noise can accumulate into an ordinary diffusive
      position signal. This is the integrator output before the full observation process.
  - - 5, main p. 8
    - Trace integrator, OMNs, plant, and feedback in a. Compare internal and actual
      position in c, then model and data MSD in d. Panels e–f rerun the statistical
      analysis on simulated recordings.
    - The full model can match filtered motion, and known simulated sources can produce
      noisy individual estimates with a useful population estimate. This checks the
      method under that model, rather than proving a unique biological source.
  caption: Reading the figures as an argument.
