## FILE guide/05-place-fields.md | ch:PLACE-FIELDS
L0001: # Field counts and gaps reveal threshold and scale {#ch:PLACE-FIELDS}
L0002: 
L0003: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0004: The study compares recordings of hippocampal place cells from bats flying through tunnels, rats moving over an arena floor, and bats flying through a room. For tunnel flights, the analysis uses distance along an approximately one-dimensional route, although the tunnel itself has width and height. The floor map uses two position coordinates, and the room map uses three.
L0005: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0006: 
L0007: A cell recorded on a short track may have one smooth firing hill. [On a long track, the same kind of cell may have several separated fields, each with a different width and height. In a room, its active regions can bend, split, and surround inactive holes. How could one model produce this variety and still make useful predictions?]{.key-statement}
L0008: 
L0009: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = NO -->
L0010: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO -->
L0011: Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak propose that a cell's spatial input is a smooth random function. This function can be interpreted as the average combined input arriving through the cell's synapses at each animal position. The model cell has positive firing rate where this input exceeds a threshold. The recordings estimate firing rates rather than measuring this input directly.[^p3-model] [The model predicts distributions of field lengths, areas, and volumes, rather than the location of a particular cell's next field. Its strongest tests check whether fitting field sizes and counts also explains field shapes and holes.]{.key-statement}[^p3-question]
L0012: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES -->
L0013: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = YES -->
L0014: 
L0015: We begin on a track, where a field starts at [an [**upcrossing**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a point where the input crosses upward through the threshold.]{.definition-explanation} Counting these crossings gives the average number of fields. Combining that count with the lengths of active fields and inactive gaps then separates the effect of the threshold from the distance over which the map changes. These calculations establish the parameters needed for the later predictions of sizes and shapes.
L0016: 
L0017: [Spatial randomness here describes different possible rate maps. Once a map is drawn, it is fixed while the animal moves through it. The spatial coordinate is not time, and the model does not by itself describe memory drift, spike timing, or learning.]{.key-statement}
L0018: 
L0019: ## Thresholding a smooth Gaussian input produces place fields {#sec:PLACE-GP}
L0020: 
L0021: To predict a field's width, we need to relate activity at nearby positions. A histogram of firing rates alone loses that information: rearranging its values could turn one broad field into many tiny fields without changing the histogram.
L0022: 
L0023: The model assigns an input $h(x)$ to each position $x$, with nearby values linked so that a cell can have a continuous active region. [A [**Gaussian process**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} specifies a distribution of maps such that the values at any finite set of positions form a jointly Gaussian random vector.]{.definition-explanation} [A [**jointly Gaussian vector**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is one for which every linear combination of its entries is Gaussian.]{.definition-explanation} This joint rule constrains how inputs at different positions can vary together, beyond requiring a Gaussian histogram at each position.
L0024: 
L0025: The mean sets the average input, and the spatial covariance describes how inputs at two positions vary together across possible maps. These functions determine the joint Gaussian laws. The chosen covariance must also make the maps smooth enough for the derivatives used below. After a map is drawn, moving the animal changes which position is evaluated, not the map itself.
L0026: 
L0027: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Mathematical definitions: Gaussian processes and spatial covariance"}
L0028: 
L0029: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0030: For chosen positions $x_1,\ldots,x_n$, the input vector is $(h(x_1),\ldots,h(x_n))$. Its mean vector and covariance matrix are obtained by evaluating two functions,
L0031: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0032: 
L0033: $$
L0034: m(x)=\mathbb E[h(x)],\qquad
L0035: C(x,y)=\mathbb E[(h(x)-m(x))(h(y)-m(y))].
L0036: $$
L0037: 
L0038: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0039: The expectation runs over possible maps, or over cells assumed to share these statistics. For jointly Gaussian vectors, zero covariance between two subvectors implies their independence. That extra property, which does not hold for general distributions, will simplify the crossing calculation. The values after thresholding will generally not have a Gaussian distribution.[^p3-model]
L0040: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0041: 
L0042: :::
L0043: 
L0044: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0045: Three assumptions have different jobs:
L0046: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0047: 
L0048:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = NO -->
L0049: - [[**Stationarity**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} means that moving the observation window does not change the map's probability law. Here it gives a constant mean and a covariance $C(x,y)=C(y-x)$ that depends on separation.]{.definition-explanation} It does not say that a particular map is flat.
L0050:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = YES -->
L0051:   <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0052: - [[**Isotropy**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} means that rotating the separation does not change the covariance: in several dimensions, $C(\Delta x)$ depends only on $\|\Delta x\|$.]{.definition-explanation} It rules out a preferred direction in the model's statistics. Stationarity alone does not imply isotropy.
L0053:   <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0054: - [[**Smoothness**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} controls whether the map has well defined spatial derivatives.]{.definition-explanation} Crossing calculations require differentiable sample functions and nondegenerate joint distributions: the relevant vector of heights and derivatives must have an invertible covariance matrix, rather than obey an exact linear constraint. Peak calculations require second derivatives and enough regularity to control their local approximation.
L0055: 
L0056: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0057: A Gaussian distribution of values does not guarantee this smoothness. For example, a covariance proportional to $e^{-|x-y|/b}$ has a sharp corner at zero separation and does not supply the differentiable paths assumed below.
L0058: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0059: 
L0060: Set the mean to zero and write $\sigma_h^2=C(0)$ for input variance. A constant input mean can be absorbed into the threshold. It is useful to normalize the input,
L0061: 
L0062: $$
L0063: z(x)=\frac{h(x)}{\sigma_h},\qquad
L0064: F(x)=a\,[z(x)-q]_+,
L0065: \quad [u]_+=\max(u,0).
L0066: $$
L0067: 
L0068: Here $z$ has variance one, $q$ is a dimensionless threshold, and $a>0$ converts normalized input to firing rate, in Hz. The paper writes $\theta$ for $q$, $r$ for covariance, and $D$ for spatial dimension. This guide uses $q,C,d$ to avoid conflicts with earlier chapters. The original input threshold is $q\sigma_h$. In the paper's Equation 3 the firing rate is proportional to $[h-q\sigma_h]_+$, which is equivalent to this form after choosing the gain.[^p3-model]
L0069: 
L0070: The input threshold summarizes how much combined input is needed before the model cell fires. It can reflect the cell's own response properties and inhibitory input. Raising $q$ at a fixed input map leaves fewer positions active. If the absolute threshold above the input mean is positive and held fixed, increasing the input standard deviation $\sigma_h$ lowers $q$, making activity more widespread. Changing $a$ instead changes rates within active regions while preserving their zero-rate boundaries. A fixed positive detection cutoff in Hz would change this last conclusion. [A fitted threshold describes the balance of input and response. It does not identify which biological quantity changed.]{.key-statement}
L0071: 
L0072: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0073: <summary>Original paper figure: Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak (2025), Figure 1</summary>
L0074: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0075: 
L0076: {{block:fig:PAPER-PLACE-1}}
L0077: 
L0078: </div>
L0079: </details>
L0080: 
L0081: [The [**excursion set**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} above $q$ is]{.definition-explanation}
L0082: 
L0083: $$
L0084: A_q=\{x:z(x)>q\}.
L0085: $$
L0086: 
L0087: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0088: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0089: Each connected piece of $A_q$ is a model place field. In one dimension its size is length. In two and three dimensions it is area and volume. [Two nearby peaks belong to one field if an above-threshold path joins them. Counting peaks and counting fields therefore answer different questions.]{.key-statement}
L0090: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0091: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0092: 
L0093: For a one dimensional stationary process, differentiation of covariance gives
L0094: 
L0095: $$
L0096: \operatorname{Var}(h')=
L0097: \left.\partial_x\partial_y C(y-x)\right|_{y=x}=-C''(0).
L0098: $$
L0099: 
L0100: The minus sign comes from differentiating $y-x$ once with respect to each variable. The paper defines the spatial scale
L0101: 
L0102: $$
L0103: \sigma=\sqrt{-\frac{C(0)}{C''(0)}};
L0104: \qquad \operatorname{Var}(z')=\frac{1}{\sigma^2}.
L0105: $$
L0106: 
L0107: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0108: Thus $\sigma$ compares typical height with typical slope and has units of distance. In an isotropic field, the same definition applies along any unit direction. For the smooth Gaussian covariance $C(\Delta x)=\sigma_h^2\exp[-\|\Delta x\|^2/(2\sigma^2)]$, it is exactly the parameter in that exponent. The paper uses a Gaussian covariance for its simulations. Other smooth covariances can share this local scale while having different longer distance structure.[^p3-scale]
L0109: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0110: 
L0111: A simulated Gaussian input map with covariance $C(\Delta x)=\exp[-(\Delta x/\sigma)^2/2]$ gives the firing map below when $q=0.8$ and $a=6$ Hz. Position is shown in units of $\sigma$.
L0112: 
L0113: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0114: 
L0115: {{block:fig:PLACE-GP-TO-FIRING}}
L0116: 
L0117: </div>
L0118: 
L0119: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0120: Thresholding changes the distribution of values and can separate one smooth input curve into several fields. Both panels describe the same fixed map over space, so neither curve is a trajectory over time. See [Gaussian input and firing rate, STAR Methods Eqs. 2–3, p. e1](../papers/mainali-2025-universal-statistics.pdf#page=13).
L0121: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0122: 
L0123: ## Upcrossings count fields, with an endpoint correction {#sec:PLACE-KAC-RICE}
L0124: 
L0125: A field starts at an upcrossing, so its expected count can be found by counting upward passages through the threshold. A small interval around each crossing has a width determined by its slope. This lets us count crossings without locating every root.
L0126: 
L0127: Consider a differentiable fixed curve and a simple upward crossing $x_0$ of $q$, with slope $v=z'(x_0)>0$. Close to the crossing,
L0128: 
L0129: $$
L0130: z(x)-q\simeq v(x-x_0).
L0131: $$
L0132: 
L0133: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0134: The curve lies in the vertical band $|z-q|<\varepsilon$ for a spatial distance approximately $2\varepsilon/v$. Integrating $z'(x)/(2\varepsilon)$ over that interval contributes one. Restricting to positive slopes counts only upward crossings. [Under the usual regularity conditions, averaging this counting identity gives the [**Kac–Rice formula**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} for the mean upcrossing density,]{.definition-explanation}
L0135: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0136: 
L0137: $$
L0138: \nu_+(q)=\int_0^\infty v\,p_{z,z'}(q,v)\,dv.
L0139: $$
L0140: 
L0141: The density $p_{z,z'}(q,v)$ describes how often height is near $q$ and slope near $v$. Multiplication by $v$ compensates for the shorter spatial window of a steep crossing. Multiplying the probability of being above threshold by a typical slope would miss this weighting.[^p3-crossings]
L0142: 
L0143: A fixed smooth curve shows how to count a field already active at the left edge and why the crossing integral weights each slope.
L0144: 
L0145: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0146: 
L0147: {{block:fig:PLACE-CROSSINGS}}
L0148: 
L0149: </div>
L0150: 
L0151: The count includes every connected active interval intersecting the track. Discarding clipped boundary fields would change this convention.
L0152: 
L0153: We can evaluate the integral because height and slope are independent here. Stationarity gives
L0154: 
L0155: $$
L0156: \mathbb E[zz']=\frac12\frac{d}{dx}\mathbb E[z(x)^2]=0.
L0157: $$
L0158: 
L0159: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0160: Height and slope are jointly Gaussian. Zero covariance therefore makes them independent. Their distributions are $z\sim\mathcal N(0,1)$ and $z'\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^{-2})$. Consequently,
L0161: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0162: 
L0163: $$
L0164: \begin{aligned}
L0165: \nu_+(q)
L0166: &=\frac{e^{-q^2/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}
L0167:   \int_0^\infty v\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2\pi}}
L0168:                   e^{-\sigma^2v^2/2}\,dv\\
L0169: &=\frac{e^{-q^2/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}
L0170:   \frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}
L0171: =\frac{e^{-q^2/2}}{2\pi\sigma}.
L0172: \end{aligned}
L0173: $$
L0174: 
L0175: <!-- vale ai-tells.LabelAndExplain = NO -->
L0176: In the integral, use $u=\sigma^2v^2/2$, so $v\,dv=du/\sigma^2$. [This result is exact for the stated smooth stationary Gaussian model. A high threshold is not needed.]{.key-statement} Its units are inverse distance: increasing $\sigma$ stretches the map and reduces crossings per metre.
L0177: <!-- vale ai-tells.LabelAndExplain = YES -->
L0178: 
L0179: On a finite track $[0,L]$, each upcrossing starts a field, but a field may already be active at the left endpoint. If $N_L$ counts all components intersecting the track, then, under regularity conditions that exclude tangencies and give nonzero slopes at crossings,
L0180: 
L0181: $$
L0182: N_L=N_+(q;[0,L])+\mathbf1\{z(0)>q\},
L0183: \qquad
L0184: \mathbb E[N_L]=L\nu_+(q)+Q(q),
L0185: $$
L0186: 
L0187: where $Q(q)=1-\Phi(q)$ and $\Phi$ is the standard normal cumulative distribution function. The last term is the endpoint correction. Removing incomplete boundary fields changes the counting convention, so the same correction cannot then be used unchanged. This identity also gives the paper's exact one dimensional mean Euler characteristic, Equation 8. In one dimension, the Euler characteristic is the number of connected active intervals, so it is the field count used here. The [later topology chapter](ch-place-tests.html#sec-place-topology) extends this count to maps with holes.[^p3-ec-source]
L0188: 
L0189: ## Mean field and gap lengths separate threshold from scale {#sec:PLACE-INFERENCE}
L0190: 
L0191: [A low field count can result from a high threshold or a slowly varying map. Field lengths help separate those possibilities.]{.key-statement}
L0192: 
L0193: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0194: At a uniformly chosen position, the probability of activity is $Q(q)$. Hence a track of length $L$ has expected occupied length $LQ(q)$. On a long stationary track, the average occupied length per field is the occupied length per unit distance divided by the number of fields per unit distance. The corresponding gap calculation uses the inactive fraction $\Phi(q)$:
L0195: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0196: 
L0197: $$
L0198: \begin{aligned}
L0199: \mu_s&=\frac{Q(q)}{\nu_+(q)}
L0200:       =2\pi\sigma Q(q)e^{q^2/2},\\
L0201: \mu_g&=\frac{\Phi(q)}{\nu_+(q)}
L0202:       =2\pi\sigma\Phi(q)e^{q^2/2}.
L0203: \end{aligned}
L0204: $$
L0205: 
L0206: These are stationary per-field and per-gap means, ignoring clipping at track boundaries. They are not an assertion that the expectation of a finite sample's ratio equals the ratio of its expectations. Subject to suitable averaging over a long track or many maps, they give the inference rule in Methods S3, Equations S14–S15.[^p3-means]
L0207: 
L0208: Adding and dividing removes one unknown at a time:
L0209: 
L0210: $$
L0211: \mu_s+\mu_g=2\pi\sigma e^{q^2/2},\qquad
L0212: \frac{\mu_s}{\mu_s+\mu_g}=Q(q).
L0213: $$
L0214: 
L0215: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0216: Thus
L0217: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0218: 
L0219: $$
L0220: q=\Phi^{-1}\!\left(\frac{\mu_g}{\mu_s+\mu_g}\right),
L0221: \qquad
L0222: \sigma=\frac{\mu_s+\mu_g}{2\pi}e^{-q^2/2}.
L0223: $$
L0224: 
L0225: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0226: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0227: For example, $q=1.1$ and $\sigma=2.4$ m, the fitted parameters reported for the 200 m bat tunnel, give $Q(q)=0.1357$, $\nu_+=0.03621$ per metre, $\mu_s=3.746$ m, and $\mu_g=23.868$ m in the continuous model. Putting these two means into the inversion recovers $q=1.1$ and $\sigma=2.4$ m. The expected number of intersecting fields on a 200 m track is $7.2425+0.1357=7.3782$, including the left endpoint term. These are calculations from the fitted model, not fresh estimates from raw recordings.
L0228: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0229: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0230: 
L0231: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0232: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0233: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0234: [The means do not imply a full gap or count distribution. At high thresholds, distant rare excursions can approach independent events if correlations decay sufficiently with distance. In that regime, field locations approach a Poisson process, counts in a long interval are approximately Poisson, and gaps are approximately exponential. Smoothness and stationarity alone do not ensure this independence.]{.key-statement} A periodic Gaussian process, for example, retains long distance dependence. Upward and downward crossings that enclose the same small field remain strongly related even when fields far apart are almost independent.
L0235: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0236: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0237: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0238: 
L0239: Experimental selection also matters. If cells with no fields are excluded, a Poisson model with unconditioned mean $\lambda$ gives
L0240: 
L0241: $$
L0242: \Pr(N=n\mid N\ge1)=
L0243: \frac{e^{-\lambda}\lambda^n}{n!(1-e^{-\lambda})},\quad n\ge1,
L0244: \qquad
L0245: \mathbb E[N\mid N\ge1]=\frac{\lambda}{1-e^{-\lambda}}.
L0246: $$
L0247: 
L0248: The mean follows because the omitted $n=0$ term contributes zero to $\sum n\Pr(N=n)=\lambda$, while conditioning divides all remaining probabilities by $1-e^{-\lambda}$. For $\lambda=1$, $36.8\%$ of cells are excluded and the observed mean is $1.582$, not one. Using $1.582$ as the unconditioned mean would infer too many fields. The paper applies this correction to its two dimensional count fit, Equation 5.[^p3-selection]
L0249:

    one. This is a guide construction, not a sampled cell or a new fit. It illustrates
    Mainali et al. (2025), Figure 1A–B and Supplemental Methods S3, Equations S11–S15.

    one gap are marked. Two local tangent plots use the same vertical band q plus
    or minus epsilon. The steeper slope has a narrower horizontal width 2 epsilon
    divided by v, and slope times width divided by 2 epsilon equals one in both cases.

    $F(x)=a[z(x)-q]_+$. Values below $q$ become zero. Values above $q$ are reduced
    by $q$ and multiplied by $a$. The shaded connected intervals are the model fields.
    This is a simulated teaching map with chosen parameters, not a recorded cell.

    a threshold at 0.8 and shaded intervals above it. The lower plot uses the same

    Its connected positive intervals align with the upper shading. All other positions
    have zero firing.

  caption: A thresholded Gaussian map explains field lengths, gaps and higher moments
    in one dimensional recordings. Original Figure 1 from Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira,
    and Burak (2025). Exact artwork excerpt. [PDF page 4](../papers/mainali-2025-universal-statistics.pdf#page=4).
    [Open image at full size](../assets/fig-paper-place-1.png).
  alt: Panels A–F show recorded and modeled fields, random input summation, length
    and gap histograms, log length moments and mouse comparisons.
  width_inches: 6.5
  html_fold: never


## FILE guide/05b-place-sizes.md | ch:PLACE-SIZES
L0001: # High peaks explain size laws across dimensions {#ch:PLACE-SIZES}
L0002: 
L0003: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = NO -->
L0004: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0005: [Knowing the average field length does not tell us how often fields fall within a given size range. Why should the same spatial model give one size distribution on a track, another in an arena, and a third in a room?]{.key-statement} The next step is to find the shape of a typical field when only unusually high input peaks rise above the threshold.
L0006: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0007: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmptyPadding = YES -->
L0008: 
L0009: Near such a peak, the smooth input is approximately a downward parabola. Its height above the threshold sets a radius, and converting that radius into length, area or volume gives the different size laws. Cuts through a map test its spatial structure, and slopes at field edges provide a separate check on the predicted field widths. [The size laws use a high threshold approximation. The boundary-slope law has a separate exact derivation under the smooth stationary Gaussian assumptions.]{.key-statement}
L0010: 
L0011: We keep the earlier notation: $z(x)$ is the spatial input normalized to variance one, $q$ is its threshold, $\sigma$ is its local distance scale, and $a$ converts positive excess input into firing rate. The number of spatial dimensions is $d$. The firing rule remains $F(x)=a[z(x)-q]_+$, where $[u]_+=\max(u,0)$.
L0012: 
L0013: ## Near a high peak, the input is nearly parabolic {#sec:PLACE-RARE-PEAKS}
L0014: 
L0015: The crossing calculation determines a mean length exactly, but it does not yet explain the shape of the length histogram. To obtain that distribution, we examine the small region around a peak that barely exceeds a high threshold.
L0016: 
L0017: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = NO -->
L0018: The following is a teaching reconstruction of the high excursion argument in Methods S4, with algebraic corrections made explicit below. It assumes:
L0019: <!-- vale ai-tells.AnthropomorphicCognition = YES -->
L0020: 
L0021: - a sufficiently smooth, stationary, isotropic, unit variance Gaussian field.
L0022: - an invertible covariance matrix for the relevant derivative variables.
L0023: - the limit $q\to\infty$.
L0024: - typical components counted once each, away from environment boundaries.
L0025: 
L0026: It is not a proof of the full excursion convergence theorem for every allowed covariance.[^p3-rare-source]
L0027: 
L0028: [The [**Hessian**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the matrix $H$ of second spatial derivatives. Its eigenvalues describe curvature along the principal directions.]{.definition-explanation} Isotropy and the definition of $\sigma$ imply
L0029: 
L0030: $$
L0031: \operatorname{Cov}(\partial_i\partial_j z,z)
L0032: =-\frac{\delta_{ij}}{\sigma^2}.
L0033: $$
L0034: 
L0035: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0036: Here $\delta_{ij}$ is one for equal indices and zero otherwise. For jointly Gaussian variables, conditioning $Y$ on a scalar $Z$ of variance one gives $\mathbb E[Y\mid Z=b]=\operatorname{Cov}(Y,Z)b$ when both means vanish. One way to see this is to write $Y=\operatorname{Cov}(Y,Z)Z+\epsilon$. The remainder has zero covariance with $Z$ and is therefore independent of it. Applying this to each Hessian entry gives
L0037: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0038: 
L0039: $$
L0040: \mathbb E[H\mid z=b]=-\frac{b}{\sigma^2}I.
L0041: $$
L0042: 
L0043: The conditional fluctuations around this mean have a variance that does not grow with $b$. For a high peak, the negative mean curvature is of order $q$, so its relative fluctuations become small. Conditioning the gradient to zero does not change this Gaussian conditional mean, because the gradient has zero covariance with both height and Hessian in a stationary isotropic field. Selecting an actual maximum also weights curvatures, but its leading high peak shape is the same.
L0044: 
L0045: Let the peak height be $q+\epsilon$, with $\epsilon>0$, and put the peak at the origin. The gradient there is zero. The Taylor expansion becomes, to leading order,
L0046: 
L0047: $$
L0048: z(x)-q\simeq\epsilon-\frac{q}{2\sigma^2}\|x\|^2.
L0049: $$
L0050: 
L0051: It is positive inside an approximately round region of radius
L0052: 
L0053: $$
L0054: r_*\simeq\sigma\sqrt{\frac{2\epsilon}{q}}.
L0055: $$
L0056: 
L0057: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0058: The remaining question is how $\epsilon$ is distributed when we select one high field at a time. In one dimension, the number of high maxima is asymptotically the number of upcrossings, proportional to $e^{-q^2/2}$. Therefore the fraction of peaks above $q$ that also exceed $q+\epsilon$ is approximately
L0059: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0060: 
L0061: $$
L0062: \frac{e^{-(q+\epsilon)^2/2}}{e^{-q^2/2}}
L0063: =e^{-q\epsilon-\epsilon^2/2}.
L0064: $$
L0065: 
L0066: Define $U=q\epsilon$. At fixed $u\ge0$ and as $q$ increases,
L0067: 
L0068: $$
L0069: \Pr(U>u)\simeq
L0070: e^{-u-u^2/(2q^2)}\longrightarrow e^{-u}.
L0071: $$
L0072: 
L0073: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = NO -->
L0074: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0075: [Thus the scaled excess height approaches an exponential variable of mean one.]{.key-statement} In several dimensions, high peak counts have the additional leading factor $q^{d-1}$. Its ratio at $q+u/q$ and $q$ tends to one, so the same excess-height limit remains. The higher dimensional count factor will also follow from the [Euler characteristic calculation](ch-place-tests.html#sec-place-topology). A rigorous conclusion requires control of the remainder and of components containing multiple peaks, beyond this local argument.
L0076: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0077: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = YES -->
L0078: 
L0079: In one dimension, the full field length is $s=2r_*$. Eliminating $\epsilon$ gives
L0080: 
L0081: $$
L0082: s\simeq\frac{2\sqrt2\sigma}{q}\sqrt U,
L0083: \qquad
L0084: \epsilon\simeq\frac{q s^2}{8\sigma^2}.
L0085: $$
L0086: 
L0087: This supplies two distinct predictions:
L0088: 
L0089:   <!-- vale ai-tells.ConsequenceParticiple = NO -->
L0090: - [The length is a scaled square root of an exponential variable, giving a [**Rayleigh distribution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}.]{.definition-explanation}
L0091:   <!-- vale ai-tells.ConsequenceParticiple = YES -->
L0092: - The peak firing rate is $a\epsilon$, so at fixed high $q$ it is proportional to $s^2$.
L0093: 
L0094: The exponent two concerns rate versus linear size. In three dimensions a suitable linear size is $s^{1/3}$ when $s$ denotes volume.
L0095: 
L0096: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0097: <!-- vale ai-tells.NounString = NO -->
L0098: At moderate thresholds the omitted shape fluctuations matter. Figure 3C reports a rate versus length exponent of $1.42$ in the 200 m bat data and $1.46$ in finite threshold model simulations. Figure 3D reports $1.75$ versus $1.71$ using cube root of volume in 3D. These are compatible with a model that approaches exponent two only as the threshold becomes large. They are not measurements of the limiting exponent. Figure 5A–C illustrates the progression toward simpler, usually single peak fields as $q$ increases. Figure 4C tests this trend in the shorter bat tunnel.[^p3-shapes]
L0099: <!-- vale ai-tells.NounString = YES -->
L0100: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0101: 
L0102: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0103: <summary>Original paper figure: Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak (2025), Figure 5</summary>
L0104: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0105: 
L0106: {{block:fig:PAPER-PLACE-5}}
L0107: 
L0108: </div>
L0109: </details>
L0110: 
L0111: ## Geometry gives different size laws in each dimension {#sec:PLACE-SIZE-LAW}
L0112: 
L0113: A round region's radius and its volume have different units and different distributions. This geometric conversion explains why the model predicts different histograms in a track, an arena, and a room.
L0114: 
L0115: Let $v_d=\pi^{d/2}/\Gamma(1+d/2)$ be the volume of a unit ball in $d$ dimensions. For $d=1,2,3$, it is $2,\pi,4\pi/3$. [The symbol $\Gamma$ is the [**gamma function**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, defined for $t>0$ by $\Gamma(t)=\int_0^\infty u^{t-1}e^{-u}\,du$. It extends factorials: $\Gamma(n+1)=n!$.]{.definition-explanation}
L0116: 
L0117: Using the radius from the preceding section, the field size is
L0118: 
L0119: $$
L0120: s\simeq v_d\left(\frac{\sqrt2\sigma}{q}\right)^d U^{d/2},
L0121: \qquad
L0122: U=\beta s^{2/d},\qquad
L0123: \beta=\frac{q^2}{2\sigma^2v_d^{2/d}}.
L0124: $$
L0125: 
L0126: The same local radius gives a length, an area, or a volume depending on the dimension of the region.
L0127: 
L0128: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0129: 
L0130: {{block:fig:PLACE-SIZE-GEOMETRY}}
L0131: 
L0132: </div>
L0133: 
L0134: The round shape and exponential excess belong to the high-threshold limit, so they do not make every measured place field spherical.
L0135: 
L0136: Since the density of $U$ is $e^{-u}$ for $u>0$, changing variables gives
L0137: 
L0138: $$
L0139: \begin{aligned}
L0140: p_d(s)&=e^{-\beta s^{2/d}}\frac{d}{ds}(\beta s^{2/d})\\
L0141: &=\frac{2\beta}{d}s^{2/d-1}e^{-\beta s^{2/d}},\qquad s>0.
L0142: \end{aligned}
L0143: $$
L0144: 
L0145: This is the paper's Equation 1. The expression for $\beta$ above is its leading high threshold geometric scale, not an exact finite threshold identity. A finite threshold simulation can share the approximate shape while having a different fitted scale.[^p3-size-source]
L0146: 
L0147: The mean and its corresponding scale are
L0148: 
L0149: $$
L0150: \mu_s=\Gamma(1+d/2)\beta^{-d/2},\qquad
L0151: \beta=\left[\frac{\Gamma(1+d/2)}{\mu_s}\right]^{2/d}.
L0152: $$
L0153: 
L0154: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Derivation: normalization and higher size moments"}
L0155: 
L0156: The same substitution verifies normalization: $\int_0^\infty p_d(s)\,ds=\int_0^\infty e^{-u}\,du=1$. It also supplies every positive moment,
L0157: 
L0158: $$
L0159: \mathbb E[s^k]
L0160: =\beta^{-kd/2}\int_0^\infty u^{kd/2}e^{-u}\,du
L0161: =\beta^{-kd/2}\Gamma(1+kd/2).
L0162: $$
L0163: 
L0164: :::
L0165: 
L0166: Size has units of distance to the power $d$, while $\beta$ has units of inverse distance squared in every dimension. This makes $\beta s^{2/d}$ dimensionless, and makes $p_d(s)$ a density per unit size. These checks catch mistakes in changing variables.
L0167: 
L0168: Writing dimensionless size as $w=\beta^{d/2}s$ removes the size scale. The dimensional size laws then differ in shape, while the transformed variable $U=\beta s^{2/d}=w^{2/d}$ has the same exponential law.
L0169: 
L0170: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0171: 
L0172: {{block:fig:PLACE-SIZE-DENSITY-COLLAPSE}}
L0173: 
L0174: </div>
L0175: 
L0176: Removing units does not erase the difference between length, area, and volume distributions. The nonlinear transformation to U removes that shape difference. Its shared exponential law is the assumption used in the change of variables. See [Field-size laws, Eq. 1, p. 1111](../papers/mainali-2025-universal-statistics.pdf#page=3).
L0177: 
L0178: <div class="table-responsive">
L0179: 
L0180: {{block:tbl:PLACE-SIZE-LAWS}}
L0181: 
L0182: </div>
L0183: 
L0184: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = NO -->
L0185: [A [**Weibull distribution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} has survival probability $\Pr(\text{size}>s)=\exp[-(s/b)^k]$ for size $s>0$, scale $b>0$, and shape $k>0$. This is the chance that a field is larger than $s$.]{.definition-explanation} Here $k=2/d$ and $b=\beta^{-d/2}$. In 3D the diverging density does not mean a positive probability of exactly zero volume. The probability of $0<s<\delta$ still tends to zero as $\delta\to0$.
L0186: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = YES -->
L0187: 
L0188: For a concrete comparison, set $\sigma=1$ m and $q=5$ in this limiting calculation. The mean size is $0.5013$ m in 1D, $0.2513$ m$^2$ in 2D, and $0.1260$ m$^3$ in 3D. These values describe the same local distance scale and threshold, rather than fields with the same numerical size in different units. More generally, the geometry and moment formula simplify to $\mu_s\simeq(\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma/q)^d$.
L0189: 
L0190: <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerLeads = NO -->
L0191: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0192: This approximation has a measurable error even for a smooth Gaussian process. At $q=1.1,\sigma=2.4$ m, its 1D mean is $5.469$ m, compared with the [exact stationary crossing result](ch-place-fields.html#sec-place-inference) $3.746$ m, an overestimate of $46.0\%$. At $q=5$ the error is $3.73\%$. [This is why the paper compares data to finite threshold simulations as well as to limiting curves.]{.key-statement}
L0193: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0194: <!-- vale ai-tells.ExplainerLeads = YES -->
L0195: 
L0196: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Source check: three printed steps in the supplement"}
L0197: 
L0198: The printed supplement appears to contain three algebraic errors in this calculation:
L0199: 
L0200: - Equation S18 prints multiplication by mean size where the preceding moment formula requires division.
L0201: - Equation S23 omits a factor $\sqrt2$ when solving the parabola in S22 for full width.
L0202: - In S25 the finite-height quadratic exponent should be negative.
L0203: 
L0204: The derivation above retains the main paper's distributional form and obtains the constants from the displayed parabola, the moment integral, and the independent crossing calculation. These corrections concern the printed derivation. They do not establish that the simulation code or empirical fits used the same mistakes.[^p3-corrections]
L0205: 
L0206: :::
L0207: 
L0208: ## Slices test spatial structure beyond field volume {#sec:PLACE-SLICES}
L0209: 
L0210: A model can fit field volumes and still place activity incorrectly within those volumes. Slicing the map tests that internal spatial structure.
L0211: 
L0212: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0213: Restrict a Gaussian field to a straight line $x=x_0+tv$. At any chosen values $t_1,\ldots,t_n$, its values are a subset of the original jointly Gaussian values. They therefore remain jointly Gaussian. Along the line the covariance is $C((t-t')v)$. For a unit direction in an isotropic field it has the same local length scale $\sigma$. The same argument applies to a flat plane. First slicing and then thresholding gives the same active portions as thresholding and then slicing.[^p3-slices-source]
L0214: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0215: 
L0216: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0217: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0218: Thus a line through a 2D or 3D map has the one dimensional excursion prediction, and a plane through a 3D map has the two dimensional prediction. The field being counted is now a connected active interval or patch in that slice. One 3D component can be missed, cut into multiple pieces, or intersect several slices.
L0219: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0220: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0221: 
L0222: Choosing one original field uniformly and cutting through its centre is different from choosing lines uniformly through space. Large fields are more likely to be hit by a spatially sampled line, and most hits pass away from their centres. The Gaussian restriction argument describes excursions along the sampled line. The radius distribution for fields counted once each cannot be used directly as the distribution of lengths observed along a line. In the high peak picture, an isotropic $d$ dimensional field has a radius of order $\sigma/q$. Converting this same radius into $d$ dimensional size and then cutting it requires both hit probability and cut position.
L0223: 
L0224: Figure 2 separates the measurements by spatial dimension:
L0225: 
L0226:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0227: - Panels A–B illustrate 2D thresholding. C measures lengths along slices, D measures original field areas, and E counts fields per observed cell.
L0228:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0229:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0230: - F–G show 3D examples. H, I, and J compare line lengths, planar areas, and full volumes.
L0231:   <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0232: 
L0233: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0234: <summary>Original paper figure: Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak (2025), Figure 2</summary>
L0235: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0236: 
L0237: {{block:fig:PAPER-PLACE-2}}
L0238: 
L0239: </div>
L0240: </details>
L0241: 
L0242: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0243: [The dashed analytical curves change with the dimension of the measurement, even within one recorded map. The alternative spherical-field model in Figure S2 matches empirical volumes and peak rates by construction but fails these slice distributions and the topology test. Matching volumes alone is therefore insufficient.]{.key-statement}[^p3-slices-source]
L0244: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0245: 
L0246: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0247: <summary>Original paper figure: Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak (2025), Figure S2</summary>
L0248: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0249: 
L0250: {{block:fig:PAPER-PLACE-S2}}
L0251: 
L0252: </div>
L0253: </details>
L0254: 
L0255: ## Why slopes at field boundaries are Rayleigh {#sec:PLACE-SLOPES}
L0256: 
L0257: The derivative at an arbitrary position is Gaussian. A field boundary is a selected position, however, and selecting it changes the derivative distribution.
L0258: 
L0259: Return to the [crossing integral](ch-place-fields.html#sec-place-kac-rice) and retain only slopes between $v$ and $v+dv$. Their contribution is proportional to $v p_{z'}(v)dv$. Dividing by the total contribution of positive slopes gives the slope density at a typical upcrossing:
L0260: 
L0261: $$
L0262: p_{\uparrow}(v)
L0263: =\frac{v p_{z'}(v)}{\int_0^\infty w p_{z'}(w)\,dw}
L0264: =\sigma^2v\exp[-\sigma^2v^2/2],\qquad v>0.
L0265: $$
L0266: 
L0267: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0268: The height density cancels because height and slope are independent. [This Rayleigh law is therefore independent of $q$ under the smooth stationary Gaussian model, without a high threshold approximation.]{.key-statement} Absolute slopes at downward boundaries have the same law. The rate slope is $av$, so its scale also depends on the firing rate gain. This selection effect explains why a half Gaussian law for a positive derivative sampled uniformly in space would be wrong at crossings.[^p3-slope-source]
L0269: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0270: 
L0271: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Independent check: recover field width from its boundary slope"}
L0272: 
L0273: There is also an independent check on the high threshold width constant. Starting at an upcrossing with slope $v$, the local excursion is approximately
L0274: 
L0275: $$
L0276: z(x)-q\simeq vx-\frac{q}{2\sigma^2}x^2.
L0277: $$
L0278: 
L0279: Its second root is $s=2\sigma^2v/q$. Changing variables in the exact crossing slope law gives $p(s)=2\beta s e^{-\beta s^2}$ with $\beta=q^2/(8\sigma^2)$, the same constant as the peak calculation for $v_1=2$.
L0280: 
L0281: :::
L0282: 
L0283: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0284: Figure 3A identifies which width, peak, and slopes are measured. B compares local peak counts, C–D compare rate and size, and E compares boundary slopes. The paper measures boundary slopes by a finite difference between samples 0.1 m apart. This approximates a derivative and has its own spatial resolution. It is not a direct observation of the underlying synaptic input.
L0285: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0286: 
L0287: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0288: <summary>Original paper figure: Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak (2025), Figure 3</summary>
L0289: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0290: 
L0291: {{block:fig:PAPER-PLACE-3}}
L0292: 
L0293: </div>
L0294: </details>

The supplement cites Adler (1981) for the peak approximation. The main article cites Nosko (1988), reference 32, for asymptotic field statistics. This chapter supplies a local derivation and does not claim to reproduce either full theorem proof.

    - length
    - $2\beta s e^{-\beta s^2}$
    - Rayleigh. Density vanishes at zero.
  - - $d=2$
    - area
    - $\beta e^{-\beta s}$
    - Exponential. Mean area is $1/\beta$.
  - - $d=3$
    - volume
    - $(2\beta/3)s^{-1/3}e^{-\beta s^{2/3}}$
    - Weibull with shape $2/3$. Density rises without bound near zero but is integrable.
  caption: Field-size laws in one, two, and three dimensions.
  first_column_role: row-labels
  html_fold: never
- id: fig:PLACE-SIZE-GEOMETRY
  kind: figure
  placement: sec:PLACE-SIZE-LAW
  asset: assets/approved/visuals/place-size-geometry.png
  caption: In the high-threshold approximation, excess peak height fixes a radius,
    and converting that radius into length, area, or volume gives powers 2, 1, and
    2/3 in $U=\beta s^{2/d}$. The round regions are a guide reconstruction of the
    limiting geometry, not observed field shapes. See Mainali et al. (2025), Equation
    1 and Supplemental Methods S4, with the parabola constants checked in this chapter.
  alt: A local parabola stands epsilon above threshold q, and its full width is twice
    r star. U equals q times epsilon and r star is approximately square root of two
    times sigma divided by q times square root of U. The same radius gives an interval
    of length 2 r star, a disk of area pi r star squared, or a ball of volume four
    pi r star cubed over three. Therefore U depends on size to powers 2, 1, and 2/3.
  width_inches: 7.29167
  html_fold: never
- id: fig:PLACE-SIZE-DENSITY-COLLAPSE
  kind: figure
  placement: sec:PLACE-SIZE-LAW
  asset: assets/approved/visuals/place-size-density-collapse.png
  caption: 'The limiting size densities differ with spatial dimension: one dimensional
    length is Rayleigh, two dimensional area is exponential, and three dimensional
    volume has a density that rises without bound as size approaches zero, while its
    integral remains one. Their survival probabilities, the probabilities of exceeding
    a threshold, coincide after transformation to $U=\beta s^{2/d}$, where $\Pr(U>u)=e^{-u}$.
    These are exact calculations from the high-threshold limiting law, not finite-threshold
    simulations or measured histograms. Based on Mainali et al. (2025), main Eq. 1,
    p. 1111, and Methods S3.'
  alt: Three density curves against dimensionless size w show the one dimensional
    curve starting at zero, the two dimensional curve starting at one, and the three
    dimensional curve rising without bound toward zero size. An arrow marks the unbounded
    end. In the second panel, three differently styled survival curves coincide on
    exp(-u) after U=w^(2/d).
  width_inches: 7.29167

## FILE guide/05c-place-tests.md | ch:PLACE-TESTS
L0001: # Holes and measurements test the spatial explanation {#ch:PLACE-TESTS}
L0002: 
L0003: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0004: [Two maps can have the same field sizes and counts while arranging their active regions differently. One may contain rings or tunnels that the other lacks. Can the Gaussian spatial model account for these differences, and how much would that agreement tell us about the circuit that produced the maps?]{.key-statement}
L0005: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0006: 
L0007: We first count connected regions and holes together, which gives a prediction across activity thresholds. The boundary of the observed environment matters for this calculation, so we keep its contributions. We then examine which measured quantities set the model's parameters and which provide further tests. Finally, a sum of many randomly weighted inputs shows one way a circuit could produce Gaussian spatial input, while making clear which assumptions and circuit measurements that explanation still needs.
L0008: 
L0009: The map is still fixed over space. As before, $q$ is the normalized activity threshold, $\sigma$ is the local spatial scale, and $a$ converts normalized input above threshold into firing rate. The number of spatial dimensions is $d$. It is separate from the diffusion coefficient $D$ used in the memory chapters.
L0010: 
L0011: ## Holes distinguish maps with the same field count {#sec:PLACE-TOPOLOGY}
L0012: 
L0013: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0014: Two maps can have the same field count and total active area but different holes. As the activity threshold rises, active regions shrink and can split or disappear. The numbers of components and holes can change.
L0015: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0016: 
L0017: [The [**Euler characteristic**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, written $\chi$ and unrelated to the common variance fraction $\chi$ in the eye chapter, combines these counts differently in each dimension:]{.definition-explanation}
L0018: 
L0019: - In 1D it equals the number of connected active intervals.
L0020:   <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0021: - In 2D it is the number of connected components minus the number of independent holes. Three disjoint disks have $\chi=3$. One connected ring has $\chi=1-1=0$.
L0022:   <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0023: - In 3D it is components minus independent tunnels plus enclosed cavities. A solid ball has $\chi=1$, a solid ring has $\chi=0$, and a hollow spherical shell has $\chi=2$.
L0024: 
L0025: [These counts are the first three [**Betti numbers**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}: numbers of independent components, loops, and cavities, respectively.]{.definition-explanation}[^p3-ec-source]
L0026: 
L0027: Thresholding one fixed smooth map separates the number of connected fields from the number of holes.
L0028: 
L0029: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0030: 
L0031: {{block:fig:PLACE-TOPOLOGY}}
L0032: 
L0033: </div>
L0034: 
L0035: A negative chi means that holes outnumber components in this 2D example. It does not mean a negative number of fields. When holes are absent, chi equals the component count.
L0036: 
L0037: The expected Euler characteristic has an exact Gaussian formula even where identifying it with field count would be wrong. We already derived the 1D case. To understand the higher dimensional factors, we use a mathematical result. [A [**critical point**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is a location where the gradient is zero.]{.definition-explanation} [For a smooth function on a compact domain with invertible Hessians at its critical points and suitable boundary conditions, [**Morse theory**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} expresses the Euler characteristic as a signed count of critical points, including boundary contributions.]{.definition-explanation} We do not prove that topological theorem here. The calculation below shows how its Gaussian expectation produces the paper's polynomials and boundary terms. The general Gaussian result is given by Taylor's kinematic formula.[^p3-taylor]
L0038: 
L0039: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0040: The signs have a geometric meaning. In 2D, lowering the threshold through a maximum creates an active component, adding one to $\chi$. Passing a saddle joins two components or opens a hole, subtracting one. Passing a minimum fills a hole, adding one. The Hessian has two negative eigenvalues at a maximum, opposite signs at a saddle, and two positive eigenvalues at a minimum. Thus $\det(-H)$ has the required signs $+,-,+$. The boundary terms account for changes at the edge of the observed region.
L0041: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0042: 
L0043: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0044: First take the interior of a $j$ dimensional region, with $j\le3$, and set $\lambda=\sigma^{-2}$. A critical point has gradient zero. The multidimensional Kac–Rice rule counts these zeros with the absolute Hessian determinant. The Morse signs replace that absolute determinant by $\det(-H)$. The resulting signed density above level $q$ is
L0045: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0046: 
L0047: $$
L0048: \eta_j(q)=p_{\nabla z}(0)
L0049: \int_q^\infty
L0050: \mathbb E[\det(-H)\mid z=b,\nabla z=0]\,\phi(b)\,db,
L0051: $$
L0052: 
L0053: where $\phi(b)=e^{-b^2/2}/\sqrt{2\pi}$. The gradient's $j$ components are independent Gaussians of variance $\lambda$, so $p_{\nabla z}(0)=(2\pi\lambda)^{-j/2}$. The determinant measures how spatial volume near a gradient zero converts to gradient volume, just as the slope did for a scalar crossing.
L0054: 
L0055: The resulting signed densities are
L0056: 
L0057: $$
L0058: \eta_1=\frac{e^{-q^2/2}}{2\pi\sigma},\qquad
L0059: \eta_2=\frac{q e^{-q^2/2}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}\sigma^2},\qquad
L0060: \eta_3=\frac{(q^2-1)e^{-q^2/2}}{(2\pi)^2\sigma^3}.
L0061: $$
L0062: 
L0063: These are signed densities per length, area, and volume, not three formulas for positive field counts.
L0064: 
L0065: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Derivation: the Gaussian determinant average and its integrals"}
L0066: 
L0067: Write the conditional Hessian as $H=-b\lambda I+G$, where $G$ has zero mean. Differentiating an isotropic covariance four times gives, for distinct indices $i,k$,
L0068: 
L0069: $$
L0070: \mathbb E[G_{ii}G_{kk}]=\kappa-\lambda^2,
L0071: \qquad
L0072: \mathbb E[G_{ik}^2]=\kappa.
L0073: $$
L0074: 
L0075: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0076: The constant $\kappa$ describes a fourth derivative of covariance. Conditioning on height subtracts $\lambda^2$ from the covariance of the two diagonal entries, but not from the off-diagonal variance. Hence each pair contributes
L0077: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0078: 
L0079: $$
L0080: \mathbb E[G_{ii}G_{kk}-G_{ik}^2]=-\lambda^2.
L0081: $$
L0082: 
L0083: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0084: For $j=1$, $\mathbb E[\det(-H)\mid b]=b\lambda$. For $j=2$, expand the two by two determinant to obtain $\lambda^2(b^2-1)$. For $j=3$, the leading term is $b^3\lambda^3$. The three index pairs each contribute $-b\lambda^3$, and all odd centered Gaussian products vanish. The result is $\lambda^3(b^3-3b)$. The unknown fourth derivative $\kappa$ has canceled.
L0085: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0086: 
L0087: Integrating the three polynomials gives
L0088: 
L0089: $$
L0090: \begin{aligned}
L0091: \int_q^\infty b\phi(b)\,db&=\phi(q),\\
L0092: \int_q^\infty(b^2-1)\phi(b)\,db&=q\phi(q),\\
L0093: \int_q^\infty(b^3-3b)\phi(b)\,db&=(q^2-1)\phi(q).
L0094: \end{aligned}
L0095: $$
L0096: 
L0097: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0098: For example, differentiate $-q\phi(q)$ to get $(q^2-1)\phi(q)$, which verifies the second integral using its vanishing upper endpoint. Combining the determinant, gradient density, and integral gives the signed densities above.
L0099: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0100: 
L0101: :::
L0102: 
L0103: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0104: Now include the boundary of an ordinary closed square or cube of side $L$, whose opposite faces are not joined and with every intersecting component retained. On a $j$ dimensional face, derivatives along the face give the same calculation as above. Each of its $d-j$ gradient components perpendicular to the face and directed out of the box must have the appropriate sign to contribute to the boundary Morse count. Symmetry and independence give a factor $2^{-(d-j)}$. There are $2^{d-j}\binom dj$ such faces, each of size $L^j$. Their combined coefficient is therefore $\binom djL^j$. Corners supply $Q(q)$ in total. With $r=L/\sigma$, the formulas become
L0105: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0106: 
L0107: $$
L0108: \begin{aligned}
L0109: \mathbb E[\chi_1]&=Q(q)+\frac{r}{2\pi}e^{-q^2/2},\\
L0110: \mathbb E[\chi_2]&=Q(q)+
L0111: \left[\frac{2r}{2\pi}+\frac{r^2q}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}\right]e^{-q^2/2},\\
L0112: \mathbb E[\chi_3]&=Q(q)+
L0113: \left[\frac{3r}{2\pi}+\frac{3r^2q}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}
L0114: +\frac{r^3(q^2-1)}{(2\pi)^2}\right]e^{-q^2/2}.
L0115: \end{aligned}
L0116: $$
L0117: 
L0118: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0119: These reproduce the paper's Equations 8–10 for interval, square, and cube geometry. They are not the formulas for a periodic box. For a rectangle or rectangular room, replace $2L$ or $3L$ by the sum of side lengths, $3L^2$ by the sum of pairwise side products, and $L^3$ by volume. A general domain requires its own geometric boundary factors. Applying only the volume term to a small room would discard real boundary contributions.
L0120: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0121: 
L0122: The formulas have two limiting checks:
L0123: 
L0124: - As $q\to-\infty$, the whole box becomes active and all three expectations tend to one.
L0125: - As $q\to+\infty$, they tend to zero.
L0126: 
L0127: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0128: <!-- vale ai-tells.OverusedVocabulary = NO -->
L0129: At intermediate thresholds the higher dimensional signed densities can be negative because tunnels or holes outnumber components. For example a cube with $L/\sigma=10$ has expected $\chi=-20.06$ at $q=0$. A negative expected Euler characteristic is meaningful. A negative expected field count would not be.
L0130: <!-- vale ai-tells.OverusedVocabulary = YES -->
L0131: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0132: 
L0133: At a sufficiently high threshold, almost every component is a small solid blob with one peak and no holes. Then $\chi\simeq N$. For a large box, retaining the leading volume term and the leading power of $q$ gives
L0134: 
L0135: $$
L0136: \mathbb E[N]\simeq
L0137: \frac{V}{(2\pi)^{(d+1)/2}\sigma^d}
L0138: q^{d-1}e^{-q^2/2},
L0139: $$
L0140: 
L0141: where $V$ is environment length, area, or volume. Dividing $VQ(q)$ by this count and using $Q(q)\simeq\phi(q)/q$ recovers $\mu_s\simeq(\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma/q)^d$, independently checking the size-law mean. [The exact EC formula and this approximate component count have different ranges of validity.]{.key-statement}[^p3-higher-count]
L0142: 
L0143: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0144: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0145: Read Figure 3F–H as whole curves, rather than single field counts. Increasing the applied firing rate cutoff by $t\ge0$ changes the normalized level to $q+t/a$. It can reveal separation between peaks or remove components. The measured maps only test levels at or above their original effective cutoff. The rest of an analytic curve is a prediction. The gain was matched using mean maximum firing rate before the EC comparison, so the horizontal conversion from Hz to normalized level also uses fitted information. Two dimensional holes and three dimensional tunnels help explain the nonmonotonic curves.[^p3-ec-source]
L0146: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0147: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0148: 
L0149: ## What was fitted and what was tested {#sec:PLACE-DATA}
L0150: 
L0151: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0152: The experiments supply estimated firing rate maps, not the hidden Gaussian input. To assess a prediction, we must therefore track both which summaries set its parameters and which processing decisions change the map's geometry.
L0153: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0154: 
L0155: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0156: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0157: The paper reanalyzes earlier bat and rodent recordings. It uses different available summaries in different conditions. The inspected article and supplement do not clearly give the final included cell count for every analysis, particularly the long-tunnel and 3D reanalyses. Counts from an original recording study should therefore not be silently substituted for those final samples.[^p3-processing]
L0158: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0159: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0160: 
L0161: <div class="table-responsive">
L0162: 
L0163: {{block:tbl:PLACE-DATA-COMPARISONS}}
L0164: 
L0165: </div>
L0166: 
L0167: The STAR Methods call the virtual-track animals rats in one sentence. Figure 1F and the main results identify them as mice. Several STAR figure cross-references also appear displaced. The table follows the main figure captions and the analyses described in full, rather than those isolated labels.
L0168: 
L0169: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0170: Figure 1C distinguishes a limiting analytical Rayleigh curve from the finite threshold simulation histogram. Its parameters were constrained by both mean length and mean gap, so the full length histogram is an additional check. Figure 1D does the same for gaps. Panel E tests whether the shape of the log-length distribution agrees with the model beyond its mean and variance.
L0171: 
L0172: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0173: For $Y=\log s$, write $m_k=\mathbb E[(Y-\mathbb EY)^k]$. [[**Skew**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, $m_3/m_2^{3/2}$, measures signed asymmetry: the cubed deviations retain their signs, so unusually large log lengths contribute positively and unusually small ones negatively.]{.definition-explanation} [[**Excess kurtosis**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, $m_4/m_2^2-3$, measures the fourth moment relative to a Gaussian with the same variance. The fourth power weights large departures from the mean strongly, on either side.]{.definition-explanation} A positive value means a larger fourth moment than that Gaussian reference. [A [**lognormal distribution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} of lengths makes $Y$ Gaussian and both quantities zero.]{.definition-explanation} Nonzero population values therefore rule out that lognormal description. Zero values alone do not prove it, and finite sample estimates fluctuate, which is why the figure compares simulated sampling distributions.[^p3-fit-tests]
L0174: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0175: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0176: 
L0177: For the long-tunnel data, the authors compared the Gaussian excursion model with a fitted lognormal using likelihoods, the probabilities or densities each model assigns to the observed data, and criteria that penalize fitted parameter count. Those comparisons favored the Gaussian model. [These are in-sample model comparisons. They are not reported held-out forecasts.]{.key-statement} Model histogram error bars come from $10^4$ simulations matched to the number of recorded cells. They do not quantify every source of animal-level or measurement uncertainty.[^p3-fit-tests]
L0178: 
L0179: The processing choices affect the quantities being compared:
L0180: 
L0181: - Bat maps were rectified at about 0.5 Hz for denoising: rates below this cutoff were set to zero, removing weak activity from the measured fields.
L0182: - The 3D maps, sampled in 10 cm bins, also received a [**median filter**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, which replaces each bin's rate with a middle-ranked rate in its local neighborhood. The paper used a neighborhood two bins wide on each axis. This reduces isolated high or low values but can also change small field features.
L0183: - Fields larger than three standard deviations above mean 3D volume were removed as possible merges, including from slice analyses.
L0184: - Peak counting excluded incomplete boundary fields and used a range of smoothing widths.
L0185: - The 1D rate versus width comparison used an additional 1 Hz cutoff.
L0186: 
L0187: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0188: [These decisions can remove small components, alter peaks, and change tails. They belong to the definition of the analyzed fields.]{.key-statement}[^p3-processing]
L0189: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0190: 
L0191: Figure S1 matches the rat maze's 15 cm minimum length cutoff in simulations and removes unusually large empirical fields. Changing that cutoff changes log-length moments. Figure S5 shows sensitivity of the higher-moment comparison across small denoising thresholds, while the rate versus width slope becomes stable above about 1 Hz. Figure S2 compares the spherical-field alternative and measures boundary curvature. [Locally, [**mean curvature**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} averages bending in two principal directions and [**Gaussian curvature**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} multiplies them: a spherical patch bends the same way in both directions, while a saddle bends in opposite directions.]{.definition-explanation} These are extra shape measurements. They are not needed to derive the field-size law. Surface representations and finite differences affect their estimates.[^p3-supp-tests]
L0192: 
L0193: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0194: <summary>Original paper figure: Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak (2025), Figure S1</summary>
L0195: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0196: 
L0197: {{block:fig:PAPER-PLACE-S1}}
L0198: 
L0199: </div>
L0200: </details>
L0201: 
L0202: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0203: <summary>Original paper figure: Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak (2025), Figure S5</summary>
L0204: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0205: 
L0206: {{block:fig:PAPER-PLACE-S5}}
L0207: 
L0208: </div>
L0209: </details>
L0210: 
L0211: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0212: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0213: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0214: Figure 4A–B summarizes fitted parameter changes across a small set of environments. The correlation length increases with environment size while the threshold generally decreases. Increasing $\sigma$ reduces crossings per metre, while decreasing $q$ increases them. Both parameters also affect mean length, so these effects must be considered together. Figure S3 reports count scaling exponents below one and hence a weak decline in count per unit environment size. These cross-experiment trends do not establish a biological scaling law. In particular, the 3D table's reported $\sigma=4.0$ has an unclear distance-unit conversion relative to the spatial binning, so it should not be read as a verified value in metres.[^p3-trends]
L0215: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0216: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0217: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0218: 
L0219: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0220: <summary>Original paper figure: Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak (2025), Figure 4</summary>
L0221: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0222: 
L0223: {{block:fig:PAPER-PLACE-4}}
L0224: 
L0225: </div>
L0226: </details>
L0227: 
L0228: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0229: <summary>Original paper figure: Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak (2025), Figure S3</summary>
L0230: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0231: 
L0232: {{block:fig:PAPER-PLACE-S3}}
L0233: 
L0234: </div>
L0235: </details>
L0236: 
L0237: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0238: The model also allows differences between cells. Figure S4 draws a positive threshold separately for each cell and keeps that threshold for two otherwise independent maps representing opposite flight directions. Low-threshold cells tend to have more and larger fields in both maps. This shared cell property creates correlations between flight directions even when field locations differ between directions. Two distribution parameters were chosen to match the two empirical correlations, so those correlations are fitted features of this extension. Its continued agreement with length and gap distributions is the additional comparison. This extension should not be described as a two-parameter model shared by all cells.[^p3-threshold-variation]
L0239: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0240: 
L0241: <details class="publisher-long-content publisher-source-figure">
L0242: <summary>Original paper figure: Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak (2025), Figure S4</summary>
L0243: <div class="publisher-fold-body">
L0244: 
L0245: {{block:fig:PAPER-PLACE-S4}}
L0246: 
L0247: </div>
L0248: </details>
L0249: 
L0250: ## Random input weights can generate Gaussian maps {#sec:PLACE-WIRING}
L0251: 
L0252: The statistical model becomes biologically useful if it suggests how a circuit could make the maps. A random weighted sum of many inputs is one possible mechanism, and the supplement makes its assumptions explicit.
L0253: 
L0254: At position $x$, each input cell contributes according to its spatial firing profile. Fixed connection weights determine how those contributions combine at the receiving cell. The source treats the sum as effective input relative to a background that can be balanced through inhibition or regulation of excitation and inhibition. The signed random weights below are part of this simplified input model. They do not establish the signs of individual biological synapses.
L0255: 
L0256: Let $u_i(x)$ be the fixed spatial tuning of input neuron $i$, and let independent weights $W_i$ have zero mean and variance $v_W$. The profiles $u_i$ may have nonzero spatial means. The expectation below is over weights: their zero means make the summed input $h(x)$ have mean zero. The summed input is
L0257: 
L0258: $$
L0259: h(x)=\sum_{i=1}^n W_i u_i(x).
L0260: $$
L0261: 
L0262: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = NO -->
L0263: [At a finite list of positions this is a sum of independent random vectors, one per input cell. Under a multivariate central limit condition, with a suitable normalization and no single input dominating, the sum approaches a Gaussian vector as the number of contributing inputs grows. Applying this at every finite list supplies the Gaussian-process description. This Gaussian limit requires many contributing inputs at each position. A large anatomical input count alone does not establish how many inputs contribute there.]{.key-statement}[^p3-wiring-source]
L0264: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerIntensifier = YES -->
L0265: 
L0266: The covariance calculation exposes which assumption supplies spatial structure:
L0267: 
L0268: $$
L0269: \begin{aligned}
L0270: \mathbb E[h(x)h(y)]
L0271: &=\sum_{i,j}\mathbb E[W_iW_j]u_i(x)u_j(y)\\
L0272: &=v_W\sum_i u_i(x)u_i(y).
L0273: \end{aligned}
L0274: $$
L0275: 
L0276: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0277: The same fixed weight multiplies an input cell's activity at both positions. For positive input profiles, cells active at both $x$ and $y$ therefore make positive contributions to covariance across possible weight choices. Little overlap gives a smaller contribution. For profiles defined as deviations from background, the products can also be negative. Broad input profiles can produce broad spatial covariance, as the Gaussian example below shows.
L0278: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0279: 
L0280: If inputs are translated copies $u_i(x)=u_0(x-x_i)$ with centres densely and uniformly covering space at density $\rho$, the sum is approximately
L0281: 
L0282: $$
L0283: C(y-x)\simeq v_W\rho\int u_0(s)u_0(s+y-x)\,ds.
L0284: $$
L0285: 
L0286: The assumptions supply different parts of the model:
L0287: 
L0288: - Uniform coverage supplies stationarity away from boundaries.
L0289: - Smooth input tuning supplies smoothness.
L0290: - Random weights supply the Gaussian limit under their independence and size conditions.
L0291: 
L0292: For input profiles with different shapes, average the last integral over the distribution of those shapes, as in Methods S2, Equation S8.
L0293: 
L0294: At fixed profile shape, increasing the weight variance $v_W$ or input density $\rho$ increases the covariance amplitude. It does not change the normalized spatial dependence in this construction. Widening each input profile instead broadens the distances over which positions share input. These are different changes: input amplitude can alter the effective threshold $q$ when the absolute threshold is held fixed, whereas input width changes the spatial scale.
L0295: 
L0296: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0297: For example, take $u_0(s)=R_0e^{-s^2/(2b^2)}$ in one dimension. Completing the square gives
L0298: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0299: 
L0300: $$
L0301: \int u_0(s)u_0(s+\Delta)\,ds
L0302: =R_0^2\sqrt\pi b\,e^{-\Delta^2/(4b^2)}.
L0303: $$
L0304: 
L0305: The covariance's Gaussian standard deviation is $\sqrt2 b$, fixing the width convention used in the comparison. Thus the spatial standard deviation is multiplied by $\sqrt2$, while its square, the variance parameter of that Gaussian shape, doubles. The supplement's description of twice the width is consistent with using the variance parameter as width.[^p3-wiring-source]
L0306: 
L0307: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = NO -->
L0308: Agreement with the Gaussian field predictions supports this random-summation account as a possible explanation. It does not uniquely recover synaptic weights from rate maps. Correlated inputs, a structured component added to many random contributions, plasticity, or recurrent processing could also produce approximately Gaussian spatial input under some conditions. The paper measures rate maps, rather than the relevant weights or subthreshold input directly. Its analyzed environments were familiar and fairly uniform. Stationarity may fail near landmarks, boundaries, rewards, or in changing tasks.[^p3-discussion]
L0309: <!-- vale ai-tells.StrawmanContrast = YES -->
L0310: 
L0311: <!-- vale ai-tells.NounString = NO -->
L0312: [Thresholding a smooth spatial Gaussian input predicts field arrangement, shape, and topology from a small set of parameters. Several comparisons with recordings support these predictions. Inferring a circuit mechanism from this agreement requires additional measurements or perturbations.]{.key-statement}
L0313: <!-- vale ai-tells.NounString = YES -->
L0314: 
L0315: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0316: [^p3-ec-source]: Mainali et al. (2025), *Universal statistics of hippocampal place fields across species and dimensionalities*, Neuron, 113, 1110–1120.e3, [doi:10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017), Figure 3F–H; STAR Methods, “Euler characteristics,” Equations 8–10, p. e3.
L0317: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0318: 
L0319: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0320: [^p3-taylor]: Taylor, J. E. (2006). *A Gaussian kinematic formula*. The Annals of Probability, 34(1), 122–158. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1214/009117905000000594); [author preprint](https://arxiv.org/pdf/math/0602545), Equation 1.4, Theorem 4.1, Section 5.1 and Equation 5.1. This chapter uses the signed critical-point and boundary-counting framework, then derives the Gaussian algebra for dimensions at most three and the face factors for a box, matching Mainali et al.'s Equations 8–10. It does not prove the underlying Morse or kinematic theorem.
L0321: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0322: 
L0323: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0324: [^p3-higher-count]: Mainali et al. (2025), *Universal statistics of hippocampal place fields across species and dimensionalities*, Neuron, 113, 1110–1120.e3, [doi:10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017), Supplemental Methods S3, Equations S16–S17, p. 12. These are high threshold, volume-dominated approximations.
L0325: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

L0329: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
