## FILE guide/06-synthesis.md | ch:SYNTHESIS
L0001: # Spatial maps and memory dynamics need separate tests {#ch:SYNTHESIS}
L0002: 
L0003: [A model can explain the shape of a neural code without explaining how that code is maintained. It can also explain fluctuations inside a circuit without uniquely locating the source of a measured behavior. Comparing the three papers requires keeping each claim with the mathematical or experimental test that supports it.]{.key-statement}
L0004: 
L0005: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0006: To connect them, we first separate the spatial map, the changing represented position, and the movement measured downstream. A short calculation then shows how a common position error can make cells with different tuning change together. Comparing that calculation with each paper's evidence and related studies identifies what a joint experiment would still need to measure.
L0007: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0008: 
L0009: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = NO -->
L0010: ## The three papers constrain maps, dynamics, and movement {#sec:SYNTHESIS-QUESTIONS}
L0011: <!-- vale ai-tells.CataphoricForecasting = YES -->
L0012: 
L0013: For a circuit representing position, three questions arise:
L0014: 
L0015: - **Spatial map:** What activity pattern corresponds to each position? The place-field model addresses this question.
L0016: - **Memory motion:** How does the represented position evolve when there is no corrective input? The persistent-activity model addresses this question.
L0017: - **Observed movement:** How does a downstream physical system transform that changing signal into an observable movement? The eye-drift model combines a candidate account of memory motion with a detailed account of this transformation.[^s-three]
L0018: 
L0019: The objects differ even when the word position appears in all three. In 2012, $\theta(t)$ locates a network state on its attractor. In 2022, the internal eye-position command and actual eye angle are separate quantities linked through motor neurons and mechanics. In 2025, $x$ locates an animal in an environment, and $h_i(x)$ is a spatial input function assigned to a cell. The Gaussian process does not update a remembered $x$ through time.
L0020: 
L0021: The [guide map](ch-persistence.html#fig-foundations-paper-map) gives a visual comparison of these three questions.
L0022: 
L0023: <div class="table-responsive">
L0024: 
L0025: {{block:tbl:SYNTHESIS-MODELS}}
L0026: 
L0027: </div>
L0028: 
L0029: The 2012 and 2022 connection is direct: neural noise can move a maintained eye-position command, which then drives muscles. The 2022 result provides evidence for a substantial shared source before the final motor neurons and shows that integrator diffusion is sufficient within the constructed model. It does not measure the circuit's Fisher information or test equality in the 2012 bound. The place-field model describes spatially irregular tuning curves. To connect those curves to memory dynamics, we must also specify how the represented position moves.
L0030: 
L0031: ## A shared position error changes rates according to their tuning slopes {#sec:SYNTHESIS-JOINT-CALCULATION}
L0032: 
L0033: Suppose a network maintains a one-dimensional estimate $x(t)$ and cell $i$ has a fixed expected-rate function $f_i(x)$. Its time-dependent expected rate is
L0034: 
L0035: $$
L0036: R_i(t)=f_i(x(t)).
L0037: $$
L0038: 
L0039: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0040: [Here $f_i$ describes spatial tuning and $x(t)$ describes dynamics. A source of time variation has entered even though $f_i$ is fixed. Actual spike counts add another layer of variability around the expected rate.]{.key-statement} This construction is a guide calculation connecting the papers' objects, not a model tested jointly by the three studies.
L0041: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0042: 
L0043: Consider a short interval of duration $t$ starting at $x_0$. Assume that the estimate's local displacement $\delta x=x(t)-x_0$ has mean zero and variance $2Dt$, and that it is small enough to linearize each rate curve. Taylor expansion gives
L0044: 
L0045: $$
L0046: f_i(x_0+\delta x)
L0047: =f_i(x_0)+f_i'(x_0)\delta x+O((\delta x)^2).
L0048: $$
L0049: 
L0050: To leading order, the change is $\delta R_i=f_i'(x_0)\delta x$. Multiplying the centered changes for neurons $i$ and $j$ and averaging yields
L0051: 
L0052: $$
L0053: \operatorname{Cov}(\delta R_i,\delta R_j)
L0054: \simeq f_i'(x_0)f_j'(x_0)\operatorname{Var}(\delta x)
L0055: =2Dt\,f_i'(x_0)f_j'(x_0).
L0056: $$
L0057: 
L0058: This covariance concerns expected-rate changes caused by the common represented displacement, conditional on the starting position. It is not a formula for every covariance in a spiking recurrent network. It neglects higher-order changes in tuning and additional private or shared fluctuations. Near a threshold or over a large spatial displacement, the linear approximation must be revisited.
L0059: 
L0060: <!-- vale Microsoft.HeadingColons = NO -->
L0061: ### Example: opposite rate changes from the same position error {#sec:SYNTHESIS-COVARIANCE-EXAMPLE}
L0062: <!-- vale Microsoft.HeadingColons = YES -->
L0063: 
L0064: <div class="publisher-worked-calculation publisher-fold-always">
L0065: 
L0066: **Calculation and figure**
L0067: 
L0068: Let the local slopes of two rate curves be $f_1'=2$ Hz/m and $f_2'=-1$ Hz/m. Suppose $D=0.01$ m²/s and the interval is 0.5 s. Then the variance of the common position displacement is $2Dt=0.01$ m². The contributions to rate variance and covariance are
L0069: 
L0070: $$
L0071: \operatorname{Var}(\delta R_1)=2^2(0.01)=0.04\ \mathrm{Hz}^2,
L0072: $$
L0073: 
L0074: $$
L0075: \operatorname{Var}(\delta R_2)=(-1)^2(0.01)=0.01\ \mathrm{Hz}^2,
L0076: \qquad
L0077: \operatorname{Cov}(\delta R_1,\delta R_2)=(2)(-1)(0.01)
L0078: =-0.02\ \mathrm{Hz}^2.
L0079: $$
L0080: 
L0081: With only this one source, the two rate changes have correlation $-1$: one rises whenever the other falls. Adding independent private noise lowers the magnitude. [The negative covariance here comes from opposite tuning slopes under a common position change. It does not establish an inhibitory connection between the cells.]{.key-statement}
L0082: 
L0083: For the same slopes, set both expected rates to 10 Hz at the starting position. The upper plot shows the fixed rate curves. The lower plot shows their changes under a common position displacement.
L0084: 
L0085: <div class="figure-scroll">
L0086: 
L0087: {{block:fig:SYNTHESIS-SHARED-POSITION}}
L0088: 
L0089: </div>
L0090: 
L0091: Both changes are set by the same position displacement, so they occupy one direction in the two-rate plane. Independent private fluctuations would spread them away from that line.
L0092: 
L0093: </div>
L0094: 
L0095: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0096: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0097: [Irregular single-cell responses can therefore coexist with population changes along just one direction. To test whether a shared position error causes that pattern, one must measure how the rate changes align with the tuning curves. Field shapes alone do not supply that evidence.]{.key-statement}
L0098: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0099: <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0100: 
L0101: ## Each model needs a test matched to its claim {#sec:SYNTHESIS-EVIDENCE}
L0102: 
L0103: The shared-position calculation predicts how rates would change together. Establishing that connection requires measurements of those changes as well as the spatial maps. Each paper tests a different part of the proposed connection:
L0104: 
L0105: - **Place-field statistics:** A model that matches one distribution may have enough adjustable parameters to do so for several different reasons. The place-field paper gains support by fitting arrangement parameters and then comparing additional features, including slices and shape statistics. The separate firing-rate multiplier used in some comparisons should remain visible when counting how many quantities were predicted without further fitting. A successful high-threshold formula is also a statement about its limiting assumptions. Finite-threshold simulations and the original recordings provide different kinds of checks.
L0106: 
L0107:   <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeCarries = NO -->
L0108:   <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0109: - **Eye drift:** The eye paper calibrates a movement-to-firing relationship, inverts it, and compares the resulting single-neuron displacement predictions with measured displacement. Shared covariance then carries information that independent private fluctuations cannot supply under the decomposition's assumptions. The additional MSD comparison tests the time structure: increasing stationary motor-noise amplitude cannot by itself reproduce the accumulating upstream signal. Calibration validity, source correlations, two animals, and horizontal movement remain part of the result's scope.
L0110:   <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0111:   <!-- vale ai-tells.FigurativeCarries = YES -->
L0112: 
L0113:   <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0114: - **Memory stability:** The 2012 inequality is a mathematical constraint rather than an experimental fit. Its derivation specifies what noise and dynamics it covers. An experimental comparison would need estimates of diffusion, the relevant time constants, and the appropriate instantaneous information rate, all under compatible conditions. Agreement with $1/N$ scaling alone would not test the entire inequality, since several mechanisms can share that scaling.
L0115:   <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0116: 
L0117: ## Related studies change assumptions and leave circuit choices open {#sec:SYNTHESIS-RELATED}
L0118: 
L0119: Related studies test the assumptions and ask questions the three papers leave open:
L0120: 
L0121: - **Integrating movement:** The eye-position account begins with Seung's line-attractor model. Aksay and colleagues then used recordings and perturbations in goldfish to constrain integrator circuitry. These establish the dynamical problem and experimental architecture behind the 2022 model. Neither supplies the macaque displacement-variance estimate. Burak and Fiete's earlier grid-cell study examines [[**path integration**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, updating a position estimate from movement.]{.definition-explanation} In its model, velocity drives recurrent population activity, and errors accumulate in the position estimate. Its accuracy ranges belong to the simulated networks and parameter choices, rather than all biological grid circuits.[^s-attractors]
L0122: 
L0123: - **Storage capacity and changing synapses:** Noise-driven storage also connects to memory capacity. Koyluoglu and colleagues ask how noise limits recall as the delay and number of stored items increase. Their comparison with human data shows why direct storage of several independent values need not capture observed behavior. Transformations before and after storage alter the performance prediction. Seeholzer and colleagues examine how short-term changes in synaptic strength affect diffusion and systematic drift. These papers change the storage assumptions rather than invalidate the 2012 calculation inside its original model.[^s-extensions]
L0124: 
L0125: - **Recordings from larger environments:** For place fields, Eliav and colleagues' long-tunnel recordings and Harland and colleagues' large-arena recordings supply the multiple-field, multiple-scale observations. Yartsev and Ulanovsky supply the three-dimensional bat recordings. The 2025 paper reanalyzes those existing data, so its agreement across dimensions is not three newly collected experiments under one protocol. The later study by Maimon and colleagues directly compares CA3 and CA1 in large tunnels: predominantly single fields in CA3 contrast with multiple fields in CA1, while field sizes are similar. That distinction supports treating the CA1 statistics and the upstream spatial circuit as related but separately constrained parts of the explanation.[^s-place-data]
L0126: 
L0127:   <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = NO -->
L0128: - **Connectivity and learning:** Gaussian spatial statistics do not identify a unique pattern of connections. Druckmann and colleagues report structured synaptic connectivity between hippocampal regions. Bittner and colleagues show how electrical events in [[**dendrite**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}s, the branching input structures of neurons,]{.definition-explanation} can induce changes in synaptic strength and form CA1 place fields. Structure and learning can therefore be present even when a random-function model describes many aggregate field statistics. The model's success leaves open which circuit mechanism produces these maps. Input, connectivity, learning, or perturbation measurements could distinguish the possibilities.[^s-wiring]
L0129:   <!-- vale ai-tells.FormalTransitions = YES -->
L0130: 
L0131: - **Benefits for perception:** The source and use of eye motion are different questions. Nghiem and colleagues combine human eye tracking with a model of how eye movement affects visual signals to explain why an intermediate amount of fixational motion can help fine visual discrimination. A movement that partly originates from internal noise can still have useful sensory consequences, and feedback can regulate its statistics. Benefit for perception does not uniquely identify the neural generator.[^s-acuity]
L0132: 
L0133: ## A joint test would compare tuning maps with population fluctuations {#sec:SYNTHESIS-NEXT}
L0134: 
L0135: [A joint test needs tuning maps and fluctuations from the same population.]{.key-statement} A possible sequence is:
L0136: 
L0137: 1. Estimate the tuning maps and their uncertainty.
L0138: 2. Measure fluctuations when external signals provide little correction of the represented position. Test whether their covariance aligns with the changes implied by the tuning maps.
L0139: 3. Fit diffusion only over a range where the local approximation and measurement corrections are justified.
L0140: 4. **If comparing behavior,** include the physical or neural filters between the representation and the measurement.
L0141: 
L0142: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0143: These measurements would test whether one system satisfies both spatial-statistics and dynamical-stability constraints. [A static Gaussian-process fit alone cannot supply the diffusion coefficient. A diffusion fit alone cannot establish the distribution of fields.]{.key-statement} Agreement with both would constrain the model more than either fit alone. A mismatch would require further tests of spatial input statistics, recurrent maintenance, the observation filter, or their coupling. This is proposed follow-up work, not a replication claimed by this guide.
L0144: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0145: 
L0146: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->

[DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109), Eqs. 1–5; Ben-Shushan, N., Shaham, N., Joshua, M., and Burak, Y. (2022). *Fixational drift is driven by diffusive dynamics in central neural circuitry*. Nature Communications, 13, 1697. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y), Figs. 2–5; Mainali, N., Azeredo da Silveira, R., and Burak, Y. (2025). *Universal statistics of hippocampal place fields across species and dimensionalities*. Neuron, 113(7), 1110–1120.e3. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017), Eq. 1 and Figs. 1–5.

# PART: Neural memory in electronic hardware
Electronic circuits can store connection settings, changing neuron states, and values represented by a network. Von Neumann (1958) motivates this comparison. Chapters 14 and 15 connect physical storage with Seung (1996) and Burak and Fiete (2012). Chapter 16 examines the separate contributions of selected hardware studies through October 30, 2026. Chapter 17 develops guide-derived experiments using the three original papers. Each contribution is attributed in the Part introduction.
<!-- vale ai-tells.SequencingMarkers = NO -->
<!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
> “Lastly, I would like to mention that systems of nerve cells, which stimulate each other in various possible cyclical ways, also constitute memories.”
<!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
<!-- vale ai-tells.SequencingMarkers = YES -->
John von Neumann, *The Computer and the Brain* (1958), [printed page 66, PDF page 78](https://vpb.smallyu.net/%5BAuthor%5D%20John.Von.Neumann/The%20Computer%20and%20The%20Brain.pdf#page=78).
Von Neumann compares mutually stimulating nerve cells with electronic circuits that preserve a state through feedback. In the following pages he also questions whether active elements alone could provide the nervous system's main large-capacity store. His comparison invites us to distinguish a memory's physical mechanism from the information it keeps. It does not establish that every brain memory uses persistent activity, or that present hardware follows one historical line from his book.
The setting here is experimental and computational neuroscience joined with electronic engineering. We ask how a silicon circuit can retain a quantity such as an angle, and what physical processes limit its accuracy. [A durable connection weight, a lasting activity pattern, and an accurately remembered value are three different achievements.]{.key-statement}
<!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
The path starts with what is stored and where, then explains what feedback preserves. With those distinctions in place, we compare digital spiking processors, analog neural circuits, and phase-change weight storage. The final chapter turns the comparison into measurements of drift, noise, decoding error, and energy. Parts I and II provide the neural models and experimental reasoning. This Part extends them to selected hardware research current to **October 30, 2026**.
<!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
For a first pass, read the physical explanations, main results, and interpretations. Worked calculations and deeper derivations are folded. The comparison assumes the calculus, basic probability, and linear algebra used earlier. Electronics terms are introduced as they become necessary.
The contribution labels describe the role of each result here. **Central** is a main finding of the cited paper. **Supporting** establishes or qualifies that finding. **Background** supplies needed concepts. **Guide-derived** identifies an explanation, comparison, or proposed test developed in this guide.
::: {.part-chapter-map}

- **[Chapter 14: A stored weight and a remembered value are different states](../chapters/ch-hardware-memory.html)**

  **Background and hardware mechanisms:** von Neumann (1958) motivates the comparison. [Pehle et al. (2022)](https://doi.org/10.3389/fnins.2022.795876), Intel Labs (2021), Scholze et al. (2026), and [Le Gallo et al. (2023)](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41928-023-01010-1) supply the architecture descriptions. Separating stored weights, changing neural states, and remembered values is the guide's organizing comparison.

- **[Chapter 15: Discrete restoration and continuous random wandering produce different memory errors](../chapters/ch-hardware-dynamics.html)**

  **Central neural distinction with supporting hardware examples:** [Seung (1996)](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.93.23.13339) and [Burak and Fiete (2012)](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109) explain graded persistence and noise. [Massoud and Horiuchi (2011)](https://doi.org/10.1109/TCSI.2010.2055310) and [Giulioni et al. (2012)](https://doi.org/10.3389/fnins.2011.00149) supply distinct silicon demonstrations. The reduced feedback and error comparisons are guide-derived.

- **[Chapter 16: Neuromorphic chips choose where physics performs the calculation](../chapters/ch-neuromorphic-systems.html)**

  **Central results of selected hardware studies:** [Kleyko et al. (2025)](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-55832-y) test temporal computation. [Aitsam et al. (2026)](https://doi.org/10.3389/fnins.2026.1910808) demonstrate persistent spatial activity. [Ortner et al. (2025)](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-56345-4) study adaptation with phase-change computing. [Wang et al. (2026)](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41563-025-02361-0) study material-state stability. [Sun et al. (2026)](https://doi.org/10.1038/s42256-026-01255-3) evaluate a proposed dual-memory design. These results concern different kinds of memory, as the chapter explains.

- **[Chapter 17: Memory experiments must separate device error from network dynamics](../chapters/ch-hardware-tests.html)**

  **Guide-derived experiments:** The proposed measurements combine the diffusion and readout questions of [Burak and Fiete (2012)](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109), the output-filtering lesson of [Ben-Shushan et al. (2022)](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y), and the spatial-versus-temporal distinction exposed by [Mainali et al. (2025)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017). They are suggested tests, not a reported common hardware experiment.

:::

## FILE guide/07-hardware-memory.md | ch:HARDWARE-MEMORY
L0001: # A stored weight and a remembered value are different states {#ch:HARDWARE-MEMORY}
L0002: 
L0003: A silicon circuit can keep a connection strength unchanged while losing the angle it was asked to remember. It can also keep that angle while its individual electrical signals change. To compare electronic memory with the neural memory in Part I, we first need to identify what information each physical quantity represents. This chapter separates the connection settings, the changing state of each artificial neuron, and the value represented by their joint activity. That separation will let us ask what feedback actually preserves.
L0004: 
L0005: ## A connection setting shapes activity. The activity can represent a cue {#sec:HARDWARE-MEMORY-ROLES}
L0006: 
L0007: Imagine giving a circuit a brief cue for an angle of $30^\circ$. After the cue ends, a connected group of artificial neurons remains active. We estimate the remembered angle from which neurons are active and how strongly. The experiment involves:
L0008: 
L0009: 1. **Connection weights** determine how a signal from one neuron changes another neuron's state. A weight can be fixed throughout the trial.
L0010: 2. **Neuron and synapse states** change during the trial. Examples include a voltage, a current left by recent inputs, or a number representing either quantity.
L0011: 3. **The remembered angle** is a property of the joint state, recovered by a readout rule. It need not occupy one dedicated storage cell.
L0012: 
L0013: [Keeping the weights unchanged preserves the circuit's rule of operation. Keeping the cue available requires the resulting activity to preserve information about that cue.]{.key-statement} Training can change the first object. Remembering a new angle can change the second and third without any further training.
L0014: 
L0015: [[**Neuromorphic computing**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} implements computations inspired by neural circuits using electronic systems organized around neurons, connections, events, or their dynamics.]{.definition-explanation} Some systems calculate neural states as digital numbers. Others use voltages and currents that evolve physically. A system can combine both approaches. The word alone does not tell us where its memory is stored or whether it demonstrates a biological memory mechanism.
L0016: 
L0017: [[**Working memory**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, in this Part, means keeping information about a recent input available for a later task.]{.definition-explanation} Persistent activity is one candidate mechanism. A slowly decaying state, a sequence of states, or an explicit digital buffer can also store recent information. We must specify the allowed delay and readout error before calling one implementation adequate.
L0018: 
L0019: <div class="publisher-worked-calculation publisher-fold-always">
L0020: 
L0021: **Example: the same weights can hold different angles.**
L0022: 
L0023: Suppose a ring of 32 artificial neurons has fixed local excitation and broader inhibition. A cue near neuron 3 creates a localized group of active neurons there. A later trial cues neuron 11 and creates a group near that location. The same connection weights support both trials. A decoder assigns an angle to the active group's center. If the group moves after the cue, the decoded angle changes even though every stored weight remains correct. This is an example constructed to illustrate the distinction. It is not a specification of a chip.
L0024: 
L0025: </div>
L0026: 
L0027: ## Bits, capacitor voltages, and material conductances store different physical states {#sec:HARDWARE-MEMORY-STORAGE}
L0028: 
L0029: The neural model still needs a physical implementation. If the model has a variable called membrane voltage, a chip may store it as a binary number or as an actual circuit voltage. Those choices expose the computation to different errors. [[**Current**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is electric charge flowing per unit time, measured in amperes (coulombs per second).]{.definition-explanation} [[**Voltage**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is an electrical energy difference per unit charge, measured in volts (joules per coulomb).]{.definition-explanation}
L0030: 
L0031: A [[**capacitor**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} stores separated electric charge. For fixed capacitance $C$, its stored charge $Q$ and voltage difference $V$ satisfy $Q=CV$.]{.definition-explanation} Charge is measured in coulombs and capacitance in farads (coulombs per volt). Supplying current changes $Q$, so it changes the stored voltage.
L0032: 
L0033: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0034: [[**Digital storage**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} uses distinguishable physical ranges to represent discrete symbols, usually bits.]{.definition-explanation} In [[**static random-access memory (SRAM)**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, powered transistor feedback maintains one of two states representing a bit.]{.definition-explanation} Several bits can encode a weight or a changing neuron variable. Reliable bits do not imply unlimited numerical precision: a finite word represents only finitely many values.
L0035: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0036: 
L0037: [[**Analog state**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} represents a model quantity through the magnitude of a physical variable, such as capacitor voltage.]{.definition-explanation} Input current changes stored charge and so changes voltage. Leakage changes that voltage during a quiet interval. Feedback or new input can oppose the leakage. In BrainScaleS-2, the membrane state evolves this way, while local SRAM stores the synaptic weights.[^hardware-pehle]
L0038: 
L0039: [[**Phase-change memory (PCM)**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} stores information in a material's atomic arrangement, including the proportions of ordered and disordered regions, which affect its electrical resistance.]{.definition-explanation} Programming pulses heat the material. Its heating and cooling history changes the arrangement. [[**Conductance**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} measures the current produced per unit applied voltage where current is approximately proportional to voltage. It is the inverse of resistance, the voltage required per unit current.]{.definition-explanation} Reading the conductance can recover a stored weight. Such storage is [[**nonvolatile**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}: it can retain its state without continuous power, within its retention limits.]{.definition-explanation} Nonvolatile weights can remain after the changing neural state has disappeared.
L0040: 
L0041: The following choices locate the stored quantity before we compare complete machines.
L0042: 
L0043: <div class="table-responsive">
L0044: 
L0045: {{block:tbl:HARDWARE-STORAGE}}
L0046: 
L0047: </div>
L0048: 
L0049: Here [[**device mismatch**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} means persistent differences between nominally identical circuit elements.]{.definition-explanation} It differs from fluctuations that vary from moment to moment. A fixed mismatch can create a repeatable bias. Changing noise can create a different trajectory on each trial. We will measure these separately.
L0050: 
L0051: ## The stored state matters through the update it produces {#sec:HARDWARE-MEMORY-UPDATES}
L0052: 
L0053: To understand a machine's memory, follow one input through it. On a digital spiking processor, an arriving event identifies connections, their weights contribute to inputs, and a program updates stored neuron variables. Those variables determine whether to send further events. Loihi 2 provides local digital state and weight memory with programmable neuron and learning rules. SpiNNaker2 uses many processor cores whose software updates neural states stored with weights in local SRAM. Their event communication does not make every model variable evolve continuously in physical time.[^hardware-intel][^hardware-spinnaker]
L0054: 
L0055: In an analog neural circuit, an input can directly supply current to a capacitor. Transistors implement leakage and other responses as the voltage evolves. Digital circuits may still route the events and store the settings. [[**Mixed-signal hardware**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} combines analog circuit variables with digital storage, communication, or calculation.]{.definition-explanation} The division of labor, rather than the label, determines which dynamics are physical and which are calculated.
L0056: 
L0057: PCM supplies a third use of physics. At a fixed, sufficiently small read voltage, a cell's current is approximately $I=GV$, where $I$ is current, $V$ is applied voltage, and $G$ is conductance, measured in siemens (amperes per volt). The stored conductance represents a weight. In Le Gallo and colleagues' chip, the duration of a read pulse represents the input magnitude. For one positive contribution with fixed $G$ and $V$, a pulse lasting $T$ seconds passes charge $Q\simeq GVT$. The contribution is proportional to the product of weight and input duration. Currents from several cells add, and the chip converts the combined current over time to digital values. Signed inputs and weights require additional polarity handling beyond this simple example.[^hardware-legallo]
L0058: 
L0059: [[**In-memory computing**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} performs some operations where the values are stored, reducing the need to transfer them to a separate arithmetic unit.]{.definition-explanation} The 64-core PCM chip demonstrates this approach to neural-network inference, with digital processing also part of the system. Its weighted response results from both the stored material state and the applied input signal.
L0060: 
L0061: [A circuit's storage medium tells us how a value is retained between updates. Its update rule tells us how that value affects subsequent computation. Both are needed to explain memory.]{.key-statement} We can now examine why feedback can preserve a bit yet let a graded estimate wander.
L0062: 
L0063: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0064: [^hardware-pehle]: Pehle, C., et al. (2022). *The BrainScaleS-2 accelerated neuromorphic system with hybrid plasticity*. Frontiers in Neuroscience, 16, 795876. [Sections 2.1–2.3](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fnins.2022.795876/full). [DOI](https://doi.org/10.3389/fnins.2022.795876).
L0065: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0066: 
L0067: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0068: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0069: [^hardware-intel]: Intel Labs. (2021). *Taking neuromorphic computing to the next level with Loihi 2*. Technology brief, pp. 5–7. [PDF](https://www.intel.com/content/dam/www/central-libraries/us/en/documents/neuromorphic-computing-loihi-2-brief.pdf#page=5). This is an architecture description, not a current availability or performance comparison.
L0070: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0071: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0072: 
L0073: <!-- vale ai-tells.OverusedVocabulary = NO -->
L0074: [^hardware-spinnaker]: Scholze, S., et al. (2026). *The SpiNNaker2 chip: A many-core platform for flexible and scalable brain-inspired computing*. arXiv:2607.24396v1, July 27, 2026. [Section 4.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.24396v1#S4.SS4). Preprint. The inspected version places demonstrated SNN states and weights in SRAM.
L0075: <!-- vale ai-tells.OverusedVocabulary = YES -->
L0076: 
L0077: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0078: [^hardware-legallo]: Le Gallo, M., et al. (2023). *A 64-core mixed-signal in-memory compute chip based on phase-change memory for deep neural network inference*. Nature Electronics, 6, 680–693. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41928-023-01010-1). [Author manuscript, Sections I–II](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2212.02872#page=2). Detailed implementation descriptions refer to that manuscript version.
L0079: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->

  kind: table
  placement: sec:HARDWARE-MEMORY-STORAGE
  headers:
  - Implementation
  - Quantity representing information
  - Example role
  - Error relevant to that role
  rows:
  - - Digital memory word
    - A pattern of bits
    - Connection weight or current neuron state

  - - Capacitor in an analog neuron

    - Changing membrane or synaptic state
    - Leakage, physical noise, or unequal circuit parameters
  - - PCM cell

  - - Recurrent network

## FILE guide/08-hardware-dynamics.md | ch:HARDWARE-DYNAMICS
L0001: # Discrete restoration and continuous random wandering produce different memory errors {#ch:HARDWARE-DYNAMICS}
L0002: 
L0003: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0004: A circuit can remain active for seconds even when its individual voltages decay in milliseconds. The explanation is feedback: activity supplies new input before old input disappears. But long-lived activity does not by itself identify what is being preserved. This chapter first explains how feedback extends a time scale, then distinguishes a restoring discrete memory from a freely changing continuous one. Measured silicon circuits show why this distinction matters beyond a mathematical model.
L0005: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0006: 
L0007: ## Feedback can extend persistence beyond a component's decay time {#sec:HARDWARE-DYNAMICS-FEEDBACK}
L0008: 
L0009: **Physical mechanism.** An isolated capacitor loses a voltage difference when charge leaks through a resistance. In a recurrent circuit, its output can cause an input current that replaces some of that charge. The competition between loss and replacement sets the decay of the collective state. For a neural population, the same competition occurs between decay of synaptic input and input supplied by recurrent spikes.
L0010: 
L0011: Let $u$ be the amplitude of one small activity disturbance around a reference state. A simple linear model is
L0012: 
L0013: $$
L0014: \tau_s\frac{du}{dt}=-u+g u+I(t).
L0015: $$
L0016: 
L0017: Here $\tau_s$ is the component decay time, $g$ is dimensionless recurrent feedback gain, and $I(t)$ is external drive expressed in the same units as $u$. The term $-u$ removes activity, and $gu$ replaces a fraction of that loss. After the cue ends, $I=0$. For $g<1$,
L0018: 
L0019: $$
L0020: u(t)=u(0)e^{-t/\tau_{\rm eff}},\qquad
L0021: \tau_{\rm eff}=\frac{\tau_s}{1-g}.
L0022: $$
L0023: 
L0024: **Interpretation.** Raising $g$ toward one slows forgetting. At $g=1$, this ideal linear mode retains every amplitude. For $g>1$, it grows until effects omitted from the linear model become important. [Long persistence requires a balance of physical processes. It is not determined by the storage element's decay time alone.]{.key-statement}
L0025: 
L0026: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Derivation: feedback reduces the net decay rate"}
L0027: 
L0028: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0029: With no external input, divide the differential equation by $\tau_s$: $du/dt=-(1-g)u/\tau_s$. Multiplying by $e^{(1-g)t/\tau_s}$ makes the derivative of $u e^{(1-g)t/\tau_s}$ zero. Its value is the initial amplitude $u(0)$. Comparing the resulting exponential with $e^{-t/\tau_{\rm eff}}$ gives the effective time constant above. This is one linear mode near a chosen state, not a full transistor or spiking-network model.
L0030: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0031: 
L0032: :::
L0033: 
L0034: <div class="publisher-worked-calculation publisher-fold-always">
L0035: 
L0036: **Example: a small gain error changes a long memory time.**
L0037: 
L0038: Choose $\tau_s=10$ ms. Gain $g=0.99$ gives $\tau_{\rm eff}=1$ s. Changing the gain to $0.98$ reduces that time to $0.5$ s. Changing it to $0.995$ increases it to $2$ s. The component decay time is unchanged in these three cases. The example predicts sensitivity to adjusting the feedback strength, not a measured lifetime on any chip.
L0039: 
L0040: </div>
L0041: 
L0042: The next issue is which disturbances the feedback corrects. Retaining any amplitude requires a different response from restoring one chosen amplitude.
L0043: 
L0044: <!-- vale ai-tells.StackedAnaphora = NO -->
L0045: ## A bit corrects small displacements. A continuous memory preserves some of them {#sec:HARDWARE-DYNAMICS-ERRORS}
L0046: <!-- vale ai-tells.StackedAnaphora = YES -->
L0047: 
L0048: **Physical mechanism.** In a two-state electronic memory, feedback pushes a slightly disturbed state back toward the same stable state. A sufficiently large disturbance can switch it to the other state. In an ideal continuous attractor, disturbances away from the family of memory states decay, but a displacement along that family represents another valid value. The circuit has no restoring signal telling it which earlier value to recover. Seung used this distinction to compare discrete electronic memory with graded eye-position memory.[^dynamics-seung]
L0049: 
L0050: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0051: This is a distinction between dynamics, not a division between all digital and all analog machines. Analog transistors can maintain discrete bits. Digital processors can calculate a continuous-state neural model to finite precision. Stored bits may remain correct while repeated numerical updates produce a wandering decoded angle.
L0052: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0053: 
L0054: **Mathematical comparison.** Let $e$ measure a local displacement from one stable state. If restoration has strength $\kappa>0$ and random perturbations have diffusion coefficient $D$, a reduced model is
L0055: 
L0056: $$
L0057: de=-\kappa e\,dt+\sqrt{2D}\,dW_t.
L0058: $$
L0059: 
L0060: The Brownian increment $dW_t$ has variance $dt$. Here $\kappa$ has units of inverse time and $D$ has units of $e^2$/time. Starting exactly at the stable state,
L0061: 
L0062: $$
L0063: \operatorname{Var}(e(t))=\frac{D}{\kappa}\bigl(1-e^{-2\kappa t}\bigr).
L0064: $$
L0065: 
L0066: For a neutral memory direction with $\kappa=0$, the corresponding result is $\operatorname{Var}(e(t))=2Dt$. Restoration limits small local fluctuations. A neutral direction accumulates them.
L0067: 
L0068: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Derivation: restoring feedback limits local spreading"}
L0069: 
L0070: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = NO -->
L0071: The linear stochastic equation has solution $e(t)=\sqrt{2D}\int_0^t e^{-\kappa(t-s)}dW_s$ when $e(0)=0$. Independent Brownian increments make its variance the integral of the squared weighting function: $2D\int_0^t e^{-2\kappa(t-s)}ds=D(1-e^{-2\kappa t})/\kappa$. As $\kappa$ tends to zero, expanding the exponential gives $2Dt$. This is a local model inside one stable state's region of attraction. It does not describe rare switches between digital states or estimate a hardware bit-error rate.
L0072: <!-- vale ai-tells.FillerPhrases = YES -->
L0073: 
L0074: :::
L0075: 
L0076: **Interpretation.** Adding restoration to an angle memory can reduce random spread by drawing nearby angles toward preferred positions. That also reduces the set of distinct values retained. [[**Pinning**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} means that imperfections or imposed structure draw a nominally continuous memory toward preferred states.]{.definition-explanation} A flat error variance can mean successful retention near one preferred value while other cues have been biased toward it. Both mean error and spread are needed.
L0077: 
L0078: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0079: [Burak and Fiete's diffusion bound concerns a specified noisy neural memory model. It is not a lower bound on the error of every electronic memory.]{.key-statement} A digital device that freezes a number, uses explicit error correction, or changes the noise and readout assumptions poses a different problem. To transfer the bound, match the dynamics, noise, available observations, and time scale first.[^dynamics-burak]
L0080: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0081: 
L0082: ## Silicon experiments distinguish discrete persistence from graded representation {#sec:HARDWARE-DYNAMICS-EVIDENCE}
L0083: 
L0084: A physical demonstration lets us test whether feedback maintains the intended state despite unequal devices and finite resolution. The following early experiments make different claims and should be read separately.
L0085: 
L0086: Massoud and Horiuchi built a head-direction circuit whose localized activity could persist after a cue and move with velocity input. It had 32 bump positions around a circle, a spacing of $11.25^\circ$. This demonstrates a physical orientation representation with finite resolution. It does not establish an exact continuum of persistent states or a measured Brownian diffusion law.[^dynamics-massoud]
L0087: 
L0088: Giulioni and colleagues built a recurrent spiking circuit with low and high activity states. Brief stimulation could initiate activity lasting seconds despite millisecond circuit dynamics. Their measurements also examined noise-driven transitions and recovery after disturbance. This is [[**bistability**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}: two locally stable states under the same ongoing conditions.]{.definition-explanation} It supplies a physical working-memory example, but its two-state retention is a different task from retaining an arbitrary angle.[^dynamics-giulioni]
L0089: 
L0090: The comparison gives a useful reading rule for current machines: determine what values the network distinguishes, then ask how long and how accurately it preserves them. Neuron count or nonvolatile weight storage cannot answer those questions alone.
L0091: 
L0092: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0093: [^dynamics-seung]: Seung, H. S. (1996). *How the brain keeps the eyes still*. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 93(23), 13339–13344. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.93.23.13339). [Discussion and nonlinear model, printed p. 13343](https://neurophysics.ucsd.edu/courses/physics_171/seung_pnas_line.pdf#page=6).
L0094: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0095: 
L0096: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0097: [^dynamics-burak]: Burak, Y., & Fiete, I. R. (2012). *Fundamental limits on persistent activity in networks of noisy neurons*. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, 109(43), 17645–17650. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109). See the guide's [information-bound derivation](ch-memory-information.html#sec-memory-bound) and its assumptions.
L0098: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0099: 
L0100: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0101: <!-- vale ai-tells.NounString = NO -->
L0102: [^dynamics-massoud]: Massoud, T. M., & Horiuchi, T. K. (2011). *A neuromorphic VLSI head direction cell system*. IEEE Transactions on Circuits and Systems I: Regular Papers, 58(1), 150–163. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1109/TCSI.2010.2055310). Sections V.F–V.G, Figure 21. [Author-posted manuscript](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/260666649_A_Neuromorphic_VLSI_Head_Direction_Cell_System).
L0103: <!-- vale ai-tells.NounString = YES -->
L0104: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0105: 
L0106: [^dynamics-giulioni]: Giulioni, M., Camilleri, P., Mattia, M., Dante, V., Braun, J., & Del Giudice, P. (2012). *Robust working memory in an asynchronously spiking neural network realized with neuromorphic VLSI*. Frontiers in Neuroscience, 5, 149. [Results and Methods](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fnins.2011.00149/full). Published February 2, 2012. The volume and DOI carry 2011.

## FILE guide/09-neuromorphic-systems.md | ch:NEUROMORPHIC-SYSTEMS
L0001: # Neuromorphic chips choose where physics performs the calculation {#ch:NEUROMORPHIC-SYSTEMS}
L0002: 
L0003: Suppose we want to study how a noisy network retains an angle. One machine can calculate every state update as arithmetic. Another can let capacitor voltages perform part of the dynamics. A third can use material conductances to multiply inputs by stored weights. The scientific choice is which physical effects we want to control, measure, or exploit. This chapter compares these implementations and then examines recent demonstrations of memory and learning.
L0004: 
L0005: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0006: The research snapshot is **October 30, 2026**. “State of the art” here means selected recent capabilities supported by primary papers, with older architecture papers supplying their physical explanation. It does not mean one machine leads every task. We distinguish measured chips, experiments partly run on a separate computer, and proposed hardware evaluated by simulation. A [[**host computer**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is a separate general-purpose computer that controls an experiment or performs calculations assigned outside the neuromorphic chip.]{.definition-explanation}
L0007: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0008: 
L0009: ## Digital and analog implementations expose different parts of the neural model {#sec:NEUROMORPHIC-SYSTEMS-ARCHITECTURES}
L0010: 
L0011: A useful platform must make the variables of interest accessible. If the question concerns a specific noise law, a digital update may allow precise control of the injected noise. If the question concerns physical circuit variability, analog dynamics expose that variability directly. Interpreting either choice as a biological result requires a matched model and experiment. [[**Hardware calibration**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} adjusts circuit settings to make measured device responses match requested responses more closely.]{.definition-explanation}
L0012: 
L0013: <div class="table-responsive">
L0014: 
L0015: {{block:tbl:NEUROMORPHIC-ARCHITECTURES}}
L0016: 
L0017: </div>
L0018: 
L0019: The first two rows describe digital neural computation, while BrainScaleS-2 directly implements parts of the differential equations in analog circuitry. Its dynamics run roughly a thousand times faster than the biological model time. That acceleration makes long model intervals accessible quickly, but communication and measurement delays still occur in physical time.[^systems-architecture]
L0020: 
L0021: [[**On-chip learning**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} means that circuitry on the chip computes and applies some weight changes.]{.definition-explanation} It is distinct from loading previously trained weights. Loihi 2 offers programmable learning rules. SpiNNaker2's 2026 technical preprint reports an on-chip learning implementation. BrainScaleS-2 combines local measurements with programmable processors that update weights. An experiment may still use weights trained beforehand or learning performed on a separate computer, so its learning setup must be checked.[^systems-learning]
L0022: 
L0023: ## Recent results improve temporal computation, adaptation, and device stability {#sec:NEUROMORPHIC-SYSTEMS-ADVANCES}
L0024: 
L0025: The following studies make progress on different parts of the memory problem. For each, ask what information persists and which physical component is responsible.
L0026: 
L0027: **A useful input history on Loihi 2, 2025.** Kleyko and colleagues implemented a stored history of recent inputs and functions of those inputs that include products. A trained readout uses these values to predict an output. This shows that changing chip state can supply useful information about recent inputs. It is not a measurement of indefinitely stable cue retention. Their pictured Hala Point system is much larger than the resources used in the experiment.[^systems-kleyko]
L0028: 
L0029: **Persistent spatial activity on SpiNNaker 2, September 2026.** Aitsam and colleagues ran a 768-neuron recurrent sheet on one chip. A host computer processed the physical event camera's output into a smoothed estimate of the target's center and sent that position to the chip. The configured neural update used deterministic integer-weight dynamics, without an added stochastic neural-noise term. During an eight-second interval without input, the decoded activity center moved by at most half a cell. This establishes persistence for that interval, not a measured lifetime or a test of noise-driven diffusion. During tracking, recurrence reduced frame-to-frame jitter by 15% relative to the host-smoothed input, but added about 115 ms of lag and increased mean position error from 2.02 to 5.65 cells. The robot-guidance evaluation used a simulated robot, and its power figures were estimates. Longer-delay tests with controlled noise would help compare this implementation with the stochastic memory theory.[^systems-aitsam]
L0030: 
L0031: **Rapid adaptation using PCM, 2025.** Ortner and colleagues used physical PCM weights for a recurrent spiking network that produced robotic-arm commands after a task-specific update. Earlier training adjusted the model for rapid adaptation to new tasks. The PCM units performed matrix-vector products. Other operations ran on a host computer. This locates the demonstrated advance in weight adaptation and mixed hardware computation. It does not show that PCM cells themselves sustained an autonomous recurrent activity pattern.[^systems-ortner]
L0032: 
L0033: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = NO -->
L0034: **Reduced material drift, online 2025 and in a 2026 issue.** Wang and colleagues measured low resistance drift in a chromium-telluride phase-change material, CrTe$_3$. Reduced drift can help a programmed conductance continue representing the intended weight. Their demonstration of several programmed resistance levels used laser programming and electrical readout. Electrical switching was a separate demonstration. Operation with several levels in nanoscale devices, performance beyond a million programming cycles, and variability across large arrays remained open. This is progress in a storage material, not a complete multilevel neuromorphic processor.[^systems-wang]
L0035: <!-- vale ai-tells.ContrastiveFormulas = YES -->
L0036: 
L0037: [Better temporal state, faster adaptation, and more stable weights solve different problems, so progress in one does not establish the others.]{.key-statement} For the Burak comparison, the missing measurement is often how a decoded value changes during an input-free delay.
L0038: 
L0039: ## Explicit slow memory is a current design choice, with distinct costs {#sec:NEUROMORPHIC-SYSTEMS-LIMITS}
L0040: 
L0041: Fast spiking units can lose earlier inputs before a later decision needs them. One solution is to add a slower state that summarizes recent activity and sends input back to the fast units. Sun and colleagues' 2026 dual-memory-pathway design takes this route. Their tested slow pathway uses a linear state based on a Legendre memory unit: several stored components summarize the shape of recent input over a sliding time window. It represents a recent input history, rather than a cue held unchanged on a continuous attractor. Its proposed digital hardware holds successive slow states in [[**registers**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, small, fast digital storage locations]{.definition-explanation}, and other variables in SRAM. The hardware results came from post-layout simulations, which model a circuit after its physical layout has been designed. They were not measurements of a fabricated chip. The weights were trained before the evaluated tasks.[^systems-sun]
L0042: 
L0043: A model with one stored number illustrates gradual forgetting without reproducing that richer input history. Let $z_k$ be current input to a stored quantity $m_k$:
L0044: 
L0045: $$
L0046: m_{k+1}=\alpha m_k+\beta z_k,\qquad 0<\alpha<1.
L0047: $$
L0048: 
L0049: Here $k$ counts updates, $\alpha$ is the retained fraction, and $\beta$ converts input units into state units. After input stops, $m_{k+n}=\alpha^n m_k$. The state remembers earlier input through its present value, while gradually forgetting it. This model illustrates slow storage. It is not the full equations or an independent performance test of Sun and colleagues' architecture.
L0050: 
L0051: <div class="publisher-worked-calculation publisher-fold-always">
L0052: 
L0053: **Example: the update interval determines the physical memory time.**
L0054: 
L0055: If updates are separated by $\Delta t$, equating $\alpha^n$ with $e^{-n\Delta t/\tau_m}$ gives $\tau_m=-\Delta t/\ln\alpha$. With $\Delta t=1$ ms and $\alpha=0.999$, $\tau_m$ is about 1 second. Executing the same updates a thousand times faster reduces that physical decay time to about 1 millisecond, unless the mapping between model time and wall time is changed. A number described as “slow” must be specified relative to other processes and the task clock.
L0056: 
L0057: </div>
L0058: 
L0059: Scaling a network also adds communication constraints. Ilmberger and Schemmel's 2025 preprint measured communication in a four-chip BrainScaleS-2 prototype. The timing matters because its analog states continue changing while messages are transmitted. A small delay in wall time can be a substantial delay in accelerated model time.[^systems-multichip]
L0060: 
L0061: The comparison needs a specified task, permitted error, delay, and system boundary. Energy for a matrix operation omits state maintenance, communication, conversion, and host work when those are outside the measured unit. The next chapter turns these qualifications into an experiment that can compare the machines on the same memory question.
L0062: 
L0063: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = NO -->
L0064: <!-- vale ai-tells.SemicolonUsage = NO -->
L0065: [^systems-architecture]: Intel Labs (2021), *Taking neuromorphic computing to the next level with Loihi 2*, [pp. 5–7](https://www.intel.com/content/dam/www/central-libraries/us/en/documents/neuromorphic-computing-loihi-2-brief.pdf#page=5); Scholze et al. (2026), *The SpiNNaker2 chip*, [version 1, Section 4.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2607.24396v1#S4.SS4); Pehle et al. (2022), *The BrainScaleS-2 accelerated neuromorphic system with hybrid plasticity*, [Sections 2.1–2.3](https://doi.org/10.3389/fnins.2022.795876); Le Gallo et al. (2023), *A 64-core mixed-signal in-memory compute chip*, [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41928-023-01010-1). Full entries are in [References](../appendices/ch-references.html).
L0066: <!-- vale ai-tells.SemicolonUsage = YES -->
L0067: <!-- vale ai-tells.EmDashUsage = YES -->
L0068: 
L0069: <!-- vale ai-tells.SemicolonUsage = NO -->

These describe capabilities and the reported demonstrations, not one common learning benchmark.
L0071: <!-- vale ai-tells.SemicolonUsage = YES -->

L0076:

  kind: table
  placement: sec:NEUROMORPHIC-SYSTEMS-ARCHITECTURES
  headers:
  - System
  - Where weights and changing states reside
  - How the neural state changes
  - Consequence for a memory experiment
  rows:
  - - Loihi 2
    - Local digital memory holds weights and neuron variables
    - Programmable, finite-precision updates; events communicate between units
    - Separate the chosen noise model and numerical precision from physical bit reliability
  - - SpiNNaker2
    - Demonstrated SNN workloads use local SRAM for parameters, weights, and states
    - Processor software executes time-stepped neuron updates
    - Check the update interval and whether the mapped workload meets its timing deadlines
  - - BrainScaleS-2
    - Local 6-bit SRAM weights; physical analog membrane states
    - Currents change capacitor voltages continuously
    - Measure remaining differences between responses after calibration, and relate
      chip time to model time
  - - PCM computing systems
    - Conductances store weights; other states depend on the surrounding design
    - Electrical reads form weighted sums; analog or digital circuits perform remaining
      operations
    - Locate the recurrent state and host work before claiming a physical memory circuit
  caption: Where selected neuromorphic systems store and update their variables.

## FILE guide/10-hardware-tests.md | ch:HARDWARE-TESTS
L0001: # Memory experiments must separate device error from network dynamics {#ch:HARDWARE-TESTS}
L0002: 
L0003: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = NO -->
L0004: After a circuit receives an angle cue, its later estimate may be wrong because the state wandered, because unequal connections pull it toward another angle, or because the observer reads it poorly. One accuracy score combines these causes. The controlled tests below connect these errors to physical parameters. The protocol and reduced models below are proposed tools for reasoning about these experiments, not results already demonstrated on every platform in the previous chapter.
L0005: <!-- vale ai-tells.VerbTricolon = YES -->
L0006: 
L0007: ## Repeated cue-and-delay trials separate drift from random spreading {#sec:HARDWARE-TESTS-PROTOCOL}
L0008: 
L0009: Begin with a circuit whose joint activity represents an angle. Choose a decoder on separate calibration data, then keep it fixed while testing retention. A [[**decoder**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is a specified rule for estimating the represented quantity from observable circuit signals.]{.definition-explanation} A decoder given every internal state has a different task from a downstream neuron receiving only spikes.
L0010: 
L0011: Use the following sequence:
L0012: 
L0013: 1. Present a brief cue for angle $\theta_0$, then remove it. Test many angles, including values between any suspected preferred positions.
L0014: 2. Hold the connection weights fixed during each retention trial. Record the actual settings, temperature, update interval, and any imposed noise.
L0015: 3. Repeat trials at several delays $t$ and decode $\hat\theta(t)$. Use a common reference for angular differences. [[**Unwrapping**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} tracks crossings of $0^\circ/360^\circ$ by adding or subtracting full turns to continue the trajectory.]{.definition-explanation} Use it only when sampling is frequent enough to identify crossings reliably.
L0016: 4. Report mean error and error variance separately as functions of cue and delay. Also report failures in which the activity pattern disappears or changes form.
L0017: 5. Repeat with changed noise, calibration, or numerical precision, changing one factor at a time. Compare with the same model running in a numerical reference implementation.
L0018: 
L0019: Record the decoded state immediately after each cue so that error in writing the value can be separated from error accumulated during retention. Report the number of repeated trials and uncertainty in the estimated drift and diffusion.
L0020: 
L0021: [An active circuit has demonstrated useful memory only when its later signals distinguish the earlier cues at the required accuracy.]{.key-statement} High firing rate alone does not establish this. Nor does a small average error if positive and negative errors cancel.
L0022: 
L0023: For short intervals, a useful local model is
L0024: 
L0025: $$
L0026: d\theta=b(\theta)dt+\sqrt{2D(\theta)}\,dW_t.
L0027: $$
L0028: 
L0029: Here $b(\theta)$ is systematic angular motion per unit time and $D(\theta)$ is half the local variance growth rate. With nearly constant coefficients over a short lag $\Delta t$, repeated trials near the same angle give $\mathbb E[\Delta\theta]\simeq b\Delta t$ and $\operatorname{Var}(\Delta\theta)\simeq2D\Delta t$. Correlated perturbations, preferred states, or boundaries can invalidate that short-lag fit. At long delays, report the distribution of angular errors around the circle. Values separated by a full turn represent the same angle, so a local unwrapped variance cannot be extended indefinitely as a measure of angular error.
L0030: 
L0031: Estimate readout noise before interpreting short-lag variance as motion of the stored value. Independent errors with constant variance in two position readings add a constant offset to the measured displacement variance.
L0032: 
L0033: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Measurement check: readout noise can look like state motion"}
L0034: 
L0035: Suppose each position estimate contains independent, zero-mean measurement error with variance $\sigma_m^2$. The difference of two estimates then adds $2\sigma_m^2$ to displacement variance, even when the circuit does not move. For independent constant-$D$ state motion, the measured short-lag displacement variance is $2D\Delta t+2\sigma_m^2$. A nonzero intercept can reveal this contribution. Correlated readout errors, overlapping observation windows, or a state that changes during the readout require a fuller model. This is the same type of separation needed when interpreting the eye-drift measurements.
L0036: 
L0037: :::
L0038: 
L0039: ## Physical parameters act through decay, bias, and noise projection {#sec:HARDWARE-TESTS-PARAMETERS}
L0040: 
L0041: A changed component affects memory through the circuit's equations. Increasing a leak current can shorten a component time constant. Changing a recurrent weight can alter restoration or introduce a preferred angle. A fluctuating current can perturb many neurons, yet only part of the resulting activity change remains as a change of the remembered angle.
L0042: 
L0043: The [projection derived in Part I](ch-memory-dynamics.html#sec-memory-linearization) makes the last statement precise. Let the vector $\mathbf r$ contain the network's activity variables, with local dynamics $d\mathbf r=\mathbf f(\mathbf r)dt+B\,d\mathbf W_t$. The matrix $B$ maps independent Brownian disturbances into those variables. Near a family $\mathbf r_*(\theta)$ of steady states, let $\boldsymbol\ell$ be the left neutral vector, the object called $\mathbf v$ in Part I, normalized so that $\boldsymbol\ell^T d\mathbf r_*/d\theta=1$. It converts an activity disturbance into its surviving angular displacement. The leading local approximation gives
L0044: 
L0045: $$
L0046: D(\theta)=\tfrac12\boldsymbol\ell^TBB^T\boldsymbol\ell.
L0047: $$
L0048: 
L0049: [Noise matters through both its size and the directions in which it pushes the circuit.]{.key-statement} Large disturbances in rapidly corrected directions can matter less than small disturbances along the memory direction. In a general network the left neutral vector differs from the visible tangent to the family. Using the tangent alone can predict the wrong sensitivity. Curvature and state-dependent noise can also contribute drift beyond this local approximation.
L0050: 
L0051: Here $B$ represents fluctuations that vary across time and trials, including noise deliberately added to a digital update or measured fluctuations in an analog circuit. A fixed device mismatch instead changes the deterministic dynamics $\mathbf f$ and can create repeatable bias or preferred angles. A configured deterministic neural rule has no stochastic term unless one is supplied; that statement does not make the physical digital chip free of timing variation or hardware errors.
L0052: 
L0053: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Derivation: project circuit fluctuations onto the remembered angle"}
L0054: 
L0055: Write $\delta\theta\simeq\boldsymbol\ell^T\delta\mathbf r$ in the local linear regime. For the neutral mode, $\boldsymbol\ell^T$ times the linearized deterministic drift is zero. The stochastic increment is $d\theta\simeq\boldsymbol\ell^TB\,d\mathbf W_t$. Since $\operatorname{Cov}(d\mathbf W_t)=I\,dt$, its variance is $\boldsymbol\ell^TBB^T\boldsymbol\ell\,dt$. Equating this to $2D\,dt$ gives the displayed coefficient. The normalization ensures that the result measures angular displacement rather than an arbitrarily scaled activity coordinate. This calculation transfers only after the hardware's local dynamics and noise assumptions have been checked.
L0056: 
L0057: :::
L0058: 
L0059: The following changes suggest tests under the model assumptions above.
L0060: 
L0061: <div class="table-responsive">
L0062: 
L0063: {{block:tbl:HARDWARE-TEST-PARAMETERS}}
L0064: 
L0065: </div>
L0066: 
L0067: <div class="publisher-worked-calculation publisher-fold-always">
L0068: 
L0069: **Example: a tighter distribution can hide a biased memory.**
L0070: 
L0071: Consider two illustrative circuits after a one-second delay. Circuit A has zero mean angular error and variance $4$ deg$^2$. Circuit B has mean error $3^\circ$ and variance $1$ deg$^2$, because it is attracted toward a preferred angle. Their mean squared errors are $4$ and $1+3^2=10$ deg$^2$. Circuit B has less random spread but worse cue retention. Reporting only its smaller variance would hide the effect of pinning.
L0072: 
L0073: </div>
L0074: 
L0075: ## Matching time and readout is necessary to compare memory accuracy {#sec:HARDWARE-TESTS-COMPARISON}
L0076: 
L0077: The source papers supply different constraints on this hardware experiment.
L0078: 
L0079: - **Burak and Fiete (2012):** compare diffusion with the information available in spikes under matched noise, dynamics, and observation assumptions. A processor that stores an exact internal angle and exposes it directly to the decoder has changed the readout problem.
L0080: - **Ben-Shushan and colleagues (2022):** a measured output can filter the internal command. Camera exposure, output smoothing, or a mechanical actuator can change the observed motion. Calibrate that path before assigning an output time scale to the memory circuit itself.
L0081: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = NO -->
L0082: - **Mainali and colleagues (2025):** patterns of firing across physical positions constrain a spatial representation. Reproducing field-size statistics does not by itself show that a network implemented in hardware retains position without input. Spatial structure and temporal persistence need separate tests.
L0083: <!-- vale ai-tells.ParallelStaccato = YES -->
L0084: 
L0085: These links concern the tested mechanisms, not a claim that a chip reproduces the animal systems. The [synthesis chapter](ch-synthesis.html#sec-synthesis-evidence) sets out the evidence supplied by the original papers.
L0086: 
L0087: Time conversion is especially important for accelerated hardware. If model time $t_m=a t_p$, where $t_p$ is physical time and $a>1$ is the acceleration factor, a diffusion law $\operatorname{Var}(\Delta\theta)=2D_m t_m$ becomes $2(aD_m)t_p$. The result is $D_p=aD_m$ when expressed per physical second. This is a change of units for the same ideal trajectory. Physical communication delay, temperature drift, or limited readout speed can prevent a real device from obeying that ideal rescaling.
L0088: 
L0089: For an energy comparison, specify the cue distribution, delay, permitted decoding error, and failed-trial rate. Measure energy over writing, waiting, and reading, including required host processing and interfaces. Then report how error and energy change as delay increases. An operations-per-second or operations-per-watt figure alone cannot establish the cost of retaining a useful value.
L0090: 
L0091: The resulting research question is concrete: **which part of the physical implementation limits the accuracy of a remembered quantity, and does changing that part improve retention under the same task and readout?** That question turns von Neumann's comparison into a test of contemporary neural and electronic memory mechanisms.

SIDECAR BLOCKS (reader-visible content; metadata like width is for interpretation only):

  - Controlled change
  - Physical or computational action
  - Quantity to measure
  rows:
  - - Increase feedback toward balance with leakage
    - Reduces net decay in a stable mode
    - Persistence time, then instability or saturation if gain is excessive
  - - Inject stronger noise with its pattern unchanged
    - Increases $BB^T$ in specified directions
    - Growth of decoded error variance
  - - Change fixed weights or calibration
    - Alters the deterministic dynamics $\mathbf f$
    - Mean drift versus cue and preferred resting angles
  - - Reduce numerical precision
    - Restricts representable states and updates

      small updates
  - - Let PCM weights age between trials
