# PART: Simulations connect model assumptions to measurable outcomes
[Burak and Fiete (2012)](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109) test memory diffusion in spiking networks. [Ben-Shushan et al. (2022)](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y) connect a simulated neural command to eye motion. [Mainali et al. (2025)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017) generate spatial maps and compare their fields with recordings. This Part explains the methods, measurements, and software behind those studies, then gives a guide-derived route for checking a reproduction.
A formula predicts behavior under stated assumptions. A simulation lets us generate the resulting neural activity, eye motion, or spatial map and measure it in the same way as a theoretical prediction or experiment. The useful question is what each computation adds to the evidence, and which physical assumptions determine its output.
The path starts with how recurrent spikes move a stored value. It then adds the motor pathway and measurement procedure needed to compare an internal command with behavior. The spatial-field study changes the independent variable from time to position. With those differences established, the final chapter explains how to choose software and check numerical resolution.
Numerical settings for each paper and implementation cautions are folded. The main text keeps the modeled objects, actions, measurements, and limits visible. Blue definitions introduce simulation terms. Basic expectation, variance, standard deviation, and probability density are assumed.
The contribution labels describe the role of each result here. **Central** is a main finding of the cited paper. **Supporting** establishes or qualifies that finding. **Background** supplies needed concepts. **Guide-derived** identifies an explanation, comparison, or proposed test developed in this guide.
::: {.part-chapter-map}

- **[Chapter 18: Network simulations test how random spikes move a stored value](../chapters/ch-simulating-memory.html)**

  **Supporting simulations of a central theory:** [Burak and Fiete (2012)](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109) compare stochastic network trajectories with their analytical diffusion prediction. The 2017 correction applies to Eq. 2. The simulations test the modeled dynamics, rather than measure memory errors in animals.

- **[Chapter 19: Eye simulations connect a noisy neural command to measured motion](../chapters/ch-simulating-eye.html)**

  **Model support for a central explanation:** [Ben-Shushan et al. (2022)](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y) combine recorded-data analysis with a noisy integrator, motor neurons, muscles, and delayed feedback. Aksay et al. (2007) supply supporting circuit architecture. The generated signals test whether that mechanism can reproduce the measured statistics.

- **[Chapter 20: Random spatial maps test the statistics of place fields](../chapters/ch-simulating-place.html)**

  **Central model comparisons:** [Mainali et al. (2025)](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017) generate thresholded Gaussian spatial maps, fit selected summaries, and test additional field properties. Original animal recordings supply the comparison data. These are static-map simulations rather than recurrent memory dynamics.

- **[Chapter 21: Reproduction depends on the model, numerical method, and measurement](../chapters/ch-simulation-tools.html)**

  **Guide-derived methods and tool comparison:** The shared reproduction procedure is assembled here from the three studies. Author code is distinguished from optional numerical tools. No new full-paper reproduction is claimed.

:::

## FILE guide/11-simulating-memory.md | ch:SIMULATING-MEMORY
L0001: # Network simulations test how random spikes move a stored value {#ch:SIMULATING-MEMORY}
L0002: 
L0003: To compare a simulated memory trace with Burak and Fiete's (2012) theory, the numerical update and the measurement of the stored value must match the model. The [foundation chapter](ch-foundations.html#sec-foundations-simulation) explains the spike-update cycle, and the [long-delay chapter](ch-memory-long-delays.html) interprets its diffusion tests. Here the additional task is to recover the published network settings, distinguish a numerical position measurement from the theoretical coordinate, and identify choices left open for an independent reproduction.
L0004: 
L0005: ## A simulation renews the same synaptic state that the theory approximates {#sec:SIMULATING-MEMORY-STATE}
L0006: 
L0007: The physical idea is continuing neural activity after a cue has ended. A spike briefly increases synaptic activation. That activation decays, but it also changes the rates at which other neurons fire. Recurrent connections can therefore renew the activity. Random event times disturb the pattern that represents the remembered value.
L0008: 
L0009: The computer stores each neuron's synaptic activation $s_i$. The recurrent input $g_i$ and firing rate $r_i$ are calculated from it:
L0010: 
L0011: $$
L0012: g_i=\sum_j W_{ij}s_j+b_i,\qquad r_i=\phi(g_i).
L0013: $$
L0014: 
L0015: Here $W_{ij}$ is the connection from neuron $j$ to neuron $i$, $b_i$ is a constant input, and $\phi$ converts total input into a rate in spikes per second. In the paper's normalization, each spike increases $s_i$ by one and activation decays with time constant $\tau$. The [earlier neural mechanism and derivation](ch-foundations.html#sec-memory-spikes) explain these variables and the continuous-time equations.[^simmem-model]
L0016: 
L0017: One short simulation step has a causal order:
L0018: 
L0019: 1. Read the current activations and calculate the recurrent inputs and firing rates.
L0020: 2. Generate spikes using those rates and the chosen time interval.
L0021: 3. Apply synaptic decay and the increments caused by those spikes.
L0022: 4. Decode the represented value and repeat after the external cue is absent.
L0023: 
L0024: The ring simulation uses the [Euler update introduced in Chapter 1](ch-foundations.html#sec-foundations-simulation): approximate a variable's change over a short interval by its current derivative times that interval. For decay alone, an interval $h$ changes $s_i$ to approximately $s_i(1-h/\tau)$. Spike increments are added according to the event model. This finite step approximates the continuous dynamics, so its accuracy must be checked at the resolution needed for the reported statistic.
L0025: 
L0026: [Each generated spike changes the recurrent inputs and can change the rates of later spikes.]{.key-statement} The theory explains when these interacting changes can be described by diffusion of one coordinate.
L0027: 
L0028: ## Ring trajectories test both the diffusion rate and the effect of angular boundaries {#sec:SIMULATING-MEMORY-RING}
L0029: 
L0030: In the ring model, neurons are ordered by preferred angle, and the position of their localized activity pattern represents the stored angle. The [earlier Figure 2 discussion](ch-memory-long-delays.html) explains the diffusion and information comparison. To reproduce that comparison, keep the exponential and sigmoid transfer functions as separate runs, with a theoretical prediction calculated from each run's steady tuning curves.[^simmem-ring]
L0031: 
L0032: The comparison separates prediction from measurement. First, solve the noise-free network for its steady activity pattern and calculate the predicted diffusion coefficient and information rate. Then run the stochastic network and measure its angular displacement. For this simulation the authors record the preferred angle of the neuron with the largest instantaneous rate. This is a numerical proxy for the represented position. The theoretical coordinate $\theta$ is assigned by following noise-free relaxation back to the attractor; the largest-rate rule does not carry out that projection. Its output can take only one of the neurons' preferred angles, and brief changes in the pattern's shape can shift its maximum during relaxation. These effects matter when interpreting very short lags. This rule also differs from the ideal spike-based estimator studied later in the paper.
L0033: 
L0034: At short lags, the displacement variance tests the local relation $\operatorname{Var}(\Delta\theta)\simeq2D\ell$, where $D$ has units of angle squared per second and $\ell$ is the lag in seconds. At long lags, the reported differences are wrapped into $(-\pi,\pi)$, so the mean square levels off. The long-lag comparison uses a diffusion calculation with periodic boundaries. This saturation limits the reported angular error. It does not recover the initial angle.
L0035: 
L0036: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Published settings: the ring network in Figure 2"}
L0037: 
L0038: The Methods specify 1,024 neurons, synaptic time constant 10 ms, and update interval 0.1 ms. Each step generates a spike with probability $r_i h$. This one-event approximation requires that probability to remain small. There are 100 trials of 1,000 seconds each. The displacement analysis excludes the first 10 ms. Error bars are standard errors across trials.
L0039: 
L0040: Writing $\alpha$ for the separation of preferred angles, the finite network uses
L0041: 
L0042: $$
L0043: w(\alpha)=e^{\cos\alpha-1}-e^{0.3(\cos\alpha-1)}.
L0044: $$
L0045: 
L0046: The common input is $b=-2$. The two transfer rules are $\tau\phi(g)=10e^g$ and $\tau\phi(g)=0.2[1+\tanh(g+4)]$. Initial activations are independently uniform between 0 and 0.01. These settings describe the finite simulation. The supporting text separately divides a connection rule by $N$ to study larger populations. Applying that extra factor here would change the reported weights.
L0047: 
L0048: The symmetric-network prediction uses the squared input slope in its numerator. Burak and Fiete's 2017 correction confirms that square, which was missing in the supplied Early Edition's Eq. 2. The correction does not change these simulation settings.[^simmem-correction]
L0049: 
L0050: :::
L0051: 
L0052: ## Other network designs test which conclusions depend on the ring {#sec:SIMULATING-MEMORY-CONTROLS}
L0053: 
L0054: A second network contains two mutually inhibiting units, interpreted also as two neural populations. Increasing one population's activity while decreasing the other's changes the stored value. Figure 2E–G tests diffusion in this family without relying on rotational symmetry. The diffusion calculation applies to both models because each has a continuous family of states and restoring dynamics toward that family.[^simmem-two]
L0055: 
L0056: The Methods report cross-couplings of $-1$, no self-coupling, common input $b=5{,}000$, time constant 100 ms, update interval 0.4 ms, and duration 200 seconds. The transfer rule is $\tau r_i=g_i$ in the valid positive-rate range. These large rates can represent aggregate population activity. They should not be read as ordinary firing rates of two biological cells.
L0057: 
L0058: Converting between activation and rate changes the units of the stored value. Let $\theta_s=s_1-s_2$ be the activation difference and $\theta_r=r_1-r_2$ the rate difference along the steady-state family. Since $s_i=\tau r_i$ there, $\theta_r=\theta_s/\tau$ and their diffusion coefficients satisfy $D_r=D_s/\tau^2$. Their units differ, so their numerical values should not be compared without this conversion. A population can also emit several spikes within one update. The ring's at-most-one-spike rule cannot be copied into this setting. The paper specifies a Poisson process but does not say how spike counts were drawn or give the complete initial state for this example.
L0059: 
L0060: The supporting text also tests a mutually excitatory pair in Figure S1. Across 4,000 trials started at the same coordinate, its variance grows linearly even though the local diffusion coefficient depends on the state. The guide's [long-delay chapter](ch-memory-long-delays.html#sec-memory-long-time) explains the coordinate and the printed factor-of-two discrepancy. That discrepancy is separate from the authors' 2017 correction.
L0061: 
L0062: ## A plotted analytical curve is different from a network simulation {#sec:SIMULATING-MEMORY-OUTPUTS}
L0063: 
L0064: Figures 3 and S2 plot formulas for readout error. They compare an ideal estimator with an estimator that weights all observations equally as the observation interval grows. The sources do not describe an additional spiking-network experiment or a biological circuit implementing ideal estimation in these figures.
L0065: 
L0066: The paper specifies its network equations and several numerical settings, but does not identify a programming language or simulator package. No public simulation-code archive was identified in the checked paper, supplement, or author publication entries. This describes the search as of October 30, 2026. It is not proof that no code exists. A new implementation must document choices the paper leaves open, including how it generates random numbers and events for aggregate populations.
L0067: 
L0068: [The simulations show that the stated network models can produce the predicted memory errors. Connecting those errors to animal behavior requires a measurement model and biological data.]{.key-statement} Ben-Shushan et al. (2022) add those elements by following an internal eye-position command through motor neurons and muscles to measured motion.
L0069: 
L0070: [^simmem-model]: Burak, Y., & Fiete, I. R. (2012). *Fundamental limits on persistent activity in networks of noisy neurons*. PNAS, 109(43), 17645–17650. [Model equations 7–11, PDF pp. 5–6](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=5); [Supporting Text, Sections I–II](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=1). [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1117386109).
L0071: 
L0072: [^simmem-ring]: Burak and Fiete (2012), [Figure 2A–D, PDF p. 3](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=3), [Network Simulations, PDF p. 6](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=6), and [Supporting Text, Eq. S32](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=8).
L0073: 
L0074: [^simmem-correction]: Burak, Y., & Fiete, I. R. (2017). *Correction for Burak and Fiete, Fundamental limits on persistent activity in networks of noisy neurons*. [Published correction to Eq. 2](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5441759/). [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1706051114).
L0075: 
L0076: [^simmem-two]: Burak and Fiete (2012), [Figure 2E–G and caption, PDF p. 3](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=3), [mutual-inhibition simulation, PDF p. 6](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity.pdf#page=6), and [Supporting Text, Figure S1, PDF p. 10](../papers/burak-fiete-2012-persistent-activity-si.pdf#page=10).

SIDECAR BLOCKS (reader-visible content; metadata like width is for interpretation only):
chapter_id: ch:SIMULATING-MEMORY
blocks: []


## FILE guide/12-simulating-eye-drift.md | ch:SIMULATING-EYE
L0001: # Eye simulations connect a noisy neural command to measured motion {#ch:SIMULATING-EYE}
L0002: 
L0003: A reproduction of Ben-Shushan et al. (2022) must distinguish the recording analysis from the simulated eye-control pathway. The [circuit chapter](ch-eye-circuit.html) explains why a noisy internal command is a possible source of drift. Here the added detail is how the published parameters, spike generator, update intervals, saved samples, and MATLAB files determine the numerical experiment, and which code or data gaps limit reproduction. The pathway below gives the mechanism needed to interpret those choices.
L0004: 
L0005: ## Recorded data constrain the question before the model generates a trajectory {#sec:SIMULATING-EYE-DATA}
L0006: 
L0007: The empirical analysis uses earlier recordings of 57 ocular motor neurons from two macaques, paired with eye-position measurements. These are separate single-cell recordings, not a simultaneous recording of all 57 neurons. The authors fit each cell's firing-rate relation to eye position, velocity, and acceleration. For firing-rate prediction, they fit all trials except one and predict the firing rate in the omitted trial, repeating this leave-one-out procedure across trials. The analysis also uses the fitted movement relation to reconstruct motion from that cell's spikes.[^simeye-data]
L0008: 
L0009: After excluding rapid eye movements, the analysis compares measured and reconstructed displacements over 350 ms segments. Their covariance supports a substantial shared drive upstream of the motor neurons. The estimate near 82% depends on calibrated gains and the assumed separation of shared and private fluctuations. It is not a measurement of noise inside the integrator itself.
L0010: 
L0011: The authors then test whether an integrator with plausible cellular properties can generate enough shared motion, with the right timing, after the motor pathway filters it. Its tuning distributions and spike variability are constrained by earlier physiological studies. Some remaining choices are adjusted to compare with the measured drift. The paper notes that several parameter combinations can match its magnitude.
L0012: 
L0013: ## The generated command acts through spikes, muscle response, and delayed feedback {#sec:SIMULATING-EYE-PATH}
L0014: 
L0015: The model has four connected stages. Each stage changes a different physical or represented quantity:
L0016: 
L0017: 1. **Integrator activity:** two opposing neural populations sustain a represented eye-position command. Spikes renew decaying synaptic activity, and recurrent input sets later firing rates. A weighted difference between the populations produces the command $E_{\mathrm{OI}}$.
L0018: 2. **Motor-neuron spikes:** each simulated motor neuron receives that command. Its tuning slope and threshold convert the commanded angle into a firing rate. Spike generation adds that neuron's private variability.
L0019: 3. **Muscle and eye response:** filtered motor activity drives a mechanical response with position, velocity, and acceleration terms. Combining the motor contributions gives the eye angle $E$.
L0020: 4. **Delayed visual feedback:** the difference between an earlier eye position and the current internal command changes the integrator's input. New changes in actual eye position enter this feedback after the delay, while the current internal-command term acts immediately.[^simeye-model]
L0021: 
L0022: [The internal command and the physical eye angle are separate outputs. The muscles smooth rapid command fluctuations, so the eye's short-lag motion need not have the same statistics as the neural state.]{.key-statement} The [eye-timing chapter](ch-eye-timing.html) derives a simplified filtering example. The [circuit chapter](ch-eye-circuit.html) explains the full model's neural assumptions.
L0023: 
L0024: The circuit structure is adapted from Aksay et al. (2007), with tuning and noise assumptions adjusted to primate evidence. Its weights are fitted so that a range of commands can persist in the mean-rate model. This is different from fitting an eye-drift trace.
L0025: 
L0026: The spikes are more regular than a Poisson process. The model generates candidate events at $M$ times the desired rate and retains every $M$th event. At constant rate, each retained interval sums $M$ exponential waiting times, giving interval coefficient of variation $1/\sqrt M$. This preserves the mean firing rate while reducing interval variability. The published simulations use integrator variability around 0.22 and motor-neuron variability around 0.07.
L0027: 
L0028: Simulated spike times are drawn by a program that generates a repeatable sequence of random-looking numbers. A [[**random seed**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} sets its starting state, allowing the same implementation and sequence of calls to reproduce those samples.]{.definition-explanation} The spike-generation rule specifies the intended distribution; the seed chooses the sample sequence.
L0029: 
L0030: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Published parameters and public-code settings have different sources"}
L0031: 
L0032: The final main Methods report an integrator size $N=30{,}000$, 1,000 motor neurons, synaptic time constant 20 ms, and feedback strength $A=0.015$. The feedback delay is about 70 ms. These are the main model settings, not values to combine with the different comparison runs in the supplement.
L0033: 
L0034: The inspected MATLAB code sets its internal update interval to $10^{-5}$ s and saves every 100 steps, giving 1 ms output samples. It uses a 10 ms motor-neuron synaptic time constant, initializes central gaze, and sets the random seed from the trajectory index. Synaptic decay is updated by first-order Euler steps. The mechanical calculation updates velocity and then position.
L0035: 
L0036: The code's `N` creates a vector in each of the paired populations, whereas the paper describes $N$ as the network's neuron number. That convention must be resolved before claiming a function call reproduces the published size. The code also uses a different motor-neuron variability regression from the final printed Eq. 35.[^simeye-code]
L0037: 
L0038: :::
L0039: 
L0040: ## Controls test which stage can explain the observed fluctuations {#sec:SIMULATING-EYE-CONTROLS}
L0041: 
L0042: One matching trajectory is weak evidence because many mechanisms can look irregular. The paper compares outputs after changing sources of variability or feedback:
L0043: 
L0044: - **Change the noise source:** compare a fixed command with private motor noise (Figure 3), the noisy internal command before muscle filtering (Figure 4), and the complete pathway (Figure 5). The [circuit chapter](ch-eye-circuit.html) explains these comparisons. Keep the measured signal explicit: internal command and final eye angle are different outputs.
L0045: - **Check the measurement procedure:** apply the recording reconstruction and covariance analysis to simulated motor spikes and eye motion. This tests recovery of the shared contribution where its origin is known. Muscle filtering and delayed feedback must remain in the complete-pathway comparison.[^simeye-figures]
L0046: 
L0047: The supplement adds checks of measurement-noise subtraction, feedback removal, and the contribution of direct output spiking noise. In the last check, noise still drives the internal integrator, so removing motor-output noise does not turn the entire model deterministic.
L0048: 
L0049: These controls support the integrator as a sufficient explanation under the tested assumptions. They do not show that it is the only central circuit capable of producing the recordings. The shared-source estimate from data and the proposed location of that source remain different conclusions.
L0050: 
L0051: ## Public MATLAB code and recordings support different reproduction tasks {#sec:SIMULATING-EYE-TOOLS}
L0052: 
L0053: The authors release MATLAB `.m` functions and `.mat` parameter files. `Run_model.m` starts trajectories and their mean-squared-displacement analysis. `Full_model_simulation.m` updates the integrator, motor neurons, and eye. `Fit_OI_circuit.m` fits a persistent mean-rate circuit, while the analysis routines compare generated trajectories with an already processed empirical MSD file.[^simeye-code]
L0054: 
L0055: The separately deposited raw recordings contain one spreadsheet per cell and one sheet per trial. The columns give horizontal and vertical eye angles and spike times. Eye samples are spaced by 1 ms. Reanalyzing those recordings tests the empirical reconstruction. Running the model tests a proposed source of the motion. Loading the processed MSD file alone does not reproduce the raw-data analysis.[^simeye-deposit]
L0056: 
L0057: The inspected repository does not specify a MATLAB version or a complete environment. Source calls include constrained least squares, random sampling, and correlation routines, whose required functions and toolboxes must be available. The repository's `rate` branch calls a function absent from the inspected file tree. These are practical limits to check before treating the package as a complete figure-reproduction workflow. No such end-to-end run is claimed here.
L0058: 
L0059: For a neurophysics study, the useful next calculation is controlled: vary one cellular or mechanical assumption, keep the measurement procedure fixed, and examine which observed statistic changes. Mainali et al. (2025) use a different form of this reasoning. Their simulations change spatial maps and measure the regions where cells would fire, without simulating eye motion or a time-dependent memory circuit.
L0060: 
L0061: [^simeye-data]: Ben-Shushan, N., Shaham, N., Joshua, M., & Burak, Y. (2022). *Fixational drift is driven by diffusive dynamics in central neural circuitry*. Nature Communications, 13, 1697. [Behavioral task, fitting, and displacement analysis, PDF pp. 9–10](../papers/ben-shushan-2022-fixational-drift.pdf#page=9). [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-022-29201-y).
L0062: 
L0063: [^simeye-model]: Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), [Methods, Eqs. 23–35, PDF pp. 10–11](../papers/ben-shushan-2022-fixational-drift.pdf#page=10). The model adapts the circuit in Aksay et al. (2007), *Functional dissection of circuitry in a neural integrator*, Nature Neuroscience, 10, 494–504. [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1038/nn1877).
L0064: 
L0065: [^simeye-code]: The Burak Lab. (2022). *Fixational drift is driven by diffusive dynamics in central neural circuitry* [MATLAB source code]. [Inspected revision, February 28, 2022](https://github.com/The-Burak-lab/Fixational-drift-is-driven-by-diffusive-dynamics-in-central-neuralcircuitry/tree/27d4093008c6756fe8ae2ad77567176ed626bd17). Code inspection, not execution, checked October 30, 2026.
L0066: 
L0067: [^simeye-figures]: Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), [Figures 3–5, PDF pp. 6–8](../papers/ben-shushan-2022-fixational-drift.pdf#page=6); [supplementary Figures 2–5 and 10, PDF pp. 8–10 and 14](../papers/ben-shushan-2022-supplement-1.pdf#page=8).
L0068: 
L0069: [^simeye-deposit]: Ben-Shushan, N., Shaham, N., Joshua, M., & Burak, Y. (2022). *Fixational drift is driven by central neural circuitry* [Dataset]. Zenodo. [Recorded eye positions and spike times](https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.6076911).

## FILE guide/13-simulating-place-fields.md | ch:SIMULATING-PLACE
L0001: # Random spatial maps test the statistics of place fields {#ch:SIMULATING-PLACE}
L0002: 
L0003: To reproduce Mainali et al.'s (2025) place-field comparisons, we need the rules for generating and fitting model maps, and for measuring both model and recorded maps. The [spatial-model chapter](ch-place-fields.html) explains the statistical assumptions, and the [tests chapter](ch-place-tests.html) interprets the evidence. Here the additional detail is the Python map-generation procedure, filter and boundary conventions, fitted summaries, field detectors, and access to code and recordings. Position is the independent variable; each generated map is fixed during analysis, so these computations do not simulate a remembered position changing over time.
L0004: 
L0005: ## A smooth random input becomes a firing map after thresholding {#sec:SIMULATING-PLACE-GENERATE}
L0006: 
L0007: For each model cell, the theory assigns a smooth Gaussian input field $h(\mathbf x)$ to locations $\mathbf x$. Its covariance controls how similar nearby input values tend to be. A threshold determines which locations activate the cell. In the guide's notation, the rate is proportional to
L0008: 
L0009: $$
L0010: \max\{h(\mathbf x)-q\sqrt{C(0)},0\}.
L0011: $$
L0012: 
L0013: Here $q$ is the dimensionless threshold, $C(0)$ is the input variance, and an overall gain converts positive excess input into a rate. The threshold and spatial correlation scale govern the shapes and sizes of the active regions. The gain supplies the firing-rate scale. The [spatial-model chapter](ch-place-fields.html) develops these quantities before deriving field-count and gap predictions.[^simplace-model]
L0014: 
L0015: The published Python archive constructs such maps directly:
L0016: 
L0017: 1. Draw independent normally distributed values at the spatial grid points for each cell.
L0018: 2. Smooth the array with a [[**Gaussian filter**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, which averages nearby values with weights that decrease with distance.]{.definition-explanation} Neighboring outputs share inputs, making them correlated.
L0019: 3. Divide each map by the square root of the mean of its squared grid values, its root mean square. This sets its sampled root mean square to one.
L0020: 4. Subtract the threshold and replace negative values with zero.
L0021: 5. Measure the connected nonzero regions as fields.
L0022: 
L0023: This procedure implements the spatial model without explicitly simulating presynaptic neurons or their synapses. The paper's random-input argument explains one possible origin of a Gaussian field. It is not a simulated recurrent hippocampal circuit in this code. [Agreement with place-field statistics tests the spatial description, while the proposed wiring remains an interpretation requiring further evidence.]{.key-statement}
L0024: 
L0025: Smoothing, amplitude normalization, and edge handling affect the finite sampled map. Matching the published implementation requires these numerical choices.[^simplace-code]
L0026: 
L0027: ## Fitted summaries and additional comparisons supply different evidence {#sec:SIMULATING-PLACE-FIT}
L0028: 
L0029: The authors choose threshold and spatial scale to match a small number of measured summaries. For the long 200 m bat tunnel, they match mean field length and mean gap. In the short 6 m tunnel, they use mean field length and occupied fraction, because many cells have only one field. The 2D comparison fits mean field size and count, while the 3D comparison uses mean volume and occupied fraction.[^simplace-fit]
L0030: 
L0031: A [[**fitted statistic**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is a measured summary used to choose a model's parameters.]{.definition-explanation} Recovering that statistic checks the fit, but gives less independent evidence than successfully describing other features. The full size distribution, lower-dimensional slices, multiple peaks, and holes in fields provide further comparisons. Some of those analyses also fit a firing-rate gain, which must remain visible when judging how independent a test is.
L0032: 
L0033: The paper compares simulated size distributions with a fitted lognormal alternative using likelihood-based criteria. This is a comparison on the available data, rather than a reported test on a separate held-out dataset. Its high-threshold analytical laws also have a different role from finite-threshold simulation: the simulations can examine regimes where those limiting formulas are only approximate.
L0034: 
L0035: For uncertainty in size histograms, the Methods describe $10^4$ model realizations, each containing the experimental number of cells. The resulting spread reflects variation between model samples of that size. It does not include every source of recording error or uncertainty about the biological model.
L0036: 
L0037: ## Field detection and preprocessing change what the simulation is asked to match {#sec:SIMULATING-PLACE-MEASURE}
L0038: 
L0039: To compare a generated field with a recorded one, both must be measured in compatible ways. A small positive noise value can connect two regions, while thresholding can split them. Smoothing can remove a peak or fill a small hole.
L0040: 
L0041: The STAR Methods specify several distinct measurements:
L0042: 
L0043: - **Lengths and gaps in 1D:** count consecutive nonzero or zero bins and convert their counts to physical lengths. The archived implementation places field edges at grid-bin boundaries.
L0044: - **Volumes in 3D:** label connected nonzero components and multiply their bin counts by bin volume. The reported analysis removes fields more than three standard deviations above the mean volume as probable mergers.
L0045: - **Peak and boundary shape:** use peak detection and local finite differences, with additional smoothing or rate thresholds stated for the relevant comparison.
L0046: - **Connectedness and holes:** use the Gudhi Python library to calculate Betti numbers and form their alternating sum, the Euler characteristic. The [place-field tests chapter](ch-place-tests.html) explains why this statistic contains information absent from field volumes alone.[^simplace-measure]
L0047: 
L0048: The experimental 3D maps have 10 cm spatial bins and receive a median filter in the described processing. Different analyses use different cutoffs. A reproduction therefore needs the rule for its target panel, rather than one generic threshold applied to every figure.
L0049: 
L0050: Figures 1 and 2 connect the generated maps to size, count, gap, and slice statistics. Figure 3 tests shapes and connectedness. Figures 4 and 5 examine how fitted parameters and threshold changes relate to field structure. The supplement checks alternative spherical fields, cell-to-cell threshold variation, and sensitivity to processing choices. These comparisons extend the evidence beyond a visually similar random map.
L0051: 
L0052: ::: {.publisher-clarification-note .publisher-fold-always title="Width and edge conventions must be checked before copying parameters"}
L0053: 
L0054: The paper defines its local correlation scale by $\ell=\sqrt{-C(0)/C''(0)}$ in one dimension. The code also uses a parameter called `sigma`, but as the width $b$ of the Gaussian smoothing filter. For ideal continuous white noise smoothed by a Gaussian of width $b$, the covariance is proportional to $\exp[-x^2/(4b^2)]$. Differentiating at zero gives $\ell=\sqrt2\,b$. The two widths are therefore different quantities.
L0055: 
L0056: This conversion follows from the smoothing construction. It is not another fitted biological result. Finite sampling and filter truncation can modify the ideal relation. The archived 1D generator uses reflected edges, whereas its 3D generator wraps the filter around the array edges. The boundary rule used for smoothing also does not, by itself, specify the boundary rule for a later topology calculation.
L0057: 
L0058: The README's dummy-data settings illustrate package use. They are not a complete list of the grid, cell count, and random seed used for each published panel. Exact reproduction needs those panel-specific choices as well as the archive.
L0059: 
L0060: :::
L0061: 
L0062: ## The released tools generate maps and analyze data, with access limits {#sec:SIMULATING-PLACE-TOOLS}
L0063: 
L0064: The paper reports using Python 3.11. The code uses NumPy for arrays and random samples, and SciPy for smoothing and map measurements. The Methods identify SciPy routines for connected components, peak detection, and density estimation, and Gudhi for topology. These are spatial and statistical computations. A spiking-neuron simulator is not required by the released method.
L0065: 
L0066: The published code is archived on Zenodo, with an accompanying public GitHub repository. The inspected archive contains source files and dependency information. It does not contain all recording data or a complete set of per-figure run records. A current repository example therefore cannot automatically establish the settings used in the 2025 publication.[^simplace-code]
L0067: 
L0068: The article points to public source datasets, including the bat-tunnel deposit. It also acknowledges directly shared full bat maps and a 20-cell sample of rat maps used for particular analyses. Access to every required map must be checked for the intended figure. Availability of the model code is different from availability of all experimental inputs.[^simplace-data]
L0069: 
L0070: For a new study, two questions can now be separated. One is whether thresholded smooth random inputs describe the measured spatial patterns. The other is which neural dynamics, connections, and learning rules create those inputs. The simulations in this paper directly address the first and provide constraints for investigating the second.
L0071: 
L0072: [^simplace-model]: Mainali, N., Azeredo da Silveira, R., & Burak, Y. (2025). *Universal statistics of hippocampal place fields across species and dimensionalities*. Neuron, 113(7), 1110–1120.e3. [STAR Methods, Eqs. 2–4, PDF pp. 13–14](../papers/mainali-2025-universal-statistics.pdf#page=13); [Methods S2, supplement PDF pp. 10–11](../papers/mainali-2025-supplement.pdf#page=10). [DOI](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017).
L0073: 
L0074: [^simplace-code]: Mainali, N. (2025). *Gaussian process place-field model* [Python source code]. [Published code archive, version DOI](https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.14650881). [Repository revision inspected October 30, 2026](https://github.com/5183nischal/gp-model/tree/eae7eab801bb6ca016b3a6ff4f35d6a47df98d6a). The archive and selected source functions were inspected. The figure-generation runs were not executed.
L0075: 
L0076: [^simplace-fit]: Mainali et al. (2025), [STAR Methods, Parameter inference in 1D, 2D and 3D, PDF p. 14](../papers/mainali-2025-universal-statistics.pdf#page=14), and [Figures 1–5](../papers/mainali-2025-universal-statistics.pdf#page=4).
L0077: 
L0078: [^simplace-measure]: Mainali et al. (2025), [STAR Methods, Data processing, PDF pp. 14–15](../papers/mainali-2025-universal-statistics.pdf#page=14), and [supplementary Figures S1–S5](../papers/mainali-2025-supplement.pdf#page=2). The source specifies the processing rule for each analysis.
L0079: 
L0080: [^simplace-data]: Mainali et al. (2025), [resource availability and acknowledgments, PDF p. 10](../papers/mainali-2025-universal-statistics.pdf#page=10), and [key resources, PDF p. 13](../papers/mainali-2025-universal-statistics.pdf#page=13). The linked source dataset is Eliav et al. (2021), *Multiscale representation of very large environments in the hippocampus of flying bats*. [Public bat-tunnel data](https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.4646728).

## FILE guide/14-simulation-tools.md | ch:SIMULATION-TOOLS
L0001: # Reproduction depends on the model, numerical method, and measurement {#ch:SIMULATION-TOOLS}
L0002: 
L0003: A simulated eye trace can look convincing while answering the wrong question. It may use different spike statistics, a different muscle response, or a different definition of displacement from the experiment. Beyond the paper-specific methods in the preceding chapters, a reproduction needs checks that its software, resolution, and saved run record preserve the intended comparison. This chapter explains how to choose those checks and distinguish the authors' program from an independent implementation or a smaller teaching model.
L0004: 
L0005: ## The original code and a teaching reconstruction serve different purposes {#sec:SIMULATION-TOOLS-ROUTES}
L0006: 
L0007: The starting point depends on the intended result. Running the authors' program tests whether its implementation produces the reported output. Rebuilding the equations in another program tests whether an independent implementation agrees. A smaller teaching model can isolate a mechanism, but its numerical output belongs to that smaller model.
L0008: 
L0009: For Burak and Fiete (2012), the paper and supporting text specify the network equations and numerical settings. The inspected publication records did not identify a public simulation-code package. For Ben-Shushan et al. (2022), public MATLAB code and deposited recordings support a more direct route. Mainali et al. (2025) provide Python code and links to the source datasets. The preceding chapters identify what was checked in each package and where the inspected defaults differ from the publication.
L0010: 
L0011: [A successful program run establishes that the chosen program executed. Agreement with a paper also requires matching its model, parameters, measurements, and uncertainty.]{.key-statement} The simulation summaries here are source-based explanations. The guide has not rerun the authors' full simulations or reprocessed their animal recordings.
L0012: 
L0013: One further distinction matters for Part III. A computer can calculate a neural model, calculate a proposed transistor circuit, or collect measurements from a fabricated chip. Sun et al. (2026) report post-layout hardware simulations. Pehle et al. (2022) describe physical analog neural circuits. Both can address memory, but their outputs support different claims about an electronic device. The [hardware comparison](ch-neuromorphic-systems.html) keeps these cases separate.
L0014: 
L0015: ## Tools should match the operation being tested {#sec:SIMULATION-TOOLS-SOFTWARE}
L0016: 
L0017: The authors' implementation is the first choice when the goal is to reproduce their figures. Other libraries are useful when the goal is to isolate a mechanism or change a model. The following tools have distinct roles. The optional tools are suggestions for a new implementation, not claims about what all three papers used.
L0018: 
L0019: <div class="table-responsive">
L0020: 
L0021: {{block:tbl:SIMULATION-TOOLS}}
L0022: 
L0023: </div>
L0024: 
L0025: NumPy's `Generator` supplies random samples for a Python implementation. To repeat a sequence using a [random seed](ch-simulating-eye.html#sec-simulating-eye-path), record the generator, software versions, and order of calls as well as the seed. A seed alone does not guarantee identical results after changing the program or library version.[^simtools-numpy]
L0026: 
L0027: SciPy's `solve_ivp` integrates ordinary differential equations from an initial state. It can handle deterministic decay or a deterministic rate model. A stochastic spiking model also needs rules for generating events and applying their jumps. A Brownian-noise model needs a numerical method with the appropriate random increments. Passing freshly drawn noise into an adaptive deterministic solver does not, by itself, specify either model.[^simtools-ode]
L0028: 
L0029: For a spatial firing map, SciPy's `ndimage.label` identifies connected groups of nonzero array entries. Its connectivity rule determines which neighboring samples belong to one field. Diagonal contact can merge two regions under one rule and leave them separate under another. That choice changes field counts and sizes, so it belongs in the scientific method.[^simtools-label]
L0030: 
L0031: Brian 2 is an optional simulator for spiking neural networks, with equation-based neuron definitions and event handling. Its convenience does not make a default neuron model equivalent to the papers' models. A replacement must preserve the actual state variables, rate rule, spike statistics, and synaptic update before its results can be compared.[^simtools-brian]
L0032: 
L0033: ## Time and space resolution must preserve the modeled physics {#sec:SIMULATION-TOOLS-RESOLUTION}
L0034: 
L0035: A simulated trajectory is recorded at finitely many times, and a generated field is sampled at finitely many positions. [[**Discretization**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} replaces a continuous variable such as time or position by a finite set of sample points.]{.definition-explanation} The resulting numerical approximation can introduce its own drift, missed spikes, or merged fields.
L0036: 
L0037: For a time simulation, reduce the update interval while preserving physical rates and time constants. Compare the estimated diffusion coefficient, correlation function, and any drift of the mean. Separately change the interval at which results are saved: a small internal update does not recover information discarded by sparse output sampling. For a spatial simulation, reduce the grid spacing while preserving the physical correlation length and domain. Then examine field counts, size distributions, and the smallest features that remain resolved.
L0038: 
L0039: A [[**convergence check**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} tests whether the quantity being reported changes acceptably little when the numerical resolution is refined.]{.definition-explanation} The acceptable difference depends on the scientific comparison and the uncertainty from repeated random realizations. A smooth-looking trajectory is not a convergence check.
L0040: 
L0041: <div class="publisher-worked-calculation publisher-fold-always">
L0042: 
L0043: **Example: a smaller time step must keep the same diffusion per second.**
L0044: 
L0045: Consider the guide's reduced Brownian model, with diffusion coefficient $D=0.5$ deg$^2$/s. Over a step of duration $h$, the angular increment has variance $2Dh$. At $h=0.01$ s, its standard deviation is $0.1^\circ$. Reducing the step to $0.0025$ s makes the standard deviation $0.05^\circ$.
L0046: 
L0047: There are now four times as many steps per second, each with one quarter of the variance. The variance accumulated in one second remains $1$ deg$^2$. If the increment's standard deviation were reduced by four instead of two, that variance would fall to $0.25$ deg$^2$. The program would be simulating a different noise strength.
L0048: 
L0049: This calculation concerns Gaussian increments of the reduced model. A network driven by discrete spikes instead generates events according to its firing-rate rule and applies the specified synaptic jumps.
L0050: 
L0051: </div>
L0052: 
L0053: The spatial counterpart is a field defined in meters whose grid spacing is halved. Its correlation scale in grid samples must double to represent the same physical length. Keeping a filter width fixed in samples would change the model while appearing to improve resolution.
L0054: 
L0055: ## A useful reproduction saves the choices that determine the conclusion {#sec:SIMULATION-TOOLS-RECORD}
L0056: 
L0057: To reproduce one panel, identify its target quantity before running the model. Figure 2 of Burak and Fiete (2012) tests memory diffusion, whereas a field-size histogram in Mainali et al. (2025) tests a spatial distribution. The following record connects a computation to the claim it can support:
L0058: 
L0059: 1. **Source and target:** paper, correction if applicable, figure or equation, code revision, and the statistic being compared.
L0060: 2. **Model and inputs:** equations, initial state, boundaries, noise law, and which parameters are measured, fitted, or chosen.
L0061: 3. **Numerical choices:** update rule, time or spatial resolution, output sampling, trial count, and random-generator settings.
L0062: 4. **Measurement:** decoder or field detector, filtering, discarded transients, normalization, and units.
L0063: 5. **Comparison:** published prediction or dataset, uncertainty over independent repeats, and the effect of refining numerical resolution.
L0064: 
L0065: [[**Monte Carlo simulation**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} estimates a quantity by repeating a calculation with random samples.]{.definition-explanation} Its uncertainty depends on the effective number of independent repeats. Hundreds of overlapping time windows from one eye trace are not hundreds of independent trajectories. Many fields from one generated map can also share the same underlying random realization. The unit of repetition should match the uncertainty being estimated.
L0066: 
L0067: A practical progression is to test one deterministic decay or one generated field, then a few repeated runs, and then the published parameter set. Saving the inputs and intermediate measurements makes a disagreement diagnosable. A mismatch in the final plot can then be traced to the dynamics, the measurement rule, or the comparison itself.
L0068: 
L0069: [The common neurophysics question is which physical assumption produces the measured pattern, and whether that pattern remains after reasonable changes to the numerical implementation.]{.key-statement} Software supplies controlled experiments on a model. The link to an animal or a fabricated circuit still comes from the evidence used to constrain and test that model.
L0070: 
L0071: [^simtools-numpy]: NumPy developers. *Random Generator*, NumPy 2.5 documentation. [Generator and reproducibility guidance](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/random/generator.html). Retrieved October 30, 2026. The guide recommends this for an optional Python reconstruction.
L0072: 
L0073: [^simtools-ode]: SciPy developers. *scipy.integrate.solve_ivp*, SciPy 1.18.0 documentation. [Ordinary differential equation solver](https://docs.scipy.org/doc/scipy/reference/generated/scipy.integrate.solve_ivp.html). Retrieved October 30, 2026.
L0074: 
L0075: [^simtools-label]: SciPy developers. *scipy.ndimage.label*, SciPy 1.18.0 documentation. [Connected-component labeling](https://docs.scipy.org/doc/scipy/reference/generated/scipy.ndimage.label.html). Retrieved October 30, 2026.
L0076: 
L0077: [^simtools-brian]: Brian developers. *Brian 2 documentation*, version 2.11.0. [Spiking-network simulator](https://brian2.readthedocs.io/en/stable/). Retrieved October 30, 2026.
